# Latin America in the Twentieth Century - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches how a group of states that had been formally independent since the 1820s spent the following century discovering that formal sovereignty and real sovereignty are two different things, and it teaches that discovery as a mechanism rather than as a grievance. It begins from the structural inheritance - concentrated landholding, narrow export economies, weak civilian institutions and armies that had learned to arbitrate politics - and shows how each of those inheritances produced a specific twentieth-century consequence. It teaches the four structural problems in the exact form the core account states them: economic underdevelopment and single-commodity exposure, with Chile on copper, Cuba on sugar and tobacco, Bolivia on tin and 80 per cent of Bolivian revenue coming from tin exports in the 1950s; a massive population rise and the shanty towns that ringed the cities; political systems without a democratic tradition except in Chile, dominated by wealthy landowners and run by military dictators; and heavy foreign investment whose profits left the country, with the United Fruit Company as the biggest landowner in Guatemala and Chilean copper and Cuban sugar under United States control. It teaches how United States power actually operated - not as government but as the setting of limits - through aid with conditions, the Alliance for Progress of 1961, covert operations, proxy invasion, economic strangulation and, in the last resort, troops; and it teaches the recorded list of interventions to remove governments deemed unacceptable: Guatemala in 1954, Cuba in 1961, Brazil in 1964, the Dominican Republic in 1965, Chile between 1970 and 1973, Nicaragua from 1979 onwards, Panama in 1989 and Haiti in 1994. It teaches the Good Neighbor interlude of the 1930s as the exception that shows how much discretion Washington normally retained. It teaches Mexico completely: the Diaz dictatorship from 1876 to 1910, the fraudulent election that broke it, the confused revolutionary decade, the Constitution of 1917, the party that became the Institutional Revolutionary Party and held power until 2000, Cardenas and the oil nationalisation of 1938, the post-war growth years, the killings at Tlatelolco on 2 October 1968, the debt emergency of 1982 with its 70 per cent devaluation and its $96 billion of overseas debt, the North American Free Trade Agreement from January 1994, the Zapatista rising of 1 January 1994, the assassination of Colosio, the peso collapse and the alternation of 2000 that ended 71 years of single-party rule. It teaches populism as a distinct political form through Vargas in Brazil and Peron in Argentina, using the core account's own judgement that where European fascist movements destroyed labour movements, the Latin American leaders they inspired created them. It teaches the economic arc as one continuous sequence - commodity dependence, import-substituting industrialisation, external borrowing at its height from 1973 to 1982, the doubling of American interest rates between 1979 and 1981, the $368 billion owed by 1985, Brazil's suspension of interest payments in February 1987, and the austerity and market opening that followed. It teaches Guatemala from the general strike of October 1944 through the Arevalo reforms, the Arbenz land reform, the Caracas resolution carried by 17 votes to one, the invasion from Honduras and Nicaragua in June 1954, and the 36 years of civil war that killed some 200,000 people, well over half of them Mayan Indians. It teaches Cuba as five recorded causes, one seizure of power on 1 January 1959, one sequence of nationalisation and counter-embargo, one failed invasion at the Bay of Pigs, one missile crisis handed to its owner, one genuinely mixed social record and one survival after 1991. It teaches Chile as the other half of the same question: the only Marxist government ever voted into office, its 36 per cent mandate, its policies, its economic unravelling, the coup of September 1973, the 17 years of Pinochet with around 3,000 killed or disappeared, and the arrest in London in 1998. It teaches the military regimes of Brazil and Argentina, the recorded charges against the Argentine junta, and the coordinated repression of the Southern Cone. It teaches Nicaragua from the marines of 1912 through the Somoza dynasty, the Sandinista victory of 1979, the land redistribution of 5 million acres to about 100,000 families, the Contra war, the International Court of Justice ruling, the Arias peace plan and the election of 1990. It teaches the social dimension that most answers omit: liberation theology and the murder of Archbishop Oscar Romero in 1980, the Zapatistas, the coca growers' union that became a governing party, Bolsa Familia, and the women who reached the presidency from Chamorro in 1990 to Rousseff in 2011. It teaches human rights, memory and transition as a distinct examinable field. It teaches the post-1998 left turn, ALBA, UNASUR, the Bank of the South and the diversification towards China. And it closes by teaching the explanatory argument itself - dependency against domestic institutional failure - and the answer architecture that converts a century into marks. Three boundaries are held throughout and stated in the lessons: the nineteenth-century independence wars belong to the independence owner, the global Cold War system belongs to the Cold War owner, and the thematic world economy belongs to the world-economy owner.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of the chapter that supplies most of the evidence, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter narrates the region's problems, then its solutions, then its crisis, then five countries one after another. That arrangement produces the commonest failing script on this topic: a string of country notes with a sentence about the United States bolted on. Here the structural inheritance comes first, then the four problems stated as mechanisms, then the instruments of external power - so that by the time the first country appears the reader already possesses the two variables whose interaction the whole century is about. The country cases are then not narratives but experiments. Mexico is placed first because it is the earliest and because it settles the question of what a successful revolution does after it wins. Guatemala, Cuba and Chile are placed in that order because together they form a controlled comparison: reform reversed, revolution survived, reform destroyed by an alliance of domestic opposition and external pressure. The debt crisis comes after all of them, because it is the moment at which the external instrument changes from the gun to the loan agreement, and that change cannot be appreciated until the reader has seen the gun used. The social lesson comes late, not because it matters least, but because a church, a union and a peasant federation only become visible as political actors once the formal political system has been shown failing. The argument lesson comes last, because a theory offered before the evidence becomes a template, and a template is exactly what this topic punishes.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Four labels run through this session. Separating them matters here because this is a part of the syllabus where indignation is cheap, where confident sentences about imperialism and about corruption are both easy to write, and where almost none of the confident sentences carry a source.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two knowledge files held for this topic, the single core world-history account read for this session, the neighbouring-topic files consulted for boundaries, the verified question ledgers, or one of the standard connecting facts checked and listed in the final ledger | Assert it. Dates, named individuals, institutions, recorded policies and stated outcomes can be written flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated official item, used exactly once in this session and limited there in the text itself | One dated sentence at most, and never as the substance of a history answer |
| **Attributed estimate** | A figure for deaths, killings, disappearances or torture, always carried with the name of whoever produced it | Attribute it in the sentence. Never state such a figure as an unowned fact |

That fourth label has no symbol because it must be visible in the sentence itself. ⚠️ This topic contains at least five episodes of mass political violence, and in every one of them the figures in circulation come from commissions, courts, historians and governments that were counting different things over different periods. An answer that writes "the dictatorships killed hundreds of thousands" has asserted something no careful source states in that form; an answer that writes "a human rights commission charged the Argentine junta with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people" has made a checkable statement and has demonstrated exactly the discipline a 20-mark question rewards.

Seven disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where the vocabulary is emotive and precision is rare.

**The two-variables discipline.** ⚠️ The organising question is never "was the cause internal or external?" It is "how did the internal and external causes hold each other in place?" ✅ The core account states both halves without ranking them: the region's states were dominated by groups of wealthy landowners and run by military dictators, **and** the United States had no hesitation in intervening in any country whose government was deemed unacceptable. ⚠️ The mechanism that joins them is specific and must be named: oligarchic landholding produced governments that could not survive a genuine redistribution, so those governments needed an external guarantor; and the external guarantor, having guaranteed them, preserved the landholding. ⚠️ An answer that chooses one variable has not simplified the topic; it has misdescribed it.

**The hegemony-not-empire discipline.** ⚠️ Latin America in the twentieth century was not colonised, and saying that it was will cost marks. ✅ Every state in the region had been formally independent since the 1820s; ✅ every one of them had its own army, its own elections at least intermittently, its own parties and its own social conflicts. ⚠️ What the United States possessed was not sovereignty over them but the power to set the outer limit of what their sovereign governments could do - and, when that limit was crossed, the power to have the government changed. ⚠️ The precise word is **hegemony**: predominance exercised through aid, trade, credit, intelligence services and local allies, rather than through administration.

**The agency discipline.** ⚠️ The corollary of the previous rule is that local actors did the deciding, the reforming, the killing and the resisting. ✅ The record states the control flatly: domestic elites, armies, parties and social movements mattered throughout. ✅ The Guatemalan army did not defend Arbenz but did not carry out the coup either; ✅ the Chilean opposition organised a massive strike before the generals moved; ✅ the Brazilian army removed Goulart and then governed for twenty years on its own terms; ✅ the Nicaraguan electorate removed the Sandinistas in 1990 and returned them in 2006. ❌ No sentence in this session describes a Latin American government as a puppet, and no answer built from this session should.

**The attribution discipline.** ⚠️ Every mortality, disappearance and torture figure in this session travels with the name of whoever produced it and the period it covers. ❌ No figure anywhere in this session is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated, combined across sources or converted from one bracket to another. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to named governments, agencies, armies, militias and leaders - never to a nation or a people.

**The sequence discipline.** ⚠️ The region's economic history is one sequence, not four separate topics, and the examiner's close option sits on the joints. ✅ Commodity dependence came first; ⚠️ import-substituting industrialisation was the attempt to escape it; ✅ external borrowing, at its height from 1973 until 1982, sustained the attempt; ✅ the doubling of American interest rates between 1979 and 1981 broke it; ✅ austerity, rescheduling and market opening followed. ⚠️ An answer that names the policies without the sequence has described the furniture and missed the room.

**The variation discipline.** ✅ The sources state the trap in one line: Latin America was not politically uniform. ✅ Brazil industrialised under a military government; ✅ Mexico institutionalised a revolution into a single party; ✅ Chile had a democratic tradition and lost it; ✅ Cuba broke with the United States and survived; ✅ Guatemala tried to reform and was reversed. ⚠️ Any answer that treats "Latin America" as one political unit has already lost marks, and the fastest way to demonstrate the opposite is to place two cases side by side and explain why they diverged.

**The boundary discipline.** Three neighbouring topics own material this session touches constantly, and the handovers are stated in the lessons rather than hidden. The wars of independence, the social hierarchy of the colonial order and caudillismo as a nineteenth-century form belong to the independence owner; this session uses them only as the inheritance it starts from. The global Cold War - containment, the missile crisis as a superpower confrontation, alliances, deterrence and detente - belongs to the Cold War owner; this session teaches what the Cold War did to Latin American states and stops at the point where the confrontation becomes a superpower story. The thematic world economy, North-South relations, aid and globalisation belong to the world-economy owner; this session teaches the region's own debt sequence and hands the general framework across.

## Roadmap

Twenty-one lessons. The count is set by the number of distinct causal problems this topic contains, not by a template, and the lessons are deliberately unequal: Lesson 15 on the debt crisis and Lesson 17 on society from below are long because the evidence is dense and contested, while Lesson 8 on Guatemala is tight because it teaches one mechanism with unusual clarity.

| # | Stage | Lesson | What it settles |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Foundation | The inheritance: what independence left behind | Why the century began with constrained sovereignty |
| 2 | Foundation | The four structural problems, as mechanisms | What actually made the region unstable |
| 3 | Foundation | How United States power worked | Hegemony, its instruments and its limits |
| 4 | Core | Mexico I: revolution, 1876-1920 | How the century's first revolution began |
| 5 | Core | Mexico II: the institutional order, 1920-2000 | What a revolution becomes when it wins |
| 6 | Core | Populism: Vargas and Peron | The distinctive Latin American political form |
| 7 | Core | The economic arc: commodities to industry | Why industrialisation was attempted and why it strained |
| 8 | Core | Guatemala, 1944-1996 | Reform reversed, and what the reversal cost |
| 9 | Core | Cuba I: why Castro came to power | The five recorded causes, in order |
| 10 | Core | Cuba II: revolution into alignment | How a domestic revolution became an international crisis |
| 11 | Core | Cuba III: the record and the cost | What the revolution achieved and what it did not |
| 12 | Core | Chile, 1970-1990 | The constitutional road and its destruction |
| 13 | Core | Military regimes and the national security state | Brazil, Argentina and coordinated repression |
| 14 | Core | Nicaragua, 1912-1990 | Revolution meeting sustained external hostility |
| 15 | Core | The debt crisis of the 1980s | How finance replaced force |
| 16 | Core | The neoliberal turn and its politics | Why the reforms were resented even when they worked |
| 17 | Core | Society from below | Church, labour, peasantry, indigenous peoples, women |
| 18 | Advanced | Human rights, memory and transition | The examinable afterlife of dictatorship |
| 19 | Advanced | The challenge to United States domination | The post-1998 left turn and regional institutions |
| 20 | Advanced | Explaining the century | Dependency against domestic institutional failure |
| 21 | Advanced | Revolution, reform and the answer architecture | Converting a century into marks |

Practice is embedded: every lesson ends with a concept check, and cumulative checks follow each major block. Verified question routing, original Mains practice with model answers, remediation, master maps and consolidated register notes come after the lesson sequence.

## Lesson 1 - The inheritance: what independence left behind, and why the twentieth century began with constrained sovereignty

Progress: 1/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the common colonial and independence inheritance; language, religion and elite continuity; concentrated landholding and the estate system; the army as political arbiter; the hemispheric declarations of 1823 and 1904 that framed outside involvement; and the exact handover to the independence owner

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's summary of events for the Latin America chapter, which defines the region, records independence from Spain and, for Brazil, from Portugal in the early nineteenth century, and states the shared difficulties of industrial and agricultural underdevelopment, poverty, illiteracy and unstable political systems
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the inheritance, item by item, and what each one became

       political class                     could be changed from outside
       - Iberian rule left no              - "no tradition of democracy,
         tradition of colonial               except in Chile"
         self-government                   - states "run by military
                                             dictators (caudillos)"

   [2] Land concentrated in very           Peru "dominated by huge
       few hands                           estates whose owners were
       - the estate, worked by             all-powerful, and who ruled
         dependent labour                  their peasants like feudal
                                           monarchs"
                                         -> narrow domestic market
                                         -> a political class that could
                                            not survive redistribution

   [3] Economies built to export           "heavily dependent for exports
       one or two commodities              on a limited range of products,
       to Europe                           sometimes even a single
                                           commodity"
                                         -> Chile: copper
                                         -> Cuba: sugar and tobacco
                                         -> Bolivia: tin (80% of revenue
                                            in the 1950s)

   [4] Armies made by the wars of          the army as permanent arbiter:
       liberation, with prestige           it blocks reform or removes
       and no civilian master              the reformer
                                         -> Guatemala 1954, Bolivia 1964,
                                            Brazil 1964, Argentina 1966,
                                            Chile 1973

   [5] A hemisphere declared               1823: no new European
       closed to European                 colonisation in the Americas
       recolonisation                     1904: the USA claims an
                                          "international police power"
                                        -> the outside power that matters
                                           is now continental, not
                                           transatlantic

⚠️ Read the right-hand column downward and one thing becomes visible that a chronological narrative hides. The twentieth-century problem is not that Latin America was still ruled from Europe. It was not. The problem is that five separate inheritances - legal, social, economic, military and geopolitical - each independently narrowed what a Latin American government could do, and they narrowed it in mutually reinforcing ways.

### 1. Start with the puzzle, not with the indignation

Here is the puzzle that organises the whole topic. ✅ The states of Latin America gained their independence from Spain - and, in the case of Brazil, from Portugal - in the early nineteenth century. ⚠️ That makes them, by a wide margin, the oldest post-colonial states in the world. India became independent in 1947; Ghana in 1957; most of Africa in the decade that followed. Latin America had a head start of well over a century.

And yet. ✅ The core account's own summary of the region records that they were underdeveloped both industrially and agriculturally, that they had massive problems of poverty and illiteracy and unstable political systems, that revolutions, coups and assassinations were commonplace, and that progress occurred only very slowly and unevenly.

⚠️ So the question is not "why were they colonised?" - they were not, in the twentieth century. The question is much sharper and much more interesting: **why did more than a century of formal independence produce so little of the substance of independence?** ⚠️ Every good answer on this topic is a version of that question, and the answer is always the same in structure: because independence changed who held sovereignty without changing the social and economic structures that determine what sovereignty can be used for.

### 2. What was transferred, and what was not

⚠️ The independence owner teaches the wars themselves, the leaders, the campaigns and the five distinct routes to independence, and this session does not repeat them. ⚠️ What it takes from that owner is one finding and one mechanism.

✅ The finding is that independence ended Iberian sovereignty far faster than it transformed social power. ✅ Creole elites - the American-born Spanish - replaced peninsular administrators at the top of a hierarchy that otherwise stayed intact: the land, the labour system, the racial hierarchy and, outside Haiti, slavery all survived the transfer.

✅ The mechanism is **caudillismo**, and it is source-grounded rather than invented: long wars of liberation left weak or contested constitutional institutions, regional landowners funded and commanded personal armed followings, personal loyalty substituted for institutional legitimacy, and the result was rule by the strongman ratified by force and clientage. ✅ The core account used in this session carries the same category into the twentieth century in plain language: states were dominated by groups of wealthy landowners and run by military dictators, whom it names **caudillos**.

⚠️ Notice what this does to the twentieth century. The army does not enter politics in 1930 or 1964 as an intruder. It has been the arbiter of politics since the 1820s. ⚠️ What changes in the twentieth century is not that the army intervenes but *what it intervenes against*: in the nineteenth century it fought rival strongmen, and in the twentieth it blocked social reform. ⚠️ That shift - from personal rivalry to class defence - is the single most useful thing to say about Latin American militarism in a 15- or 20-mark answer.

### 3. The estate, and why land is the master variable

✅ Agriculture remained backward because peasant labour was so plentiful and cheap that wealthy estate owners had no need to go to the trouble of modernizing. ✅ Peru, for example, was dominated by huge estates whose owners were all-powerful, and who ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs.

⚠️ Read that second sentence slowly, because it contains three separate political facts, not one agricultural one.

⚠️ First, it is a statement about **markets**. If most of the rural population is poor and dependent, there is no mass domestic demand, which means that any attempt at industrialisation will run into a small internal market almost immediately. Lesson 7 will show exactly that happening.

⚠️ Second, it is a statement about **technology**. Where labour is cheap and abundant, there is no incentive to mechanise; where there is no mechanisation, productivity stays low; where productivity stays low, the estate needs the labour to remain cheap. The system is self-locking.

⚠️ Third, and decisively, it is a statement about **power**. An owner who rules his peasants like a feudal monarch is not merely rich; he is a local sovereign. ⚠️ Any government that proposes land reform is therefore not adjusting a policy but dismantling a system of local government - which is why, in this region, land reform and coups appear together so often that the pairing is almost a law. Guatemala in 1954, Chile after 1970 and Nicaragua after 1979 are three versions of the same collision, and you will meet all three in this session.

### 4. One commodity, and the politics of a price

✅ Many countries found themselves heavily dependent for exports on a limited range of products, sometimes even a single commodity. ✅ A fall in the world price of that commodity would be a major disaster. ✅ Chile relied on copper, Cuba on sugar and tobacco, and Bolivia on tin; during the 1950s, in fact, 80 per cent of all Bolivia's revenue came from tin exports.

⚠️ The examinable point is not the list. It is what the list does to a government's calendar. ⚠️ When four-fifths of revenue comes from one export, the state's budget is set in a commodity exchange in another country. A price fall is not an economic event followed by a political one; it *is* a political event. Teachers go unpaid, subsidies lapse, the currency weakens, imports become dearer, and a government that did nothing wrong finds itself facing riots.

⚠️ Hold on to this, because it recurs constantly. ✅ Venezuela's revenue fell with world oil prices in the early 1980s; ✅ Mexico's oil revenues collapsed suddenly in 1986; ✅ Ecuador's 1998 crisis began with a fall in the world price of oil, its main export, after which inflation rose to over 40 per cent and the poverty rate reached around 70 per cent. ⚠️ Three different decades, three different countries, one mechanism.

### 5. The hemispheric frame: 1823 and 1904

⚠️ One element of the inheritance is not social or economic but geopolitical, and it needs stating precisely because it is constantly misquoted.

✅ In December 1823, in his annual message to Congress, President James Monroe declared that the American continents, by the free and independent condition which they had assumed and maintained, were henceforth not to be considered as subjects for future colonization by any European powers, and that the United States would consider any attempt by European powers to extend their system to any portion of the hemisphere as dangerous to its peace and safety. ⚠️ This is a **negative** doctrine: it says what Europe may not do. It confers no right of intervention on anybody.

✅ In his annual message of December 1904, President Theodore Roosevelt added the clause that changed its meaning: chronic wrongdoing, or an impotence which results in a general loosening of the ties of civilized society, might in the Americas as elsewhere ultimately require intervention by some civilized nation, and adherence to the Monroe Doctrine might force the United States, however reluctantly, in flagrant cases of such wrongdoing or impotence, to the exercise of **an international police power**. ⚠️ That is the **positive** clause, and it is the one that matters for this topic, because it converts a warning addressed to Europe into a self-assigned licence addressed to the hemisphere.

⚠️ Two cautions, both worth marks. ⚠️ First, do not attribute the police-power language to Monroe; it is Roosevelt's, eighty-one years later, and a close option will be built on exactly that gap. ⚠️ Second, do not treat either declaration as the *cause* of twentieth-century intervention. ⚠️ They are the vocabulary in which intervention was justified, not the reason it happened; the reasons, as Lesson 3 will show, were strategic, commercial and ideological, and they varied by case.

### Comparative note: why this is not "Latin America was still a colony"

⚠️ A very common answer treats the region as effectively colonial throughout the twentieth century. The evidence does not support that, and saying so precisely is a discriminator.

✅ These were independent republics with their own constitutions, armies, parties and elections. ✅ Chile had a tradition of democracy, a constitution modelled in part on that of the United States, and an army that played little part in politics before 1973. ✅ Mexico nationalised its oil industry in 1938 against the open opposition of both the United States and the United Kingdom, and made the nationalisation stick. ✅ Venezuela raised its share of oil revenues to 60 per cent in 1958 and created a state oil company in 1976. ⚠️ None of that is possible in a colony.

⚠️ The accurate formulation - and it is worth memorising in this exact shape - is that these were **sovereign states with constrained sovereignty**. ⚠️ They could do a great deal; there was a specific band of policy, touching land, foreign-owned assets and external alignment, within which action attracted an external response. ⚠️ The century's tragedies cluster inside that band, and its successes mostly lie outside it.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The General Studies Paper I syllabus covers world history from the eighteenth century onward, including colonization and decolonization, and political philosophies such as capitalism, socialism and communism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ Latin America is the syllabus's best available case of what happens *after* formal decolonisation is complete - which is why it is examined through the effects clause rather than the colonisation clause.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This lesson is almost never examined alone. It is the first paragraph of an answer on why the region remained unstable, on the nature of United States domination, or on whether independence brought real freedom. ⚠️ The mark is earned by compressing it to three or four sentences that establish *structural continuity across independence*, not by narrating the wars of liberation again.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that Latin America was colonised in the twentieth century. ✅ It was not; the states had been independent since the 1820s.
2. ⚠️ Attributing the "international police power" phrase to the Monroe Doctrine. ✅ It belongs to the Roosevelt Corollary of 1904.
3. ⚠️ Naming individual nineteenth-century caudillos. ✅ The category and its mechanism are source-grounded; no list of individuals is held here, and inventing one is a direct factual risk.
4. ⚠️ Treating land concentration as an agrarian detail. ✅ It is the master variable, because it simultaneously suppresses the domestic market, blocks mechanisation and creates a political class that cannot accept reform.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Latin America comprises South America, Central America including Mexico, and Caribbean islands such as Cuba, Jamaica and Hispaniola, which is divided into Haiti and the Dominican Republic.
2. ✅ Independence from Spain, and for Brazil from Portugal, came in the early nineteenth century; Spanish is spoken in most of these countries and Portuguese in Brazil.
3. ✅ Shared difficulties: industrial and agricultural underdevelopment, massive poverty and illiteracy, unstable political systems; revolutions, coups and assassinations commonplace; progress slow and uneven.
4. ✅ Agriculture stayed backward because plentiful cheap peasant labour removed the incentive to modernise; Peru was dominated by huge estates whose owners ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs.
5. ✅ Single-commodity exposure: Chile on copper, Cuba on sugar and tobacco, Bolivia on tin, with 80 per cent of Bolivian revenue from tin exports in the 1950s.
6. ✅ No tradition of democracy except in Chile; states dominated by wealthy landowners and run by military dictators, termed caudillos.
7. ✅ Caudillismo is a source-grounded category - personalist rule by a strongman resting on armed clientage - and is the region's characteristic nineteenth-century political form; individual caudillos are not named here.
8. ✅ Monroe, December 1823: the American continents are not to be considered subjects for future European colonization, and any attempt to extend the European system to the hemisphere would be dangerous to United States peace and safety.
9. ✅ Roosevelt Corollary, December 1904: chronic wrongdoing or impotence may force the United States to the exercise of an international police power.
10. ⚠️ The five inheritances - sovereignty without a civilian political class, concentrated land, single-commodity exports, an army habituated to arbitration, and a declared hemispheric sphere - are the topic's starting conditions, not its causes in isolation.
11. ⚠️ Master formulation: independence transferred sovereignty without transforming social power, so the twentieth century produced sovereign states with constrained sovereignty rather than colonies.
12. ⚠️ Boundary held: the independence wars, their leaders and their five routes belong to the independence owner; this session begins where that owner ends.

### Concept check

**Question:** Latin America had been independent for roughly a century longer than most of Asia and Africa. Explain, using structural rather than moral terms, why that head start produced so little advantage by 1945.

**Model answer:** Because independence changed the holders of sovereignty without changing the structures that determine what sovereignty can achieve. The land system survived: estates worked by plentiful cheap labour gave owners no incentive to modernise and gave the countryside no purchasing power, so there was neither an efficient agriculture nor a domestic market for industry. The export structure survived: economies remained heavily dependent on a narrow range of products, sometimes a single commodity, so state revenue was set by world prices - Bolivia drew 80 per cent of its revenue from tin exports in the 1950s. The political structure survived: there was no tradition of democracy except in Chile, states were dominated by wealthy landowners and run by military dictators, and the armies made by the wars of liberation remained the real arbiters of politics. A century of formal independence therefore accumulated no institutional capital, because the institutions that would have accumulated it did not exist. When outside pressure resumed in a continental rather than a transatlantic form after 1904, it met states that were legally sovereign and structurally fragile - which is precisely the combination that produces constrained sovereignty.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from this that Latin America was effectively a colony. It was not, and the difference is examinable. These states nationalised foreign assets, created state companies, fought wars, changed constitutions and conducted their own foreign policies. What they could not do without external consequence was act inside a narrow band touching land, foreign-owned property and external alignment. An answer that flattens that distinction into "colonialism by another name" loses the very mechanism the question is asking about.

## Lesson 2 - The four structural problems, stated as mechanisms rather than as complaints

Progress: 2/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: economic underdevelopment and single-commodity exposure; the population rise, urban migration and the shanty towns; political systems without a democratic tradition and the army's veto; foreign investment and the repatriation of profit; and the recorded attempts at a remedy

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the era of United States domination, which sets out under four numbered heads the problems facing the countries of Latin America, followed by its section on attempted solutions
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four problems, four mechanisms, four political consequences

| # | The problem as recorded | ⚠️ The mechanism inside it | ⚠️ The political consequence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Economically underdeveloped industrially and agriculturally; factory industry existed but was well below the level of Europe, the USA and Japan; markets were restricted because the vast majority were too poverty-stricken to provide purchasing power, and exporting was difficult because of competition from advanced industrial nations; heavy dependence on a narrow export range; agriculture backward because cheap labour removed the incentive to modernise | Poverty suppresses demand, which suppresses industry, which keeps employment low, which keeps poverty in place; meanwhile one commodity price sets the national budget | A government cannot buy consent through growth, so it must either redistribute - and confront the owners - or repress |
| 2 | ✅ Massive rise in population, driven by advances in medicine and hygiene and by the Roman Catholic Church's refusal to promote birth control; holdings too small to support large families; scarce jobs in the cities; almost all major cities ringed by improvised shanty towns, known as favelas in Brazil, without water, sewage disposal or electricity; the gap between rich and poor widened | Rural push plus urban pull produces a new urban mass that is neither peasant nor industrial working class, concentrated where it is politically visible | Creates the constituency for populism and, later, for revolution - an audience that can be mobilised quickly and cheaply |
| 3 | ✅ Political systems, for the most part, inadequate; no tradition of democracy except in Chile; states dominated by wealthy landowners and run by military dictators; where post-war democratic governments attempted reform, landowners used the army to block the reforms or overthrow the government - in Guatemala (**1954**, the year of the reversal; 1950 was the year Arbenz was elected), Bolivia (1964), Brazil (1964), Argentina (1966) and Chile (1973) | The army functions as a standing veto over the elected government's social policy | Elections can change the government but not the policy band; democracy exists but is bounded |
| 4 | ✅ Heavy foreign investment in industry and agriculture caused problems because much of the profit was taken out of the countries; most of the oil in Bolivia and Venezuela was extracted by American-owned companies; the United Fruit Company was the biggest landowner in Guatemala; Chilean copper mines and Cuban sugar plantations were also under United States control | Investment raises output without raising national income proportionately, because the surplus leaves | Creates a permanent nationalist grievance that any politician can use, and makes nationalisation the obvious remedy - and the obvious trigger |

⚠️ Read the third column downward. Each problem, on its own, is an economic or demographic fact. Taken together, they produce a single political situation: a state that cannot satisfy its population, cannot reform without confronting the army, and cannot capture its own surplus. ⚠️ That is the condition in which twentieth-century Latin American politics takes place, and every case in this session is a different attempt to escape it.

### 1. The first problem: an economy that cannot grow out of its poverty

Start with the trap inside the phrase "underdeveloped". ✅ The core account is careful: factory industries **did** exist, and the Second World War had acted as a stimulus because manufactured goods from Europe were almost impossible to come by. ⚠️ So this is not a pre-industrial region. It is a partly industrialised region that cannot complete the transition, and the reason it cannot is worth stating as a loop.

✅ Markets were restricted because the vast majority of people were too poverty-stricken to provide enough purchasing power. ✅ Exporting was difficult because of competition from the advanced industrial nations. ⚠️ Put those together and a domestic manufacturer faces a market too poor to buy at home and too competitive to sell abroad. ⚠️ The only way out is either to protect the home market and raise incomes inside it, or to accept permanent dependence on commodity exports. ⚠️ Lesson 7 is the story of the region choosing the first option; Lesson 15 is the story of the bill arriving.

⚠️ And beneath the industrial problem sits the agrarian one, which is its cause rather than its parallel. ✅ Agriculture remained backward because peasant labour was so plentiful and cheap that wealthy estate owners had no need to modernise. ⚠️ Cheap labour is therefore not a comparative advantage here; it is the thing that prevents the advantage from ever being realised.

### 2. The second problem: people, cities and the shanty town

✅ There was a massive rise in population, mainly because of advances in medicine and hygiene and the refusal of the Roman Catholic Church to promote birth control. ✅ Peasants found their holdings too small to support large families, but when they moved to the cities they found that jobs were scarce. ✅ Almost all the major cities were surrounded by improvised shanty towns - known as favelas in Brazil - without water, sewage disposal or electricity. ✅ The gap between rich and poor grew wider and little progress was made in eliminating poverty and illiteracy.

⚠️ This is the most under-used paragraph in the whole topic, and it deserves a mechanism of its own, because it creates a new political actor.

⚠️ A dispersed peasantry is hard to mobilise: it is scattered, illiterate, dependent on a landlord and physically distant from the capital. ⚠️ The same people, once they are living in an improvised settlement ringing the capital city, are concentrated, visible, reachable by radio and by a speech, and within walking distance of the presidential palace. ⚠️ They have not become a classical industrial proletariat - most of them have no factory job - but they have become an **urban poor with political weight**.

⚠️ That is the constituency that Vargas and Peron discovered and organised, which is Lesson 6. ⚠️ It is the constituency that Chavez recovered fifty years later; ✅ his political emergence followed his disgust when the Venezuelan government sent troops into poor neighbourhoods to put down protests in 1992. ⚠️ And it is the constituency whose existence explains why Latin American politics produces *populism* far more often than it produces classical social democracy: the mass is urban and poor but not unionised in the European sense, so it is organised by a leader rather than by a party of labour.

### 3. The third problem: democracy with a standing veto

✅ There was no tradition of democracy, except in Chile, and states were dominated by groups of wealthy landowners and run by military dictators. ✅ After the Second World War, democratic systems were introduced in some of the states. ✅ But when the newly elected governments tried to introduce reforms, they faced strong opposition from the landowners who were determined to protect their privileged positions. ✅ They were able to use the army either to block the reforms or to overthrow the reforming government. ✅ This happened in Guatemala, Bolivia (1964), Brazil (1964), Argentina (1966) and Chile (1973). ⚠️ One date in that recorded list needs correcting before it is used: the Guatemalan reversal came in **June 1954**, and **1950** was the year Arbenz was elected president, not the year he was removed.

⚠️ Five dates, five countries, one sentence - and it is the single most efficient piece of evidence in this topic. Commit it to memory as a set, because it does three jobs at once.

⚠️ First, it proves the mechanism is regional rather than anecdotal: the same sequence in five states across twenty-three years is a structure, not a coincidence.

⚠️ Second, it locates the veto correctly. ⚠️ The initiative belongs to the **landowners**; the army is the instrument. ⚠️ An answer that presents the coups as purely military ambition has missed the social content, and an answer that presents them as purely American has missed the domestic client. The coup is a joint product.

⚠️ Third, it disposes of a common confusion about democratisation. ⚠️ These countries were not undemocratic because elections were absent. ⚠️ Elections happened; what was missing was the elected government's ability to act inside the contested policy band without being removed. ⚠️ That is a precise and unusual claim, and examiners reward it.

### 4. The fourth problem: investment whose profit leaves

✅ Heavy investment by foreigners in industry and agriculture caused problems because much of the profit was taken out of the countries. ✅ Most of the oil in Bolivia and Venezuela, both potentially rich countries, was extracted by American-owned companies. ✅ The United Fruit Company was the biggest landowner in Guatemala, while Chilean copper mines and Cuban sugar plantations were also under United States control.

⚠️ Take the qualifier seriously - "both potentially rich countries". ⚠️ The complaint recorded here is not that investment was absent. It is that investment arrived, extracted, and exported the surplus, so that measured output rose while national income and state revenue did not rise proportionately.

⚠️ This creates a political asset that every reformer in this session will reach for. ⚠️ Nationalisation is the obvious remedy: it captures the surplus, it is popular, it costs nothing to announce, and it converts an economic grievance into a patriotic achievement. ✅ Cardenas nationalised Mexican oil in 1938, much to the delight of the general public; ✅ Betancourt raised Venezuela's share of oil revenues to 60 per cent; ✅ Castro nationalised American-owned estates and factories; ✅ Allende nationalised the remainder of the copper industry, textiles and banks; ✅ Chavez fully nationalised the oil industry in 2007.

⚠️ And it is simultaneously the most reliable trigger for external retaliation in the topic. ⚠️ That is the pairing to hold: **the surplus problem makes nationalisation rational, and nationalisation makes retaliation likely.** ⚠️ Most of the confrontations in this session begin exactly there.

### 5. The recorded remedies, and why they under-delivered

✅ The account records three attempted solutions, and it is worth knowing that the region and the world did try.

✅ First, international organisations were set up to help, including the Organization of American States and the Latin American Common Market of 1960, which had some success in reducing tariffs. ✅ Second, the United Nations helped by providing technical experts and holding conferences to discuss how underdeveloped nations might increase exports. ✅ Third, the United States provided massive economic aid, with President Kennedy starting the Alliance for Progress, launched in 1961, which aimed to pump billions of dollars into Latin America to enable economic and social reform to be carried out.

⚠️ The instructive part is the account's verdict, which is neither cynical nor admiring. ✅ Kennedy did genuinely want to help the poor nations of Latin America, and American aid was put to good use; ✅ but this kind of aid did not always work out for the best and sometimes created extra problems, and American motives were mixed - they hoped, by solving basic economic and social problems, to encourage the election of moderate reforming governments that would be popular enough to prevent communists from coming to power, and it was understood that much of the cash would be spent buying American goods.

⚠️ That last clause is the mechanism, and it is why aid on this model could not solve problem one. ✅ Sometimes the aid was in the form of loans made on condition that a large proportion of the loan should be spent on buying United States products; ✅ this did nothing to help the development of local industry and involved governments in large interest payments. ⚠️ An aid programme that finances imports of finished goods and leaves a debt behind has, on the account's own terms, strengthened the very dependence it was meant to relieve. ⚠️ Hold that thought until Lesson 15.

### Criticism and reply: is "structural problems" just determinism?

⚠️ **The strongest objection** to a lesson like this is that it is fatalistic. If four structural problems fix the outcome, the actors are irrelevant, the coups were inevitable, and history becomes a machine.

⚠️ **The reply has three parts, and all three are evidenced.** ⚠️ First, the same structure produced different outcomes: ✅ Chile alone had a democratic tradition and kept it until 1973, and ✅ Cuba alone succeeded in breaking with the United States and surviving. ⚠️ Structures set the odds; they do not set the result. ⚠️ Second, leaders made choices that changed trajectories: ✅ Betancourt proceeded cautiously in Venezuela, not wanting to alienate the United States in case aid was stopped, and completed his presidency; ✅ Arbenz did not proceed cautiously and was removed. ⚠️ Third, the structures themselves were changed by political action: ✅ Mexican oil was nationalised and stayed nationalised; ✅ Venezuela's oil share was raised; ✅ Brazil moved from commodity exporter to a state with, by 2011, arguably the most advanced industrial sector in Latin America, responsible for about one-third of total gross domestic product.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict**, and the one to write: the four problems explain the *recurrence* of instability across the region, not the outcome of any particular episode. For each episode you still need the actors.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson is the evidence base for any "why was the region unstable" or "assess the obstacles to development" demand, and it is the paragraph that converts a general answer into a specific one.

**How it is framed.** ✅ The core account's own end-of-chapter question asks what problems faced the countries of Latin America at the end of the Second World War and why progress in solving them was so slow. ⚠️ That is very close to the shape a General Studies question would take, and the four numbered problems plus the three attempted remedies answer it directly.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Listing the problems without their mechanisms. ✅ "Poverty" is not an answer; "poverty suppressed the domestic market, which prevented industry from completing the transition" is.
2. ⚠️ Attributing the five reform-blocking coups to the military alone. ✅ The landowners used the army; the initiative is social.
3. ⚠️ Treating the Alliance for Progress as simple generosity or as simple cynicism. ✅ The account records genuine intent, good use of some aid, mixed motives and tied loans together.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting the demographic problem. ⚠️ It is the one that creates the political constituency the rest of the topic runs on.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Problem one: industrial and agricultural underdevelopment; factory industry existed, stimulated by the Second World War, but far below Europe, the USA and Japan.
2. ✅ Restricted markets because of mass poverty; export difficulty because of competition from advanced industrial nations.
3. ✅ Narrow export base - Chile copper, Cuba sugar and tobacco, Bolivia tin at 80 per cent of revenue in the 1950s; a price fall was a major disaster.
4. ✅ Agriculture backward because plentiful cheap peasant labour removed the incentive to modernise; Peru's estate owners ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs.
5. ✅ Problem two: massive population rise from advances in medicine and hygiene and the Church's refusal to promote birth control; holdings too small; scarce urban jobs; shanty towns, called favelas in Brazil, without water, sewage disposal or electricity; widening rich-poor gap.
6. ✅ Problem three: no democratic tradition except in Chile; domination by wealthy landowners and military dictators; post-war democratic experiments blocked when landowners used the army - Guatemala 1954, Bolivia 1964, Brazil 1964, Argentina 1966, Chile 1973 (1950 was Arbenz's election year, not the reversal).
7. ✅ Problem four: foreign investment with profit repatriation; American-owned oil extraction in Bolivia and Venezuela; the United Fruit Company as Guatemala's biggest landowner; Chilean copper and Cuban sugar under United States control.
8. ✅ Remedies: the Organization of American States and the Latin American Common Market of 1960, which had some success in reducing tariffs; United Nations technical experts and conferences; the Alliance for Progress launched by Kennedy in 1961.
9. ✅ Aid verdict: genuine intent and some good use, but mixed motives, an anti-communist purpose, and loans tied to purchases of United States products that did nothing for local industry and created interest obligations.
10. ⚠️ Mechanism chain to memorise: poor market blocks industry; one commodity sets the budget; the army vetoes reform; the surplus leaves. Together they make redistribution necessary and dangerous at the same time.
11. ⚠️ The demographic shift converts a dispersed peasantry into a concentrated urban poor - the constituency for populism and for revolution alike.
12. ⚠️ Nationalisation is the rational remedy to problem four and the most reliable trigger of external retaliation; that pairing structures most of the confrontations in this topic.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "The coups of 1964 in Brazil and 1973 in Chile show that Latin American armies were simply power-hungry." Correct the claim while keeping everything true in it.

**Model answer:** The correction is that the armies were the instrument of a social veto rather than its author, and that the pattern is regional rather than personal. The record states that where post-war democratic governments tried to introduce reforms, they faced strong opposition from landowners determined to protect their privileged positions, and that the landowners were able to use the army either to block the reforms or to overthrow the reforming government - in Guatemala in 1954, Bolivia in 1964, Brazil in 1964, Argentina in 1966 and Chile in 1973. Five cases across twenty-three years make this a structure. In Brazil the trigger was Goulart's proposals to extend the vote, limit the profits multinationals could remove, open relations with the USSR, nationalise oil refineries and oppose sanctions on Cuba; in Chile it was nationalisation and accelerated land redistribution, and the opposition organised a massive strike and won the army's support before the generals moved. What is true in the candidate's claim is that armies then governed for their own purposes once installed - Brazil's ruled for twenty years - so ambition is part of the story. What is false is the sequence: the army entered because a social interest needed it to, and it stayed because nothing was left to make it leave.

**Misconception to avoid:** Substituting an external explanation for the domestic one, and saying instead that the coups were simply American operations. The United States was materially involved in both cases - President Johnson told the American ambassador that everything possible must be done to help overthrow Goulart's government, and naval vessels with ammunition and fuel were readied; the Central Intelligence Agency played a vital role in preparing the Chilean coup. But an intervention needs a domestic partner willing to act, and in both cases that partner existed and had its own reasons. An answer that erases the landowners and the officers has removed the actors who actually carried out the coup.

## Lesson 3 - How United States power actually worked: the instruments of hegemony, and their limits

Progress: 3/21 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the recorded motive for American involvement; the Good Neighbor interlude of the 1930s; the Cold War frame after 1945; the four instruments - aid and conditionality, covert and proxy action, economic strangulation, and troops; the recorded list of interventions; the Organization of American States as a multilateral instrument; and the limits of the whole system

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's summary of events for the Latin America chapter, its section on attempted solutions to the region's problems, and the cross-chapter section on further United States interventions
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the escalation ladder, and where each case sits on it

```text
  RUNG 5  DIRECT MILITARY INTERVENTION
          Dominican Republic 1965: 20,000 American troops used to
            crush an attempted comeback by a reforming president
          Panama 1989: US forces invaded and bombed; at least 2,000
            civilians killed; Noriega captured and removed for trial;
            OAS resolution "to deeply regret the military intervention
            in Panama" carried 20 votes to 1, the one being the USA
             ^
             |  used rarely; politically expensive; visible
             |
  RUNG 4  PROXY INVASION AND ARMED REBELLION
          Guatemala 1954: rebels supplied with weapons; an American-
            backed force under Colonel Castillo Armas invaded from
            Honduras and Nicaragua; American planes bombed Guatemala City
          Cuba 1961: about 1,400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs
          Nicaragua 1980s: the Contras financed by the USA
             ^
             |  deniable; needs a local force; can fail badly
             |
  RUNG 3  COVERT DESTABILISATION
          Chile 1970-3: CIA role in preparing the coup; Nixon reported
            as saying they must "make the Chilean economy scream"
          Cuba: trade embargo; bomb attacks on factories, oil refineries
            and sugar refineries; Operation Mongoose approved Nov 1961
          Brazil 1964: Johnson instructs the ambassador to do everything
            possible to help overthrow "this left-wing government";
            naval vessels readied
             ^
             |  cheapest; hardest to prove; most used
             |
  RUNG 2  ECONOMIC LEVERAGE
          Aid cut short when an unacceptable government came to power
            (Castro's Cuba; Allende's Chile) and resumed when the
            government changed
          Tied loans: a large proportion to be spent on US products
          Trade: Venezuelan steel imports cut from 550,000 tonnes a year
            (about 85% of its steel exports) to 110,000 tonnes for five
            years, announced March 1985
             ^
             |  routine; continuous; rarely described as intervention
             |
  RUNG 1  STRUCTURAL PRESENCE
          Ownership: United Fruit Company the biggest landowner in
            Guatemala; Chilean copper and Cuban sugar under US control;
            American companies held controlling interests in all Cuban
            industries, owned half the land, about three-fifths of the
            railways, all electricity production and the entire
            telephone system
          Diplomacy: the US ambassador in Havana usually referred to as
            the second most important man in Cuba

  THE POINT: the ladder is climbed only when a lower rung fails, and
  most of the century is spent on rungs 1 and 2, which is why the
  system is correctly called hegemony rather than empire.
```

⚠️ The ladder is the most useful single structure in this topic, because it converts a list of grievances into an argument. ⚠️ A weak answer says "the USA intervened repeatedly". ⚠️ A strong answer says: the United States possessed a graduated set of instruments, used the cheapest that would work, and escalated only on failure - which is precisely why its predominance survived so long at so little cost to itself.

### 1. The recorded motive, stated exactly

✅ The account states the motive without euphemism and without exaggeration. ✅ The United States provided economic aid for some of the states of Latin America, but its motives were not entirely selfless. ✅ In return the Americans expected to be able to exert political influence in order to prevent socialist or communist governments from gaining power. ✅ They had no hesitation in intervening in any Latin American country whose government was deemed unacceptable to them.

⚠️ Three words in that passage do a great deal of work, and an answer that reproduces them exactly will be more precise than one that paraphrases.

⚠️ "**Not entirely selfless**" is a genuine mixed judgement, and it should be preserved as mixed. ⚠️ "**Expected to be able to exert**" describes an expectation of influence, not a claim of sovereignty; that is the hegemony-not-empire distinction in a single verb. ⚠️ "**Deemed unacceptable to them**" places the criterion in Washington, and this is the heart of the matter: ✅ the account records elsewhere that regimes labelled communist and targeted for removal were often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests.

⚠️ That last point deserves its own sentence in an answer, because it is what separates a description from an analysis. ⚠️ If the test for intervention is what Washington deems unacceptable rather than what is in fact communist, then anti-communism is doing two jobs at once: it is a genuine strategic doctrine and it is also a category into which inconvenient governments could be placed. ✅ Goulart in Brazil is the account's own demonstration - accused of being a communist, though by no stretch of the imagination could this be taken seriously; in fact he was a millionaire landowner and a devout Roman Catholic.

### 2. The Good Neighbor interlude: the exception that measures the rule

⚠️ The twentieth century was not one continuous intervention, and pretending otherwise is a factual error as well as a weak argument. ⚠️ There was a distinct period in which Washington deliberately stood back, and knowing it is a discriminator because most candidates do not.

✅ From 1933, under Franklin Roosevelt, United States policy towards the region was reframed as the **Good Neighbor Policy**. ✅ At the Montevideo conference of American states in December 1933 the United States accepted the principle that no state has the right to intervene in the internal or external affairs of another. ✅ The Platt Amendment, which had provided the formal basis for intervention in Cuba, was abrogated in 1934. ✅ The marines were withdrawn from Nicaragua in 1933 and the occupation of Haiti was ended in 1934.

⚠️ Two inferences follow, and both are usable.

⚠️ First, the interlude shows that the interventionism of the Cold War decades was a **policy choice rather than a geographical necessity**. ⚠️ Nothing about proximity compelled it; a different administration facing a different international threat had chosen differently within living memory.

⚠️ Second, the interlude explains a specific historical fact you will meet in Lesson 4. ✅ Mexico nationalised its oil industry in 1938 and made it stick; ✅ the United States and the United Kingdom responded with a boycott of Mexican goods, and a compensation agreement was reached in November 1941. ⚠️ A boycott and a negotiated settlement, rather than a coup - that is the Good Neighbor period in action, and the contrast with Guatemala sixteen years later is the most efficient way to prove that the Cold War changed the response rather than the provocation.

### 3. The Cold War frame after 1945

✅ Following the end of the Second World War the United States was able to exercise a huge amount of economic, political and military influence, and Latin America found itself dragged into the Cold War. ✅ Republican presidents in particular were constantly suspicious that the USSR was trying to forge a Soviet-Latin American Axis, which would give the communists a clear advantage and pose a threat right on the USA's doorstep.

⚠️ "Dragged into" is the account's own phrase and it is worth keeping, because it makes the correct causal claim: the region did not generate the Cold War and mostly did not want it, but the Cold War supplied the framework through which its internal conflicts were interpreted from outside. ⚠️ A land reform in Guatemala is a domestic matter about estates; read through the Cold War frame, it becomes an outpost of a global adversary.

⚠️ This is the point at which to state the boundary. ⚠️ The global Cold War - its origins, containment, the arms race, alliance systems, deterrence, non-alignment and detente - belongs to the Cold War owner. ⚠️ This session teaches what the Cold War frame did **to Latin American states**: it converted domestic distributional conflicts into international security questions, and thereby removed them from the domestic arena where they might have been settled.

### 4. The recorded list, and how to use it

✅ United States interventions to remove "suspect" governments took place in Guatemala (1954), Cuba (**1961**), Brazil (1964), the Dominican Republic (1965), Chile (1970-3), Nicaragua (from 1979 onwards), Panama (1989) and Haiti (1994). ✅ The attempt to remove Fidel Castro from Cuba failed miserably. ⚠️ One entry in that list needs correcting before it is reproduced: the attempt to remove the Cuban government was the **Bay of Pigs landing of April 1961**. ⚠️ The confrontation of **October 1962** was the missile crisis - a superpower confrontation which ended in a United States pledge *not* to invade, and therefore not an attempt at government removal at all.

⚠️ Learn the list as a list, because reproducing eight dated cases is the fastest available proof that you know the topic. ⚠️ But use it as an argument, not as a recitation, and there are three arguments inside it.

⚠️ **The span argument.** Forty years, from 1954 to 1994. ⚠️ The Cold War ended around 1991, and two of the eight cases - Panama in 1989 and Haiti in 1994 - sit at or after its end. ⚠️ That matters because it complicates the claim that anti-communism explains everything. ✅ The account itself records that the demise of the communist "enemy" removed the Americans' justification for their constant interventions - and yet Panama and Haiti followed. ⚠️ The honest conclusion is that anti-communism was the dominant but not the sole driver.

⚠️ **The variety argument.** These eight cases are not one kind of event. ⚠️ Guatemala was a proxy invasion; Cuba in 1962 refers to a confrontation the United States did not win; Brazil was a domestic coup with external encouragement and contingency support; the Dominican Republic saw 20,000 American troops; Chile was covert preparation plus a domestic coup; Nicaragua was a decade of financed insurgency; Panama was a direct invasion; Haiti in 1991 saw a democratically elected president removed in a military coup backed by the Central Intelligence Agency, and he was removed a second time in a similar coup in 2004. ⚠️ Naming the *type* of each intervention is what separates a good answer from a list.

⚠️ **The failure argument.** ✅ The attempt to remove Castro failed miserably, and in 2012 his brother Raul was still in power. ⚠️ Hegemony is not omnipotence. ⚠️ The one case that escaped is as analytically important as the seven that did not, and Lesson 11 explains exactly why it escaped.

### 5. The multilateral instrument, and a nuance about it

⚠️ Hegemony is cheaper when it is collective, and the region's own institution was used for that purpose.

✅ Early in the Guatemalan crisis a resolution was carried at the Organization of American States declaring that communist domination of any state in the Western hemisphere posed a threat to the security of all member states; ✅ it was passed by 17 votes to one, the one being Guatemala. ✅ In 1962 the Latin American states, most of which had right-wing governments, expelled Cuba from the Organization of American States, which only made her more dependent on the USSR.

⚠️ But the same institution cuts the other way once the regional balance shifts, and this is a genuinely valuable nuance. ✅ After the Panama invasion of December 1989, the Organization of American States proposed a resolution to deeply regret the military intervention in Panama, and the resolution was approved by a vote of 20 to 1 - the one being the United States. ✅ Similarly, at a meeting of the Latin American parliament, founded in 1968, held in Guatemala City in April 1986, 16 out of 18 members voted in favour of a motion condemning the United States attitude to Nicaragua, with Honduras and El Salvador the exceptions.

⚠️ Set those votes side by side - 17 to 1 against Guatemala in 1954, 20 to 1 against the United States in 1989 - and you have a two-line demonstration of the whole century's trajectory. ⚠️ That is an answer-grabbing contrast, and it costs one sentence.

### 6. The limits of the system, stated honestly

⚠️ Four limits are recorded, and an answer that names them will read as balanced rather than polemical.

⚠️ **A local partner was required.** ✅ In Guatemala the official army took no part in the coup, but neither did it attempt to defend Arbenz; ⚠️ that abstention, not the invasion force, is what decided the outcome. ⚠️ No instrument on the ladder works without domestic collaboration or at least domestic acquiescence.

⚠️ **Failure was possible.** ✅ The Bay of Pigs operation was so badly planned and carried out that Castro's forces and his two jet planes had no difficulty crushing it.

⚠️ **Costs were incurred.** ✅ After Guatemala, anti-American feeling spread and "Yankee go home" became a common slogan throughout Latin America; ✅ the account judges that the Eisenhower government overestimated the threat from communism in Guatemala and was prepared to sacrifice a reforming government even though it meant violating the principle of non-intervention and souring relations with the rest of Latin America.

⚠️ **Domestic American politics constrained it.** ✅ The Nicaragua policy was controversial in the United States itself, and in March 1987, following the Irangate scandal, Congress voted that aid to the Contras should be stopped.

### Criticism and reply: does the ladder exaggerate American agency?

⚠️ **The objection.** A model that arranges everything as American instruments risks the very error this session forbids - making Latin Americans the objects of somebody else's policy.

⚠️ **The reply.** The ladder is a description of one actor's toolkit, not of the whole field, and the evidence repeatedly shows the other actors deciding outcomes. ✅ The Guatemalan army's abstention; ✅ the Chilean opposition's massive strike and its own fear about the 1976 election; ✅ the Brazilian landowners and officers who wanted Goulart gone for domestic reasons; ✅ the Nicaraguan electorate's decision in 1990 and its reversal in 2006; ✅ Venezuelan officers loyal to Chavez rescuing him from officers who had kidnapped him. ⚠️ In every one of those, the decisive move is local. ⚠️ The correct formulation is that the ladder describes **what the external constraint was made of**, while the outcome in each case is determined by how domestic forces responded to it.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This is the lesson that answers the most probable framing of the whole topic - the nature of United States domination - and it supplies the analytical vocabulary for every country case that follows.

**How it is framed.** ✅ The account's own end-of-chapter question asks in what ways and with what motives the United States was involved in the affairs of Latin America during the second half of the twentieth century, and separately whether the Cold War had profound effects on the region's economic and political systems. ⚠️ The ladder answers "in what ways"; section 1 answers "with what motives"; section 3 answers the Cold War question.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing "the USA controlled Latin America". ✅ The recorded claim is influence, expectation and intervention when a government was deemed unacceptable - not control.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the Good Neighbor period. ⚠️ Its existence proves interventionism was a choice, and its contrast with 1954 is a free analytical point.
3. ⚠️ Treating anti-communism as wholly sincere or wholly cynical. ✅ The account supports both readings simultaneously and an answer should say so.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting that the list contains a failure. ✅ Cuba survived, and hegemony that can fail is a different thing from empire.
5. ⚠️ Using the Organization of American States only as a United States instrument. ✅ The 1989 vote of 20 to 1 against the United States shows it working the other way.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Motive as recorded: aid provided, motives not entirely selfless; political influence expected in return, to prevent socialist or communist governments; no hesitation in intervening where a government was deemed unacceptable.
2. ✅ Regimes labelled communist and targeted for removal were often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests; Goulart, a millionaire landowner and devout Roman Catholic, was accused of being a communist.
3. ✅ Good Neighbor Policy from 1933: the Montevideo principle of non-intervention, December 1933; the Platt Amendment abrogated 1934; marines withdrawn from Nicaragua 1933 and Haiti 1934.
4. ✅ After 1945 the United States exercised huge economic, political and military influence and the region was dragged into the Cold War; Republican presidents especially feared a Soviet-Latin American Axis on the doorstep.
5. ✅ The intervention list: Guatemala 1954, Cuba 1961 - the Bay of Pigs, the 1962 crisis being the missile confrontation rather than a removal attempt - Brazil 1964, Dominican Republic 1965, Chile 1970-3, Nicaragua from 1979, Panama 1989, Haiti 1994; the attempt to remove Castro failed.
6. ✅ Rung five: 20,000 American troops in the Dominican Republic in 1965; Panama December 1989, at least 2,000 civilians killed, Noriega removed for trial after having worked for the CIA and been backed until 1987.
7. ✅ Rung four: weapons supplied to rebels in Guatemala in 1954, invasion from Honduras and Nicaragua, American planes bombing Guatemala City; about 1,400 men at the Bay of Pigs; the Contras financed as the Nicaraguan Democratic Force.
8. ✅ Rung three: Operation Mongoose approved November 1961; bomb attacks on Cuban factories and refineries; Nixon reported as demanding that the Chilean economy be made to scream; Johnson's instruction on Goulart and the readied naval vessels.
9. ✅ Rung two: aid cut when Castro and Allende came to power and resumed when the government changed; loans tied to purchases of United States goods; Venezuelan steel imports cut from 550,000 tonnes to 110,000 tonnes for five years, March 1985.
10. ✅ Rung one: American companies held controlling interests in all Cuban industries, half the land, about three-fifths of the railways, all electricity and the entire telephone system; the US ambassador in Havana was called the second most important man in Cuba.
11. ✅ Multilateral use: the Caracas-era resolution passed 17 to 1 with Guatemala dissenting; Cuba expelled from the Organization of American States in 1962; the Panama resolution of regret carried 20 to 1 against the United States; the Latin American parliament condemned United States policy on Nicaragua 16 to 2 in April 1986.
12. ✅ Limits: a local partner or an acquiescent army was needed; the Bay of Pigs failed; Guatemala spread anti-American feeling and the slogan "Yankee go home"; Congress stopped Contra aid in March 1987 after Irangate.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: hegemony is a graduated toolkit used from the cheapest rung upward; it is continuous at the bottom, episodic at the top, dependent on local partners throughout, and capable of failing.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why "the USA controlled Latin America" is both a natural conclusion from the evidence and an answer that would lose marks, and give the formulation you would write instead.

**Model answer:** It is natural because the structural presence was overwhelming: American companies held controlling interests in all Cuban industries and owned half the land, three-fifths of the railways, all electricity and the entire telephone system, the United Fruit Company was Guatemala's biggest landowner, and Chilean copper was under United States control; and because governments were in fact removed in Guatemala, Brazil, the Dominican Republic, Chile, Nicaragua, Panama and Haiti. It loses marks because control is a claim about administration, and the record shows something different and more precise: the United States expected to exert political influence in return for aid and intervened where a government was deemed unacceptable, which is a statement about limits, not about government. Within those limits states acted independently - Mexico nationalised oil in 1938 and kept it, Venezuela raised its oil share to 60 per cent, Brazil's generals governed on their own terms for twenty years - and outside them the system could fail, as at the Bay of Pigs. The formulation to write is: the United States exercised hegemony rather than empire, setting the outer boundary of permissible policy through a graduated set of instruments running from ownership and trade through conditional aid and covert action to proxy invasion and, rarely, troops; it climbed that ladder only when a cheaper rung failed, and every rung required a domestic partner or an acquiescent army.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring from the word "hegemony" that the relationship was mild. It was not. It included a proxy invasion with air bombardment, a decade of financed insurgency that destroyed bridges, schools and health clinics, the preparation of a coup, sustained economic strangulation and, in Panama, an invasion in which at least 2,000 civilians were killed. Hegemony is a claim about the *form* of predominance, not about its severity, and an answer that uses the word to soften the record has misused it as badly as one that calls the region a colony.

---

## Lesson 4 - Mexico I: the revolution of 1910, and why a fraudulent election destroyed a thirty-four-year dictatorship

Progress: 4/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the nineteenth-century inheritance including the loss of Texas and the war of 1846-8; the Porfirio Diaz dictatorship of 1876-1910 and its recorded achievements and costs; the fraudulent election of 1910 and the outbreak of revolution; the confused revolutionary decade and the civil war between revolutionaries and counter-revolutionaries; the United States incursion of 1916-17; and the constitutional settlement that emerged

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
