Book context: queried - the core account's Mexico section, running from independence in 1821 through the Diaz dictatorship, the 1910 election, the revolutionary decade and the emergence of the Institutional Revolutionary Party
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the Diaz balance sheet, and the fault line running through it

```text
      PORFIRIO DIAZ, 1876-1910 (except one short interlude)
      "a period of relative stability"

   WHAT WAS BUILT                       WHO RECEIVED IT
   ==============                       ===============
   oil production developed        ->   "most of the industry was
   mining developed                     owned by foreigners"
   manufacturing developed         ->   "little of the wealth
   education improved                   generated percolated down
   health care improved                 to the masses"
   infrastructure improved         ->   workers who formed trade
      (all "largely thanks to              unions "were quickly
       foreign investment")                suppressed"
                                    ->   "Mexico had become
                                         uncomfortably dependent
                                         on the USA"

                        |
                        v
        1910: Diaz, aged 80, stands again.
        "declared the winner by a huge majority,
         but the election was so blatantly fraudulent
         that a revolution broke out, forcing him to resign"
                        |
                        v
        1910-1920: "extremely confused"; the revolution
        "became a civil war as revolutionaries and
         counter-revolutionaries fought to gain control"
        1916-17: US troops sent into northern Mexico
        against the revolutionaries; war narrowly averted
                        |
                        v
        After 1920: the party later known as the PRI
        "gradually gained control"
```

⚠️ The diagram makes the lesson's central claim visible before it is argued. ⚠️ The Diaz regime did not fail because it stagnated. It failed because it modernised successfully and distributed the proceeds to foreigners and to a domestic elite - and then denied the excluded majority the one cheap safety valve a regime can offer, which is the ability to change the government by voting. ⚠️ Development without distribution, plus development without representation, is the formula; the election of 1910 was only the detonator.

### 1. The nineteenth-century opening, and why it is not a digression

✅ The Mexicans won their independence from Spain in 1821, and until 1877 they were ruled by an assortment of two emperors, several dictators and some presidents.

✅ Two events in that period must be carried forward. ✅ Large numbers of Americans had settled in the thinly populated northern area of Mexico known as Tejas; calling themselves Texans, they declared themselves an independent republic, defeated a Mexican army sent to suppress them, and Texas became a state of the USA in 1845. ✅ Mexico was then defeated in a war with the USA from 1846 to 1848 which resulted in the loss of about one-third of Mexican territory, including the areas now known as California, New Mexico, Nevada, Utah and Arizona, together with parts of Wyoming and Colorado; ✅ the account records that the United States paid Mexico $18 million and waived its debts. ⚠️ The treaty terms were in fact a payment of **$15 million** to Mexico together with the United States **assumption of $3.25 million** of claims by its own citizens against Mexico, which is how the larger combined figure arises.

⚠️ This is not background colour. ⚠️ It is the reason why Mexican nationalism has a permanent territorial dimension that no other case in this session possesses. ⚠️ Cuba resented American ownership; Guatemala resented an American company; Mexico had lost a third of its national territory to the same neighbour within living memory of the revolutionary generation. ⚠️ That is why the oil nationalisation of 1938, which you meet in the next lesson, was received with such delight, and why Mexican governments across the entire century have had to manage a relationship that is simultaneously their largest economic opportunity and their deepest national wound.

### 2. The Diaz regime: what "relative stability" concealed

✅ From 1876 until 1910 the country was ruled, except for one short interlude, by a dictator, Porfirio Diaz. ✅ This was a period of relative stability: oil production, mining and manufacturing industries were developed, largely thanks to foreign investment, while education, health care and the country's infrastructure were improved.

⚠️ Pause on that sentence, because a candidate who skips it will write a much worse answer. ⚠️ The account is crediting the dictatorship with real achievements. ⚠️ A revolution against a regime that built nothing is easy to explain; a revolution against a regime that built railways, schools and an oil industry needs a better explanation, and the account supplies it in the next breath.

✅ The problem was that most of the industry was owned by foreigners, and little of the wealth generated percolated down to the masses. ✅ When workers formed trade unions in an attempt to improve their conditions, they were quickly suppressed. ✅ Also Mexico had become uncomfortably dependent on the USA.

⚠️ Three exclusions, and they are of three different kinds. ⚠️ **Economic exclusion**: the growth was real but the gains left, either abroad or upward. ⚠️ **Organisational exclusion**: the one legal channel through which workers might have captured a share - the union - was closed by force. ⚠️ **National exclusion**: the development itself deepened dependence on the neighbour that had already taken a third of the country.

⚠️ When all three are closed at once, the only remaining channel is the electoral one. ⚠️ And in 1910 that channel was closed too.

### 3. The detonator: an eighty-year-old and a rigged count

✅ In 1910 Diaz decided to stand for re-election, although he was 80 years old by that time. ✅ He was declared the winner by a huge majority, but the election was so blatantly fraudulent that a revolution broke out, forcing him to resign.

⚠️ Two analytical points, both worth marks.

⚠️ First, on **causation**. The fraud did not create the grievances; it removed the last device by which they could be expressed without violence. ⚠️ This is the general form of the argument and it transfers to other cases: a regime survives exclusion so long as it offers some channel, and collapses quickly when it closes the last one. ⚠️ Write it as "the fraud converted a structural grievance into an immediate political crisis" and you have said in one clause what most answers take a paragraph to fumble.

⚠️ Second, on **succession**. An eighty-year-old standing for another term is not a personal detail; it is evidence that the regime had no mechanism for orderly transfer of power. ⚠️ Personalist regimes characteristically fail at the succession, and this is the same weakness that will destroy the Somoza dynasty in Nicaragua seven decades later.

### 4. The revolutionary decade, and the honesty of "confused"

✅ The following decade was extremely confused and the revolution became a civil war as revolutionaries and counter-revolutionaries fought to gain control.

⚠️ Do not tidy this up. ⚠️ The Mexican Revolution is not a single insurgency with a single programme; it is a multi-sided civil war between factions with genuinely different social bases and genuinely different aims, and the confusion is itself the examinable fact. ⚠️ A liberal-constitutionalist demand for honest elections, a northern movement of ranchers and cowboys, and a southern peasant movement demanding the return of village lands are three different revolutions that happened to have the same enemy in 1910 and fought each other once he was gone.

⚠️ The connecting names are standard and should be known, because a question on the Mexican Revolution that omits them will look thin. ✅ **Francisco Madero** initiated the challenge to Diaz on a platform of democracy and honest elections, with much less emphasis on land or labour. ✅ **Emiliano Zapata** led the southern peasant movement, demanding land and directly shaping the agrarian settlement that followed. ✅ **Pancho Villa** commanded the northern revolutionary forces. ✅ **Venustiano Carranza** led the Constitutionalist faction, became president, and convened the constitutional convention. ✅ **Alvaro Obregon**, a military leader under Carranza, was instrumental in consolidating the revolution's gains and later became president himself.

⚠️ The division matters analytically, not just factually. ⚠️ Madero wanted a political revolution; Zapata wanted a social one. ⚠️ The revolution succeeded politically almost at once and socially only partially and slowly - which is exactly the pattern that Lesson 5 is about.

### 5. The neighbour intervenes, and then does not

✅ In 1916-17 the USA sent troops into northern Mexico against the revolutionaries, and a war between Mexico and the USA was only narrowly averted.

⚠️ Two things follow, and the second is the more interesting.

⚠️ The incursion confirms that the hemispheric relationship was already coercive well before the Cold War, and it removes any temptation to treat 1945 as the start of the story.

⚠️ But notice what did **not** happen. ⚠️ The intervention did not install a government, and the revolution proceeded. ⚠️ Compare this with Guatemala in 1954 or Chile in 1973, where far smaller provocations produced regime change. ⚠️ The difference is instructive: in 1916 the United States was distracted by the European war, Mexico was large and armed, and - decisively - the Cold War frame that would later make any social reform legible as a communist advance did not yet exist. ⚠️ Mexico's revolution survived partly because it happened at the right time.

### 6. The settlement: what the revolution actually produced

✅ After 1920 the party eventually known as the Institutional Revolutionary Party gradually gained control. ✅ It was dominated by revolutionary and reformist politicians and its programme was based on economic reform designed to narrow the gap between rich and poor.

⚠️ The constitutional settlement that made that programme possible is a standard connecting fact and is needed here, because without it the revolution has no legal content and the later nationalisation has no legal basis. ✅ The Constitution was promulgated at Queretaro on 5 February 1917. ✅ **Article 27** reasserted national ownership of land, waters and subsoil resources and provided the basis for expropriation and redistribution, including the communal landholding known as the ejido - a direct response to the agrarian demands that Zapata had carried. ✅ **Article 123** established a comprehensive body of labour rights: the right to organise and to strike, an eight-hour day, a minimum wage, workplace protections and restrictions on child labour - a response to the suppression of unions under Diaz.

⚠️ That pairing is the whole revolution in two articles, and it is the cleanest way to answer a question about what the Mexican Revolution achieved. ⚠️ Article 27 answers Zapata's demand; Article 123 answers the suppressed unions; and together they make the state, rather than the market or the foreign investor, the owner of the nation's resources and the guarantor of its labour.

⚠️ One caution, which the next lesson develops. ⚠️ A constitutional article is a legal instrument, not an outcome. ⚠️ Article 27 made land reform lawful; it did not perform it, and the pace at which it was performed became one of the central political questions of the next eighty years.

### Comparative note: Mexico 1910 against Russia 1917 and China 1949

⚠️ Candidates reach instinctively for comparison here, and the comparison is worth making provided it is made as a contrast.

⚠️ The Mexican Revolution began as a **constitutional** protest against a fraudulent election, not as a seizure of power by an ideological party. ⚠️ It produced a constitution before it produced a ruling party, which is the reverse of the Russian and Chinese sequence. ⚠️ It did not abolish private property or capitalism; ✅ its programme was economic reform designed to narrow the gap between rich and poor, and Mexico remained an economy open to foreign investment throughout. ⚠️ And it produced a party that held power for seventy-one years through elections it won rather than through the formal abolition of opposition.

⚠️ The useful conclusion, and it is a strong line in an answer: Mexico shows that a genuine social revolution can produce a **hegemonic party in a formally competitive system** rather than a one-party state, and that this outcome is more durable and less visible than the alternative. ⚠️ Those regimes and doctrines themselves belong to their own owners; the comparison is drawn here and no narrative is imported.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ The Mexican Revolution is the earliest major social revolution of the twentieth century and is directly available for any question on revolutions, on the effects of political philosophies on society, or on the sources of twentieth-century Latin American politics.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Two framings recur: what caused the revolution, and what it achieved. ⚠️ This lesson answers the first completely and opens the second; Lesson 5 closes it.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Describing the Diaz regime as simply backward. ✅ The account credits it with real industrial, educational, health and infrastructural development; the failure was distributive, organisational and national.
2. ⚠️ Treating the 1910 fraud as the cause. ⚠️ It was the detonator of grievances already thirty years old.
3. ⚠️ Presenting the revolutionary decade as unified. ✅ It became a civil war between revolutionaries and counter-revolutionaries, and the revolutionary factions themselves differed over land.
4. ⚠️ Dating the revolution loosely. ✅ It begins in 1910; the constitution is 1917; the ruling party consolidates after 1920.
5. ⚠️ Assuming Article 27 delivered land reform automatically. ⚠️ It authorised it; delivery was a separate political struggle.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Independence from Spain 1821; rule until 1877 by two emperors, several dictators and some presidents.
2. ✅ Texas: American settlers in Tejas declared an independent republic, defeated a Mexican army, and Texas became a state of the USA in 1845.
3. ✅ War with the USA 1846-8: about one-third of Mexican territory lost, including California, New Mexico, Nevada, Utah and Arizona and parts of Wyoming and Colorado; under the treaty the United States paid $15 million and assumed $3.25 million of its citizens' claims against Mexico, which the account combines as $18 million paid with debts waived.
4. ✅ Porfirio Diaz ruled 1876-1910 except one short interlude; relative stability; oil, mining, manufacturing, education, health care and infrastructure developed largely through foreign investment.
5. ✅ Costs: most industry foreign-owned; little wealth percolated down; unions quickly suppressed; Mexico uncomfortably dependent on the USA.
6. ✅ 1910: Diaz, aged 80, stood again and was declared winner by a huge majority; the fraud was so blatant that revolution broke out and forced his resignation.
7. ✅ The following decade was extremely confused; the revolution became a civil war between revolutionaries and counter-revolutionaries.
8. ✅ Connecting names: Madero initiated the challenge on a democratic platform; Zapata led the southern agrarian movement; Villa commanded in the north; Carranza led the Constitutionalists and convened the convention; Obregon consolidated the gains and later became president.
9. ✅ 1916-17: United States troops sent into northern Mexico against the revolutionaries; war narrowly averted.
10. ✅ Constitution promulgated at Queretaro, 5 February 1917; Article 27 asserted national ownership of land, water and subsoil and authorised expropriation and redistribution including the ejido; Article 123 guaranteed union rights, the right to strike, an eight-hour day, a minimum wage and workplace protections.
11. ✅ After 1920 the party eventually known as the Institutional Revolutionary Party gradually gained control, dominated by revolutionary and reformist politicians, with a programme of economic reform to narrow the gap between rich and poor.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: Diaz fell because successful modernisation was combined with distributive, organisational and national exclusion, and because the last peaceful channel - the vote - was closed in 1910.
13. ⚠️ Comparative line: Mexico produced a constitution before a ruling party and a hegemonic party inside a competitive form, rather than a one-party state.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Diaz regime delivered thirty-four years of stability and genuine economic modernisation. Explain why it nevertheless produced a revolution, and identify the precise role of the 1910 election in that outcome.

**Model answer:** Because the modernisation was real but the distribution of its benefits was not, and because every channel through which the excluded might have claimed a share was closed in turn. Oil, mining and manufacturing were developed, and education, health care and infrastructure were improved, but most industry was foreign-owned and little of the wealth percolated down to the masses; workers who formed trade unions were quickly suppressed; and the development itself made Mexico uncomfortably dependent on the United States, a neighbour that had already taken about a third of Mexican territory in the war of 1846-8. Economic, organisational and national exclusion therefore accumulated together for three decades. The election of 1910 is not the cause but the detonator: when Diaz, aged 80, was declared winner by a huge majority in a contest so blatantly fraudulent that it could not be believed, the last non-violent channel was closed and the grievance converted immediately into armed revolt. The regime also had no mechanism for succession, which is why an eighty-year-old was standing at all, and personalist regimes characteristically break at exactly that point.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the revolution as a single unified movement with a single programme. The decade that followed was extremely confused, and the revolution became a civil war between revolutionaries and counter-revolutionaries, with the revolutionary factions themselves divided - Madero wanting honest elections, Zapata wanting land. That division explains why the settlement of 1917 delivered the political demand almost at once and the agrarian one slowly and partially, and an answer that describes a unified revolution cannot explain that gap.

---

## Lesson 5 - Mexico II: what a revolution becomes when it wins, 1920-2000

Progress: 5/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the institutionalisation of the revolution and the seventy-one-year party; Cardenas and the oil nationalisation of 1938; Mexico in the Second World War; the twenty-five post-war years of growth; Tlatelolco, 2 October 1968; the debt emergency of 1982 and the rescheduling; privatisation and the North American Free Trade Agreement; the Zapatista rising and the Colosio assassination of 1994; the peso collapse; and the alternation of 2000

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's Mexico narrative from the consolidation of the ruling party through Cardenas, the post-war growth period, 1968, the debt crisis, NAFTA, the 1994 shocks and the election of 2000, together with its later section on Mexico under the National Action Party
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: seventy-one years, in six movements

```text
  1920s-30s  INSTITUTIONALISATION
             the revolutionary factions absorbed into one party
             PNR (1929) -> PRM (1938) -> PRI (1946)
             |
  1938       THE HIGH POINT OF REVOLUTIONARY NATIONALISM
             Cardenas (1934-40) nationalises oil
             "much to the delight of the general public"
             USA and UK boycott Mexican goods
             -> Mexico sells oil to Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy
             -> compensation agreement November 1941
             -> after Pearl Harbor, Mexican oil becomes vital
             -> Mexico joins the Allies 1942; the "Aztec Eagles"
                fly with the American Fifth Air Force in the
                liberation of the Philippines, 1945
             |
  1945-70    THE MEXICAN MIRACLE
             investment in agriculture, fuel, railways, primary
             education; growth averaging 3-4% a year; sixth largest
             petroleum producer in the world; cotton, coffee, sugar
             exports; by 1960 manufacturing employment overtakes
             agriculture
             |
  1968       THE LEGITIMACY BREAK
             2 October: troops fire on an estimated 10,000 students
             in Mexico City demanding revolution and a return to
             democracy. Estimates of the numbers killed vary
             between 30 and 300.
             |
  1982       THE FISCAL BREAK
             banks nationalised; currency devalued by 70%; drastic
             cuts in public services; de la Madrid negotiates an IMF
             loan and a rescheduling of half the country's overseas
             debts of $96 billion
             |
  1994-2000  THE POLITICAL BREAK
             NAFTA in force January 1994; Zapatista rising 1 January;
             Colosio assassinated March; peso collapse; 1997 PRI
             loses control of Congress with 38% of the vote;
             2000 Fox (PAN) 43% beats Labastida (PRI) 36% -
             "single-party rule by the PRI had been brought to an
             end after 71 years"
```

⚠️ Read the six movements as a single argument about legitimacy. ⚠️ The party's claim to rule rested on three assets: revolutionary origin, national assertion and delivered growth. ⚠️ 1968 damaged the first, 1982 destroyed the third, and 1994 exposed the hollowing of all three at once. ⚠️ The end came in 2000 not through revolution but through a count - which is itself the most revealing fact about the system.

### 1. Institutionalisation: the paradox in the party's own name

✅ After 1920 the party eventually known as the Institutional Revolutionary Party gradually gained control, and it remained in power until 2000.

⚠️ Sit with the name for a moment, because it contains the entire lesson. ⚠️ "Institutional Revolutionary Party" is, read literally, a contradiction: a revolution is precisely what is not institutional. ⚠️ But the contradiction was the strategy, and it worked for seven decades.

✅ The party's institutional sequence is standard and worth knowing: the National Revolutionary Party founded in 1929 to unify the revolutionary factions, restructured in 1938 to incorporate labour, peasant, military and popular sectors, and renamed the Institutional Revolutionary Party in 1946.

⚠️ That middle step is the important one, and it is the key to Mexican stability. ⚠️ The party did not merely defeat its rivals; it **incorporated the social sectors** - organised labour, organised peasants, the army and the middle classes - as constituent parts of itself. ⚠️ A union or a peasant league inside the ruling party gets access, patronage and a hearing; it also loses the capacity for independent opposition. ⚠️ This is why Mexico, alone among the large states in this session, had neither a successful coup nor a revolution after 1920 - not because its social conflicts were milder, but because they had been brought inside the state.

⚠️ And it explains the characteristic defect. ⚠️ A party that contains everybody has no external check, so the discipline that competition normally supplies must come from inside - and when it stops coming from inside, the result is exactly what the account describes in 1994: corruption at the highest level and a presidential candidate shot dead when he promised drastic reform of the corrupt political system.

### 2. Cardenas 1938: the high point, and what made it possible

✅ In 1938 President Lazaro Cardenas (1934-40) nationalized the oil industry, much to the delight of the general public. ✅ However, this was not welcomed by the USA or the UK, both of which started a boycott of Mexican goods. ✅ This forced Mexico to sell oil to Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy, but after a compensation agreement was reached in November 1941, the USA was prepared to buy Mexican oil again. ✅ In fact after the USA entered the Second World War in December 1941, Mexican oil became vital. ✅ Following the sinking of some of their oil tankers by German submarines, the Mexicans joined the Allied side in 1942. ✅ An air-force squadron known as the "Aztec Eagles" worked alongside the American Fifth Air Force in the liberation of the Philippines in 1945.

⚠️ This short passage is the single most useful comparative case in the topic, and most candidates never use it. Four observations.

⚠️ **First: the provocation is the same one that destroys other governments.** Expropriating foreign-owned assets in the extractive sector is exactly what Arbenz did in Guatemala, what Castro did in Cuba and what Allende did in Chile. Mexico did it in 1938 and survived.

⚠️ **Second: the response was economic, not military.** A boycott of Mexican goods - rung two of the ladder in Lesson 3 - and not a coup. ⚠️ The Good Neighbor Policy was in force, and the contrast with Guatemala sixteen years later is therefore a clean natural experiment: similar provocation, different international frame, opposite outcome.

⚠️ **Third: the counter-move shows real sovereign agency.** ⚠️ Faced with a boycott by its two largest markets, Mexico sold to Germany and Italy. ⚠️ That is not a puppet's behaviour; it is a state exploiting the existence of a rival buyer. ⚠️ Hold this, because it is the same structural fact that saves Cuba after 1960 and that reappears as the China option after 2000: **a second buyer converts dependence into bargaining.**

⚠️ **Fourth: the resolution was negotiated.** A compensation agreement in November 1941, and then a wartime alliance complete with a Mexican squadron in the Pacific. ⚠️ The relationship was coercive and negotiable at the same time, and an answer that can hold both halves is a strong answer.

### 3. The miracle years, and the strain underneath them

✅ For 25 years following the end of the war, Mexico enjoyed a period of economic progress. ✅ The government invested in agriculture, fuel production, the railway system and primary education. ✅ A modest but consistent annual growth rate averaging 3-4 per cent was achieved; ✅ Mexico became a major producer of petroleum - the sixth largest in the world; ✅ and exports of cotton, coffee and sugar were also important sources of revenue. ✅ By 1960 the number of workers employed in manufacturing industries had overtaken those working in agriculture.

⚠️ That last sentence is a genuine structural transformation and should be recognised as such: a country whose labour force is majority-industrial is not the country described in Lesson 2. ⚠️ Mexico did partially escape the trap.

✅ But in the late 1960s the economy began to show signs of strain, partly because the government had accumulated massive external debts thanks to its extravagant borrowing. ✅ Confident that oil revenues would always be sufficient to cover interest payments, successive governments seemed to have abandoned restraint.

⚠️ Name the mechanism precisely, because it recurs in Venezuela, Ecuador and Nigeria and because examiners like the phrase: this is **borrowing against a forecast price**. ⚠️ Oil revenue is collateral only so long as the oil price holds. ⚠️ When it does not - ✅ and in 1986 there was a sudden collapse in world oil prices and a consequent reduction in Mexico's oil revenues - the borrowing that looked prudent becomes insolvency overnight. ⚠️ The resource that was supposed to underwrite development underwrote a debt crisis instead.

### 4. Tlatelolco, 2 October 1968: the legitimacy break

✅ There was wide protest, and on 2 October 1968 troops fired on a demonstration by an estimated 10,000 students in Mexico City demanding a revolution and a return to democracy. ✅ Estimates of the numbers killed vary between 30 and 300. ✅ The government claimed that snipers among the demonstrators had fired at the army first. ✅ It later emerged that the snipers were actually members of the presidential guard who had been ordered to fire on the army in order to provoke them to attack the students. ✅ Coming as it did ten days before the Olympic Games were about to open in Mexico City, this caused grave concern about security; ✅ in response the USA sent riot control experts, weapons and ammunition to Mexico in case of further violence.

⚠️ **Attribution first.** The mortality figure is a range - between 30 and 300 - and it must be written as a range with its source attached. ❌ Do not state a single figure; ❌ do not pick the higher or the lower end; ❌ do not convert it into "hundreds".

⚠️ Now the analysis, which has three parts.

⚠️ **The demand is the diagnosis.** The students demanded "a revolution and a return to democracy" - from a party whose name contained the word revolutionary and which held regular elections. ⚠️ That is a direct statement that the revolutionary claim and the electoral form had both become formal rather than real.

⚠️ **The provocation shows what the state had become.** ⚠️ Members of the presidential guard fired on the army to provoke it into attacking students. ⚠️ A state that manufactures the pretext for its own repression is not defending order; it is performing it. ⚠️ This single fact is worth more in an answer than a paragraph of adjectives.

⚠️ **The external response shows the hemispheric system working normally.** ⚠️ Riot control experts, weapons and ammunition were sent - and note that Mexico was not a reforming government under threat but an established partner receiving assistance. ⚠️ The same hemispheric relationship that removed governments also equipped them.

### 5. 1982: the fiscal break

✅ As the 1970s progressed Mexico's exports were badly hit by the world recession, leading to a shortage of capital for investment, to inflation and to difficulties in meeting interest repayments. ✅ Unemployment was rising and the gap between rich and poor continued to widen, until by 1980 it was estimated that about nine-tenths of Mexico's total wealth was owned by fewer than half a million people out of a total population of 85 million.

⚠️ Note the qualifier "it was estimated". ⚠️ Preserve it; this is a distribution estimate, not a measured constant.

✅ In 1982 the government introduced desperate measures: the banks were nationalized, the currency was devalued by 70 per cent and there were drastic reductions in spending on public services. ✅ The new president, Miguel de la Madrid, elected in 1982, negotiated a deal with the IMF for a loan and a rescheduling of half the country's overseas debts of $96 billion. ✅ However, Mexico failed to fulfil the conditions attached, and the IMF was preparing to cancel the agreement when Mexico City suffered a severe earthquake measuring 8.1 on the Richter scale, which caused widespread damage and killed at least 7,000 people; ✅ clearly this was not the time to cause Mexico any further misery, and so the agreement went ahead.

⚠️ That earthquake detail is not colour. ⚠️ It is the clearest single illustration in this session of what conditionality means in practice: the continuation of a sovereign state's financing depended, at that moment, on a creditor's judgement about the political acceptability of enforcing its own terms. ⚠️ And the political consequence followed immediately: ✅ the governing party was fast losing popularity and attracted further criticism for what was seen as its incompetent handling of the earthquake relief efforts; ✅ for the first time since the 1930s the party began to face challengers at elections.

✅ And the president said so publicly. ✅ As de la Madrid put it: "We have reached the limit of being able to sustain this net transfer of resources to the rest, which violates economic logic and is tremendously inequitable."

⚠️ That quotation is worth memorising verbatim. ⚠️ It is a head of government describing debt service as a **net transfer of resources** from a poor country to a rich one - the dependency argument of Lesson 20 stated not by a theorist but by a sitting president under a stabilisation programme.

### 6. 1994 and 2000: the political break

✅ The reform response included privatization, which did nothing to solve the unemployment problem but did help to bring inflation down to single figures and eliminated the budget deficit. ✅ Mexico joined the North American Free Trade Agreement, along with the USA and Canada, which came into force in January 1994; ✅ this removed tariffs on more than half of Mexico's exports to the USA and on about one-third of US exports to Mexico, with all tariffs between the two countries to be removed after 15 years. ✅ Opinions differ on whether or not this has been beneficial for Mexico: ✅ certainly Mexican exports to the USA increased and the country was opened up to US and Canadian investment; ✅ on the other hand, Mexican farmers suffered because imports of US agricultural produce, especially meat, increased substantially.

⚠️ Keep that even-handed formulation. ⚠️ The regional-integration question is genuinely contested and an answer that declares a verdict without the counterweight will look partisan.

✅ The year 1994 saw two shocking events. ✅ First, on 1 January, there was an armed uprising in the southern province of Chiapas by the Zapatista Army of National Liberation. ✅ Chiapas was one of the most deprived parts of Mexico; the majority of the population were poverty-stricken Mayan Indians who had no land of their own and were angered by the blatant corruption and incompetence of the ruling elite. ✅ Demanding land reform, full civil rights and genuine democracy, the Zapatistas - named after Emiliano Zapata, one of the leaders of the 1910 revolution - occupied several towns, setting fire to police stations and army barracks. ✅ Within a few days they were crushed by the Mexican army, suffering heavy casualties. ✅ Having decided to abandon violence, they concentrated on an internet campaign that brought them widespread publicity and growing support.

⚠️ The timing is the argument, and it is not a coincidence: the rising began on the day the free trade agreement came into force. ⚠️ The claim being made was that market integration with the richest economy in the world was proceeding while the land question of 1910 remained unsettled for the Mayan population of Chiapas. ⚠️ And the choice of name states it: eighty-four years after the revolution, a peasant movement had to invoke a leader of that revolution to demand land reform. ⚠️ That is the most economical available proof that the institutionalised revolution had not completed its own agenda.

✅ Then in March 1994 the governing party's candidate in the coming presidential election, Luis Donaldo Colosio, was shot dead at a political rally in Tijuana. ✅ Mario Aburto, a factory worker, was jailed for the murder, but many still believe that he was a scapegoat and that the murder was arranged by the party itself; ✅ it was alleged that with his promise of drastic reforms of the corrupt political system, Colosio was breaking party ranks and therefore had to be eliminated.

⚠️ Report this exactly as the account does - a conviction, a widespread belief and an allegation, not a finding. ❌ Do not assert who ordered the killing.

✅ Both events frightened off investors at a time when the outgoing president had just indulged in a year of high spending resulting in a huge budget deficit and inflation, and this was made worse when members of his family helped themselves to enormous sums. ✅ The historian Peter Calvert's assessment is recorded: the Mexican crisis called into question the ability of international finance to meet such a crisis and therewith the willingness of financiers worldwide to support Latin American governments pursuing economic policies dependent on foreign loans and investment; the collapse of the Mexican peso dismayed all Latin American countries, where economic growth was desperately needed for its own economic ends and as a prerequisite for political stability; in Mexico the gap between rich and poor widened, and insurrection became more widespread and better armed.

✅ In fact by 2000, underneath the outward appearance of prosperity, about one-third of Mexico's population still lived below the poverty line. ✅ The party seemed to be in a state of stagnation and blocked all moves designed to lessen the gap between rich and poor. ✅ In the Congressional elections of 1997 it lost control of Congress, gaining only 38 per cent of the vote. ✅ In the presidential election of 2000 its candidate, Francisco Labastida, was opposed by Vicente Fox of the centre-right National Action Party; ✅ Fox won a comfortable victory with 43 per cent of the vote against Labastida's 36 per cent, and single-party rule had been brought to an end after 71 years.

⚠️ Two closing observations.

⚠️ **The manner of the ending is the point.** ⚠️ No coup, no revolution, no external intervention - a count. ⚠️ Mexico is the one large case in this session where a seventy-one-year monopoly of power ended through the mechanism the constitution provided. ⚠️ That is a genuine institutional achievement and should be credited.

⚠️ **The direction is instructive.** ⚠️ In the same years that Venezuela, Brazil, Argentina, Chile, Bolivia and Ecuador were electing presidents of the left, Mexico moved to the centre-right. ✅ The account notes the divergence explicitly. ⚠️ That is the variation discipline in one sentence: the same region, the same decade, opposite directions, because the thing being voted against in Mexico was a nominally left-wing party that had held power for seventy-one years.

### Criticism and reply: was the Mexican system a democracy?

⚠️ **The objection.** A party that holds power for seventy-one years, incorporates the unions, and whose candidate is accused of being killed by his own party is not meaningfully democratic, whatever the constitution says.

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** ⚠️ First, concede the substance: ✅ the account records that the revolution's party ruled for 71 years, ✅ that it blocked moves to lessen the gap between rich and poor, ✅ that the corruption was blatant, and ✅ that in 1968 the state manufactured a pretext to fire on students. ⚠️ Second, note what distinguishes it nonetheless: elections were held continuously, the opposition existed legally and won, ✅ Congress was lost in 1997 and the presidency in 2000, and ✅ the transfer of power was accepted. ⚠️ Third, name the category: this is a **hegemonic-party system** - competitive in form, monopolistic in practice, and reformable from within by exactly the mechanism that eventually reformed it.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ Mexico bought seventy years of stability at the price of seventy years of unaccountability, and the bill was presented in 1968, 1982 and 1994 before being settled in 2000.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ Mexico is the topic's answer to "what happens after a successful revolution", and it is also the region's clearest case of the debt sequence running through a single country from borrowing to rescheduling to liberalisation to political change.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect it as a comparison case - Mexico against Cuba, or Mexico against Brazil - or as the evidence for a claim about revolutions institutionalising. ⚠️ A recorded probable framing is precisely that revolution did not quickly become pluralist democracy.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying the party ruled for seventy years "as a dictatorship". ✅ It ruled through elections it won, lost Congress in 1997 and left office in 2000.
2. ⚠️ Giving a single figure for Tlatelolco. ✅ The record is a range of between 30 and 300, and the range must be preserved.
3. ⚠️ Treating the 1982 crisis as purely external. ✅ The extravagant borrowing against expected oil revenue was a domestic decision.
4. ⚠️ Declaring a verdict on the free trade agreement. ✅ The record states increased exports and investment alongside damage to farmers, and says opinions differ.
5. ⚠️ Asserting responsibility for the Colosio assassination. ✅ A man was convicted; the rest is recorded belief and allegation.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The revolutionary party consolidated after 1920 and remained in power until 2000; the institutional sequence is 1929, restructured 1938 to incorporate labour, peasant, military and popular sectors, renamed 1946.
2. ✅ Cardenas (1934-40) nationalised oil in 1938 to public delight; the USA and UK boycotted Mexican goods; Mexico sold oil to Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy; compensation agreement November 1941.
3. ✅ After December 1941 Mexican oil became vital; Mexico joined the Allies in 1942 after German submarines sank its tankers; the "Aztec Eagles" flew with the American Fifth Air Force in the liberation of the Philippines in 1945.
4. ✅ Twenty-five post-war years of progress: investment in agriculture, fuel, railways and primary education; growth averaging 3-4 per cent; sixth largest petroleum producer in the world; cotton, coffee and sugar exports; manufacturing employment overtook agriculture by 1960.
5. ✅ Late 1960s strain from massive external debts built on the assumption that oil revenues would always cover interest.
6. ✅ 2 October 1968: troops fired on an estimated 10,000 students demanding a revolution and a return to democracy; estimates of those killed vary between 30 and 300; the snipers turned out to be presidential guards ordered to fire on the army to provoke it; the Olympics opened ten days later; the USA sent riot control experts, weapons and ammunition.
7. ✅ By 1980 it was estimated that about nine-tenths of Mexico's wealth was owned by fewer than half a million people out of 85 million.
8. ✅ 1982: banks nationalised; currency devalued by 70 per cent; drastic public-service cuts; de la Madrid negotiated an IMF loan and rescheduling of half of $96 billion of overseas debt; the earthquake of 8.1 magnitude killed at least 7,000 and the agreement went ahead.
9. ✅ De la Madrid on the limit of sustaining "this net transfer of resources to the rest, which violates economic logic and is tremendously inequitable".
10. ✅ 1986: sudden collapse in world oil prices and a consequent reduction in oil revenues.
11. ✅ Privatisation brought inflation to single figures and eliminated the budget deficit but did nothing for unemployment; the North American Free Trade Agreement came into force January 1994, removing tariffs on more than half of Mexican exports to the USA and about a third of US exports to Mexico, all tariffs to go after 15 years; exports and investment rose, farmers suffered from increased American agricultural imports, and opinions differ on the net effect.
12. ✅ 1 January 1994: the Zapatista Army of National Liberation rose in Chiapas, where most of the population were poverty-stricken Mayan Indians without land; they demanded land reform, full civil rights and genuine democracy, occupied towns, were crushed within days with heavy casualties, then turned to an internet campaign.
13. ✅ March 1994: Colosio shot dead at Tijuana; Aburto convicted; many believe he was a scapegoat and allege the party arranged it because Colosio promised drastic reform.
14. ✅ Peter Calvert: the crisis questioned international finance's capacity and financiers' willingness to support Latin American governments dependent on foreign loans; the peso collapse dismayed the region; the rich-poor gap widened and insurrection became more widespread and better armed.
15. ✅ By 2000 about a third of Mexicans lived below the poverty line; the party lost Congress in 1997 with 38 per cent; Fox won in 2000 with 43 per cent against Labastida's 36 per cent, ending 71 years of single-party rule.
16. ⚠️ Master formulation: the Mexican Revolution succeeded by institutionalising itself, and the institution eventually became what the revolution had been made against - which is why the ending came through an election rather than a rising.

### Concept check

**Question:** Mexico nationalised its oil industry in 1938 and survived; Guatemala expropriated foreign-held land in the early 1950s and its government was removed. Using the evidence, explain what differed.

**Model answer:** Three things differed, and none of them is the provocation. First, the international frame. Mexico acted while the Good Neighbor Policy was in force, when the United States had accepted the non-intervention principle at Montevideo in December 1933, abrogated the Platt Amendment in 1934 and withdrawn its marines from Nicaragua and Haiti; Guatemala acted in the Cold War, when Republican administrations feared a Soviet-Latin American Axis on the doorstep and a resolution on communist domination of any hemispheric state was carried at the Organization of American States by 17 votes to one. The same act was read as a fiscal dispute in one decade and as a security threat in the next. Second, the response available. Against Mexico the instrument used was a trade boycott by the United States and the United Kingdom; against Guatemala the instruments were weapons for rebels, an invasion from Honduras and Nicaragua led by Colonel Castillo Armas, and the bombing of Guatemala City. Third, the alternative market. Boycotted by its two main buyers, Mexico sold oil to Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy, which converted a dependence into a bargaining position and brought the United States back to a compensation agreement in November 1941; Guatemala, a small agricultural exporter of bananas and coffee, had no equivalent leverage. Size, timing and the availability of a second buyer, not the radicalism of the measure, decided the two outcomes.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Mexico was therefore treated gently or was somehow exempt. The relationship remained coercive throughout: a third of Mexican territory had been lost in the war of 1846-8, American troops entered northern Mexico in 1916-17, riot control equipment was supplied after 1968, and the terms of the 1982 rescheduling were set by creditors to the point where a president publicly described debt service as a net transfer of resources that violated economic logic. What differed was the rung of the ladder used, not the existence of the ladder.

---

## Lesson 6 - Populism as a political form: Vargas in Brazil, Peron in Argentina, and why the comparison with fascism both helps and misleads

Progress: 6/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the urban mass as a new constituency; Vargas from 1930 through the New State, the suicide of 1954 and the successor presidencies; Peron's two periods in office; the recorded influence of European fascism and the decisive difference identified in the sources; and the structural legacy of populism for the region's politics

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's Brazil narrative from 1889 through Vargas, Kubitschek, Quadros and Goulart, together with its chapter on Japan and Spain, which records the influence of fascism on Latin American politicians and the historian's judgement distinguishing the two
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the populist bargain, and what each party to it received

```text
                        THE POPULIST LEADER
                     (Vargas 1930-45, 1950-4;
                      Peron: junta 1943-46,
                      president 1946-55, 1973-4)
                               |
        +----------------------+----------------------+
        |                      |                      |
        v                      v                      v
   URBAN WORKERS          INDUSTRIALISTS          THE STATE ITSELF
   receive:               receive:                receives:
   - recognition of       - protection for        - a mass base that
     unions                 domestic industry       does not depend on
   - labour law           - state credit and        landowners
   - social benefits        planning              - a reason to expand
   - symbolic dignity     - a disciplined          - an industrial policy
   give:                    workforce             gives:
   - loyalty to the       give:                   - patronage, jobs,
     leader, not to a     - acceptance of           licences, contracts
     party of labour        labour regulation
                                                       |
                                                       v
                          WHAT NOBODY AT THIS TABLE RECEIVES:
                          the landless rural population.
                          Populism is an URBAN bargain.
                          Land reform is not on the agenda.
                                                       |
                                                       v
                          WHAT THE BARGAIN NEEDS TO SURVIVE:
                          continuous growth to pay for benefits
                          without taxing the landed elite.
                          When growth stops, the bargain breaks
                          and the army is asked to resolve it.
```

⚠️ The diagram explains both the appeal and the fragility of the form. ⚠️ Populism solved the problem of Lesson 2 - a large, poor, urban and unorganised population - by organising it from above. ⚠️ It did not solve the problem of Lesson 1, which was land. ⚠️ And because it avoided the landed elite rather than confronting it, it had to finance its promises out of growth; when growth faltered, it had no second resource and the army returned.

### 1. The problem populism answered

⚠️ Recall the demographic shift from Lesson 2: a rapid population rise, holdings too small to support families, migration to cities where jobs were scarce, and improvised settlements ringing every major city.

⚠️ Now ask the political question that follows. ⚠️ Who speaks for those people? ⚠️ The landowner does not; they have left his estate. ⚠️ The traditional parties do not; they were built by and for a propertied electorate. ⚠️ A classical socialist party of organised labour might, but the industrial working class in most of the region was too small and too new to have built one. ⚠️ The Church spoke to them but did not contest elections.

⚠️ Into that vacuum steps a specific political figure: a leader who addresses the urban mass **directly**, offers tangible material benefits through the state, dispenses with intermediary institutions, and derives legitimacy from the relationship itself. ⚠️ That is populism, and it is not a term of abuse here but a description of a structure.

### 2. Vargas: the Brazilian case, in full

✅ Brazil declared its independence from Portugal on **7 September 1822**; Portugal recognised it in **1825**, which is the date the core account gives. ✅ It was a monarchy until 1889, when it became a republic. ✅ Until 1930 the country was ruled mainly by military dictatorships, but none of them succeeded in establishing a stable system; ✅ there were economic, social and political problems which caused several revolutions and attempted coups.

✅ The country began to make genuine economic progress after 1930 when the army replaced the ultra-conservative government of wealthy landowners with the more progressive and liberal President Getulio Vargas. ✅ For the first time the government took over economic planning, and Vargas was especially keen to encourage industry. ✅ Thousands of extra jobs were created, especially in electrical and steel manufacture. ✅ He soon became popular and was able to stay in power right through the Second World War.

⚠️ Read the first of those sentences carefully, because it contains something a candidate will otherwise miss: **the army installed him**. ⚠️ The army replaced a landowners' government with a modernising one. ⚠️ That is the same institution that in 1964 would remove a reforming president - which tells you that the Latin American army is not consistently reactionary. ⚠️ It is an autonomous institution with its own view of national development, and it has intervened in both directions. ⚠️ Saying so precisely is a discriminator.

✅ However, by the time of the war the army was turning against him. ✅ They were worried by his popularity with the working classes and felt that he had become too powerful. ✅ In fact, he had been acting as a dictator since 1937, and no elections had taken place. ✅ The army wanted a president whom they could control, and so in 1945 Vargas was forced to step down.

✅ The dictatorship had a name and a period: Vargas led the **Estado Novo**, or New State, in Brazil, and it ran from its proclamation in **1937** to **1945**. ⚠️ The core account dates it from 1939; the earlier date is the correct one and is consistent with the same account's own statement that he had been acting as a dictator since 1937.

⚠️ Now the return, which is the part that proves populism was a genuine social force rather than a personal trick. ✅ The army faced a dilemma when he was re-elected in 1950 for a five-year term: should they prevent him from taking office or not? ✅ The younger army officers favoured Vargas and in the end he was allowed to return. ✅ He stayed in power until 1954, tried to continue acting as a dictator, and once again the army grew tired of him; ✅ they accused him of corruption and incompetence and asked him to resign. ✅ Instead, he committed suicide, claiming that his death was "a sacrifice on behalf of the Brazilian workers".

⚠️ A dictator deposed in 1945 is re-elected in 1950. ⚠️ That single fact disposes of any reading in which populism is merely imposed from above: the constituency he built voted him back. ⚠️ And the suicide note - a sacrifice on behalf of the Brazilian workers - is the populist claim in its purest form: the leader as the personal embodiment of the workers' cause, opposed by an oligarchy and a foreign interest. ⚠️ Quote it; it does a great deal of work in a short space.

✅ The succession is examinable too. ✅ The election of 1955 was won by Juscelino Kubitschek, whose first major action was to increase the army's pay, hoping thereby to guarantee their support; ✅ he completed his term in 1961, but his presidency was a disappointment, his only memorable achievement being the building of a new capital, Brasilia, which was arguably an extravagance the country could ill afford. ✅ The winner in 1961 was Janio Quadros, but he resigned after only seven months and the vice-president, Julio Goulart, took over.

⚠️ Kubitschek's first act is the most efficient one-line illustration of the army's veto power in the whole session: the first thing an incoming elected president does is pay the institution that can remove him.

### 3. Peron: the Argentine case, and the necessary honesty about its extent

✅ Juan Peron held office in Argentina from **1943** until **1955** and again in **1973-4**. ⚠️ The dates need care, because a single span conceals two different kinds of office: he rose through ministerial and vice-presidential posts in the military government that seized power in 1943, and he became head of state only after being **elected president on 24 February 1946** and **inaugurated on 4 June 1946**. ⚠️ The core account compresses this into leadership from 1943; the distinction between a colonel inside a junta and an elected president is exactly the distinction this topic is about, so it is preserved here.

⚠️ State the source position honestly, because this session will not manufacture detail. ⚠️ The sources held for this session treat Peron comparatively - as one of the South American politicians influenced by fascism, alongside Vargas - and do not supply a full narrative of his domestic programme in the way they do for Brazilian or Mexican politics. ⚠️ What is held is his period in office, his place in the comparison, and the structural judgement recorded about both men. ⚠️ That is enough to teach the form and to answer a comparative question; it is not enough to write a Peron narrative, and an answer should not attempt one from memory.

✅ What the sources do establish, and it is the analytically decisive part: ✅ Peron and Vargas were two of those who were impressed by the apparent success of Fascist Italy and Nazi Germany; ✅ they adopted some of the European fascist ideas, especially the mobilization of mass support; ✅ they won huge support from the poor working classes in the mass union movement; ✅ but they were not really like Mussolini and Hitler either, and their governments can best be summed up as **a combination of nationalism and social reform**.

### 4. The decisive distinction, in one sentence

✅ The historian Eric Hobsbawm's formulation is recorded and it is the single best line available on this subject: ✅ "European fascist movements destroyed labour movements, the Latin American leaders they inspired, created them."

⚠️ Unpack it, because a candidate who merely quotes it has used half its value.

⚠️ European fascism arose in societies that already possessed large, organised, independent labour movements with socialist or communist parties attached, and its central domestic function was to destroy them - to break the unions, outlaw the parties and remove the class conflict by removing one of the classes' organisations.

⚠️ Latin American populism arose in societies where such a movement barely existed, and its central domestic function was to **build** one - to recognise unions, legislate labour rights, enrol workers in state-sponsored federations and thereby create the very constituency that European fascism existed to abolish.

⚠️ The surface resemblance is therefore real and the underlying function is opposite. ⚠️ Both mobilised masses, both used charismatic leadership, both were nationalist, both were authoritarian in practice - ✅ Vargas acted as a dictator from 1937 with no elections held. ⚠️ But one was demobilising a working class that existed and the other was constituting a working class that did not.

⚠️ There is a corollary worth stating, because it explains the long afterlife of these movements. ⚠️ Because populism created labour organisations, those organisations survived their creators and continued to structure politics for decades. ⚠️ Where European fascism left a vacuum after 1945, Latin American populism left an institutional legacy: unions, labour codes, social insurance and a durable political identity. ⚠️ That is why a Brazilian Workers' Party could win the presidency in 2002 and why Argentine politics remained organised around Peronism long after Peron.

### 5. What populism did not do, and why that matters more than what it did

⚠️ Return to the visual. ⚠️ The bargain had three parties and a fourth group outside it.

⚠️ **The landless rural population was excluded.** ⚠️ Populism was an urban coalition financed by industrial growth, and confronting the landed elite would have destroyed it. ⚠️ So the master variable of Lesson 1 - concentrated land - went untouched in exactly the period when a broad popular government had the political capital to touch it.

⚠️ **The strategy required growth.** ⚠️ Benefits to workers plus protection and credit to industrialists plus patronage to the state's own clients can be financed out of an expanding economy without taxing the oligarchy. ⚠️ When the expansion stops, the coalition has to be paid for out of redistribution - and the coalition was constructed precisely to avoid redistribution.

⚠️ **The resolution was therefore military.** ⚠️ It is not an accident that both leaders ended their principal periods in office through military pressure: ✅ Vargas forced out by the army in 1945 and asked to resign in 1954, ✅ Peron's first period ending in 1955.

⚠️ **The consequence for the next generation is exact.** ⚠️ Populism showed that the urban mass could be mobilised without a social revolution. ⚠️ When that proved insufficient, the two available successors were the revolutionary road - Cuba - and the technocratic-authoritarian road - the military regimes of Lesson 13. ⚠️ Both are responses to the exhaustion of the populist settlement, and framing them that way in an answer converts a list of cases into a sequence.

### Criticism and reply: is "populism" a useful category or a smear?

⚠️ **The objection.** "Populism" is often used to dismiss any leader with mass support, and applying it to Vargas and Peron may simply reproduce the hostility of the elites they challenged.

⚠️ **The reply.** The category earns its place here because it names a **structure**, not a sentiment: direct leader-to-mass appeal, material benefits delivered through the state, organisational weakness of intermediate institutions, an urban coalition, and the avoidance of the agrarian question. ⚠️ Every element of that structure is evidenced: ✅ the mobilisation of mass support, ✅ the huge support from the poor working classes in the mass union movement, ✅ the characterisation as nationalism plus social reform, ✅ dictatorship without elections from 1937. ⚠️ And the category is explanatory rather than evaluative, because it predicts the failure mode - dependence on growth, vulnerability to the army - that both cases exhibit.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ Populism was the first political form in the region to treat the urban poor as a constituency rather than as a problem, and it delivered real gains to them. ⚠️ It was also authoritarian, personalist and structurally incapable of addressing the land question. ⚠️ Both halves belong in an answer.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ Populism connects directly to the syllabus clause on political philosophies and their effect on society, and it is the natural bridge between the fascism topic and this one.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The likeliest framings are comparative - Latin American populism against European fascism - or developmental, asking why the region produced this form rather than social democracy.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Calling Vargas or Peron fascists. ✅ The sources record fascist influence and explicitly deny the identification, summing their governments up as nationalism plus social reform.
2. ⚠️ Forgetting Hobsbawm's inversion. ✅ European fascism destroyed labour movements; the Latin American leaders it inspired created them.
3. ⚠️ Treating the army as uniformly reactionary. ✅ The Brazilian army installed Vargas in 1930 against a landowners' government and removed him in 1945.
4. ⚠️ Writing a Peron narrative from memory. ⚠️ What is established here is his periods in office and his place in the comparison; unsourced detail is a factual risk.
5. ⚠️ Presenting populism as a solution to inequality. ⚠️ It was an urban bargain that left the land question untouched.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Brazil: independence declared 7 September 1822 and recognised by Portugal in 1825; monarchy until 1889; ruled mainly by military dictatorships until 1930, none of which established a stable system.
2. ✅ 1930: the army replaced the ultra-conservative government of wealthy landowners with the more progressive and liberal Vargas; the government took over economic planning for the first time and encouraged industry, creating thousands of jobs especially in electrical and steel manufacture.
3. ✅ Vargas acted as a dictator from 1937 with no elections; he led the Estado Novo, or New State, from 1937 to 1945; the army, worried by his popularity with the working classes, forced him out in 1945.
4. ✅ Re-elected in 1950 for five years; allowed to take office because younger officers favoured him; in power until 1954, when the army accused him of corruption and incompetence and asked him to resign; he committed suicide, calling his death "a sacrifice on behalf of the Brazilian workers".
5. ✅ Kubitschek won in 1955 and increased the army's pay as his first major act; completed his term in 1961; built Brasilia, arguably an extravagance the country could ill afford.
6. ✅ Quadros won in 1961 and resigned after seven months; Vice-President Goulart took over.
7. ✅ Peron held office in Argentina from 1943 to 1955 and again in 1973-4: posts inside the military government from 1943, then elected president on 24 February 1946 and inaugurated on 4 June 1946.
8. ✅ Both men were impressed by the apparent success of Fascist Italy and Nazi Germany and adopted some fascist ideas, especially mass mobilisation; both won huge support from the poor working classes in the mass union movement; neither was really like Mussolini or Hitler; their governments are best summed up as a combination of nationalism and social reform.
9. ✅ Hobsbawm: "European fascist movements destroyed labour movements, the Latin American leaders they inspired, created them."
10. ⚠️ The populist bargain: recognition and benefits to urban workers, protection and credit to industrialists, patronage to the state's clients - financed by growth and avoiding the landed elite.
11. ⚠️ Structural exclusions: the landless rural population is outside the coalition, so land reform stays off the agenda.
12. ⚠️ Failure mode: when growth stops, the coalition cannot be financed without redistribution it was designed to avoid, and the army is asked to resolve the impasse.
13. ⚠️ Legacy: because populism created labour organisations rather than destroying them, those organisations outlived their founders and continued to structure politics for decades.
14. ⚠️ The Latin American army has intervened in both directions - installing a moderniser in 1930 and removing reformers in 1964 - and is best described as an autonomous institution with its own developmental view.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Latin American populism resembled European fascism on the surface and differed from it at the root, and state the consequence of that difference for the region's later politics.

**Model answer:** The resemblance was real and was acknowledged by the leaders themselves: Peron, who rose through the military government from 1943, was elected president on 24 February 1946 and inaugurated on 4 June 1946, holding office until 1955 and again in 1973-4, and Vargas, who led the Estado Novo in Brazil from 1937 to 1945, were impressed by the apparent success of Fascist Italy and Nazi Germany and adopted some of their ideas, particularly the mobilisation of mass support; both were nationalist, both governed personally, and Vargas acted as a dictator from 1937 with no elections held. The difference is functional and is captured in Hobsbawm's formulation that European fascist movements destroyed labour movements while the Latin American leaders they inspired created them. European fascism arose where large independent labour movements already existed and existed to break them; Latin American populism arose where the urban mass was newly formed, unorganised and politically unclaimed, and it recognised unions, legislated labour rights and enrolled workers in state-sponsored federations. The sources accordingly sum these governments up as a combination of nationalism and social reform rather than as fascism. The consequence is that populism left an institutional legacy rather than a vacuum: the unions, labour codes and political identities it created outlived their founders and continued to organise politics for decades, which is why a Workers' Party could carry a Brazilian presidential election in 2002.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring that populism was therefore a programme of social justice. It was an urban bargain: benefits to workers and protection to industrialists, financed out of industrial growth and constructed so as not to confront the landed elite. The landless rural majority was outside it, and the land concentration identified as the region's master structural problem went untouched in precisely the period when a broad popular government had the political capital to address it. That omission is why the bargain broke when growth stopped, and why the next generation had to choose between the revolutionary and the military roads.

---

## Lesson 7 - The economic arc, part one: from commodity dependence to state-led industrialisation

Progress: 7/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why commodity dependence was a political and not merely an economic condition; the wartime stimulus to manufacturing; the logic and vocabulary of import-substituting industrialisation; the regional institution that supplied the doctrine; the recorded regional remedies; the internal limits of the strategy; and the turn to external borrowing that follows

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's statement of the region's economic problems, its section on attempted solutions including the Latin American Common Market, and the Brazilian growth record of the military period
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the arc, with the joints marked

```text
  STAGE 1   COMMODITY EXPORT DEPENDENCE
            Chile: copper | Cuba: sugar, tobacco | Bolivia: tin
            (80% of Bolivian revenue in the 1950s)
            Venezuela: oil | Guatemala: bananas, coffee
            WEAKNESS: "A fall in the world price of that commodity
            would be a major disaster"
                |
                |  <-- JOINT 1: the Depression and the Second World
                |      War cut off European manufactures.
                |      "Factory industries did exist (the Second
                |      World War had acted as a stimulus because
                |      manufactured goods from Europe were almost
                |      impossible to come by)"
                v
  STAGE 2   IMPORT-SUBSTITUTING INDUSTRIALISATION
            protect the home market behind tariffs and licences
            + state investment + subsidised credit
            + state enterprise in steel, oil, power
            EXAMPLES: Vargas takes over economic planning and
            encourages industry; Venezuela builds steel plants to
            exploit local iron ore, and iron and steel become its
            most valuable export
                |
                |  <-- JOINT 2: the internal limits arrive.
                |      markets "restricted because the vast majority
                |      of people were too poverty-stricken to provide
                |      enough purchasing power"; capital goods and
                |      fuel must still be imported; fiscal deficits
                v
  STAGE 3   EXTERNAL BORROWING SUSTAINS THE STRATEGY
            "the borrowing was at its height from 1973 until 1982"
            Brazil under the generals borrows extensively from abroad;
            growth 10% a year 1968-74; exports quadruple
            Mexico borrows against expected oil revenue
                |
                |  <-- JOINT 3 (Lesson 15): US interest rates double,
                |      1979-81. The arc breaks.
                v
  STAGE 4   DEBT CRISIS, AUSTERITY, LIBERALISATION  (Lessons 15-16)
```

⚠️ The joints, not the stages, are where the marks are. ⚠️ Stage one to two is driven by an **external shock that creates an opportunity** - war cutting off imports. ⚠️ Stage two to three is driven by an **internal limit** - a domestic market too poor to absorb the output the strategy created. ⚠️ Stage three to four is driven by an **external shock that removes the financing**. ⚠️ An answer that names those three transitions has explained the region's economic century in three sentences.

### 1. Why the commodity problem is political

⚠️ Return to the evidence from Lesson 2 and push it one step further. ✅ Many countries were heavily dependent for exports on a limited range of products, sometimes even a single commodity, and a fall in the world price of that commodity would be a major disaster.

⚠️ Now ask what "major disaster" means concretely for a government, because the concreteness is what earns marks.

⚠️ A commodity price fall does four things simultaneously. ⚠️ It reduces **export earnings**, so the country can afford fewer imports. ⚠️ It reduces **government revenue**, because export taxes and state-company profits are the fiscal base - ✅ 80 per cent of all Bolivia's revenue came from tin exports in the 1950s. ⚠️ It reduces **employment** in the export sector and everything attached to it. ⚠️ And it weakens the **currency**, which raises the price of every imported good, including food and fuel.

⚠️ Those four effects arrive together, within months, and they are entirely outside the government's control. ⚠️ A minister who has done nothing wrong faces a budget crisis, an import crisis, an unemployment crisis and an inflation crisis at once. ⚠️ That is why commodity dependence is correctly described as a **political** vulnerability: it makes the survival of governments a function of prices set elsewhere.

⚠️ The evidence for the recurrence is spread across this session and should be assembled in an answer as a set. ✅ Venezuela: a fall in world oil prices in the early 1980s adversely affected revenue, at the same time as difficulties in iron and steel exports. ✅ Mexico: a sudden collapse in world oil prices in 1986 and a consequent reduction in revenues. ✅ Ecuador: a fall in the world price of oil in 1998 caused an economic crisis, inflation above 40 per cent and a poverty rate of around 70 per cent. ✅ Cuba: an economy at the mercy of fluctuations in world sugar prices, and in 1980 a sugar crop reduced by fungus infection and a tobacco crop seriously affected by another, plunging the island into economic crisis. ✅ Chile: the world price of copper fell suddenly by about 30 per cent during the Allende government, causing a further decrease in government revenue.

⚠️ Five countries, four decades, one mechanism. ⚠️ That set is worth more than any general statement about dependency.

### 2. The opening: how a world crisis created an industrial opportunity

✅ Factory industries did exist, and the Second World War had acted as a stimulus because manufactured goods from Europe were almost impossible to come by.

⚠️ This deserves a moment because the logic is counter-intuitive and therefore memorable. ⚠️ For a region whose manufacturers could not compete with European and American goods, the disappearance of those goods was not a hardship but a protective tariff imposed by history. ⚠️ Domestic producers suddenly faced a captive market. ⚠️ The Depression had already done something similar by collapsing export earnings and forcing import compression.

⚠️ The policy conclusion that Latin American governments drew was direct: **if involuntary protection produced industry, deliberate protection could sustain it.** ⚠️ That conclusion is the whole of stage two.

### 3. The strategy named, defined and given its vocabulary

⚠️ **Import-substituting industrialisation** means deliberately replacing imported manufactures with domestically produced ones behind tariff and licensing protection, financed and often directly undertaken by the state. ⚠️ It is the standard analytical vocabulary for the region's mid-century development strategy and should be used by name.

⚠️ Its four instruments are worth listing because they recur across every case in this session:

⚠️ **Protection** - tariffs, quotas and import licences to reserve the home market for domestic producers. ⚠️ **State investment** - the state building what private capital will not, especially in steel, energy and transport. ✅ Venezuela's steel plants, built to exploit local iron-ore deposits, made iron and steel its most valuable export. ⚠️ **Subsidised credit** - development banks lending below market rates to favoured sectors. ⚠️ **Planning** - ✅ in Brazil, for the first time the government took over economic planning, and Vargas was especially keen to encourage industry.

⚠️ The doctrine behind it has an institutional home, and naming it lifts an answer. ✅ The United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America, known by its Spanish acronym CEPAL and led from 1950 by the Argentine economist **Raul Prebisch**, supplied the region's structuralist analysis: that the world economy is divided into an industrial centre and a primary-producing periphery, that the terms of trade tend to move against the periphery over time, and that the periphery cannot therefore develop by specialising in what it already exports. ⚠️ Deliberate industrialisation follows as the policy conclusion.

⚠️ One qualification, and it matters for accuracy. ⚠️ Import substitution was not invented by an economist and then applied; ⚠️ it began as an improvised response to the Depression and the war and was **theorised afterwards**. ⚠️ Saying so is more accurate than presenting the region as executing a blueprint.

### 4. The regional attempts at a remedy, and their scale

✅ Several international organizations were set up to help: the Organization of American States, and the Latin American Common Market of 1960, which had some success in reducing tariffs. ✅ The United Nations helped by providing technical experts and holding conferences to discuss how underdeveloped nations might go about increasing exports. ✅ The United States provided massive economic aid through the Alliance for Progress.

⚠️ The regional integration attempt is the interesting one, because it addresses the strategy's central weakness directly. ⚠️ If the problem with import substitution is that each national market is too poor and too small to support efficient industry, then the obvious remedy is to make the market larger by pooling several of them. ⚠️ That is exactly what a common market is for.

⚠️ The recorded verdict is modest - ✅ "some success in reducing tariffs" - and the modesty is itself examinable. ⚠️ Regional integration among economies that produce similar primary goods and compete for the same external markets has less to trade with itself than integration among complementary industrial economies. ⚠️ This is also why the later regional bodies you meet in Lesson 19 - a customs union established in 1991, a union of South American nations in 2008 - matter politically more than they do commercially.

### 5. The internal limits, stated as a chain

⚠️ The strategy worked, and then ran into four constraints in sequence. ⚠️ Learn the chain; it is the substance of any question about why import substitution faltered.

```text
  [1] THE MARKET CONSTRAINT
      "markets were restricted because the vast majority of people
       were too poverty-stricken to provide enough purchasing power"
      -> the new factories can supply more than the country can buy
      -> and since the strategy deliberately avoided land reform,
         the rural majority never became consumers
                |
                v
  [2] THE IMPORT CONSTRAINT
      substituting consumer goods requires importing the machines
      that make them, plus fuel and industrial inputs
```
