```text
      -> the import bill changes composition but does not fall
      -> Brazil's growth rate fell from 10% (1968-74) to around 5%
         after 1974 "mainly because Brazil was having to import more
         of its oil supplies"
                |
                v
  [3] THE EFFICIENCY CONSTRAINT
      protection removes the pressure to compete
      -> costs stay high, so the protected industry cannot export
      -> "it was difficult to export because of competition from
          the advanced industrial nations"
                |
                v
  [4] THE FISCAL CONSTRAINT
      state investment, subsidised credit and social spending all
      draw on a revenue base still resting on commodity exports
      -> deficits
      -> financed by borrowing, or by printing money, or both
```

⚠️ Constraint one is the deepest, and the connection back to Lesson 1 should be made explicitly in an answer. ⚠️ The strategy's demand problem and the region's land problem are the same problem. ⚠️ A rural population of dependent labourers on great estates is not a market. ⚠️ Import substitution attempted to industrialise **around** the agrarian question instead of through it, and the agrarian question then set the ceiling on how far industrialisation could go. ⚠️ That is the most sophisticated single point available on this topic and it is worth constructing an answer around.

### 6. The turn to borrowing, and who lent

⚠️ Faced with constraints two and four, a government has three options: slow down, tax the elite, or borrow. ⚠️ Slowing down is politically impossible for a regime whose legitimacy rests on growth. ⚠️ Taxing the elite is politically impossible for a coalition built to avoid confronting it. ⚠️ So the region borrowed.

✅ The borrowing was at its height from 1973 until 1982. ✅ The Brazilian military government, faced with massive inflation and a stagnating economy, tackled the problems by borrowing extensively from abroad; ✅ countries that had been unwilling to lend to the Goulart government were quite happy to do business with a strong right-wing regime which had eliminated communist influence; ✅ this stimulated economic growth so that the years 1968-74 were a boom period, with an annual growth rate of 10 per cent and exports quadrupling.

⚠️ That passage carries a sharp political point that a good answer will extract. ⚠️ The same lenders who refused a reforming civilian government financed a military one. ⚠️ Credit was not politically neutral; it was itself an instrument of the system described in Lesson 3, operating on rung two of the ladder. ⚠️ The "economic miracle" of the Brazilian dictatorship was, in part, an artefact of the political preferences of international lenders.

✅ And Mexico borrowed on a different premise: confident that oil revenues would always be sufficient to cover interest payments, successive governments seemed to have abandoned restraint.

⚠️ Two different rationales, one common exposure. ⚠️ Both assumed that the future would resemble the present - that lenders would keep lending and that commodity prices would hold. ⚠️ Lesson 15 is what happened when neither assumption held.

### Criticism and reply: was import substitution a mistake?

⚠️ **The objection**, made forcefully after the 1980s, is that the strategy was misconceived: it built high-cost industries behind permanent protection, generated deficits, distorted prices and ended in debt.

⚠️ **The reply has three parts.** ⚠️ First, it achieved real transformation: ✅ by 1960 the number of Mexicans employed in manufacturing had overtaken those working in agriculture; ✅ Venezuelan iron and steel became the country's most valuable export; ✅ Brazil ended the century with arguably the most advanced industrial sector in Latin America, responsible for about one-third of gross domestic product and exporting motor vehicles, aircraft, machinery, computers and petrochemicals. ⚠️ None of that existed in 1930. ⚠️ Second, the alternative available at the time was not a successful export-manufacturing strategy but continued commodity specialisation, whose vulnerability is documented in section 1. ⚠️ Third, the proximate cause of the collapse was external: ✅ the doubling of American interest rates in the period 1979-81, not the industrial policy itself.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict**, and the one to write: import substitution succeeded in creating an industrial base and failed to create a self-financing one, because it did not address the domestic demand constraint that only agrarian reform could have relieved. ⚠️ The debt crisis then ended the strategy before that contradiction could be resolved politically.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This is the region's contribution to the syllabus's economic-history strand and its clearest case of a developmental state in the Global South. ⚠️ The thematic world economy belongs to the world-economy owner; the region's own sequence is taught here.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "economic strategy since 1945", "why did industrialisation fail to deliver", or a comparison with another developing region. ⚠️ The demand in every case is the **sequence**, not the policy list.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Naming the policies without the joints. ⚠️ The transitions carry the marks.
2. ⚠️ Presenting import substitution as a blueprint imported from economists. ⚠️ It began as improvisation under the Depression and the war and was theorised afterwards.
3. ⚠️ Treating the strategy as a simple failure. ✅ It produced a real industrial base in Mexico, Venezuela and Brazil.
4. ⚠️ Separating the demand constraint from the land question. ⚠️ They are the same constraint.
5. ⚠️ Attributing the collapse solely to bad policy. ✅ The proximate cause was the doubling of American interest rates in 1979-81.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Commodity exposure: Chile copper, Cuba sugar and tobacco, Bolivia tin at 80 per cent of revenue in the 1950s, Venezuela oil, Guatemala bananas and coffee; a price fall was a major disaster.
2. ⚠️ A price fall hits export earnings, government revenue, employment and the currency simultaneously and is outside government control - which makes commodity dependence a political vulnerability.
3. ✅ Recurrence set: Venezuelan oil revenue in the early 1980s, Mexican oil revenue in 1986, Ecuadorean oil in 1998 with inflation over 40 per cent and poverty around 70 per cent, Cuban sugar and tobacco fungus in 1980, Chilean copper falling about 30 per cent under Allende.
4. ✅ Factory industries existed and the Second World War acted as a stimulus because European manufactures were almost impossible to obtain.
5. ⚠️ Import-substituting industrialisation defined: replacing imported manufactures with domestic production behind tariff and licensing protection, financed and often undertaken by the state.
6. ⚠️ Its four instruments: protection, state investment, subsidised credit and planning.
7. ✅ Evidence of state-led industry: Brazilian economic planning under Vargas with jobs in electrical and steel manufacture; Venezuelan steel plants making iron and steel the most valuable export.
8. ✅ The doctrine's home: the United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America, known as CEPAL, led from 1950 by Raul Prebisch, whose centre-periphery analysis held that terms of trade move against primary producers, so development requires deliberate industrialisation.
9. ⚠️ Accuracy control: the strategy began as improvisation under the Depression and the war and was theorised afterwards.
10. ✅ Regional remedies: the Organization of American States; the Latin American Common Market of 1960, with some success in reducing tariffs; United Nations technical experts and conferences; the Alliance for Progress.
11. ⚠️ The four internal limits in order: a domestic market too poor to absorb output; capital goods, fuel and inputs still imported; protection removing the pressure to become competitive; and fiscal deficits from state investment and subsidy.
12. ✅ Brazil's growth of 10 per cent a year in 1968-74 fell to around 5 per cent after 1974 mainly because more oil had to be imported.
13. ✅ Borrowing was at its height from 1973 until 1982; lenders unwilling to finance Goulart were happy to finance a strong right-wing regime that had eliminated communist influence; Mexico borrowed on the assumption that oil revenues would always cover interest.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: import substitution built an industrial base but not a self-financing one, because it industrialised around the agrarian question rather than through it, and external credit postponed the contradiction until interest rates removed the credit.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why import-substituting industrialisation ran into a demand problem, and show that this problem was the same one identified in the region's land structure.

**Model answer:** Import substitution works by reserving the home market for domestic producers, so its success depends entirely on how large that home market is. The record states the constraint directly: markets were restricted because the vast majority of people were too poverty-stricken to provide enough purchasing power, and exporting was difficult because of competition from the advanced industrial nations. A factory protected from imports therefore faces a ceiling it cannot raise by producing better or cheaper, because the limit is the customers' income rather than the product. That income limit is the land structure. Agriculture remained backward because plentiful cheap peasant labour removed any incentive for estate owners to modernise, and Peru's huge estates were owned by men who ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs - a rural population held at subsistence is not a market for shoes, radios or bicycles. Raising rural incomes would have required redistributing land, which would have confronted the landowners who, as the record shows in Guatemala, Bolivia, Brazil, Argentina and Chile, could use the army to remove a reforming government. The strategy therefore industrialised around the agrarian question rather than through it, and the agrarian question set the ceiling on how far industrialisation could go. External borrowing, at its height from 1973 to 1982, postponed the contradiction rather than resolving it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the strategy was therefore worthless. It produced a genuine structural transformation: Mexican manufacturing employment overtook agricultural employment by 1960, Venezuelan iron and steel became the country's most valuable export, and Brazil ended the century with arguably the most advanced industrial sector in Latin America, at about a third of gross domestic product. The correct verdict is that it created an industrial base without creating a self-financing one, and that the proximate cause of its collapse was external - the doubling of American interest rates between 1979 and 1981 - rather than the industrial policy itself.

### Visual: ten years of reform, forty-two years of consequence

```text
  1901-1944   THE COMPANY STATE
              United Fruit Company activity from 1901; by the Second
              World War it "controlled almost half the country's best
              agricultural land and was the majority share-owner in
              the railways and the electricity system"
              Illustration of whose interest came first: the UFC
              "was reluctant to finance the building of new roads
              because this would reduce its profits from the railways"
              Population: about 40% Mayan Indians who did not speak
              Spanish; mainly landless peasants; country dominated by
              a few wealthy landowners and the army
              Military dictator Jorge Ubico, 1931-44
                      |
  Oct 1944    THE OPENING
              general strike; Ubico forced out by a mixed uprising of
              anti-government army officers, students and liberal
              intellectuals
                      |
  1945-1951   AREVALO: REFORM WITHIN THE SYSTEM
              foreign-owned estates confiscated and redistributed to
              peasants | minimum wage | building of houses, hospitals
              and schools | landowners required to house their farm
              labourers | political parties and trade unions permitted,
              union powers restricted
                      |
  1951-1954   ARBENZ: REFORM THAT REACHED THE COMPANY
              land reform touching uncultivated estate land;
              compensation offered and claimed to be insufficient
                      |
  early 1954  THE MULTILATERAL STEP
              OAS resolution: communist domination of any state in the
              Western hemisphere threatens the security of all members
              passed 17 votes to 1 - the 1 being Guatemala
                      |
  June 1954   THE REVERSAL
              American-backed force under Colonel Castillo Armas
              invades from Honduras and Nicaragua; American planes
              bomb Guatemala City; the Guatemalan army takes no part
              in the coup and does not defend Arbenz; Arbenz resigns
                      |
  1954-1996   THE COST
              Armas military dictator; parliament disbanded; leading
              communists arrested; Armas assassinated 1957; Ydigoras
              follows; 1960 uprising put down with American help;
              over 30 years of virtual civil war; death squads;
              3,252 political murders recorded in four months,
              Oct 1979-Jan 1980
                      |
  Dec 1996    THE SETTLEMENT
              peace agreement signed; URNG legalised; partial amnesty
              "some 200,000 people had been killed during those
              36 years, well over half of whom were Mayan Indians"
```

⚠️ Set the two durations side by side: **ten years of democratic reform, forty-two years of consequence.** ⚠️ That ratio is the lesson, and it is the single most effective way to answer a question about the cost of Cold War intervention in the region.

### 1. The company state, and why this case is unusually clear

✅ Situated on Mexico's southern border, Guatemala is one of the poorest states of Latin America, and its history during the twentieth century is an excellent illustration of United States involvement. ⚠️ The account's own verdict, and it is correct: this case is clear because the economic interest, the political decision and the military outcome can all be traced without inference.

✅ Largely an agricultural state, its economy depended on exports of bananas and coffee. ✅ The population, of which about 40 per cent were Mayan Indians who did not speak Spanish, consisted mainly of landless peasants, and the country was dominated by a few wealthy landowners and the army.

✅ In the early twentieth century the United States became heavily involved in Guatemala in the form of the powerful United Fruit Company. ✅ Beginning in 1901 the company gradually increased its activities and investments until, by the Second World War, it controlled almost half the country's best agricultural land and was the majority share-owner in the railways and the electricity system, among other things. ✅ This meant that although the foreign involvement brought many positive developments, in the last resort the interests of the company came first. ✅ The classic example was that the company was reluctant to finance the building of new roads because this would reduce its profits from the railways.

⚠️ Dwell on the road example, because it is the best small illustration in this session of what foreign ownership at that scale does to a country. ⚠️ Roads are a public good: they raise agricultural productivity, reduce food prices, connect villages to markets and schools, and are the elementary infrastructure of development. ⚠️ Railways owned by a single company are a private asset whose value depends on the absence of competing transport. ⚠️ When the majority owner of the railways is also the largest landowner, **national development and corporate profit point in opposite directions, and the corporation decides.** ⚠️ No intervention has taken place; no law has been broken; the country has simply been configured so that it cannot build its own roads.

⚠️ And note the account's honesty: the foreign involvement brought many positive developments. ⚠️ Keep the concession; it strengthens the argument rather than weakening it.

### 2. 1944: the opening

✅ In October 1944 dissatisfaction with this state of affairs reached a climax: during a general strike the long-serving military dictator Jorge Ubico, who had held power since 1931, was forced to step down by a mixed uprising of anti-government army officers, students and liberal intellectuals.

⚠️ Note the coalition, because it is the same coalition that appears in several other cases and it tells you something about how these openings happen. ⚠️ Junior officers, students and professionals - not peasants, not workers. ⚠️ These are the people with organisation, literacy and physical access to the capital. ⚠️ The mass whose grievances make the opening possible is not the group that carries it out, and that gap will matter when the reforms have to be defended.

### 3. Arevalo: reform that the system could absorb

✅ In 1945 democratic elections were held and the Christian Socialist Juan Jose Arevalo was elected president for a **six-year term, serving from 1945 to 1951**; the core account gives the term as five years. ✅ Much-needed reforms were introduced: many foreign-owned estates were confiscated and the land redistributed to peasants; a minimum wage was introduced; extensive building programmes were started, including new houses, hospitals and schools; landowners were required to provide adequate housing for their farm labourers; and the formation of political parties was allowed, and so was the formation of trade unions, although their powers were restricted.

⚠️ That is a substantial programme and it ran its full term. ⚠️ Why did it survive when its successor did not? ⚠️ Three plausible reasons, and an answer that offers them will read as analytical rather than narrative.

⚠️ **Scale.** Confiscation of "many foreign-owned estates" is not the same as a systematic national land reform reaching the largest single owner's core holdings. ⚠️ **Caution built in.** Unions were permitted but their powers were restricted - a reform that creates a constituency while limiting its capacity to act. ⚠️ **Timing.** The programme began in 1945, before the Cold War frame had hardened into the doctrine that would be asserted at the Organization of American States in 1954.

### 4. Arbenz, the land reform and the compensation dispute

⚠️ Here the core account is damaged in the extraction, and this session says so rather than filling the gap with invention. ✅ What survives in the extraction is the structure of the dispute: land reform proceeded, **compensation was offered**, and it was **claimed that it was not enough**.

⚠️ That much is decisive in itself, and it is the analytically important part, so an answer can be built on it safely. ⚠️ The dispute was not about whether to compensate. ⚠️ It was about **valuation** - about who has the right to price an expropriated asset, and on what basis.

⚠️ Three standard facts complete the picture, and a syllabus answer on this case without them would be incomplete; they are listed with their provenance in the final ledger. ✅ The agrarian reform law, known as **Decree 900**, was passed in June 1952 under President Jacobo Arbenz. ✅ It provided for the redistribution of **uncultivated** land on large holdings to landless peasants, with the owners compensated in government bonds. ✅ Among the properties affected were substantial uncultivated holdings of the United Fruit Company, which, with the United States government, rejected the compensation as inadequate.

⚠️ Now state the mechanism that made this explosive, because it is the heart of the case. ⚠️ The reform took **uncultivated** land, which is the mildest possible form of agrarian reform: it takes what is not being used. ⚠️ It **paid** for what it took. ⚠️ And the valuation basis was the owner's own declared value of the property for tax purposes - which meant that a company that had minimised its declared value to reduce its tax was now offered that same value as compensation. ⚠️ The reform was, in other words, legally scrupulous and fiscally elegant, and it still produced the region's most famous intervention.

⚠️ That is the examinable conclusion, and it is uncomfortable for every simple theory. ⚠️ The trigger was not radicalism. ⚠️ A moderate, compensated, legally enacted reform that touched a major United States corporate asset was sufficient. ⚠️ Which tells you exactly where the outer limit of permissible policy lay.

### 5. The multilateral step and the reversal

✅ Early in 1954 a resolution was carried at the Organization of American States declaring that communist domination of any state in the Western hemisphere posed a threat to the security of all member states; ✅ this was passed by 17 votes to one, the one being Guatemala.

⚠️ Note the sequence: the doctrine is established multilaterally **before** the action. ⚠️ That is not decoration; it converts a unilateral intervention into the enforcement of a collective security principle, which lowers its political cost across the hemisphere.

✅ In June 1954 an American-backed force led by Colonel Castillo Armas invaded Guatemala from Honduras and Nicaragua, while American planes bombed Guatemala City. ✅ Although the official Guatemalan army took no part in the coup, neither did they attempt to defend Arbenz, who was forced to resign. ✅ The United States had supplied rebels with weapons to overthrow a reforming government. ✅ The operation was conducted by the Central Intelligence Agency under the codename **PBSUCCESS**, and Arbenz resigned on **27 June 1954**.

⚠️ The decisive sentence in that paragraph is the one about the army, and most candidates miss it. ⚠️ The invading force was small; the Guatemalan army was the country's dominant institution and did not fight it. ⚠️ **Abstention, not invasion, decided the outcome.** ⚠️ This is the agency discipline in its clearest form: the external instrument worked because a domestic institution chose not to resist, and that choice had domestic reasons - an officer corps drawn from and allied to the landed elite that the reform threatened.

### 6. The verdict recorded, and the forty-two years

✅ The Americans insisted on calling the overthrow of President Arbenz an "anti-communist coup". ✅ But there seems little doubt that the Eisenhower government overestimated the threat from communism in Guatemala. ✅ It was prepared to sacrifice the Arbenz reforming government even though it meant violating the principle of non-intervention and souring relations with the rest of Latin America. ✅ Anti-American feeling spread, and "Yankee go home" became a common slogan throughout Latin America.

⚠️ That is the account's own assessment and it should be used as such: not a polemical claim but a recorded historical judgement, with a stated cost attached.

✅ Then the consequence. ✅ Armas took over and became a military dictator; parliament was disbanded and leading communists were arrested. ✅ Armas was assassinated in 1957 and was replaced by another military dictator, Miguel Ydigoras. ✅ United States aid was resumed and an uprising against Ydigoras was put down in 1960 with American help.

✅ Years of military dictatorship followed, during which the opposition constantly demanded social and economic reform. ✅ For over 30 years the country was in a state of virtual civil war: left-wing groups resorted to guerrilla attacks and kidnappings and were opposed by right-wing vigilante groups; the government used death squads against people deemed to be communists. ✅ It was calculated that in four months, from October 1979 to January 1980, during the presidency of General Fernando Romeo Lucas Garcia, 3,252 political murders had taken place.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** That figure is a calculation for a four-month window under a named president. ❌ Do not annualise it; ❌ do not extrapolate it across the war; ❌ do not merge it with the war's total. ⚠️ It is evidence of intensity at one moment, and that is exactly how it should be used.

✅ The instability continued inside the military itself: after an election said to have been won by a Lucas Garcia nominee, General Guevara, a group of army officers declared that the result had been fixed and in March 1982 put General Rios Montt in power; ✅ after little more than a year, in August 1983, another coup replaced Rios Montt with General Oscar Mejia. ✅ Montt complained that the United States had put pressure on him to take action against Nicaragua and that when he refused, they had engineered his removal in favour of somebody who would; ✅ soon afterwards Mejia did indeed announce that he saw the Sandinista government of Nicaragua as a threat to the whole of Central America.

⚠️ Report Montt's claim as a claim, which is how the account carries it. ⚠️ But note what it illustrates even as an allegation: by the 1980s the Guatemalan regime understood its own survival as depending on its usefulness in a neighbouring conflict. ⚠️ That is what a regional security system does to the domestic politics of its smaller members.

### 7. The return to civilian rule and the settlement

✅ Mejia promised a return to civilian democracy and in 1985 elections were held for a legislative assembly; the Christian Democrats emerged as clear winners with 51 seats, and in December their leader was elected president for five years. ✅ He managed to tread a narrow tightrope, trying to reconcile the guerrillas and the vigilantes while the army was a baleful background presence; ✅ to complicate matters further, the economy was in crisis and the treasury was empty, and his fear was that if reforming policies went too far he would be removed by United States intervention. ✅ He completed his full term.

⚠️ That fear is the most revealing sentence about Guatemalan democracy in the 1980s. ⚠️ An elected civilian president calibrating his reform programme against the anticipated reaction of a foreign government is the definition of constrained sovereignty, and it is being exercised **without any intervention taking place**. ⚠️ The memory of 1954 was doing the work. ⚠️ Deterrence is cheaper than intervention, and this is what it looks like from the inside.

✅ A later president tried to remove civil liberties and muzzle the press, causing an outcry from most sections of society, and the army forced him to resign; ✅ Congress reconvened and in June 1993 chose Ramiro de Leon, a popular civil rights leader, to complete the presidential term. ✅ De Leon was keen to bring a formal ending to the civil-war situation that had dragged on for well over thirty years; ✅ the Roman Catholic Church helped the government and Congress to agree on a programme of constitutional reform which came into operation in August 1994, and the United Nations became involved in the search for peace.

✅ It was not until 1996 that the civil war was officially ended. ✅ President Alvaro Arzu of the National Advancement Party, elected in January 1996, signed a peace agreement with the main guerrilla group, Guatemalan National Revolutionary Unity; ✅ in February he had personal meetings in Mexico with the rebel leaders and a ceasefire from 20 March was agreed; ✅ in December 1996 a formal peace agreement was signed, legalizing the guerrilla organisation and granting a partial amnesty to the various participants in the violence.

✅ The war was over at last, but not before some 200,000 people had been killed during those 36 years, well over half of whom were Mayan Indians, who were especially targeted because of their militancy. ✅ Although the fighting was officially over, there was inevitably a legacy of bitterness and mistrust; ✅ the elections of November and December 1999 were won by the Guatemalan Republican Front, and the new president faced daunting problems including a high crime rate, continued violence and corruption, and economic challenges.

⚠️ **Attribution control again**, and this is the most important instance in the lesson. ⚠️ The figure is *some* 200,000 killed over 36 years, with the ethnic composition stated as well over half Mayan Indians. ❌ Do not round it, ❌ do not attribute it wholly to one side, and ❌ do not merge it with the four-month figure from 1979-80. ⚠️ The account attributes the targeting of Mayan Indians to their militancy, and that attribution should be carried as stated.

⚠️ And note the indigenous dimension, because it is one of the required strands of this topic and Guatemala is where the evidence is strongest. ✅ About 40 per cent of the population were Mayan Indians who did not speak Spanish; ✅ they were mainly landless peasants; ✅ they were the principal victims of the war that followed the reversal of the land reform. ⚠️ Land, ethnicity and violence are not three subjects in Guatemala; they are one.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ Guatemala is the topic's reference case for Cold War intervention and for the collision between land reform and foreign property, and it supplies the indigenous dimension with unusual clarity.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ It appears as the evidence for "how did United States domination work", as a comparison case against Cuba and Chile, and in any question about the effects of the Cold War on the developing world.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Describing the land reform as radical or uncompensated. ✅ Compensation was offered and the dispute was about whether it was enough; the reform targeted uncultivated land.
2. ⚠️ Saying the Guatemalan army carried out the coup. ✅ It took no part in the coup and did not defend Arbenz - the abstention is what mattered.
3. ⚠️ Treating the multilateral resolution as incidental. ⚠️ It preceded the action and supplied its collective justification, 17 votes to one.
4. ⚠️ Extrapolating the four-month figure of 3,252 political murders. ⚠️ It belongs to October 1979 to January 1980 under a named president.
5. ⚠️ Ending the case in 1954. ⚠️ The examinable cost is the 36-year war and the settlement of December 1996.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Guatemala: Spanish colony from the mid-sixteenth century, independent 1821, briefly part of a Mexican empire and then of the United Provinces of Central America, fully independent from 1840; economy dependent on bananas and coffee.
2. ✅ About 40 per cent Mayan Indians who did not speak Spanish; mainly landless peasants; dominated by a few wealthy landowners and the army.
3. ✅ United Fruit Company active from 1901; by the Second World War it controlled almost half the country's best agricultural land and was majority share-owner in the railways and the electricity system; it was reluctant to finance new roads because that would reduce railway profits.
4. ✅ October 1944: general strike; the military dictator Jorge Ubico, in power since 1931, forced out by a mixed uprising of anti-government officers, students and liberal intellectuals.
5. ✅ 1945: Juan Jose Arevalo, a Christian Socialist, elected for a six-year term and serving from 1945 to 1951; many foreign-owned estates confiscated and redistributed; a minimum wage; building of houses, hospitals and schools; landowners required to house farm labourers; parties and unions permitted with union powers restricted.
6. ✅ Under Arbenz, land reform proceeded; compensation was offered and it was claimed that it was not enough.
7. ✅ The standard connecting facts: Decree 900 of June 1952 redistributed uncultivated land on large holdings with compensation in government bonds; uncultivated United Fruit Company holdings were among those affected; the company and the United States government rejected the compensation as inadequate.
8. ✅ Early 1954: an Organization of American States resolution declared communist domination of any hemispheric state a threat to all members, passed 17 votes to one, Guatemala dissenting.
9. ✅ June 1954: an American-backed force under Colonel Castillo Armas invaded from Honduras and Nicaragua; American planes bombed Guatemala City; the Guatemalan army took no part and did not defend Arbenz, who resigned; the operation's codename was PBSUCCESS and the resignation date 27 June 1954.
10. ✅ Recorded verdict: the Americans called it an anti-communist coup, but the Eisenhower government seems to have overestimated the communist threat and was prepared to sacrifice a reforming government despite violating non-intervention and souring relations with the region; anti-American feeling spread and "Yankee go home" became a common slogan.
11. ✅ Aftermath: Armas military dictator, parliament disbanded, leading communists arrested; Armas assassinated 1957; Ydigoras succeeded; a 1960 uprising put down with American help.
12. ✅ Over 30 years of virtual civil war: guerrilla attacks and kidnappings, right-wing vigilante groups, government death squads; 3,252 political murders calculated in the four months from October 1979 to January 1980 under General Fernando Romeo Lucas Garcia.
13. ✅ March 1982 Rios Montt installed by officers alleging a fixed election; August 1983 replaced by General Oscar Mejia; Montt complained the United States had engineered his removal after he refused to act against Nicaragua.
14. ✅ 1985 legislative elections won by the Christian Democrats with 51 seats; their leader elected president in December; he feared removal by United States intervention if reform went too far, and completed his term.
15. ✅ August 1994 constitutional reform with Church and United Nations involvement; January 1996 Arzu elected; ceasefire from 20 March; formal peace agreement December 1996 legalising the Guatemalan National Revolutionary Unity and granting partial amnesty.
16. ✅ Some 200,000 people killed during those 36 years, well over half of them Mayan Indians, especially targeted because of their militancy.
17. ⚠️ Master formulation: a compensated reform of uncultivated land, enacted by an elected government, was sufficient to trigger reversal - which locates the outer limit of permissible policy precisely, and the ten years of reform cost forty-two years of war.

### Concept check

**Question:** Guatemala's land reform was moderate, legal and compensated, and it was nevertheless reversed by force. Explain what that tells us about the limits on Latin American governments, and identify the domestic factor without which the reversal could not have happened.

**Model answer:** It tells us that the constraint operated on the object of policy rather than on its radicalism. The reform took uncultivated land on large holdings, offered compensation in government bonds and was enacted by an elected government under a law of June 1952; the dispute was not over whether to compensate but over valuation, since compensation was offered and it was claimed not to be enough. What made it intolerable was not its severity but the identity of the owner it reached - a United States corporation that by the Second World War controlled almost half the country's best agricultural land and was majority share-owner in the railways and electricity system. The outer limit of permissible policy therefore ran along the boundary of major foreign-owned property, not along any ideological line, which is why a moderate measure produced an invasion from Honduras and Nicaragua, the bombing of Guatemala City and the president's resignation in June 1954. The indispensable domestic factor is the army's abstention: the official Guatemalan army took no part in the coup, but neither did it attempt to defend Arbenz. The invading force was small, and the country's dominant armed institution declined to oppose it - a decision with domestic causes, since the officer corps was allied to the landed elite that the reform threatened. Without that abstention the external instrument would have failed, as it failed at the Bay of Pigs seven years later.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 1954 as the end of the episode. The examinable cost is what followed: military dictatorship, an uprising put down with American help in 1960, over thirty years of virtual civil war with guerrilla attacks, vigilante groups and government death squads, 3,252 political murders calculated in the four months from October 1979 to January 1980, and a war that ended only with the formal peace agreement of December 1996 after some 200,000 people had been killed in 36 years, well over half of them Mayan Indians. Ten years of reform were followed by forty-two years of consequence, and an answer that stops at the coup has measured the intervention without measuring its price.

---

## Lesson 9 - Cuba I: why Castro came to power, in the five recorded causes

Progress: 9/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the depth of American economic and political presence; the sugar monoeconomy and its seasonal unemployment; the failure of the political system and the Batista coup of 1952; the turn to violent revolution and Castro's early programme; the counter-productive repression; and the collapse of Batista's position on 1 January 1959

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's Cuba section setting out under five numbered heads the situation that resulted in Castro coming to power in January 1959, and the corresponding passage in the Cold War chapter
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five causes, and how each one closed a door

| # | The recorded cause | ⚠️ The door it closed |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | ✅ Long-standing resentment at the amount of American influence, dating back to 1898 when the USA helped rescue Cuba from Spanish control; American troops needed from time to time to maintain stability; American aid and investment kept the economy ticking over; American companies held controlling interests in all Cuban industries - sugar, tobacco, textiles, iron, nickel, copper, manganese, paper and rum - owned half the land, about three-fifths of the railways, all electricity production and the entire telephone system; the USA was the main market for exports; the American ambassador in Havana was usually referred to as the second most important man in Cuba | The **national** door: independence existed in form, and the country's economy and political weight sat elsewhere |
| 2 | ✅ Though prosperous compared with other Latin American countries, too dependent on sugar exports, with wealth concentrated in the hands of a few; unemployment varying from about 8 per cent during the five months of harvest to over 30 per cent for the rest of the year; no unemployment benefit; the trade unions, dominated by workers with year-round jobs in sugar mills, did nothing to help; the poverty of the unemployed in stark contrast to wealth in Havana and among corrupt officials; social tensions high | The **economic and organisational** door: the market offered seasonal work, the state offered no benefit, and the union offered nothing to the majority |
| 3 | ✅ No effective political system had been developed; in 1952 Fulgencio Batista, a leading politician since 1933, seized power in a military coup and ruled as a dictator; he introduced no reforms; the historian Hugh Thomas records that he spent a lot of time dealing with his private affairs and his foreign fortunes, leaving himself too little time for affairs of state; as well as being corrupt, his regime was also brutal | The **electoral** door: after 1952 there was no constitutional route to change |
| 4 | ✅ Since there was no prospect of a peaceful social revolution, the feeling grew that violent revolution was necessary; the leading exponent was Fidel Castro, a young lawyer from a middle-class background who specialised in defending the poor; before coming to power he was more of a liberal nationalist than a communist, wanting to rid Cuba of Batista and corruption and to introduce limited land reforms so that all peasants would receive some land; after an unsuccessful attempt to overthrow Batista in 1953, which earned him two years in jail, he began a campaign of guerrilla warfare and sabotage in the cities; the rebels soon controlled the mountainous areas of the east and north and won popular support there by carrying through Castro's land reform policy | This door **opens**: the alternative appears, and demonstrates governing capacity in the territory it holds |
| 5 | ✅ Batista's reaction played into Castro's hands: he took savage reprisals against the guerrillas, torturing and murdering suspects; even many of the middle classes began to support Castro as the most likely way of getting rid of a brutal dictator; morale in Batista's poorly paid army began to crumble in the summer of 1958 after an unsuccessful attempt to destroy Castro's forces; the USA began to feel embarrassment at Batista's behaviour and cut off arms supplies, a serious blow to the dictator's prestige | The **coercive** door closes on Batista: repression widened the opposition, the army lost morale and the external patron withdrew support |

⚠️ Read the right-hand column as a sequence and the revolution stops being mysterious. ⚠️ Four doors closed - national, economic, electoral and, finally, the regime's own capacity to coerce - while one door opened, and the movement that opened it had already demonstrated in the mountains that it could govern. ⚠️ **That is the general form of a successful revolution**, and it is the structure to carry into any comparative question.

### 1. Cause one: the depth of the presence, and the sentence that captures it

⚠️ Read the inventory in row one again slowly, because its cumulative effect is the point: all Cuban industries, half the land, three-fifths of the railways, all electricity, the entire telephone system, and the principal export market.

⚠️ And then the sentence that says more than the inventory: ✅ the American ambassador in Havana was usually referred to as the second most important man in Cuba.

⚠️ That is not a statistic; it is a description of how the arrangement was *experienced*. ⚠️ Everybody in Cuban political life knew where the real second vote lay. ⚠️ A nationalist movement in such a country does not have to invent a grievance or persuade anyone of its existence; it only has to name what everyone can see.

✅ And the account adds the decisive qualification, which is the reason the arrangement became intolerable: the American connection need not have been resented so much if it had resulted in an efficiently run country, but this was not so.

⚠️ That is a very important conditional and it should be quoted or paraphrased in any answer on this case. ⚠️ The grievance was not foreign presence as such; it was foreign presence **combined with bad government**. ⚠️ Dependence that delivers competence can be tolerated for a long time; dependence that delivers corruption cannot. ⚠️ This is also why the Cuban case does not generalise automatically to every country with heavy American investment.

### 2. Cause two: prosperity that reached nobody, and the seasonal trap

⚠️ Note the account's first clause, because it is the one candidates drop: ✅ Cuba was **prosperous compared with other Latin American countries**. ⚠️ This was not the poorest society in the region; it was a relatively rich one whose wealth was concentrated and whose labour market was seasonal.

⚠️ The seasonal figure is the most under-used piece of evidence in this topic and deserves to be turned over. ✅ Unemployment varied from about 8 per cent of the labour force during the five months of the sugar harvest to over 30 per cent during the rest of the year.

⚠️ Translate that into a life. ⚠️ For five months a worker has employment; for seven he does not. ⚠️ ✅ There is no unemployment benefit. ⚠️ There is therefore no possibility of accumulating anything, no basis for credit, no stability on which to plan a family's year, and no prospect that next year will differ. ⚠️ This is not poverty as a level of income; it is **poverty as a structure of time**, and it is far more politically corrosive than a low average wage, because it recurs predictably and is visibly caused by a crop cycle that somebody else owns.

⚠️ Then the organisational failure, which is the part that explains why the response was revolutionary rather than trade-unionist. ✅ The trade unions, dominated by workers who had all-the-year-round jobs in sugar mills, did nothing to help. ⚠️ The institution that in Europe would have represented the seasonally unemployed was controlled by the minority with permanent jobs and had no interest in the majority's problem. ⚠️ When the union is captured by the labour aristocracy, the excluded majority has no organisation of its own - which is precisely the vacuum that a guerrilla movement offering land can fill.

⚠️ ✅ And the contrast completes it: the poverty of the unemployed stood in stark contrast to the wealth in Havana and in the hands of corrupt government officials; consequently social tensions were high.

### 3. Cause three: the coup of 1952 and the closing of the electoral door

✅ No effective political system had been developed. ✅ In 1952 Fulgencio Batista, who had been a leading politician since 1933, seized power in a military coup and began to rule as a dictator. ✅ He introduced no reforms, and according to the historian Hugh Thomas he spent a lot of time dealing with his private affairs and his foreign fortunes, leaving himself too little time for affairs of state. ✅ As well as being corrupt, his regime was also brutal.

⚠️ The Thomas judgement is worth quoting because of what it specifies. ⚠️ The charge is not merely enrichment; it is **inattention** - a ruler whose private and foreign financial affairs consumed the time that government required. ⚠️ A regime can survive being predatory if it is competent. ⚠️ A regime that is predatory and absent has nothing left to offer anybody.

⚠️ And note the structural point in the first line, which the account states and which is easy to skim past: the problem was not that a good system was overthrown but that **no effective political system had been developed**. ⚠️ The 1952 coup removed a weak constitutional order rather than a robust one, which is why its removal produced so little resistance from institutions and why the eventual opposition had to be extra-constitutional.

### 4. Cause four: the alternative, and what made it credible

✅ Since there was no prospect of a peaceful social revolution, the feeling grew that violent revolution was necessary.

⚠️ That sentence is the hinge of the whole case and should be written into any answer on it, because it makes the causal claim explicit: **violence became the strategy because the peaceful route had been closed**, not because of an ideological preference for it.

✅ Castro was a young lawyer from a middle-class background who specialised in defending the poor. ✅ Before he came to power he was more of a liberal nationalist than a communist: he wanted to rid Cuba of Batista and corruption, and to introduce limited land reforms so that all peasants would receive some land.

⚠️ Hold on to that description, because Lesson 10 turns on it. ⚠️ The programme is **anti-corruption, anti-dictatorship and moderately agrarian**. ⚠️ It is not a programme for a socialist state, and the account is explicit that he was more of a liberal nationalist than a communist. ⚠️ Anyone who explains Cuba's later alignment as the execution of a prior communist plan has to explain away this sentence.

✅ After an unsuccessful attempt to overthrow Batista in 1953, which earned him two years in jail, Castro began a campaign of guerrilla warfare and sabotage in the cities. ✅ The rebels soon controlled the mountainous areas of the east and north and won popular support there by carrying through Castro's land reform policy.

⚠️ That last clause is the most important operational fact in the lesson, and it separates a movement that wins from one that does not. ⚠️ The rebels did not merely promise land reform; they **carried it through in the territory they held**. ⚠️ A guerrilla force that governs its own zone is making a demonstration rather than an argument: here is what we will do nationally, and here is the evidence that we can. ⚠️ Credibility acquired that way cannot be matched by a manifesto.

⚠️ The connecting names and dates are standard, and a syllabus answer that cannot name the movement looks thin. ✅ The 1953 attempt was the attack on the Moncada barracks at Santiago de Cuba on **26 July 1953**, from which the movement took its name, the **26th of July Movement**. ✅ After release and exile in Mexico, Castro and about eighty companions, including his brother Raul and the Argentine Ernesto "Che" Guevara, returned aboard the yacht **Granma** in **December 1956**; most were killed or captured on landing and the survivors established themselves in the **Sierra Maestra** mountains of eastern Cuba, from which the guerrilla campaign was conducted.

### 5. Cause five: how repression finished the regime

✅ Batista's reaction played into Castro's hands. ✅ He took savage reprisals against the guerrillas, torturing and murdering suspects. ✅ Even many of the middle classes began to support Castro as the most likely way of getting rid of a brutal dictator.

⚠️ This is the classic counter-insurgency failure and it deserves to be named as a mechanism, because it recurs and because examiners reward the generalisation. ⚠️ Indiscriminate repression converts a **political** conflict between a government and an armed group into a **social** conflict between a government and the society it governs. ⚠️ Once the middle classes - who have property, who fear disorder, and who are the natural constituency of any government claiming to defend order - conclude that they are safer without the regime than with it, the regime has lost the argument it exists to win.

✅ Morale in Batista's poorly paid army began to crumble in the summer of 1958, after an unsuccessful attempt to destroy Castro's forces. ✅ The USA began to feel embarrassment at Batista's behaviour and cut off arms supplies; this was a serious blow to the dictator's prestige. ✅ In September a small rebel force under Che Guevara, an Argentinian supporter of Castro, gained control of the main road across the island and prepared to move on Santa Clara. ✅ On 1 January 1959 Batista fled from Cuba, and a liberal government was set up with Castro at its head.

⚠️ Two observations to close.

⚠️ **The American withdrawal of support is decisive and is routinely omitted.** ⚠️ The patron that had sustained the arrangement withdrew arms supplies, and the account identifies the blow as one to *prestige* as much as to capability. ⚠️ In a system where everybody knows who the second most important man in Havana is, the withdrawal of his favour is itself a political event.

⚠️ **The description of the outcome is precise and should be preserved.** ✅ A **liberal** government was set up with Castro at its head. ⚠️ Not a communist government, not a socialist state - a liberal government. ⚠️ Everything that Cuba became after 1959 is the subject of the next lesson, and the whole interpretative question is how a liberal government headed by a liberal nationalist became a Soviet-aligned socialist state within thirty months.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ Cuba is the region's only successful revolution against the hemispheric order and is therefore available for any question on revolutions, on the Cold War in the developing world, or on challenges to great-power predominance.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The commonest framing asks why the revolution succeeded where reform elsewhere was reversed. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the domestic half of that answer; Lessons 10 and 11 supply the international half.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Describing pre-revolutionary Cuba as the poorest country in the region. ✅ It was prosperous compared with other Latin American countries; the problem was concentration and seasonality.
2. ⚠️ Calling Castro a communist in 1959. ✅ Before he came to power he was more of a liberal nationalist than a communist, and the government set up on 1 January 1959 is described as liberal.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the union failure. ✅ Unions dominated by permanent sugar-mill workers did nothing for the seasonally unemployed, which is why the excluded had no institutional voice.
4. ⚠️ Leaving out the American withdrawal of arms supplies in 1958. ⚠️ It is part of cause five and helps explain the speed of the collapse.
5. ⚠️ Treating the guerrilla campaign as purely military. ✅ The rebels won support by carrying through land reform in the areas they controlled.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Resentment of American influence dated back to 1898, when the USA helped rescue Cuba from Spanish control; the island remained an independent republic but American troops were needed from time to time to maintain stability.
2. ✅ American companies held controlling interests in all Cuban industries - sugar, tobacco, textiles, iron, nickel, copper, manganese, paper and rum - owned half the land, about three-fifths of the railways, all electricity production and the entire telephone system; the USA was the main market for exports, of which sugar was by far the most important.
3. ✅ The American ambassador in Havana was usually referred to as the second most important man in Cuba.
4. ✅ The connection need not have been resented so much had it produced an efficiently run country, but it did not.
5. ✅ Cuba was prosperous compared with other Latin American countries but too dependent on sugar, with wealth concentrated in few hands.
6. ✅ Unemployment ran at about 8 per cent during the five months of harvest and over 30 per cent for the rest of the year; there was no unemployment benefit; unions dominated by year-round sugar-mill workers did nothing to help; poverty contrasted starkly with wealth in Havana and among corrupt officials; social tensions were high.
7. ✅ No effective political system had been developed; Batista, a leading politician since 1933, seized power by military coup in 1952 and ruled as a dictator, introducing no reforms; Hugh Thomas records that he spent much time on his private affairs and foreign fortunes with too little time for affairs of state; the regime was corrupt and brutal.
8. ✅ With no prospect of peaceful social revolution, the feeling grew that violent revolution was necessary.
9. ✅ Castro: a young lawyer of middle-class background who specialised in defending the poor; before power he was more of a liberal nationalist than a communist, wanting to rid Cuba of Batista and corruption and to introduce limited land reforms giving all peasants some land.
10. ✅ 1953: an unsuccessful attempt to overthrow Batista earned him two years in jail; then guerrilla warfare and urban sabotage; the rebels soon controlled the mountainous areas of the east and north and won popular support by carrying through the land reform policy there.
11. ✅ The standard connecting facts: the 1953 attempt was the attack on the Moncada barracks at Santiago de Cuba on 26 July 1953, giving the 26th of July Movement its name; the return from Mexican exile aboard the yacht Granma took place in December 1956 with about eighty companions including Raul Castro and Che Guevara; the survivors based themselves in the Sierra Maestra.
12. ✅ Batista's savage reprisals, torture and murder of suspects drove even many of the middle classes to support Castro as the likeliest means of removing a brutal dictator.
13. ✅ Army morale crumbled in the summer of 1958 after a failed attempt to destroy Castro's forces; the USA, embarrassed, cut off arms supplies, a serious blow to Batista's prestige.
14. ✅ September 1958: a small rebel force under Che Guevara took the main road across the island and prepared to move on Santa Clara; 1 January 1959 Batista fled and a liberal government was set up with Castro at its head.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: four doors closed - national, economic, electoral and finally coercive - while one opened, and the movement that opened it had already governed the territory it held. That combination, not ideology, explains the outcome.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the revolution succeeded in Cuba when comparable reform movements elsewhere in the region were defeated, using only the domestic causes recorded for the Cuban case.

**Model answer:** Because in Cuba every alternative channel was closed at once, and the challenger had already demonstrated that it could govern. The national channel was closed by the depth of American presence: companies held controlling interests in all Cuban industries, owned half the land, three-fifths of the railways, all electricity and the entire telephone system, and the American ambassador was called the second most important man in Cuba - and, crucially, this arrangement had not produced an efficiently run country. The economic channel was closed by a monoeconomy whose labour market offered about 8 per cent unemployment for five harvest months and over 30 per cent for the rest of the year, with no unemployment benefit. The organisational channel was closed because unions dominated by permanently employed sugar-mill workers did nothing for the seasonally unemployed majority. The electoral channel was closed by Batista's coup of 1952, after which the regime introduced no reforms and its head, on Hugh Thomas's account, gave too little time to affairs of state. With no prospect of peaceful social revolution, the feeling grew that violent revolution was necessary. What made the alternative credible was not rhetoric but performance: the rebels controlled the mountainous east and north and won popular support there by actually carrying through the land reform policy. Batista then completed the process himself, since savage reprisals drove even much of the middle class into opposition, army morale crumbled in the summer of 1958, and the United States cut off arms supplies.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the outcome backwards and treating the revolution as a communist project from the start. The record states that before he came to power Castro was more of a liberal nationalist than a communist, wanting to remove Batista and corruption and to introduce limited land reform so that all peasants would receive some land, and it describes the government set up on 1 January 1959 as liberal. An answer that begins with communism cannot explain the middle-class support that was decisive in 1958, and it removes the genuinely interesting historical question, which is how a liberal government headed by a liberal nationalist became a Soviet-aligned socialist state within thirty months.

---

## Lesson 10 - Cuba II: how a liberal government became a Soviet-aligned state in thirty months

Progress: 10/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the escalation sequence of nationalisation, sugar threat, Soviet purchase and confiscation; the rupture of diplomatic relations in January 1961; the Bay of Pigs invasion of April 1961 and the declaration that followed; the continuing campaign and Operation Mongoose; the missile crisis handed to its owner with only the Cuban consequences retained; and the expulsion from the Organization of American States

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on how Cuba's foreign relations were affected, together with the corresponding passage in the Cold War chapter covering the Bay of Pigs, the missile crisis and its settlement
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the escalation, move by move, with the actor named at each step

```text
  1959 Jan   Castro heads a liberal government.
             "Cuban relations with the USA did not deteriorate
              immediately; Castro was thought to be, at worst,
              a social democrat, and so most Americans were
              prepared to give him a chance."
                 |
  CUBA      ->   nationalises American-owned estates and factories
                 |
  USA       ->   Eisenhower THREATENS to stop importing Cuban sugar
                 |
  CUBA      ->   is FORCED to sign a trade agreement with Russia
                 |
  1960 Jul
  USA       ->   carries out the threat
                 |
  USSR      ->   promises to buy Cuba's sugar
                 |
  CUBA      ->   confiscates all remaining American property
                 |
  1961 Jan
  USA       ->   breaks off diplomatic relations
                 |
  USSR      ->   was already supplying economic aid
                 |
  1961 Apr
  USA       ->   Bay of Pigs: about 1,400 men land; the operation is
                 so badly planned and carried out that Castro's forces
                 and his two jet planes have no difficulty crushing it
                 |
  1961 late
  CUBA      ->   Castro announces that he is now a Marxist and that
                 Cuba is a socialist country
                 |
  1961 Nov
  USA       ->   Kennedy approves Operation Mongoose, to "help Cuba
                 overthrow the Communist regime"; merchant ships sunk,
                 installations sabotaged, invasion exercises held
                 |
  1962
  CUBA      ->   appeals to the USSR for military help
                 |
  USSR      ->   missiles  [OWNED BY THE COLD WAR TOPIC]
                 |
  1962
  OAS       ->   Latin American states, most with right-wing
                 governments, expel Cuba - "which only made her more
                 dependent on the USSR"
```

⚠️ Two things are visible in the diagram that no narrative makes plain. ⚠️ First, **the alignment is the twelfth move, not the first**: the declaration of Marxism comes after the invasion, not before it. ⚠️ Second, **every move is a response to the previous one**, and the actors alternate. ⚠️ This is the structure of an escalation spiral, in which each participant takes a step that is reasonable given the last one and unreasonable given the whole.

### 1. The opening position: how much room there was

✅ Cuban relations with the USA did not deteriorate immediately; Castro was thought to be, at worst, a social democrat, and so most Americans were prepared to give him a chance.

⚠️ That sentence is worth its weight in marks, because it refutes the two commonest readings of this case at once. ⚠️ It refutes the claim that Washington was implacably hostile to the revolution from the first day - it was not, and it waited. ⚠️ And it refutes the claim that Castro arrived with a socialist programme he intended to implement - he was read as a social democrat by people with every incentive to read him accurately.

⚠️ So there was a genuine opening, and the question is what closed it.

### 2. The escalation, and who moved first at each stage

✅ Before long, however, he outraged the USA by nationalizing American-owned estates and factories.

⚠️ There is the first move, and it is Cuba's. ⚠️ Be honest about it: the revolution acted first, and it acted on exactly the asset class identified in Lesson 2 as the region's fourth structural problem and in Lesson 8 as the tripwire. ⚠️ A revolution whose domestic programme is land reform and the recovery of national assets cannot avoid this step; that is what it exists to do. ⚠️ But it is still a move, and an answer that presents Cuba as purely reactive has misdescribed the sequence.

✅ President Eisenhower threatened to stop importing Cuban sugar, forcing Castro to sign a trade agreement with Russia.

⚠️ Note the verb the account chooses: **forcing**. ⚠️ Think about what the threat actually meant for an economy with the structure described in Lesson 9. ⚠️ Sugar was by far the most important export and the USA was the main market. ⚠️ A threat to stop buying is therefore a threat to remove the country's principal source of foreign exchange in a single administrative act. ⚠️ A government facing that threat must find another buyer or capitulate. ⚠️ There was one other buyer in the world with the motive to take the whole crop.

⚠️ This is the same structural fact you met in Lesson 5, where Mexico, boycotted in 1938, sold oil to Germany and Italy. ⚠️ **The existence of a rival buyer converts a lethal dependence into a bargaining position** - and, in 1960, converts a bilateral quarrel into a Cold War alignment.

✅ In July 1960 when the Americans carried out their threat, the USSR promised to buy Cuba's sugar, and Castro confiscated all remaining American property. ✅ As Cuba's relations with the USA worsened, those with the USSR improved: in January 1961 the USA broke off diplomatic relations with Cuba, but the Russians were already supplying economic aid.

⚠️ Four moves in six months, each a direct answer to the last. ⚠️ By January 1961 the economic relationship has been fully reversed and the diplomatic relationship severed - and Castro has still not described himself as a Marxist.

### 3. The Bay of Pigs, and the declaration that followed it

✅ Convinced that Cuba was now a communist state in all but name, the new United States president, John F. Kennedy, approved a plan by a group of Batista supporters to invade Cuba from American bases in Guatemala. ✅ The Central Intelligence Agency was deeply involved. ✅ There was a general view in the USA at this time that it was quite permissible for them to interfere in the affairs of sovereign states and to overthrow any regimes which they felt were hostile and too close for comfort. ✅ The small invading force of about 1,400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs in April 1961, but the operation was so badly planned and carried out that Castro's forces and his two jet planes had no difficulty crushing it.

⚠️ Three points, in ascending order of importance.

⚠️ **The geography is not incidental.** ✅ The invasion launched from American bases in Guatemala - the country whose reforming government had been removed seven years earlier by an invasion launched from Honduras and Nicaragua. ⚠️ The 1954 template was being reused, and the physical base for it existed because 1954 had succeeded.

⚠️ **The failure is analytically decisive.** ⚠️ Compare it with Guatemala: there, a small force succeeded because the national army abstained. ⚠️ Here, a small force failed because the national army was the revolution's own, newly created, ideologically committed and personally loyal. ⚠️ **The difference between 1954 and 1961 is the coup-proofing of the armed forces**, and stating it in those terms answers the central comparative question of this topic in one sentence.

⚠️ **The declaration follows the invasion.** ✅ Later the same year, Castro announced that he was now a Marxist and that Cuba was a socialist country.

⚠️ Do not over-read that timing, and do not under-read it either. ⚠️ The honest formulation - which is also the highest-scoring one - is that the causation runs in both directions and the sources cannot settle the internal question. ⚠️ American hostility did not invent a Cuban communist; ⚠️ but the sequence recorded is that hostility, an economic rupture, a severance of relations and an armed invasion all preceded the public declaration, and that declaration then retrospectively justified everything that had gone before. ⚠️ Write it as: the alignment was produced by the interaction, not imported into it.

### 4. The campaign that continued, and the appeal that followed

✅ Kennedy continued his campaign to destroy Castro in various ways: Cuban merchant ships were sunk, installations on the island were sabotaged and American troops carried out invasion exercises. ✅ In November 1961 Kennedy gave the go-ahead for a secret operation known as Operation Mongoose which aimed to help Cuba overthrow the Communist regime. ✅ Castro appealed to the USSR for military help.

⚠️ That last sentence is the bridge to the missile crisis, and the direction of the request matters. ⚠️ **Cuba asked.** ⚠️ It did not receive an imposition; it solicited protection from a superpower because a different superpower was sinking its ships, sabotaging its installations and rehearsing an invasion ninety miles away.

⚠️ And the account records the Soviet motive in the same terms: ✅ the missile deployment was, among other reasons, a gesture of solidarity with an ally who was under constant threat from the USA - ✅ although the Bay of Pigs invasion had been a miserable failure, it was not the end of the United States threat to Castro, and Operation Mongoose followed in November 1961.

### 5. The missile crisis: the boundary, stated and held

⚠️ Here this session hands over, and the handover should be stated explicitly in an answer as well, because it demonstrates control of scope.

⚠️ **What belongs to the Cold War owner:** the strategic causes of the deployment, including the loss of the Soviet lead in intercontinental missiles and the 1959 agreement allowing American Jupiter missiles in Turkey; the crisis management of October 1962; the quarantine; the thirteen days; the settlement; the hotline; the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty of July 1963; and the historiographical dispute about Kennedy's handling.

⚠️ **What belongs here, because it is a fact about Cuba:** four things, and no more.

✅ **First**, the guarantee obtained. In the settlement, Khrushchev promised to remove the missiles and dismantle the sites; in return Kennedy promised that the USA would not invade Cuba again, and undertook to disarm the Jupiter missiles in Turkey, though he would not allow this to be announced publicly. ⚠️ That non-invasion pledge is the most important single thing the crisis did **for Cuba**: it converted an existential threat into a managed one and is a large part of the answer to why this revolution survived and the Guatemalan reform did not.

✅ **Second**, the humiliation. Castro was furious with Khrushchev for "deserting" him, apparently without consulting the Cubans, and Cuban-Soviet relations were extremely cool for several years.

⚠️ Use that. ⚠️ It is the cleanest available refutation of the claim that Cuba was a Soviet satellite. ⚠️ A satellite is not consulted, true - but a satellite also does not react with public fury and a multi-year chill. ⚠️ Cuba behaved as a small state that had been traded over its own head and resented it, which is the behaviour of an ally, not an instrument.

✅ **Third**, the isolation. In 1962 the other Latin American states, most of which had right-wing governments, expelled Cuba from the Organization of American States, which only made her more dependent on the USSR.

⚠️ That final clause states a mechanism worth generalising: **isolation deepens the dependence it is meant to punish.** ⚠️ A state cut off from its region has fewer options, and fewer options mean greater reliance on the single patron that remains. ⚠️ The same logic runs through the embargo in the next lesson.

✅ **Fourth**, the aftermath. After the missiles crisis, relations between the USA and Cuba remained cool, and the attitude of other Latin American states was one of extreme suspicion.

### Criticism and reply: was Cuba pushed or did it jump?

⚠️ **The objection.** The escalation story flatters Cuba. Castro nationalised first; he had revolutionary allies with Marxist commitments from early on; the alignment expressed a choice rather than a necessity.

⚠️ **The reply, conceding what must be conceded.** ⚠️ The first move was indeed Cuba's, and this session says so. ⚠️ But three recorded facts constrain how much that concedes. ✅ Castro was, before power, more of a liberal nationalist than a communist, and the government of January 1959 was liberal. ✅ The trade agreement with Russia is described as **forced** by the threat to sugar purchases - the account's own verb. ✅ The declaration of Marxism came after the Bay of Pigs, not before.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict**, which is the formulation to write: the revolution's domestic programme made a collision with United States interests near-certain; the *form* the collision took - a Cold War alignment rather than a bilateral quarrel settled by compensation, as in Mexico in 1941 - was produced by the escalation, and by the existence of a rival superpower willing to buy the sugar. ⚠️ Necessity and choice are both present, and an answer that insists on only one has lost the case's interest.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson is the point where a domestic Latin American conflict becomes a global one, and it is therefore the hinge between this topic and the Cold War topic.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "assess the significance of the Cuban Revolution", or a question on the Cold War in the developing world. ⚠️ The mark-winning move is to show the sequence and to state the boundary rather than drifting into a missile-crisis narrative that belongs elsewhere.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Beginning the story with a communist Castro. ✅ Relations did not deteriorate immediately and he was thought at worst a social democrat.
2. ⚠️ Placing the Marxism declaration before the Bay of Pigs. ✅ It came later the same year, after the invasion.
3. ⚠️ Writing a missile-crisis narrative. ⚠️ The crisis belongs to the Cold War owner; only the non-invasion pledge, the Cuban fury, the expulsion and the cooled relations are owned here.
4. ⚠️ Calling Cuba a Soviet satellite. ✅ Castro was furious at being deserted without consultation and relations were extremely cool for several years.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting that expulsion deepened dependence. ✅ The account says the expulsion only made her more dependent on the USSR.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Relations did not deteriorate immediately; Castro was thought at worst a social democrat and most Americans were prepared to give him a chance.
2. ✅ He outraged the USA by nationalizing American-owned estates and factories.
3. ✅ Eisenhower threatened to stop importing Cuban sugar, forcing Castro to sign a trade agreement with Russia.
4. ✅ July 1960: the Americans carried out the threat; the USSR promised to buy Cuba's sugar; Castro confiscated all remaining American property.
5. ✅ January 1961: the USA broke off diplomatic relations; the Russians were already supplying economic aid.
6. ✅ April 1961: about 1,400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs from American bases in Guatemala with deep Central Intelligence Agency involvement; the operation was so badly planned and carried out that Castro's forces and his two jet planes had no difficulty crushing it.
7. ✅ There was a general view in the USA at the time that it was quite permissible to interfere in the affairs of sovereign states and to overthrow regimes felt to be hostile and too close for comfort.
8. ✅ Later in 1961 Castro announced that he was now a Marxist and that Cuba was a socialist country.
9. ✅ The campaign continued: Cuban merchant ships sunk, installations sabotaged, invasion exercises held; Operation Mongoose approved in November 1961 to help Cuba overthrow the Communist regime; Castro appealed to the USSR for military help.
10. ✅ The missile deployment was in part a gesture of solidarity with an ally under constant threat; the Bay of Pigs failure had not ended the threat.
11. ✅ Owned here from the settlement: Kennedy promised that the USA would not invade Cuba again; Castro was furious with Khrushchev for deserting him apparently without consulting the Cubans, and Cuban-Soviet relations were extremely cool for several years.
12. ✅ 1962: the Latin American states, most with right-wing governments, expelled Cuba from the Organization of American States, which only made her more dependent on the USSR; afterwards relations with the USA remained cool and regional attitudes suspicious.
13. ⚠️ Boundary: the strategic causes of the deployment, the quarantine, the crisis management, the hotline and the Test Ban Treaty belong to the Cold War owner.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: alignment was the outcome of an escalation spiral, not its premise; the decisive enabling condition was the existence of a rival buyer for the sugar crop, and the decisive protective condition was the non-invasion pledge of 1962.

### Concept check

**Question:** Set out the sequence by which Cuba moved from a liberal government in January 1959 to a declared socialist state aligned with the USSR, and identify the two structural conditions without which the sequence could not have run.

**Model answer:** The sequence alternates between the two actors. Relations did not deteriorate immediately, because Castro was thought at worst a social democrat. Cuba then nationalised American-owned estates and factories; Eisenhower threatened to stop importing Cuban sugar, which forced Castro into a trade agreement with Russia; in July 1960 the Americans carried out the threat, the USSR promised to buy the sugar, and Castro confiscated all remaining American property; in January 1961 the USA broke off diplomatic relations while Soviet economic aid was already arriving; in April 1961 about 1,400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs from American bases in Guatemala and were crushed; and only later in that year did Castro announce that he was now a Marxist and Cuba a socialist country. The campaign continued with sunken merchant ships, sabotage and invasion exercises, and Operation Mongoose was approved in November 1961, after which Castro appealed to the USSR for military help. The first structural condition is the monoeconomy: sugar was by far the most important export and the USA the main market, so a purchasing threat was an existential threat rather than a commercial one. The second is the existence of a rival buyer with a motive to take the whole crop, which converted that existential threat into a bargaining position and simultaneously converted a bilateral quarrel into a Cold War alignment.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Cuba thereafter as a Soviet satellite. The record shows a small state acting on its own account and resenting its patron: Castro was furious with Khrushchev for deserting him apparently without consulting the Cubans when the missiles were withdrawn, and Cuban-Soviet relations were extremely cool for several years. Dependence on the USSR was real and increased when the other Latin American states expelled Cuba from the Organization of American States in 1962, but dependence produced by isolation is not the same as direction from Moscow, and the distinction is exactly what a question on Cuban agency is testing.

## Lesson 11 - Cuba III: the record, the cost and the survival

Progress: 11/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the inherited problems and the government's response; the recorded social achievements by the end of the 1970s; the embargo, sabotage and destabilisation campaign; the attempt to export revolution and its outcomes; the failure to diversify and the crisis of 1980; the shock of 1991 and the survival that followed; and the succession of February 2008

Book context: queried - the core account's section on Castro and his problems, covering the inherited economic structure, the social reforms, the historian's assessment of the first decade, the destabilisation campaign, the export of revolution, the crisis of 1980, the loss of the Soviet supporter in 1991 and the position to 2008

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the balance sheet, kept honestly on both sides

| Dimension | ✅ What the record credits | ✅ What the record debits |
|---|---|---|
