| **Social provision** | By the end of the 1970s all children were receiving some education, instead of fewer than half before 1959; sanitation, hygiene and health care were much improved; unemployment and corruption were reduced; there was a greater sense of equality and stability than ever before; the government seemed popular with the vast majority | - |
| **Equality** | Equality for black people and more rights for women; touring cinemas, theatres, concerts and art exhibitions | - |
| **Economic structure** | Agricultural land taken over by the government and collective farms introduced; factories and businesses nationalised; attempts to modernise sugar production and increase output and to introduce new industries | The attempt to diversify industrial and agricultural output was disappointing, so the economy still depended unhealthily on the quality of the sugar harvest, the world price of sugar and the willingness of the USSR and her satellites to buy Cuba's exports |
| **External environment** | These successes were achieved against a background of continual harassment and attempted destabilisation | Trade embargo; bomb attacks on Cuban factories, oil refineries and sugar refineries; under Nixon the campaign intensified to such an extent that it amounted to United States government-sponsored state terrorism; in the 1990s the embargo became more stringent than ever, condemned by the European Union, with the Clinton administration rejecting this "interference" |
| **Exporting revolution** | Cuban support for rebels who seized power in Algeria; backing for the Marxist MPLA in Angola in 1975 and for Mengistu in Ethiopia in 1977; Castro seen as a hero by most Third World countries | The attempt in Argentina was crushed by the Argentinian army in early 1964; involvement in the former Belgian Congo was unsuccessful; Che Guevara's attempt in Bolivia ended in disaster with his capture and execution in October 1967; Castro's popularity slumped when he supported the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan in 1979 |
| **Resilience** | The regime continued to survive after 1991; tourism boosted the economy in the closing years of the century; Venezuela agreed in October 2000 to supply oil at favourable prices | 1980 sugar crop reduced by fungus and tobacco seriously affected by another, plunging the island into economic crisis, with unemployment rising again, thousands emigrating to the USA, food rationing introduced and the whole economy heavily subsidised by the USSR; by 1991 Cuba had lost its most powerful supporter; a new crisis in 2002 from drought, a poor 2001 sugar harvest and the effect of the September 2001 attacks on tourism; most Latin American states still viewed her as an outcast and Cuba was the only country in the Americas not invited to the third Summit of the Americas at Quebec in 2001 |

⚠️ Keep both columns. ⚠️ An answer that reproduces only the left column is propaganda; one that reproduces only the right is polemic; ⚠️ one that holds both and then explains the relationship between them is an answer.

### 1. What was inherited, and what the government did with it

✅ Cuba was heavily dependent on the USA - and later the USSR - buying most of her sugar exports; the economy relied far too much on the sugar industry and was at the mercy of fluctuations in world sugar prices. ✅ The whole government and administration were riddled with corruption, and in addition there was serious unemployment and poverty. ✅ The new government launched itself into tackling the problems with enthusiasm and dedication.

✅ The historian David Harkness writes that, during his first ten years, Castro took this poor and backward country by the scruff of the neck and shook it into new and radically different patterns of life.

⚠️ Attribute that judgement, because it is a historian's characterisation and not a neutral statement. ⚠️ But note what makes it useful in an answer: it is an assessment of **intensity** rather than of success, and it is compatible with both columns of the balance sheet.

✅ The measures: agricultural land was taken over by the government and collective farms were introduced; factories and businesses were nationalised; attempts were made to modernize sugar production and increase output, and to introduce new industries and reduce Cuba's dependence on sugar. ✅ Social reform included attempts to improve education, housing, health, medical facilities and communications. ✅ There was equality for black people and more rights for women. ✅ There were touring cinemas, theatres, concerts and art exhibitions. ✅ Castro himself seemed to have boundless energy; he was constantly travelling around the island, making speeches and urging people to greater efforts.

⚠️ Two items in that list belong to strands this topic must cover and are usually omitted from answers.

⚠️ **Race.** ✅ Equality for black people is recorded as an explicit achievement. ⚠️ In a region whose social hierarchy descended directly from a colonial racial order, and in which the indigenous and Afro-descendant populations were disproportionately landless and poor, this is a substantive claim and it is rarely matched elsewhere in the region in this period.

⚠️ **Gender.** ✅ More rights for women is likewise recorded. ⚠️ It belongs with the other gender evidence in this session - Chamorro's election in 1990, Bachelet and Rousseff, and the Venezuelan requirement that at least half of each party's election candidates be female - and together they make it possible to answer a gender-dimension question from evidence rather than from assertion.

### 2. The achievements, dated and bounded

✅ By the end of the 1970s the government could claim considerable success, especially in the area of social reform. ✅ All children were now receiving some education, instead of fewer than half before 1959; sanitation, hygiene and health care were much improved; unemployment and corruption were reduced; and there was a greater sense of equality and stability than ever before. ✅ The government seemed to be popular with the vast majority of people.

⚠️ Three disciplines apply to this paragraph.

⚠️ **Date it.** The claim is "by the end of the 1970s". ⚠️ It is not a claim about 1985 or 1995, and the crisis that begins in 1980 is in the next section.

⚠️ **Preserve the comparator.** ✅ "Instead of fewer than half before 1959" is what makes the education claim meaningful. ⚠️ Without the baseline the statement is an assertion; with it, it is a measured change.

⚠️ **Note the qualifier on popularity.** ✅ The government *seemed* to be popular with the vast majority. ⚠️ That is a careful formulation about a state without competitive elections, and it should be carried as carefully as it is given.

### 3. The external environment, and why it belongs in the assessment

✅ These successes were achieved against a background of continual harassment and attempts at destabilization by the USA. ✅ These included a trade embargo, bomb attacks on Cuban factories, oil refineries and sugar refineries. ✅ Under President Nixon the campaign intensified to such an extent that it amounted to United States government-sponsored state terrorism. ✅ During the 1990s the economic embargo on Cuba became more stringent than ever; it was condemned by the European Union, but the Clinton administration rejected this "interference".

⚠️ That is a strong characterisation and it is the account's own; report it as such rather than softening or sharpening it.

⚠️ The analytical use is specific and it cuts both ways, which is why it is valuable. ⚠️ **In Cuba's favour:** any assessment of the economic record must control for a sustained campaign of embargo and physical sabotage against the country's industrial plant. ⚠️ Comparing Cuban economic performance with an unembargoed neighbour's is not comparing like with like. ⚠️ **Against Cuba's defenders:** the account itself does not treat the embargo as a complete explanation, and neither should an answer. ✅ It states that the destabilisation policy and the embargo meant that *some* of Castro's economic policies had little success - a qualified attribution, not a blanket one.

⚠️ And the European condemnation is worth one clause in an answer, because it shows the policy isolating its author as well as its target.

### 4. Exporting revolution: the record, including the failures

✅ Undeterred by all this, Castro and his supporters, especially Che Guevara, did their best to spread their revolution, first into Che's native Argentina. ✅ In early 1964 this attempt was crushed by the Argentinian army. ✅ The Cubans turned their attention to Africa, helping rebels to seize power in Algeria and then becoming unsuccessfully involved in the civil war in the former Belgian Congo. ✅ In 1966 Che Guevara tried to organize a revolution in Bolivia but his expedition ended in disaster when he was captured and executed in October 1967.

✅ The Bolivian detail from that country's own section completes the picture: in 1967 the army, with the help of United States advisers, easily defeated a guerrilla campaign led by Ernesto "Che" Guevara, who was captured and murdered on Central Intelligence Agency orders.

⚠️ Report both formulations - executed, and murdered on Central Intelligence Agency orders - as the account carries them, and note that the same source supplies both.

✅ Turning their attention back to Africa, the Cubans backed the Marxist MPLA in Angola in 1975 and the Marxist leader Mengistu in Ethiopia in 1977. ✅ Castro was now seen as a hero by most Third World countries, though his popularity slumped when he declared his support for the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan in 1979.

⚠️ Four observations, and the third is the one that wins marks.

⚠️ **The record is mixed, and the failures are in Latin America.** ⚠️ Argentina crushed; Bolivia a disaster; Congo unsuccessful. ⚠️ The successes recorded are in Africa. ⚠️ This is evidence against the proposition that the Cuban revolution was exportable within its own region, and it is the beginning of an answer to why no second Cuba appeared.

⚠️ **Why it failed in the region is answerable from this session.** ⚠️ The Cuban conjunction of causes described in Lesson 9 was unusually complete: a closed electoral system after 1952, a uniquely deep foreign presence, a seasonal labour market, captured unions and a regime that destroyed its own middle-class support. ⚠️ Elsewhere at least one of those doors was still ajar.

⚠️ **Cuba is a Global South actor, not merely an object.** ⚠️ A small Caribbean island shaped outcomes in Angola and Ethiopia and was regarded as a hero across the developing world. ⚠️ That is the strongest single piece of evidence in this session for Latin American agency in world politics, and it should be used whenever a question invites a "periphery as passive" framing.

⚠️ **The limit of that standing is recorded too.** ✅ Popularity slumped over Afghanistan in 1979. ⚠️ Non-alignment had a price, and endorsing a superpower invasion was above it. ⚠️ An answer that notes this looks balanced rather than admiring.

### 5. The economic failure that the achievements could not cover

✅ The attempt to diversify industrial and agricultural output was disappointing, and so the island's economy still depended unhealthily on the quality of the sugar harvest, the world price of sugar and the willingness of the USSR and her satellites to buy Cuba's exports.

⚠️ Read that sentence against Lesson 9. ⚠️ The pre-revolutionary economy depended on the sugar harvest, the world price of sugar and the willingness of the United States to buy. ⚠️ The post-revolutionary economy depended on the sugar harvest, the world price of sugar and the willingness of the USSR to buy. ⚠️ **The structure did not change; only the buyer did.**

⚠️ That is the most important single judgement available on the Cuban Revolution's economic record and it should be written in exactly that form. ⚠️ The revolution transformed distribution - education, health, employment, racial and gender equality - and did not transform production. ⚠️ And because it did not transform production, the vulnerability that had made the old regime fragile was inherited intact.

✅ The proof arrived in 1980, when the sugar crop was reduced by a fungus infection while the tobacco crop was seriously affected by another; ✅ this plunged the island into an economic crisis, unemployment rose again and thousands of people began to emigrate to the USA; ✅ food rationing was introduced and the whole economy was being heavily subsidized by the USSR.

⚠️ A crop disease produced a national emergency and an emigration wave. ⚠️ Twenty-one years after the revolution, that is a monoeconomy behaving exactly as Lesson 7 says a monoeconomy behaves.

### 6. 1991 and after: the survival that requires explaining

✅ By 1991, when the USSR split up and ceased to be communist, Cuba had lost its most powerful supporter. ✅ However, the Castro regime continued to survive.

⚠️ Sit with the scale of that. ⚠️ A small island economy, heavily subsidised by a patron, under an embargo that became more stringent than ever in the same decade, lost the patron entirely - and the regime did not fall. ⚠️ Explaining that is a genuine analytical task, and the account supplies three components.

✅ **A new sector.** During the closing years of the twentieth century the economy was boosted by a growth in tourism.

✅ **A new patron, on different terms.** Castro continued to enjoy good relations with Venezuela: in October 2000 the Venezuelan government agreed to provide Cuba with oil at favourable prices. ⚠️ Lesson 19 completes this: ✅ Chavez gave economic support to Cuba, providing a lifeline for a country that had lost its main supporter when the USSR collapsed; ✅ Venezuela supplied oil from its state-owned refineries at very reasonable prices and in return Cuba sent thousands of doctors and teachers to work in Venezuela, and Cuban doctors also worked in Bolivia and provided medical supplies.

⚠️ That exchange is worth a sentence of its own in any answer on South-South cooperation. ⚠️ Oil for doctors is a barter between two states each rich in what the other lacks, conducted outside the dollar economy and outside the institutions of Lesson 16. ⚠️ And it turns the Cuban social achievement into an export: the education and health systems built in the 1960s became, forty years later, the country's tradeable asset.

⚠️ **Continued isolation nonetheless.** ✅ Most Latin American states still viewed her as an outcast; ✅ Cuba was the only country in the Americas not invited to the third Summit of the Americas, held in Quebec in 2001. ✅ A new economic crisis developed in 2002, caused partly by drought and the consequent poor sugar harvest in 2001, and partly because the terrorist attacks of September 2001 in the USA adversely affected tourism.

⚠️ Note the vulnerability in that last sentence: having diversified into tourism, Cuba had exchanged exposure to the sugar price for exposure to the international travel market - and an event in New York produced a recession in Havana. ⚠️ Diversification reduced the dependence without removing the exposure.

✅ Finally, the succession: in February 2008 ill health forced Castro, aged 80, to hand over the presidency to his younger brother Raul, aged 78. ✅ Since then there have been some modest improvements: in March 2008 the use of mobile phones was legalized, a measure designed to appeal to the young; ✅ peasants are now allowed to cultivate unused land on collective farms and there have been improvements in the pricing of agricultural products that led to farmers bringing more food to market; ✅ hurricanes in the autumn of 2008 caused extensive damage and held up progress. ✅ The regional record also notes that the attempt to remove Castro failed miserably and that in 2012 his brother Raul was still in power.

⚠️ The agricultural measures are the analytically interesting ones. ⚠️ Allowing cultivation of unused collective land and improving producer prices is an admission that the collectivised structure was under-producing and that price incentives raise output - a small, late, pragmatic correction of the 1960s settlement, and the same kind of correction other one-party states in the developing world made in the same decades.

### Criticism and reply: was the Cuban Revolution worth it?

⚠️ **The objection.** Universal education and improved health were purchased at the cost of political liberty, a permanently distorted economy, dependence first on one superpower and then on a regional patron, and decades of emigration.

⚠️ **The reply.** All of that is conceded and is in the right-hand column. ⚠️ But the comparison that matters is not with an ideal alternative; it is with the counterfactual that the region actually supplies. ⚠️ The available alternative for a small Caribbean or Central American state in this period was the Guatemalan path - reform attempted, reform reversed, thirty-six years of civil war and some 200,000 dead - or the Nicaraguan path, where a revolution that did permit elections faced a decade of financed insurgency and ended, on the account's own record, as the poorest country in the Western hemisphere.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ Cuba is the only case in the region where a government that expropriated major United States assets was still in office fifty years later, and it achieved that by exchanging one external patron for another and by accepting a permanent closure of domestic political competition. ⚠️ Both halves of that sentence are the answer.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson supplies the evidence for the effects of a socialist regime on society, which is a direct syllabus clause, and it is the region's principal case of Global South agency.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect an assessment demand - the significance, or the achievements and failures, of the Cuban Revolution. ⚠️ The structure that scores is the two-column balance sheet followed by the single judgement that distribution changed and production did not.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Undated achievement claims. ✅ The social record is stated as a position by the end of the 1970s, and the 1980 crisis follows immediately.
2. ⚠️ Dropping the education baseline. ✅ All children receiving some education, instead of fewer than half before 1959.
3. ⚠️ Using the embargo as a complete explanation. ✅ The account says it meant that *some* of Castro's economic policies had little success.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the failures of exported revolution. ✅ Argentina crushed in 1964, Congo unsuccessful, Bolivia a disaster ending in October 1967.
5. ⚠️ Presenting Cuba as a passive object of the Cold War. ✅ It backed the MPLA in Angola in 1975 and Mengistu in Ethiopia in 1977 and was seen as a hero by most Third World countries.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting the Afghanistan reversal. ✅ Popularity slumped after he supported the Soviet invasion in 1979.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Inherited: heavy dependence on the USA, and later the USSR, buying most sugar exports; excessive reliance on sugar; exposure to world sugar prices; corruption throughout government and administration; serious unemployment and poverty.
2. ✅ David Harkness: during his first ten years Castro took this poor and backward country by the scruff of the neck and shook it into new and radically different patterns of life.
3. ✅ Measures: agricultural land taken over and collective farms introduced; factories and businesses nationalised; attempts to modernise sugar production and to introduce new industries; social reform in education, housing, health, medical facilities and communications; equality for black people and more rights for women; touring cinemas, theatres, concerts and art exhibitions.
4. ✅ By the end of the 1970s: all children receiving some education instead of fewer than half before 1959; much improved sanitation, hygiene and health care; reduced unemployment and corruption; a greater sense of equality and stability than ever before; the government seemed popular with the vast majority.
5. ✅ Against a background of continual harassment and attempted destabilisation: a trade embargo and bomb attacks on Cuban factories, oil refineries and sugar refineries; under Nixon the campaign amounted to United States government-sponsored state terrorism; in the 1990s the embargo became more stringent than ever and was condemned by the European Union, with the Clinton administration rejecting this interference.
6. ✅ Exported revolution: Argentina crushed by the Argentinian army in early 1964; rebels helped to seize power in Algeria; unsuccessful involvement in the former Belgian Congo; Che Guevara's Bolivian expedition of 1966 ending in his capture and execution in October 1967, recorded elsewhere as murder on Central Intelligence Agency orders after the army, helped by United States advisers, defeated the campaign; the Marxist MPLA backed in Angola in 1975 and Mengistu in Ethiopia in 1977.
7. ✅ Castro was seen as a hero by most Third World countries, but his popularity slumped when he supported the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan in 1979.
8. ✅ Diversification was disappointing, so the economy still depended unhealthily on the sugar harvest, the world sugar price and the willingness of the USSR and her satellites to buy.
9. ✅ 1980: sugar crop reduced by fungus, tobacco seriously affected by another; economic crisis; unemployment rose; thousands emigrated to the USA; food rationing introduced; the economy heavily subsidised by the USSR.
10. ✅ 1991: the USSR split up and Cuba lost its most powerful supporter, but the regime continued to survive.
11. ✅ Survival components: growth in tourism in the closing years of the century; Venezuelan oil at favourable prices agreed in October 2000, developed under Chavez into oil from state-owned refineries at very reasonable prices in exchange for thousands of Cuban doctors and teachers, with Cuban doctors also serving in Bolivia.
12. ✅ Continued isolation: most Latin American states still viewed Cuba as an outcast and she was the only country in the Americas not invited to the third Summit of the Americas at Quebec in 2001; a new crisis in 2002 from drought, a poor 2001 sugar harvest and the effect of the September 2001 attacks on tourism.
13. ✅ February 2008: ill health forced Castro, aged 80, to hand the presidency to Raul, aged 78; mobile phones legalised in March 2008; peasants allowed to cultivate unused land on collective farms with improved agricultural pricing bringing more food to market; hurricanes in autumn 2008 caused extensive damage.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: the revolution transformed distribution and did not transform production, so the structural vulnerability of the old order was inherited intact; survival after 1991 came from tourism, a new regional patron and the conversion of its own social achievements into an export.

### Concept check

**Question:** Assess the Cuban Revolution's economic record, and explain why its social achievements did not remove the vulnerability it inherited.

**Model answer:** The social record is substantial and dated: by the end of the 1970s all children were receiving some education instead of fewer than half before 1959, sanitation, hygiene and health care were much improved, unemployment and corruption were reduced, and there was equality for black people and more rights for women. The structural record is the opposite. The attempt to diversify industrial and agricultural output was disappointing, so the island's economy still depended unhealthily on the quality of the sugar harvest, the world price of sugar and the willingness of the USSR and her satellites to buy Cuba's exports - which is, term for term, the dependence described before 1959 with a different buyer substituted. The revolution therefore transformed distribution and did not transform production. The consequence appeared on schedule: in 1980 a fungus infection reduced the sugar crop and another damaged tobacco, plunging the island into economic crisis, raising unemployment, prompting emigration to the USA and requiring food rationing while the USSR heavily subsidised the economy. Two qualifications belong in a fair assessment: these outcomes were achieved against continual harassment, a trade embargo and bomb attacks on factories and refineries, which the record says meant that some of Castro's economic policies had little success; and the later diversification into tourism worked but substituted one exposure for another, since the September 2001 attacks in the United States helped produce a Cuban crisis in 2002.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the embargo as a complete explanation of the economic record. The account attributes only *some* of the policy failures to destabilisation and the embargo, and the diversification failure was substantially internal - a planned economy built on a single crop did not succeed in building the new industries it set out to build. Equally, an answer that ignores the embargo has failed to control for a sustained campaign of economic warfare and physical sabotage against industrial plant, and is not comparing Cuba with its neighbours on equal terms.

---

## Lesson 12 - Chile, 1970-1990: the constitutional road, its destruction, and the seventeen years that followed

Progress: 12/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Chile's democratic exception and the Frei reforms; Allende's election with 36 per cent and why it happened; the programme and its early success; the five recorded causes of the government's difficulties; the constitutional fear that precipitated the coup; the coup itself and external involvement; the Pinochet regime, its economic record and its human cost; and the transition after 1990

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's dedicated section on Chile under Salvador Allende, covering the democratic tradition, the Frei presidency, Allende's election, his problems and policies, the reasons for his overthrow and the Pinochet years, together with the later Chilean material in the Latin America chapter
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five pressures converging on one government

```text
                        ALLENDE, elected Sept 1970
                        36% against 35% for his nearest rival
                        "the world's first Marxist leader to be
                         voted into power through a democratic
                         election"
                                    |
    +---------------+---------------+---------------+---------------+
    |               |               |               |               |
    v               v               v               v               v
  AGRARIAN       INVESTMENT       COPPER          THE LEFT        THE USA
  Land           Private          Long strikes    Impatient       "did everything
  redistribution investors        for higher      communists      in their power
  caused a fall  frightened off;  wages;          wanting a       to undermine
  in production  government       production      Castro-style    Chile's economy"
  because        short of funds   down; world     approach        Other South
  farmers due    for housing,     price of        formed the      American
  to lose land   education and    copper fell     MIR, seizing    governments
  stopped sowing social services  suddenly by     farms and       "nervous in case
  and often                       about 30%;      evicting        the Chileans
  slaughtered                     revenue down    owners          tried to export
  their cattle                                                    their revolution"
    |               |               |               |               |
    +---------------+---------------+---------------+---------------+
                                    |
                                    v
               THE PRECIPITANT: what happens in September 1976?
               Allende cannot stand again under the constitution.
               "no Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out
                of power." The opposition feared he would change the
                constitution or use a referendum to postpone the
                election - "or so they afterwards claimed"
                                    |
                                    v
               A massive strike + the army's support
               -> military coup, September 1973
```

⚠️ The diagram shows why this case is so instructive: **five pressures, three of them consequences of the government's own policies and two of them external**, converging on a constitutional question. ⚠️ Any answer that reduces the coup to a single cause - American intervention, or economic mismanagement, or military ambition - has thrown away four-fifths of the evidence.

### 1. The exception: Chile's democratic tradition

✅ Chile, unlike most other South American states, had a tradition of democracy. ✅ There were three main parties or groups of parties: the Unidad Popular on the left, the Christian Democrats, also left-inclined, and the National Party, a liberal or conservative coalition. ✅ The army played little part in politics, and the democratic constitution - similar to that of the USA, except that the president could not stand for re-election immediately - was usually respected.

⚠️ Hold all four elements, because each will be destroyed in 1973. ⚠️ A genuine party system spanning left to right; a politically abstinent army; a respected constitution; and a term-limit rule that will turn out to be the precipitant of the whole crisis.

⚠️ And note the comparative significance. ⚠️ Chile is the control case for this entire session. ⚠️ If the region's instability were caused only by the absence of democratic tradition, Chile should have been immune. ⚠️ It was not. ⚠️ Which means the causes reach deeper than political culture, into the land and ownership structures that Lessons 1 and 2 identified.

### 2. Frei: reform from the centre, and why it failed to hold

✅ The election of 1964 was won by Eduardo Frei, leader of the Christian Democrats, who believed in social reform. ✅ Frei began vigorously: inflation was brought down from 38 per cent to 25 per cent, the rich were made to pay their taxes instead of evading them, 360,000 new houses were built, the number of schools was more than doubled, and some limited land reform was introduced - over 1,200 private holdings which were being run inefficiently were confiscated and given out to landless peasants. ✅ He also took over about half the holdings in the American-owned copper mines, with suitable compensation. ✅ The American government admired his reforms and poured in lavish economic aid.

⚠️ Stop there and notice something that complicates every simple account of United States policy. ⚠️ A government that taxed the rich, built houses, doubled schools, confiscated over 1,200 estates and took half of the American copper holdings received **lavish economic aid and American admiration**. ⚠️ This is the Alliance for Progress logic of Lesson 3 working exactly as intended: reform, provided it is moderate, compensated and anti-communist, was supported rather than opposed.

⚠️ The corollary matters for the whole topic. ⚠️ The line was not drawn at reform. ⚠️ It was drawn at reform that was uncompensated, that reached a whole sector rather than half of it, or that came attached to an alignment Washington read as hostile. ⚠️ Knowing where the line was drawn is far more useful than asserting that it existed.

✅ By 1967, however, the tide was beginning to turn against Frei: the left thought his land reforms too cautious and wanted full nationalization of the copper industry, Chile's most important export, whereas the right thought he had already gone too far. ✅ In 1969 there was a serious drought in which a third of the harvest was lost; large quantities of food had to be imported, causing inflation to soar again; ✅ there were strikes of copper miners demanding higher wages and several miners were killed by government troops.

⚠️ That is the classic squeeze on a reforming centre, and it is worth naming as a general pattern: a moderate reform programme satisfies neither the constituency that wants more nor the one that wants none, and a commodity shock removes its fiscal room to buy time. ⚠️ ✅ Allende made skilful use of this ammunition during the 1970 election campaign, pointing out that Frei's achievements fell far short of his promises.

### 3. The election: 36 per cent, and what it did and did not authorise

✅ In September 1970 Salvador Allende, a Marxist doctor of medicine from a middle-class background, won the presidential election as leader of a left-wing coalition of communists, socialists, radicals and social democrats which called itself Unidad Popular. ✅ It was a narrow victory, with Allende winning 36 per cent of the votes against the 35 per cent of his nearest rival. ✅ But it was enough to make him president - the world's first Marxist leader to be voted into power through a democratic election.

✅ Allende's coalition had a much better campaign organization than the other parties and could get thousands of supporters out on the streets. ✅ Allende himself inspired confidence: elegant and cultured, he appeared the very opposite of the violent revolutionary; ✅ appearances were not deceptive, since he believed that communism could succeed without a violent revolution. ✅ In the 1970 election 36 per cent of the voters were in favour of trying his policies.

⚠️ The 36 per cent is the pivot of the whole case and it must be handled with care, because it can be misused in either direction.

⚠️ **It was a legitimate victory.** ⚠️ Under the rules in force, a plurality was enough; he was president by the constitution's own terms; and the question of whether a plurality president may govern is answered by the constitution, not by his opponents' arithmetic.

⚠️ **It was also a structurally weak mandate.** ⚠️ Nearly two-thirds of voters chose somebody else, and the coalition did not control Congress. ⚠️ A transformative programme resting on a plurality must either persuade part of the opposition or govern around the legislature, and the second route is what makes the constitutional question of 1976 so explosive.

⚠️ ✅ And the account records that the government retained real support as it proceeded: Unidad Popular won **49.7 per cent** of the votes in the **municipal elections of April 1971** and slightly increased their seats in the 1973 elections for Congress. ⚠️ The core account places the local result in 1972; the contest was the municipal election of April 1971. ⚠️ That evidence is essential, because it disposes of the claim that the coup removed a collapsing government. ⚠️ The government's vote had risen.

### 4. The programme, and the four recorded problems it caused

✅ The problems facing the new government were enormous: inflation was running at over 30 per cent, unemployment at 20 per cent, industry was stagnating and 90 per cent of the population lived in such poverty that half the children under 15 suffered from malnutrition.

⚠️ Note the malnutrition figure and preserve it as given - half the children under fifteen, within the 90 per cent living in poverty. ⚠️ It establishes that this was a government facing an emergency rather than an ideological project in search of a grievance.

✅ Allende believed in a redistribution of income, which would enable the poor to buy more and thereby stimulate the economy. ✅ All-round wage increases of about 40 per cent were introduced and firms were not allowed to increase prices. ✅ The remainder of the copper industry, textiles and banks were nationalized - the copper nationalisation being enacted in **1971** - and Frei's land redistribution was speeded up. ✅ The army was awarded an even bigger pay rise than anybody else to make sure of keeping its support. ✅ In foreign affairs, Allende restored diplomatic relations with Castro's Cuba, China and East Germany.

⚠️ The economic logic is recognisably the demand-side argument of Lesson 7: raise the incomes of the poor, and the domestic market for domestic industry expands. ⚠️ It is the correct diagnosis of the region's structural problem. ⚠️ What followed is a lesson in the difference between a correct diagnosis and a workable transition.

⚠️ And note the army pay rise. ⚠️ Allende understood the veto described in Lesson 2 perfectly and tried to buy it off, exactly as Kubitschek had in Brazil in 1955. ⚠️ It did not work, and the reason is instructive: ⚠️ the army's institutional interest was not primarily its pay.

⚠️ Now the recorded consequences, which an answer must give in full.

✅ **Agriculture.** Land redistribution caused a fall in agricultural production, mainly because farmers whose land was due to be taken stopped sowing and often slaughtered their cattle; this caused food shortages and further inflation. ⚠️ That is the anticipatory-response problem and it is general: an announced expropriation destroys the incentive to invest in the asset before the expropriation occurs, so output falls in the interval between announcement and transfer.

✅ **Investment.** Private investors were frightened off and the government became short of funds to carry out social reforms in housing, education and social services as rapidly as it would have liked.

✅ **Copper.** Nationalisation was disappointing: there were long strikes for higher wages, production went down, and the world price of copper fell suddenly by about 30 per cent, causing a further decrease in government revenue.

⚠️ The copper strikes deserve a sentence because they are counter-intuitive and therefore memorable. ⚠️ Workers in a nationalised industry struck against a socialist government. ⚠️ The copper miners were the best-paid workers in the country; nationalisation transferred the employer, not the wage structure; and a government committed to redistribution had every reason to resist further increases for an already privileged group. ⚠️ **Nationalisation converted a dispute with a foreign company into a dispute with the government itself.**

✅ **The left.** Some communists who wanted a more drastic Castro-style approach grew impatient with Allende's caution; they refused to make allowances for the fact that he did not have a stable majority in parliament, and they formed the Movement of the Revolutionary Left, which embarrassed the non-violent Unidad Popular by seizing farms and evicting the owners.

⚠️ This is the pressure from the left, and it is routinely omitted. ⚠️ Illegal seizures by a group to the government's left made the government look unable to control its own supporters - which is precisely the charge that justifies military intervention, and it was supplied by people who considered the government too moderate.

### 5. The external pressure, stated exactly

✅ The USA disapproved strongly of Allende's policies and did everything in their power to undermine Chile's economy. ✅ Other South American governments were nervous in case the Chileans tried to export their "revolution".

✅ And, from the account of the coup itself: the Central Intelligence Agency, helped by the Brazilian government - a repressive military regime - played a vital role in the preparations for the coup, as part of its policy of preventing the spread of communism in Latin America. ✅ There is evidence that the agency had been considering a coup as soon as Allende won the election in 1970. ✅ There is no doubt that the Nixon administration had done its best to destabilize the Allende government over the next three years by undermining the economy. ✅ Nixon himself was reported as saying that they must "make the Chilean economy scream".

⚠️ Two points. ⚠️ **The Brazilian role should be named**, because it complicates the bilateral framing: this was not simply an American operation but a regional one, in which an existing military regime assisted in producing another. ⚠️ **The Nixon remark is reported**, and should be given as reported rather than as verified verbatim.

### 6. The precipitant: a constitutional question about 1976

✅ Looming above everything else was the question of what would happen in September 1976, when the next presidential election was due. ✅ Under the constitution, Allende would not be able to stand, but no Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out of power. ✅ The opposition feared, perhaps with justification, that Allende was planning to change the constitution. ✅ As things stood, any president finding his legislation blocked by Congress could appeal to the nation by means of a referendum, and with sufficient support Allende might be able to use the referendum device to postpone the election. ✅ It was this fear, or so they afterwards claimed, which caused the opposition groups to draw together and take action before Allende did.

⚠️ This passage repays close reading, and an answer that handles it well will stand out.

⚠️ **The fear was about an event three years in the future.** ⚠️ The coup was not a response to something Allende had done to the constitution; it was a response to what the opposition believed he would do.

⚠️ **The account's own qualifiers must be preserved.** ✅ "Perhaps with justification" and ✅ "or so they afterwards claimed" are both there. ⚠️ The first concedes the fear was not absurd; the second signals that the justification was offered retrospectively by the people who acted on it. ❌ An answer that drops either qualifier has taken a side the source does not take.

⚠️ **The precedent argument is real and is the hardest thing for a defender of Allende to answer.** ✅ No Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out of power. ⚠️ In 1973 that was a true generalisation, and a Chilean conservative could hold it in good faith. ⚠️ The counter-argument is equally real: Allende had not in fact suspended anything, his coalition's vote had risen in the 1972 and 1973 elections, and acting pre-emptively against an anticipated constitutional violation by committing an actual one is not a defence of constitutionalism.

⚠️ That is the genuine dilemma at the heart of the case, and the best answers present it as a dilemma.

### 7. The coup and the regime

✅ They organized a massive strike and, having won the support of the army, the right staged a military coup. ✅ It was organized by leading generals, who set up a military dictatorship in which General Pinochet came to the fore. ✅ Left-wing leaders were murdered or imprisoned and Allende himself was reported to have committed suicide; ✅ however, the cause of death has been controversial, many of his supporters claiming that he was gunned down in the presidential palace, and in 2011 Chilean television reported that a newly discovered document proved beyond doubt that he had been assassinated.

⚠️ Report the death exactly as the account does: a reported suicide, a contested cause, a supporters' claim and a 2011 television report of a document. ❌ Do not state a conclusion the source does not state.

✅ The new Chilean regime soon provoked criticism from the outside world for its brutal treatment of political prisoners and its violations of human rights. ✅ However, the American government, which had reduced its economic aid while Allende was in power, stepped up its assistance again.

⚠️ That single sentence is the aid instrument of Lesson 3 demonstrated in two clauses: reduced for the elected government, increased for the dictatorship that replaced it.

✅ The Pinochet regime had some economic success and by 1980 had brought the annual inflation rate down from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions. ✅ Pinochet was in no hurry to return the country to civilian rule. ✅ During his 17 years as president, around 3,000 people were killed or "disappeared", while tens of thousands were tortured, imprisoned or driven into exile.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** The figure is "around 3,000 people killed or disappeared" across seventeen years, with tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled. ❌ Do not separate killed from disappeared; ❌ do not supply a precise number; ❌ do not merge these with the Argentine figures in the next lesson.

⚠️ And keep both halves of the economic record. ✅ Inflation fell from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980, and ✅ the account elsewhere says he did much to improve Chile's economy while it was a period of brutal repression. ⚠️ The uncomfortable conjunction is the point of the case, and Lesson 16 returns to what it does and does not prove.

### 8. Transition, accountability and the sequel

✅ Pinochet eventually allowed presidential elections in 1989, when the civilian candidate he supported was heavily defeated, winning less than 30 per cent of the votes. ✅ He permitted the winner, Christian Democrat leader Patricio Aylwin, to become president in 1990, but the constitution **approved in 1980 and in force from 11 March 1981** allowed Pinochet himself to remain Commander-in-Chief of the Armed Forces for a further eight years.

⚠️ Name that arrangement, because it describes most Latin American transitions of the period: a **negotiated exit** in which the outgoing regime writes the constitutional terms of its own departure and retains institutional power after leaving office. ⚠️ Democracy returns; the military's autonomy is written into the settlement.

✅ Pinochet duly stepped down in 1998, but his retirement did not work out as he had planned: on a visit to London later that year he was arrested and held in Britain for 16 months after the Spanish government requested his extradition to face charges of torturing Spanish citizens in Chile. ✅ He was eventually allowed to return to Chile on medical grounds in March 2000. ✅ However, one of his most bitter opponents, Ricardo Lagos, had just been elected president in January 2000 - the first socialist president since Allende. ✅ Pinochet soon found himself facing over 250 charges of human rights abuses, but in July 2001 the Chilean Court of Appeal decided that the general, now aged 86, was too ill to stand trial; ✅ he died in 2006 at the age of 91.

⚠️ The London arrest is a genuinely important fact for this topic and Lesson 18 develops it. ⚠️ A former head of state was detained in a third country on a warrant from a fourth, for acts committed in his own. ⚠️ That is the principle of universal jurisdiction operating against exactly the kind of immunity that negotiated transitions were designed to preserve.

✅ The later sequence completes the case: following the return to democracy in 1990 Chile was ruled by presidents from the centre-left Coalition of Parties for Democracy - the Christian Democrats Patricio Aylwin, 1990-4, and Eduardo Frei, 1994-2000, son of the earlier president - then the socialists Ricardo Lagos, 2000-6, and Michelle Bachelet, 2006-10, Chile's first woman president, a former paediatrician whose father had been a victim of the Pinochet regime.

✅ Bachelet faced almost immediately a strike by thousands of students demanding educational reforms, calmed the situation and promised to put things right. ✅ At first she continued her predecessor's economic policies and increased social spending; ✅ it was during her presidency that Chile began to move out of the period of transition from military dictatorship to genuine democracy. ✅ Clearly the classic neo-liberal economic policies were not sufficient to bring full recovery, and so the government broke the neo-conservative rules with a dose of state intervention, deploying the revenues of the world's largest copper producer, Codelco, and introducing government control of capital so that the president could finance new social policies. ⚠️ One correction belongs here, because the core account's wording invites an error. ❌ Codelco was **not** taken into state hands at this point. ✅ Chilean copper was nationalised under Allende in **1971**, and Codelco has existed as the **state-owned copper corporation since 1976**, remaining in state ownership through the dictatorship and after it. ⚠️ What changed after 1990 was the use made of its revenues and the introduction of capital controls, not its ownership. ✅ As the December 2009 election approached, Bachelet's popularity, which had dropped to around 40 per cent during the world debt crisis in 2008, had risen to 84 per cent.

✅ The constitution does not allow presidents to serve two consecutive terms, so the former president Eduardo Frei was endorsed as the coalition's candidate; ✅ the main right-wing opponent, Sebastian Pinera, whom Bachelet had defeated in 2006, won the presidency in the second round in January 2010; ✅ the explanation for the coalition's defeat was probably that Frei failed to generate any enthusiasm and was seen as representing old-style politics, while Pinera concentrated his campaign on the need for greater government efficiency. ✅ Shortly after the January run-off, Chile was hit by a devastating earthquake that killed 500 people, left around a million homeless and caused damage estimated at between $15 and 30 billion.

⚠️ Two closing observations. ⚠️ The daughter of a victim of the regime became president of the country in 2006 - which is as compact a statement of what a successful transition means as this session contains. ⚠️ And the alternation of January 2010 returned the right to power by election, seventeen years after the left had been removed by force: ⚠️ the test of a democracy is not that one's own side wins but that the other side can, and Chile passed it in both directions.

### Criticism and reply: could the Allende government have survived?

⚠️ **The objection to a purely external explanation.** The government faced falling agricultural output caused by its own announced expropriations, an investment strike, a 30 per cent copper price fall, strikes in its own nationalised industry and illegal seizures by its own left flank. ⚠️ A government with 36 per cent and no congressional majority attempting simultaneous nationalisation, land reform and a 40 per cent wage rise under price controls was attempting more than its political base could carry.

⚠️ **The objection to a purely internal explanation.** ✅ The United States did everything in its power to undermine the economy; ✅ the Central Intelligence Agency, helped by Brazil's military regime, played a vital role in preparing the coup; ✅ it had been considering a coup as soon as Allende won in 1970; ✅ aid was reduced under Allende and stepped up after the coup. ⚠️ A government confronting the economic problems listed above while a superpower worked to worsen them is not being tested on the same terms as one that is not.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ The economic difficulties were substantially self-inflicted and would have produced a serious crisis in any case; the transformation of that crisis into the destruction of Chilean democracy required a domestic opposition willing to solicit the army, an army willing to act, and an external power working actively to bring both about. ⚠️ Removing any one of the three and the coup becomes improbable; that is why single-cause answers fail here.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ Chile is the topic's test case for whether socialism can be achieved through constitutional means, and it is the region's clearest single instance of Cold War intervention in a functioning democracy.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect a comparison with Cuba - the revolutionary and the constitutional roads - or a question on the Cold War's effects on democracies in the developing world.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying Allende won a majority. ✅ It was 36 per cent against 35 per cent for his nearest rival.
2. ⚠️ Explaining the coup solely by American intervention. ✅ Four domestic economic and political problems are recorded alongside it.
3. ⚠️ Explaining it solely by economic failure. ✅ The coalition's vote rose in 1972 and 1973, and the agency's role in preparing the coup is recorded as vital.
4. ⚠️ Stating that Allende was assassinated, or that he certainly killed himself. ✅ The record is a reported suicide, a controversial cause, a supporters' claim and a 2011 television report of a document.
5. ⚠️ Omitting the Brazilian role. ✅ The Brazilian military government helped, which makes the coup regional as well as bilateral.
6. ⚠️ Forgetting the provision of the 1980 constitution, in force from 11 March 1981, or dating that constitution to 1981. ✅ Pinochet remained Commander-in-Chief for eight years after leaving the presidency.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Chile, unlike most other South American states, had a tradition of democracy: three party groups - Unidad Popular, the Christian Democrats and the National Party; the army played little part in politics; the constitution, similar to that of the USA except that the president could not stand for immediate re-election, was usually respected.
2. ✅ Frei, elected 1964: inflation down from 38 to 25 per cent; the rich made to pay taxes; 360,000 houses built; schools more than doubled; over 1,200 inefficiently run private holdings confiscated and given to landless peasants; about half the holdings in the American-owned copper mines taken over with suitable compensation; American admiration and lavish aid.
3. ✅ By 1967 the left thought his land reform too cautious and wanted full copper nationalisation; the right thought he had gone too far; the 1969 drought cost a third of the harvest; copper strikes and several miners killed by government troops; Allende used this in 1970.
4. ✅ September 1970: Allende, a Marxist doctor of medicine from a middle-class background, won with 36 per cent against 35 per cent, leading a coalition of communists, socialists, radicals and social democrats - the world's first Marxist leader voted into power democratically; he believed communism could succeed without violent revolution.
5. ✅ Inherited conditions: inflation over 30 per cent, unemployment 20 per cent, stagnating industry, 90 per cent of the population in such poverty that half the children under 15 suffered from malnutrition.
6. ✅ Policies: wage increases of about 40 per cent with firms forbidden to raise prices; the remainder of copper, plus textiles and banks, nationalised, the copper nationalisation enacted in 1971; Frei's land redistribution speeded up; the army given the biggest pay rise of all; diplomatic relations restored with Cuba, China and East Germany.
7. ✅ Support held: 49.7 per cent in the municipal elections of April 1971 and a slight increase in seats in the 1973 congressional elections.
8. ✅ The recorded problems: farmers due to lose land stopped sowing and slaughtered cattle, cutting production and raising prices; private investors were frightened off; copper nationalisation brought long strikes, falling production and a sudden fall of about 30 per cent in the world price; impatient communists formed the Movement of the Revolutionary Left and seized farms, evicting owners; the USA did everything in its power to undermine the economy, and other South American governments feared export of the revolution.
9. ✅ The precipitant: under the constitution Allende could not stand in September 1976, but no Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out of power; the opposition feared, perhaps with justification, that he would change the constitution or use a referendum to postpone the election - or so they afterwards claimed.
10. ✅ The coup: a massive strike, the army's support, a coup organised by leading generals with Pinochet coming to the fore; left-wing leaders murdered or imprisoned; Allende reported to have committed suicide, with the cause controversial and a 2011 Chilean television report of a document said to prove assassination.
11. ✅ External involvement: the Central Intelligence Agency, helped by Brazil's repressive military regime, played a vital role in preparing the coup; there is evidence it had been considering one since Allende's 1970 victory; Nixon was reported as saying they must make the Chilean economy scream; American aid, reduced under Allende, was stepped up after the coup.
12. ✅ The regime: criticism for brutal treatment of political prisoners and human rights violations; inflation brought down from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980; around 3,000 people killed or disappeared in 17 years, with tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or driven into exile.
13. ✅ Transition: elections allowed in 1989, Pinochet's candidate heavily defeated with under 30 per cent; Aylwin president in 1990; the 1980 constitution, in force from 11 March 1981, let Pinochet remain Commander-in-Chief for eight more years; he stepped down in 1998, was arrested in London and held for 16 months on a Spanish extradition request over torture of Spanish citizens, returned on medical grounds in March 2000, faced over 250 charges, was ruled too ill to stand trial in July 2001 and died in 2006 aged 91.
14. ✅ After 1990: Aylwin 1990-4 and Frei 1994-2000 for the Christian Democrats, then the socialists Lagos 2000-6 and Bachelet 2006-10, Chile's first woman president, a former paediatrician whose father was a victim of the regime; state copper revenues deployed and capital controls introduced when neo-liberal policies proved insufficient - Codelco was not taken into state hands then, having been the state-owned copper corporation since 1976 after the nationalisation of 1971; her popularity rose from around 40 per cent in 2008 to 84 per cent; Pinera won the run-off in January 2010; an earthquake shortly afterwards killed 500, left around a million homeless and caused damage estimated at between $15 and 30 billion.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: the destruction of Chilean democracy required three simultaneous conditions - a genuine economic crisis partly of the government's own making, a domestic opposition willing to solicit the army, and an external power working to produce both. No one of them suffices.

### Concept check

**Question:** Chile is often used to prove that the United States destroyed Latin American democracy, and equally often to prove that socialism cannot be achieved constitutionally. Explain why both uses are incomplete, and state what the case does establish.

**Model answer:** The first use is incomplete because four of the five recorded pressures on the government were domestic and three flowed from its own policies. Land redistribution cut agricultural production because farmers due to lose land stopped sowing and slaughtered cattle; private investors were frightened off, leaving the government short of funds for housing, education and social services; copper nationalisation produced long strikes, falling production and, with a sudden fall of about 30 per cent in the world price, reduced revenue; and impatient communists formed the Movement of the Revolutionary Left and seized farms, embarrassing a government that had won 36 per cent and lacked a stable parliamentary majority. The second use is incomplete because the government was not collapsing electorally: Unidad Popular took 49.7 per cent in the municipal elections of April 1971 and slightly increased its congressional seats in 1973, and the removal came by force rather than by vote, with a massive strike, the army's support, and the Central Intelligence Agency - helped by Brazil's military regime - playing a vital role in preparing the coup after considering one since 1970. What the case establishes is the conjunction: a transformative programme resting on a plurality generated a genuine economic crisis; that crisis was deliberately worsened from outside, as the Nixon administration undermined the economy; and the crisis was converted into the destruction of a democratic system by a domestic opposition that solicited an army previously abstinent from politics, acting on a fear about a presidential election three years away.

**Misconception to avoid:** Resolving the constitutional question in either direction more confidently than the evidence allows. The opposition's stated justification was that Allende might change the constitution or use a referendum to postpone the 1976 election, and the account both concedes that this fear was perhaps justified and notes that the justification was offered afterwards by those who acted on it. The counterweight is that Allende had suspended nothing, his coalition's vote had risen, and pre-empting an anticipated constitutional violation by committing an actual one is not a defence of constitutional government. An answer that presents either the fear or its refutation as settled has taken a position the source declines to take.

---

## Lesson 13 - Military regimes and the national security state: Brazil, Argentina, Bolivia and the coordinated repression of the Southern Cone

Progress: 13/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: how Goulart's programme produced the Brazilian coup of April 1964; twenty years of military rule, repression and the growth record; the controlled return to civilians in 1985; the Argentine junta of 1976-83 and the recorded charges against it; the Falklands miscalculation and its economic consequence; Bolivia under American economic direction and military rule; and the cross-border coordination of repression

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's Brazil narrative covering Goulart, the 1964 coup, the twenty years of military government, the economic record and the handover of 1985, together with its Argentina section on the junta, the dirty war and the Falklands, and its Bolivia section on American economic control and military rule
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the common architecture of the national security state

```text
   TRIGGER                          INSTALLATION                  DOCTRINE
   =======                          ============                  ========
   a reforming or populist          the army acts, with           the internal enemy:
   government touches:              civilian and often            subversion is a
   - foreign profit remittance      external encouragement        domestic war, so
   - land                                                         normal law does
   - external alignment                                           not apply
        |                                    |                        |
        +------------------+-----------------+------------------------+
                           |
                           v
   THE STATE THAT RESULTS - four consistent features
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   1. PARTIES BANNED / CONGRESS SUSPENDED
      Brazil: "the old political parties were banned"
      Guatemala 1954: "parliament was disbanded"
   2. PRESS CENSORED
      Brazil: "a strict press censorship"
      Venezuela 1948-58: "a strict press censorship was imposed"
   3. TARGETED SOCIAL GROUPS, NOT ARMED REBELS ALONE
      Brazil: "trade unionists and left-wing students were a
      favourite target and there were reports of widespread
      torture and violent treatment of prisoners"
   4. ECONOMIC POLICY DELEGATED TO TECHNICIANS + FOREIGN CREDIT
      Brazil: borrowed extensively abroad; lenders who refused
      Goulart "were quite happy to do business with a strong
      right-wing regime which had eliminated communist influence"
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
                           |
                           v
   THE EXIT - negotiated, not defeated
   Brazil 1985: "the military decided this was an appropriate
     time to hand power over to civilians"
   Argentina 1983: return to democracy after the Falklands defeat
   Chile 1990: Pinochet remains Commander-in-Chief for 8 more years
```

⚠️ The architecture is the lesson. ⚠️ These were not simply strongman dictatorships in the caudillo mould of Lesson 1. ⚠️ They were **institutional** regimes: the armed forces governed as an organisation rather than a general governing as a person, they had a doctrine about internal war, they delegated the economy to technicians, and they left on terms they largely set themselves. ⚠️ Naming that difference is the single most efficient way to show that you understand twentieth-century Latin American militarism rather than merely disapproving of it.

### 1. Brazil: what Goulart did, and why it was intolerable

✅ Julio Goulart, who took over when Quadros resigned after seven months in 1961, wanted to move Brazil gradually towards democracy and proposed to give more people the right to vote. ✅ He also planned to limit the amount of profit that large multinational companies could take out of the country, so that the government could use the extra revenue to help improve social conditions for the masses. ✅ Worse still, as far as the USA was concerned, he opened diplomatic relations with the USSR, promised to nationalize Brazil's oil refineries, and opposed economic sanctions against Cuba.

⚠️ Take that list apart, because each item targets a different interest and together they explain why the opposition was so broad.

⚠️ **Extending the franchise** threatens the landed and urban elites directly, since the excluded are the poor. ⚠️ **Limiting profit remittance** is the exact remedy to the fourth structural problem of Lesson 2 and strikes at foreign investors. ⚠️ **Nationalising oil refineries** repeats the Mexican move of 1938 - and by 1963 the international frame had changed. ⚠️ **Opening relations with the USSR and opposing sanctions on Cuba** places Brazil, in Washington's reading, on the wrong side of the hemispheric division created four years earlier.

⚠️ Note that none of this is socialism. ⚠️ ✅ Goulart was a millionaire landowner and a devout Roman Catholic, and the account says that by no stretch of the imagination could the charge of communism be taken seriously. ⚠️ That is the clearest illustration in this session of the point from Lesson 3: the test was what Washington deemed unacceptable, not what was in fact communist.

✅ All this was much too radical for the army and for the right, and tension between them and Goulart's supporters looked like developing into civil war. ✅ United States President Lyndon Johnson told the American ambassador in Brazil that the USA must do everything possible to help overthrow "this left-wing government". ✅ In April 1964 Goulart was removed in a military coup. ✅ Fortunately there was no civil war, but it emerged later that President Johnson had ordered United States naval vessels, including an aircraft carrier and two destroyers, together with ammunition and fuel, to be made ready in case the Brazilian military needed assistance.

⚠️ That contingency detail is exactly the right level of evidence for an answer. ⚠️ **The force was readied and not used.** ⚠️ That is the difference between Brazil in 1964 and Guatemala in 1954: here the domestic instrument was sufficient, so the external one stayed in reserve. ⚠️ It also shows the ladder of Lesson 3 working as designed - the higher rung is prepared, not climbed.

✅ And the account adds a judgement about the policies themselves that a balanced answer will include: although well-intentioned, Goulart's policies left the country in economic difficulties; he had failed to attract sufficient foreign investment, which had been discouraged by the USA, and inflation increased rapidly while economic growth was minimal.

⚠️ Note the causal loop inside that sentence. ⚠️ Investment was discouraged **by the USA**, and the resulting economic difficulty then became part of the case against the government. ⚠️ Destabilisation produces the failure that justifies the removal - the same loop that appears in Chile nine years later.

### 2. Twenty years: repression, growth, and the relationship between them

✅ For the next 20 years Brazil was ruled by the military. ✅ For the first few years their policy was one of harsh repression: the old political parties were banned, there was a strict press censorship, opponents were arrested and the jails soon filled with political prisoners; ✅ trade unionists and left-wing students were a favourite target and there were reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners. ✅ After 1974, when General Ernesto Geisel became president, repression was gradually relaxed and it was announced that the army would return the country to full democracy, albeit slowly.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ✅ "Reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners" is what the account states, and no figure is given for Brazil. ❌ Do not supply one; ❌ do not import the Argentine or Chilean numbers.

⚠️ Note who the favourite targets were: trade unionists and left-wing students. ⚠️ Not armed guerrillas. ⚠️ That is the national security doctrine in practice - the enemy is defined as a social category rather than as a combatant, which is precisely what removes the limits that normally govern the use of force.

✅ During the years of military dictatorship the government had great success with its economic policies, achieving what many described as an "economic miracle". ✅ Faced with massive inflation and a stagnating economy, they tackled the problems by borrowing extensively from abroad. ✅ Countries that had been unwilling to lend to the Goulart government were quite happy to do business with a strong right-wing regime which had eliminated communist influence. ✅ This stimulated economic growth so that the years 1968-74 were a boom period; the annual growth rate was 10 per cent and exports quadrupled. ✅ After 1974 the growth rate fell to around 5 per cent, mainly because Brazil was having to import more of its oil supplies, much of it from Iraq.

⚠️ Now the analytical move that separates a good answer from a moralising one. ⚠️ **The miracle was credit-financed, and the credit was politically conditioned.** ⚠️ The same lenders refused the civilian reformer and financed the military regime. ⚠️ The "economic miracle" therefore cannot be read as evidence that authoritarian government produces growth; it is evidence that this particular authoritarian government could borrow on terms the civilian one was denied.

⚠️ And the growth carried its own bill, which arrived on schedule. ✅ By 1980 it seemed that the good times were over: Brazil had incurred huge foreign debts, there was a slump in export markets, there was a yawning gap between rich and poor and there was widespread unrest among the rural poor in the north east of the country. ✅ In an attempt to find substitute fuels the government, which had its own supplies of uranium, turned to nuclear power and bought reactors from West Germany. ✅ There was no immediate improvement, and in provincial elections in 1982 the government suffered significant defeats.

⚠️ "A yawning gap between rich and poor" after fifteen years of rapid growth is the finding that matters most, and it is the region's central lesson stated in one country. ⚠️ **Development and social justice are separable.** ⚠️ Brazil grew at 10 per cent a year and quadrupled exports while its poor stayed poor, because the growth was designed to raise output rather than to redistribute it.

### 3. The negotiated exit, and the civilians who inherited the bill

✅ Faced with escalating economic and social problems, the military decided this was an appropriate time to hand power over to civilians.

⚠️ The verb is "decided". ⚠️ The armed forces were not defeated, not overthrown and not compelled; they judged that the costs of governing had come to exceed the benefits and left at a moment of their choosing. ⚠️ That is what a negotiated transition means, and it explains why accountability for the repression proved so difficult afterwards across the region.

✅ In 1985 the presidential electoral college chose the 75-year-old Tancredo Neves as the first civilian president for over 20 years; ✅ he was taken ill almost immediately and died before he could be sworn in. ✅ His deputy, Jose Sarney, took over and for the next four years struggled to stabilize the economy. ✅ In February 1987 the government announced that it was suspending interest payments, but the IMF came to the rescue with credits; ✅ Brazil was able to pay the interest on time, but Sarney's emergency policies caused hyper-inflation, and in the 1989 election he was defeated by Ferdinando Collor.

✅ In an attempt to stem the rocketing inflation Collor introduced even more stringent policies: the currency was devalued, government expenditure was reduced, and bank accounts of over 50,000 cruzeiros - about £1,300 United States at that time - were frozen for 18 months. ✅ This was a disastrous move, since it meant that the economy was deprived of some $80 billion at a time when it was most needed; ✅ the result was a wave of business failures and massive unemployment. ✅ In the midst of the chaos Collor was accused of corruption, impeached by the Senate and forced to resign at the end of 1992.

⚠️ The account of the account-freezing deserves a moment, because it is a vivid instance of a general problem. ⚠️ A government fighting inflation immobilised the savings of the middle class and thereby removed working capital from the economy it was trying to stabilise. ⚠️ **Stabilisation measures that attack the symptom can deepen the disease**, and the political cost fell on the new democracy rather than on the regime that had contracted the debt.

⚠️ That is the structural injustice of these transitions and it is worth stating plainly: the military governments borrowed, and the civilian governments repaid. ⚠️ Austerity was administered by the democracies, which meant that democracy itself became associated with hardship - a point Lesson 16 develops.

### 4. Argentina: the junta, the charges, and the war that ended it

✅ Argentina was ruled by a military junta from 1976 until 1983, when the country returned to democracy. ✅ The junta was responsible for thousands of deaths in what became known as "the dirty war" to restore order and eliminate opponents. ✅ A human rights commission charged the junta with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people.

⚠️ **Attribution control, and this is the most important instance in the session.** ⚠️ Three distinct figures, all from **a human rights commission**, each counting a different thing: murders, arrests, disappearances. ❌ Do not add them together; ❌ do not convert disappearances into deaths; ❌ do not round the 30,000, which is given as an upper bound with "up to"; ❌ do not merge these with the Chilean figure of around 3,000 killed or disappeared over seventeen years.

⚠️ The body has a standard name, which an answer on transitional justice needs: ✅ the commission is the **National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons**, established after the return to democracy, whose report was published in 1984 under the title *Nunca Mas* - "Never Again". ⚠️ Lesson 18 returns to what that report did.

⚠️ Note the word "disappearance" and treat it as a technical term rather than a euphemism, because the distinction is examinable. ⚠️ A disappearance is the detention of a person whose detention the state then denies. ⚠️ It is chosen precisely because it leaves no body, no record and no jurisdiction: there is nothing to prosecute, nothing to bury and nobody formally responsible. ⚠️ It is a method designed to defeat accountability in advance, which is why the transitional-justice struggle of the following decades was so difficult and so prolonged.

✅ In an attempt to win some popularity the junta made the mistake of invading the Malvinas Islands, held by the UK as the Falkland Islands, in April 1982. ✅ Britain won an unexpected victory, recapturing the islands, and leaving Argentina with an unprecedentedly high foreign debt and inflation of around 900 per cent. ✅ There was a return to democracy for the presidential election of 1983, but the economy continued in crisis.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ✅ Inflation of around 900 per cent is stated for Argentina and only for Argentina. ❌ Do not generalise it to the region.

⚠️ Two analytical points. ⚠️ **The motive is recorded as domestic**: an attempt to win popularity. ⚠️ A regime that has lost legitimacy at home reaches for an external cause that unites the nation - a classic and dangerous manoeuvre, and in this case it destroyed the regime that attempted it. ⚠️ **The economic legacy is the link to Lesson 15**: the junta left behind an unprecedentedly high foreign debt and near-hyperinflation, and the democratic government elected in 1983 inherited both.

⚠️ The Argentine sequel belongs with this: ✅ by 1991 there were riots in protest at high food prices and unemployment; ✅ President Carlos Menem, who took office in 1991, resorted to classic neo-liberal policies - protectionist trade and business regulations removed, strict austerity measures introduced and a wave of privatisations of state-owned industries; ✅ it was all to no avail, and in September 1998 Argentina moved into the worst recession for years; ✅ de la Rua, elected in 1999, introduced more austerity measures, the recession continued, the IMF came to the rescue twice in 2001, and in November the economy seemed on the verge of total collapse; ✅ there was a financial panic, and serious rioting in Buenos Aires in December forced the president to resign.

⚠️ That sequence is the fullest single demonstration in this session that austerity is not self-validating: ten years of orthodox policy ended in a recession, two rescues, a panic, riots and the fall of a president.

### 5. Bolivia: the case that shows economic direction without a coup

✅ One of the poorest states in Latin America, Bolivia gained independence from Spain in 1825 and had a long history of instability and military dictatorships. ✅ Since the Second World War the Bolivian economy was controlled by the USA, which, among other things, processed all Bolivia's tin exports. ✅ In the 1950s, when the country was trying to become more self-sufficient in spite of its limited available capital, the USA insisted that this capital should be used to pay the country's foreign debts rather than to finance its own developments designed to increase revenue.

⚠️ That is the purest example in this session of the constraint operating without any intervention at all. ⚠️ No coup, no invasion, no covert operation. ⚠️ A creditor simply directed a sovereign state's scarce capital to debt service rather than to investment. ⚠️ And the consequence is a development trap stated in one sentence: **a country cannot grow out of its debt if it is not permitted to invest, and it cannot escape its debt without growing.**

⚠️ Note also the connection to Lesson 1: ✅ 80 per cent of all Bolivia's revenue came from tin exports in the 1950s, ✅ and the USA processed all of them. ⚠️ Commodity concentration plus processing control equals total leverage.

✅ In 1964 the military seized power, and in 1967 the army, with the help of United States advisers, easily defeated a guerrilla campaign led by Che Guevara, who was captured and murdered on Central Intelligence Agency orders. ✅ The army stayed in control until 1982, when Bolivia returned to democracy with a string of presidents who took care not to antagonize the USA.
