⚠️ "Took care not to antagonize" is the same phenomenon as the Guatemalan president's fear of removal in the 1980s: **anticipated reaction shaping policy in the absence of any action**. ⚠️ That is the most economical form of hegemony and the hardest to document, which is why these two recorded instances are valuable.

### 6. Coordinated repression across borders

⚠️ One feature of the Southern Cone regimes is not described in the core account and has to be stated separately, because a question on this period that omits it would be incomplete.

✅ From 1975 the military governments of the Southern Cone - Chile, Argentina, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia and Brazil - operated a coordinated campaign, known as **Operation Condor**, to track, detain, transfer and kill political opponents across each other's borders, with intelligence sharing and cross-border operations.

⚠️ Two inferences, and the first is already visible in the bounded evidence. ✅ The Central Intelligence Agency was **helped by the Brazilian government, a repressive military regime**, in preparing the Chilean coup. ⚠️ That is cross-border cooperation between military regimes recorded in the core base, and Condor is the institutional form it took.

⚠️ The analytical significance is that it changes the unit of analysis. ⚠️ If each dictatorship is examined in isolation, the repression looks like a series of national tragedies. ⚠️ Seen together, the regimes formed a **system** with a shared doctrine of internal war, shared intelligence and shared personnel, and an exile fleeing one country could be seized in another. ⚠️ That is also why accountability later required cross-border legal instruments - which is exactly what the London arrest of 1998 provided.

### Criticism and reply: did the military regimes deliver anything?

⚠️ **The objection to a purely condemnatory account.** ✅ Brazil grew at 10 per cent a year from 1968 to 1974 and quadrupled exports; ✅ Pinochet did much to improve Chile's economy and brought inflation down from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980; ✅ several regimes did stabilise economies that civilian governments had not.

⚠️ **The reply, in three moves.** ⚠️ First, the mechanism was often external credit rather than superior management: ✅ lenders unwilling to finance Goulart were happy to finance a right-wing regime that had eliminated communist influence. ⚠️ Second, the growth left the social structure untouched: ✅ a yawning gap between rich and poor and widespread unrest among the rural poor in the north east by 1980. ⚠️ Third, the debts contracted during the boom were repaid under austerity by the democracies that followed, so the accounting that credits the regimes with the growth must also debit them with the crisis.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ The national security state achieved measurable macroeconomic results, financed by politically favourable credit, at the cost of parties, press, unions, universities and, on the recorded charges, thousands of lives - and it exported the bill to its democratic successors.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson supplies the authoritarianism and human-rights strand, and it is the region's principal evidence on the relationship between development and political freedom.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect a question on military rule and development, on the Cold War's effects on political systems in the developing world, or a comparison between authoritarian and democratic development paths.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating these as personalist caudillo dictatorships. ⚠️ They were institutional regimes of the armed forces with a doctrine of internal war.
2. ⚠️ Crediting the Brazilian miracle to authoritarian efficiency. ✅ It was financed by lenders who had refused the civilian reformer.
3. ⚠️ Adding or merging the Argentine figures. ✅ 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people are three separate charges by a human rights commission.
4. ⚠️ Supplying a Brazilian torture figure. ✅ The record states reports of widespread torture without a number.
5. ⚠️ Presenting the transitions as victories over the military. ✅ In Brazil the military decided that it was an appropriate time to hand power to civilians.
6. ⚠️ Ignoring Bolivia. ⚠️ It is the case where economic direction operated without any coup at all.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Goulart's programme: gradual movement towards democracy with a wider franchise; limits on multinational profit remittance to fund social conditions; diplomatic relations with the USSR; promised nationalisation of oil refineries; opposition to sanctions on Cuba.
2. ✅ He was a millionaire landowner and a devout Roman Catholic, and the communist charge could not by any stretch be taken seriously.
3. ✅ Johnson told the American ambassador that the USA must do everything possible to help overthrow this left-wing government; Goulart was removed in April 1964; naval vessels including an aircraft carrier and two destroyers, with ammunition and fuel, were readied in case the Brazilian military needed assistance; no civil war occurred.
4. ✅ Goulart's policies, though well-intentioned, left economic difficulties: insufficient foreign investment, discouraged by the USA; rapidly rising inflation; minimal growth.
5. ✅ Twenty years of military rule: old political parties banned; strict press censorship; opponents arrested; jails filled with political prisoners; trade unionists and left-wing students a favourite target; reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners; repression gradually relaxed after General Geisel became president in 1974.
6. ✅ The economic miracle: extensive foreign borrowing; lenders unwilling to fund Goulart happy to fund a strong right-wing regime that had eliminated communist influence; 1968-74 growth of 10 per cent a year with exports quadrupling; growth falling to around 5 per cent after 1974 because more oil had to be imported, much of it from Iraq.
7. ✅ By 1980: huge foreign debts, a slump in export markets, a yawning gap between rich and poor, widespread unrest among the rural poor in the north east; nuclear reactors bought from West Germany; significant government defeats in the 1982 provincial elections.
8. ✅ 1985: the military decided it was an appropriate time to hand power to civilians; the electoral college chose the 75-year-old Tancredo Neves, who died before being sworn in; Sarney took over; February 1987 interest payments suspended and IMF credits followed, their magnitude disputed in the source and not asserted; hyper-inflation; Collor won in 1989.
9. ✅ Collor devalued, cut expenditure and froze accounts over 50,000 cruzeiros - about £1,300 United States - for 18 months, depriving the economy of some $80 billion and producing business failures and massive unemployment; he was accused of corruption, impeached and forced to resign at the end of 1992.
10. ✅ Argentina: military junta 1976-83; the dirty war to restore order and eliminate opponents; a human rights commission charged the junta with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people.
11. ✅ The body is the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons, whose report was published in 1984 as *Nunca Mas*.
12. ✅ April 1982: the junta invaded the Malvinas, held by the UK as the Falkland Islands, to win popularity; Britain won an unexpected victory, leaving Argentina with an unprecedentedly high foreign debt and inflation of around 900 per cent; democracy returned for the 1983 election but the economy remained in crisis.
13. ✅ 1991 riots over food prices and unemployment; Menem's classic neo-liberal programme of deregulation, austerity and privatisation; the worst recession for years from September 1998; de la Rua's further austerity; two IMF rescues in 2001; near-total collapse and financial panic in November; December rioting in Buenos Aires forcing the president's resignation.
14. ✅ Bolivia: independent 1825; long instability and military dictatorship; after the Second World War the economy was controlled by the USA, which processed all Bolivia's tin exports; in the 1950s the USA insisted that scarce capital be used to pay foreign debts rather than finance development.
15. ✅ 1964 military seizure of power; 1967 defeat of Che Guevara's guerrilla campaign by the army with United States advisers, his capture and murder on Central Intelligence Agency orders; army control until 1982, then democracy with presidents who took care not to antagonise the USA.
16. ✅ Also on the record: from 1975 the Southern Cone military governments - Chile, Argentina, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia and Brazil - coordinated the pursuit, detention, transfer and killing of opponents across borders under the name Operation Condor; the core record independently shows Brazil's military regime helping to prepare the Chilean coup.
17. ⚠️ Master formulation: the national security state was an institutional regime of the armed forces with a doctrine of internal war, financed by politically favourable credit, which delivered macroeconomic results without social change and left both the debt and the accountability problem to its democratic successors.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the Brazilian "economic miracle" of 1968-74 is weak evidence for the proposition that authoritarian government promotes development.

**Model answer:** Because the mechanism was external credit rather than superior administration, and because the credit was allocated politically. Faced with massive inflation and a stagnating economy, the military government tackled the problem by borrowing extensively from abroad, and the record states explicitly that countries unwilling to lend to the Goulart government were quite happy to do business with a strong right-wing regime that had eliminated communist influence. The same economy, under a civilian president who had failed to attract sufficient foreign investment precisely because it was discouraged by the United States, was judged uncreditworthy; under generals it was not. Growth of 10 per cent a year with exports quadrupling therefore measures the political preferences of lenders at least as much as the competence of the borrower. Two further considerations complete the case. The growth did not alter the social structure: by 1980 there were huge foreign debts, a slump in export markets, a yawning gap between rich and poor and widespread unrest among the rural poor in the north east. And the bill fell on the successors: the military decided in 1985 that it was an appropriate time to hand power to civilians, after which Sarney suspended interest payments in February 1987 and Collor's stabilisation froze accounts over 50,000 cruzeiros, depriving the economy of some $80 billion and producing business failures and massive unemployment.

**Misconception to avoid:** Overcorrecting into the claim that the regimes achieved nothing. Growth of 10 per cent a year and a quadrupling of exports were real, and in Chile inflation was brought down from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980 while the regime did much to improve the economy. The accurate verdict is that these governments produced measurable macroeconomic results, purchased with politically favourable credit and at the cost of parties, press, unions and - on the recorded charges against the Argentine junta by a human rights commission - thousands of murders, arrests and disappearances, while transferring the resulting debt to the democracies that followed.

## Lesson 14 - Nicaragua, 1912-1990: revolution meeting sustained external hostility

Progress: 14/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the marines and the origins of the Somoza dynasty; forty-two years of family rule and its uses for United States policy; the Sandinista victory of 1979 and its recorded reform programme; the Reagan administration's response and the Contra war; the International Court of Justice ruling and the congressional revolt; the Arias peace plan; the election of 1990 and the transfer of power; and the condition of the country afterwards

Book context: queried - the core account's Nicaragua section from the nineteenth-century party struggle through the marines, the Somoza dynasty, the Sandinista victory and reforms, the Contra war, the peace plan and the 1990 election, together with its later section on Nicaragua after 2006
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there

### Visual: eight decades, and the question each one answers

```text

    1912  US marines sent in to prop up the conservative government
          and restore order; they stay until 1925

          US forces return

          the USA; the situation gradually stabilises, partly
          because American troops stay until 1933
                    |
  1936-1979  WHO OWNS THE STATE?   -> one family
    "the Somoza family ruled Nicaragua with an iron fist until
     1979, supported by the USA. Political opponents were exiled
     and each of the Somozas amassed a large fortune."
    USED THREE TIMES as a base against neighbours the USA
    disapproved of: Guatemala 1954, Cuba 1961, Dominican
    Republic 1965
    The last Somoza, Anastasio, "was so blatantly corrupt that
    he even became an embarrassment to the USA"; Carter urged
    reform and attention to human rights, with little effect
                    |
  1979       CAN A REVOLUTION GOVERN?   -> briefly, and well
    Driven out by the Sandinista National Liberation Front,
    named after Augusto Sandino, who led an unsuccessful
    revolution in 1933 and was later murdered on Somoza's orders
    REFORMS: 5 million acres redistributed, including confiscated
    Somoza property, to about 100,000 families; a literacy drive;
    health improvements that eliminated polio and reduced other
    diseases; a mixed economy of state and private business
    1985: Oxfam reported the government's efforts and commitment
    to improving conditions were exceptional
                    |
  1981-1990  CAN IT SURVIVE HOSTILITY?   -> not economically
    All aid stopped; a trade blockade and credit squeeze, with
    other states encouraged to join; the Nicaraguan Democratic
    Force - the Contras - financed
    CONTRA METHOD: blowing up bridges, schools and health clinics
    and burning crops
    After three harbours were mined, the International Court of
    Justice condemned the CIA's backing of the Contras and ordered
    compensation; the USA rejected the ruling and refused to pay
                    |
  1990       WILL A REVOLUTION ACCEPT DEFEAT?   -> yes
    Violeta Barrios de Chamorro elected - the first female
    president elected in Latin America; Ortega and the
    Sandinistas accepted the result
```

⚠️ Read the four questions in the left-hand column. ⚠️ Nicaragua is the case that answers all of them, which is why it is worth a full lesson even though it is a small country. ⚠️ And the last answer is the one that most surprises candidates: ✅ the revolutionary party lost an election and accepted the result.

### 1. Before 1979: how a dynasty was made

✅ Like Guatemala, Nicaragua was a Spanish colony from the mid-sixteenth century until it became independent in 1821; it was briefly part of the Mexican empire and then a member of the United Provinces of Central America until 1840, when it achieved full independence. ✅ The country had a disturbed history: politically unstable, punctuated by periods of ruthless military dictatorship and plagued by foreign intervention, especially from the USA. ✅ For the remainder of the nineteenth century internal politics were dominated by the power struggle between liberals, whose main power base was in Leon, and the conservatives, based in Granada.

✅ The president from 1893 to 1909 was Jose Santos Zelaya, who was responsible for some important changes: great improvements in education, transport with new railways, and communications; expanded coffee production and increased exports; and a modest prosperity. ✅ He also began the building of a new and neutral capital city, Managua, which helped to reduce the long rivalry and feuding between Leon and Granada and between liberals and conservatives. ✅ Unfortunately Zelaya had several faults: he was violent and corrupt, and developed delusions of further grandeur. ✅ He had many of his conservative opponents arrested, tortured and executed, and he and his associates helped themselves shamelessly to the state's assets - selling privileges and concessions to foreign interests and increasing taxes, but keeping the extra revenue for themselves. ✅ Finally he had visions of a united states of Central America with himself as president, and to further this ambition he stirred up unrest in other states: in 1906 his troops invaded Guatemala in an attempt to overthrow the government, and when that failed he supported a rebellion in Honduras and then invaded it, occupying Tegucigalpa with help from the army of El Salvador. ✅ By 1909 most Nicaraguans had had enough.

⚠️ Zelaya is a useful corrective at this point in the session, because he is a wholly domestic explanation of instability. ⚠️ Improvements in education and transport, a new capital designed to end a regional feud, and then torture of opponents, looting of the treasury and invasions of two neighbours. ⚠️ No external power required. ⚠️ Any answer implying that Central American disorder was manufactured abroad has to account for him.

✅ The power struggle between liberals and conservatives broke out again, and in 1912 United States marines were sent in to prop up the conservative government and restore order. ✅ They stayed until 1925, but as soon as they left violence broke out again, and after only a few months United States forces returned. ✅ In 1927 the Somoza family came to power with the approval of the USA, and the situation gradually stabilized, partly because American troops stayed until 1933.

⚠️ The 1925-6 detail is the most instructive in the passage and is almost always missed. ⚠️ Thirteen years of occupation produced order that lasted **a few months** after withdrawal. ⚠️ That is direct evidence that foreign troops can suppress disorder without building the institutions that would prevent it, and it is a general finding rather than a Nicaraguan curiosity.

✅ After that the Somoza family ruled Nicaragua with an iron fist until 1979, supported by the USA. ✅ Political opponents were exiled and each of the Somozas amassed a large fortune. ✅ On three occasions the USA was able to use Nicaraguan territory and troops for attacks on other Latin American governments that it didn't approve of - Guatemala in 1954, Cuba in 1961 and the Dominican Republic in 1965.

⚠️ That last sentence converts Nicaragua from a case into an **instrument**, and it should be used that way in an answer. ⚠️ The dynasty's value was not economic; it was logistical. ⚠️ A reliable client state in Central America provided the territory from which the operations of Lessons 8 and 10 were launched. ⚠️ **The Somozas were part of the infrastructure of hegemony**, and that is why their survival mattered beyond Nicaragua's own borders.

✅ The last of the Somozas, Anastasio, was so blatantly corrupt that he even became an embarrassment to the USA; ✅ President Carter urged him to reform and pay more attention to human rights, but this had little effect.

⚠️ Note what that concedes and what it does not. ⚠️ It concedes that the relationship was not unconditional and that a later administration pressed for reform. ⚠️ It also demonstrates the limit of such pressure: urging produced nothing, because a dynasty whose survival depended on the apparatus of repression could not dismantle it and survive.

### 2. 1979: the revolution and what it did first

✅ In 1979 he was driven out by the Sandinista National Liberation Front, named after Augusto Sandino, who had led an unsuccessful revolution in 1933 and was later murdered on the orders of Somoza. ✅ The Sandinistas had widespread support among ordinary people and from a section of the Roman Catholic Church which was highly critical of the excesses of capitalism.

⚠️ Two things to register. ⚠️ **The name is a claim about continuity**: a movement of the 1970s named itself after a leader murdered in the 1930s, asserting that it was completing an interrupted national project rather than importing a foreign one. ⚠️ **The Church is part of the coalition**, which is unusual and which connects directly to Lesson 17: this is liberation theology operating as a political force, and it means that the revolution cannot be described as a secular Marxist import.

✅ The new Sandinista government immediately introduced a programme of long overdue reform: a redistribution of 5 million acres of land, including some confiscated Somoza property, to about 100,000 families; a literacy drive; and health improvements which eliminated polio and reduced other diseases. ✅ There were other social and economic reforms, and in 1985 Oxfam reported that the efforts of the government and their commitment to improving the conditions of their people were exceptional. ✅ Although the Sandinistas allowed a mixed economy of state and privately owned business, the United States Reagan administration which took office in 1981 saw them as dangerous communists, especially when they formed close links with Cuba.

⚠️ Three features of that programme deserve emphasis because they are what make the subsequent response so instructive.

⚠️ **The land reform was substantial and partly self-financing**: 5 million acres to about 100,000 families, including confiscated property of the ruling family itself - which is an unusually clean form of redistribution, since the assets had been accumulated by the dynasty in office.

⚠️ **The public-health result is concrete and verifiable**: polio eliminated. ⚠️ That is a specific, measurable achievement of a kind that rhetoric cannot manufacture.

⚠️ **The economy was mixed.** ✅ The Sandinistas allowed a mixed economy of state and privately owned business. ⚠️ Hold that against the charge that followed, and note the Oxfam assessment for what it is: an independent international non-governmental organisation's judgement, reported as such, that the government's commitment was exceptional.

### 3. The response, and the method

✅ The USA did everything it could to undermine them and bring them down. ✅ All aid was stopped; the United States began, and encouraged other states to join, a trade blockade and a credit squeeze against Nicaragua; and they financed the Nicaraguan Democratic Force, known as the Contras. ✅ The Contras waged a damaging guerrilla campaign, blowing up bridges, schools and health clinics and burning crops.

⚠️ Read the target list slowly, because it is the analytical core of this lesson. ⚠️ **Bridges, schools, health clinics and crops.** ⚠️ Those are not military objectives; they are precisely the outputs of the reform programme described in the previous section. ⚠️ The literacy drive requires schools; the health improvements require clinics; the land reform requires that the crops reach a market, which requires bridges.

⚠️ So the method was not to defeat the Sandinista army but to **destroy the evidence that the revolution worked**. ⚠️ A revolution that cannot show improvement loses the argument it was making, and a government forced to divert its budget from clinics to defence has been defeated without a battle being won. ⚠️ Naming that mechanism - the destruction of developmental gains as a political instrument - is worth a great deal in an answer on this case.

✅ After they had mined three harbours, the International Court of Justice condemned the American Central Intelligence Agency's backing of the Contras and ordered them to pay compensation for damages caused; ✅ the USA rejected the ruling and refused to pay compensation.

⚠️ This is one of the most useful single facts in the whole topic, and it should be deployed in any question touching international law or the post-1945 legal order. ⚠️ The principal architect of that order was condemned by its principal judicial organ, at the suit of a small Central American state, and declined to comply. ⚠️ **That is a statement about the limits of international adjudication against a great power**, made with a concrete case rather than a generalisation.

### 4. The opposition the policy generated

✅ United States policy was not popular with most of Nicaragua's neighbours. ✅ A meeting of the Latin American parliament, founded in 1968, was held in Guatemala City in April 1986, when 16 out of 18 members voted in favour of a motion condemning the United States attitude; Honduras and El Salvador were the exceptions. ✅ The policy was controversial in the USA itself, and in March 1987, following the Irangate scandal, Congress voted that aid to the Contras should be stopped.

⚠️ Two limits on hegemony, from two different directions.

⚠️ **Regionally**, 16 out of 18 is near-unanimity, and the two dissenters are named - Honduras and El Salvador, ✅ both of which declined to cooperate with the regional peace plan under United States pressure. ⚠️ The exceptions confirm rather than weaken the pattern.

⚠️ **Domestically**, the American legislature stopped the funding after a scandal. ⚠️ Hegemony is exercised by a government that is itself accountable, and that accountability is a real constraint. ⚠️ An answer that presents United States policy as a monolith cannot explain March 1987.

✅ This provided a ray of hope for embattled Nicaragua and her president, Daniel Ortega, who had been elected in 1984 for six years. ✅ In 1987 President Oscar Arias of Costa Rica persuaded all the Central American presidents to support his peace plan for the region, an achievement that won him the Nobel Peace Prize. ✅ However, the plan proved difficult to carry out, mainly because the Reagan administration was still doing its utmost to destabilize Nicaragua, and under United States pressure both Honduras and El Salvador declined to co-operate with the peace plan.

⚠️ Register the Arias plan properly, because it is the best available evidence of regional agency in this session. ⚠️ A Central American head of state assembled a regional settlement, without either superpower, and was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize for it. ⚠️ ⚠️ That the plan was then obstructed does not diminish the point; it sharpens it. ⚠️ **The region could produce its own solutions and could not implement them against external opposition** - which is constrained sovereignty stated at the regional rather than the national level.

### 5. 1990: the election, and the fact that changes the argument

✅ Ortega's co-operation with Castro's Cuba outraged the Americans, and during the 1990 election campaign the Bush administration threatened that violence would continue if the Sandinistas won the election. ✅ Even so, it was a surprise when the National Opposition Union candidate, Violeta Barrios de Chamorro, became the first female president to be elected in Latin America. ✅ Ortega and the Sandinistas accepted the result and she was able to serve her six-year term.

⚠️ Three points, and the second is the one that matters most.

⚠️ **The campaign condition is recorded**: an explicit threat that violence would continue if the incumbent won. ⚠️ An election conducted under that condition is a real election held under duress, and both halves of that description belong in an answer.

⚠️ **The revolutionary party lost and accepted the result.** ⚠️ Return to Lesson 12 and the Chilean opposition's premise that no Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out of power. ⚠️ In 1990 a revolutionary government in Central America did exactly that. ⚠️ ✅ And it went further: ✅ Ortega was again defeated in the 1996 election and in the 2001 election, and returned by winning the presidency in November 2006 with 62 per cent of the vote. ⚠️ Four consecutive acceptances of an electoral verdict, including two defeats and a return.

⚠️ **Ortega's later record is also given and should not be omitted from a balanced answer.** ✅ He certainly got the Supreme Court to cancel the ban on presidents standing for consecutive terms, enabling him to stand again in November 2011, when he won comfortably with 62 per cent; ✅ democrats claim that he is well on the way towards becoming a dictator like the Somozas; ✅ some former left-wing supporters left the party, accusing him of kowtowing to the neo-liberals simply in order to stay in power; ✅ there were allegations of fraud during the 2006 election, after which both the USA and the European Union suspended their aid programmes.

⚠️ Report all of that. ⚠️ An answer that celebrates 1990 and suppresses 2011 has selected its evidence.

✅ Chamorro's main achievement was to disarm some of the guerrilla groups that had been terrorising the country for years, and most of the fighting ceased; ✅ things became more stable and some of the Sandinista social reforms were allowed to stay; ✅ but the economy was in total ruins and government debts were astronomical.

### 6. The condition of the country afterwards

✅ At the turn of the century the country was still in dire straits. ✅ In 1998 there was a devastating hurricane which killed 9,000 people, left around 2 million homeless and caused damage amounting to $10 billion. ✅ In 2002 former president Aleman was charged with corruption and embezzlement and later sentenced to 20 years' imprisonment. ✅ The situation was so bad that in 2004 the World Bank and the IMF waived $4.5 billion of Nicaragua's debts. ✅ As the Sandinistas took office again in January 2007, they faced a challenging prospect - Nicaragua had the distinction of being the poorest country in the Western hemisphere.

✅ Under Ortega after 2007: he had become less "revolutionary" and had toned down his anti-capitalist stand during his years in opposition since 1990, claiming that he was now motivated by Christian principles rather than by Marxism; ✅ he introduced new schemes to improve healthcare, social services, education including scholarships for poor students, and housing; ✅ progress was slow, and in 2011, towards the end of his term, the country was still the poorest in Latin America with 46 per cent of the population living below the poverty line; ✅ private businesses had been allowed to continue without state interference and the government could claim with some justification that the mixed economy had produced a period of sustained economic growth. ✅ Robin Yapp, writing in the *Telegraph* on 7 October 2011, recorded that Nicaragua was helped by cheap oil from Hugo Chavez's Venezuela, which had helped to prop up social schemes like subsidised housing, and that Ortega had also been able to attract foreign investors who saw Nicaragua as a safe haven compared to neighbouring Honduras or El Salvador, which had the world's highest murder rates.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ⚠️ The hurricane figures are for 1998; ❌ they must never be mixed with war casualties. ⚠️ The murder-rate comparison is attributed to a named journalist in a named newspaper on a stated date; ❌ do not restate it as an unowned fact.

⚠️ And the closing judgement writes itself. ⚠️ A revolution with a substantial and independently praised reform programme, a decade of financed insurgency against its schools and clinics, an electoral defeat honoured, a hurricane, a debt write-off, and at the end of it the poorest country in the Western hemisphere with 46 per cent below the poverty line. ⚠️ **That is what the full cost of the confrontation looks like**, and it is the strongest answer available to any question about the human consequences of Cold War policy in Central America.

### Criticism and reply: was the hostility to the Sandinistas justified?

⚠️ **The case for the prosecution, as recorded.** ✅ The Sandinistas formed close links with Cuba; ✅ Ortega's co-operation with Castro's Cuba outraged the Americans; ✅ the Reagan administration saw them as dangerous communists; ⚠️ and a neighbouring military government independently announced that it saw the Sandinista government as a threat to the whole of Central America.

⚠️ **The case for the defence, equally recorded.** ✅ The Sandinistas allowed a mixed economy of state and privately owned business; ✅ they held an election in 1984, another in 1990, and left office when they lost; ✅ their programme was land redistribution, literacy and public health, and Oxfam judged their commitment exceptional in 1985; ✅ the International Court of Justice condemned the backing of the Contras and ordered compensation; ✅ the Latin American parliament condemned United States policy 16 to 2; ✅ the United States Congress itself stopped the funding in March 1987.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ The alignment with Cuba was real and, within the Cold War frame of Lesson 3, made the confrontation predictable. ⚠️ What the evidence does not support is the proportionality: a decade of insurgency directed at bridges, schools, clinics and crops, conducted against a mixed-economy government that held and honoured elections, was condemned by the region, by the World Court and eventually by the American legislature.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ Nicaragua is the topic's case of revolution under sustained external pressure and its principal illustration of the limits of international law against a great power.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect it in questions on Cold War intervention, on the costs of superpower rivalry for small states, or as a comparison with Cuba on why one revolution survived and another was worn down.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Dating the American presence to 1979. ✅ Marines were sent in 1912 and stayed until 1925, returned within months, and remained until 1933.
2. ⚠️ Describing the Sandinista economy as socialist. ✅ They allowed a mixed economy of state and privately owned business.
3. ⚠️ Omitting the Contra target list. ✅ Bridges, schools, health clinics and crops - the outputs of the reform programme.
4. ⚠️ Leaving out the International Court of Justice ruling and the refusal to comply. ⚠️ It is the topic's sharpest evidence on the limits of international adjudication.
5. ⚠️ Treating 1990 as a defeat imposed from outside only. ✅ The threat of continued violence is recorded, and so is the fact that the Sandinistas accepted the result.
6. ⚠️ Idealising Ortega's later record. ✅ The consecutive-term ban was cancelled by the Supreme Court and democrats claim he is on the way to becoming a dictator like the Somozas.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Spanish colony from the mid-sixteenth century; independent 1821; briefly in the Mexican empire; in the United Provinces of Central America until 1840; thereafter unstable, with ruthless military dictatorships and foreign intervention especially from the USA; nineteenth-century politics dominated by liberals based in Leon and conservatives in Granada.
2. ✅ Zelaya, president 1893-1909: improvements in education, transport and communications; expanded coffee production and exports; modest prosperity; a new neutral capital at Managua reducing the Leon-Granada feud. Also violent and corrupt: opponents arrested, tortured and executed; state assets looted; privileges and concessions sold to foreign interests; taxes raised and the revenue kept; invasions of Guatemala in 1906 and then Honduras with Salvadorean help, occupying Tegucigalpa.
3. ✅ 1912: United States marines sent to prop up the conservative government and restore order; they stayed until 1925; violence resumed within a few months of their departure and forces returned; in 1927 the Somoza family came to power with American approval and the situation stabilised, partly because troops stayed until 1933.
4. ✅ The Somoza family ruled with an iron fist until 1979, supported by the USA; opponents exiled; each Somoza amassed a large fortune; Nicaraguan territory and troops used three times for attacks on governments the USA disapproved of - Guatemala 1954, Cuba 1961, Dominican Republic 1965.
5. ✅ Anastasio Somoza was so blatantly corrupt that he became an embarrassment to the USA; Carter urged reform and attention to human rights with little effect.
6. ✅ 1979: driven out by the Sandinista National Liberation Front, named after Augusto Sandino, who led an unsuccessful revolution in 1933 and was later murdered on Somoza's orders; widespread popular support and support from a section of the Roman Catholic Church highly critical of the excesses of capitalism.
7. ✅ Reform programme: 5 million acres redistributed, including confiscated Somoza property, to about 100,000 families; a literacy drive; health improvements that eliminated polio and reduced other diseases; a mixed economy of state and private business; in 1985 Oxfam reported the government's efforts and commitment as exceptional.
8. ✅ The Reagan administration, in office from 1981, saw them as dangerous communists, especially after close links with Cuba; all aid stopped; a trade blockade and credit squeeze begun, with other states encouraged to join; the Nicaraguan Democratic Force, the Contras, financed.
9. ✅ The Contras blew up bridges, schools and health clinics and burned crops; after they mined three harbours the International Court of Justice condemned the Central Intelligence Agency's backing and ordered compensation; the USA rejected the ruling and refused to pay.
10. ✅ April 1986: the Latin American parliament, founded 1968, meeting in Guatemala City, voted 16 to 2 to condemn the United States attitude, with Honduras and El Salvador the exceptions; March 1987: after the Irangate scandal, Congress voted to stop aid to the Contras.
11. ✅ Ortega elected in 1984 for six years; in 1987 President Oscar Arias of Costa Rica persuaded all the Central American presidents to support his regional peace plan, winning the Nobel Peace Prize; the plan proved difficult to carry out because the Reagan administration continued to destabilise Nicaragua and, under United States pressure, Honduras and El Salvador declined to co-operate.
12. ✅ 1990: the Bush administration threatened during the campaign that violence would continue if the Sandinistas won; Violeta Barrios de Chamorro of the National Opposition Union was elected, the first female president elected in Latin America; Ortega and the Sandinistas accepted the result and she served her six-year term; she disarmed some guerrilla groups and most fighting ceased; some Sandinista social reforms were allowed to stay; the economy was in ruins and government debts astronomical.
13. ✅ Ortega was defeated again in 1996 by Aleman and in 2001 by Bolanos, and returned in November 2006 with 62 per cent.
14. ✅ 1998 hurricane: 9,000 killed, around 2 million homeless, $10 billion of damage; 2002 Aleman charged with corruption and embezzlement and later sentenced to 20 years; 2004 the World Bank and the IMF waived $4.5 billion of debt; by January 2007 Nicaragua was the poorest country in the Western hemisphere.
15. ✅ After 2007 Ortega was less revolutionary, claiming Christian rather than Marxist motivation; new health, social service, education and housing schemes; in 2011 still the poorest in Latin America with 46 per cent below the poverty line; private business allowed to continue and a mixed economy producing sustained growth; Robin Yapp in the *Telegraph*, 7 October 2011, recorded cheap Venezuelan oil propping up social schemes and investors seeing Nicaragua as safer than Honduras or El Salvador, which had the world's highest murder rates; allegations of fraud in 2006 led the USA and the European Union to suspend aid; the Supreme Court cancelled the consecutive-term ban and he won November 2011 with 62 per cent, while democrats claimed he was becoming a dictator like the Somozas.
16. ⚠️ Master formulation: the Contra campaign targeted the outputs of the reform programme rather than its army, which is how a revolution is defeated without being beaten - and the revolutionary party nevertheless lost an election in 1990 and accepted the result.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain what the Contra campaign's choice of targets tells us about how the Sandinista government was defeated, and identify two independent bodies that criticised the policy.

**Model answer:** The targets were bridges, schools, health clinics and crops - precisely the outputs of the government's own programme, which consisted of redistributing 5 million acres to about 100,000 families, a literacy drive requiring schools, and health improvements that eliminated polio and required clinics. The campaign therefore aimed not at defeating the Sandinista army but at destroying the demonstrable evidence that the revolution improved people's lives, while forcing a poor government to divert its budget from clinics and schools to defence. Combined with the stopping of all aid and a trade blockade and credit squeeze that other states were encouraged to join, this produced the outcome recorded in 1990: the Sandinistas left office with the economy in total ruins and government debts astronomical, and by January 2007 Nicaragua had the distinction of being the poorest country in the Western hemisphere. Two independent bodies criticised the policy. The International Court of Justice, after the Contras mined three harbours, condemned the Central Intelligence Agency's backing of them and ordered the payment of compensation, which the United States rejected and refused to pay. And the Latin American parliament, meeting in Guatemala City in April 1986, voted 16 to 2 to condemn the United States attitude, the exceptions being Honduras and El Salvador. The United States Congress itself stopped the funding in March 1987 after the Irangate scandal.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the Sandinistas were therefore removed by external force alone. The Bush administration did threaten during the 1990 campaign that violence would continue if they won, and that duress is part of the record. But the election was held, Violeta Barrios de Chamorro won it as the first female president elected in Latin America, and Ortega and the Sandinistas accepted the result and allowed her to serve her full six-year term - as they accepted further defeats in 1996 and 2001 before returning by election in 2006. The revolutionary party's acceptance of an electoral verdict is a fact about Nicaraguan agency, and it directly contradicts the premise on which the Chilean opposition had justified the coup of 1973.

---

## Lesson 15 - The debt crisis of the 1980s: how finance replaced force

Progress: 15/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the policy framework that generated the borrowing; who lent and how profitable it was; the interest-rate shock of 1979-81 and the compounding trap; the regional totals and the capital drain; the Brazilian suspension and the Mexican rescheduling; the rescue institutions and their conditions; the four channels through which austerity reached society; and the recovery that must be conceded

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the crisis of the 1980s, covering the neo-liberal framework, the borrowing period, the profitability of lending, the interest-rate doubling, the regional total, the Brazilian and Mexican cases and the institutional rescues, together with the country sections that record the social consequences
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the debt machine, stage by stage

```text
   [1] THE POLICY SETTING
       "under US domination, the countries of Latin America had
        been obliged to follow economic policies known as
        'neo-liberalism'. This involved privatization, deregulation
        of finance, cuts in social spending and other austerity
        measures. Basically this was designed to make use of a
        country's resources in order to benefit a wealthy elite at
        home, foreign investors, big business and bankers,
        particularly those in the USA."
                    |
   [2] THE BORROWING
       "This had forced Latin American governments to borrow
        massively from foreign banks, in order to develop their
        amenities and industries. Many of these banks were in the
        USA, and the borrowing was at its height from 1973
        until 1982."
                    |
   [3] HOW PROFITABLE THE LENDING WAS
       1970: the seven largest US banks made 2% of their profits
             from interest on loans to Third World countries
       1982: the same seven banks made 60%
                    |
   [4] THE SHOCK
       "With the doubling of American interest rates in the period
        1979-81, many of the debtor nations could not even pay the
        interest, let alone repay the debts"
                    |
   [5] THE COMPOUNDING TRAP
       "the amount of interest they failed to pay each year was
        added on to the existing debt. They were forced to borrow
        from new sources merely to keep up the interest payments
        on the original loans."
                    |
   [6] THE SCALE AND THE DRAIN
       By 1985 Latin America owed some $368 billion
       "there was a constant drain of capital to the USA, leaving
        Latin America increasingly impoverished"
       By 1987, as export earnings steadily declined, the situation
       was approaching catastrophe
                    |
   [7] THE BREAKS
       Brazil, February 1987: suspends interest payments
       Mexico: considering repudiating its debts
       Mexico 1982: banks nationalised, currency devalued 70%,
         drastic cuts in public services, IMF loan and rescheduling
         of half of $96 billion of overseas debts
                    |
   [8] THE RESCUE, AND ITS TERMS
       "the IMF and the World Bank, desperate to avoid an economic
        catastrophe, arranged credits amounting to several billion
        dollars for Brazil. The Mexican government secured an annual
        loan for the next 30 years from the IMF and was able to
        reschedule its debts. Similar arrangements helped other
        debtor countries to survive."
```

⚠️ Stage three is the one candidates never use and it is the most eloquent item in the sequence. ⚠️ A rise from 2 per cent to 60 per cent of the profits of the seven largest United States banks in twelve years is not a description of a region's imprudence; ⚠️ it is a description of an extremely lucrative business that had every incentive to keep lending.

### 1. The framework: what the account says the policies were and whom they served

✅ By the early 1980s it was clear that the problems of Latin America had not been solved, and two problems in particular - those of debt and finance - had reached crisis proportions. ✅ The trouble was that, under United States domination, the countries of Latin America had been obliged to follow economic policies known as "neo-liberalism", involving privatization, deregulation of finance, cuts in social spending and other austerity measures. ✅ Basically this was designed to make use of a country's resources in order to benefit a wealthy elite at home, foreign investors, big business and bankers, particularly those in the USA.

⚠️ Three cautions about how to use that passage, because it is strongly worded.

⚠️ **It is an assessment, and should be attributed.** It is the core account's characterisation of the policy framework, and it should be presented as such rather than as a neutral definition. ⚠️ Lesson 16 sets it against the technical definition and against the recorded results, which is the properly balanced treatment.

⚠️ **The chronology needs care.** ⚠️ The account places the neo-liberal framework before the borrowing. ⚠️ Read strictly, the full privatisation-and-liberalisation package belongs to the later 1980s and the 1990s, which is where the account's own country narratives place it - Mexico's privatisation after 1986, Menem's programme from 1991, Brazil's *Plano Real* from 1994. ⚠️ The safe formulation, which preserves the account's point without importing a chronological error, is that **financial deregulation and openness to foreign capital** characterised the borrowing period, and that **privatisation and austerity** characterised the adjustment period that followed the crisis.

⚠️ **The beneficiaries listed are the analytical payload.** ✅ A wealthy elite at home, foreign investors, big business and bankers. ⚠️ Note the first item: the domestic elite is named alongside the external beneficiaries. ⚠️ This is not a story of foreigners against a nation; it is a story of a coalition across the border, which is exactly the two-variables discipline from the opening.

### 2. The borrowing, and the profitability of lending

✅ This had forced Latin American governments to borrow massively from foreign banks in order to develop their amenities and industries. ✅ Many of these banks were in the USA, and the borrowing was at its height from 1973 until 1982. ✅ In 1982 the seven largest United States banks made 60 per cent of their profits from the interest on loans to Third World countries, as against only 2 per cent in 1970.

⚠️ Dwell on those two percentages, because they carry an argument that most answers never make.

⚠️ **They establish that the lending was a business, not a favour.** ⚠️ If in 1982 three-fifths of the profits of the seven largest American banks came from interest on Third World loans, then the supply of credit was being driven by the lenders' search for return at least as much as by the borrowers' demand for capital.

⚠️ **They imply a lending boom.** ⚠️ A jump from 2 to 60 per cent in twelve years means that a great deal of money was actively pushed into these markets. ⚠️ The period is exactly 1973 to 1982, and it is worth knowing why the money was there: the oil price rises of the 1970s created very large deposits in Western banks which had to be lent somewhere, and middle-income sovereign borrowers were the obvious destination.

⚠️ **They change the moral geometry of the crisis.** ⚠️ A debt crisis is conventionally narrated as borrower imprudence. ⚠️ These two numbers show that it was also lender concentration: banks that earned three-fifths of their profits from one class of borrower had built an exposure that made both sides captive.

⚠️ And keep the account's stated purpose of the borrowing: ✅ **to develop their amenities and industries**. ⚠️ This was not, in the main, consumption. ⚠️ It was the external financing of the industrialisation strategy of Lesson 7 - which is why the arc is one arc and not two.

### 3. The shock and the trap

✅ With the doubling of American interest rates in the period 1979-81, many of the debtor nations could not even pay the interest, let alone repay the debts, and the amount of interest they failed to pay each year was added on to the existing debt. ✅ They were forced to borrow from new sources merely to keep up the interest payments on the original loans.

⚠️ This is the mechanical heart of the crisis and it must be understood rather than merely quoted.

⚠️ **The shock was exogenous and unilateral.** ⚠️ A monetary decision taken in one country doubled the cost of servicing debts contracted by dozens of others. ⚠️ No Latin American government did anything in 1979 to cause it; no Latin American government was consulted; and no Latin American government could offset it. ⚠️ That is the purest illustration in this session of what structural dependence means: a country's fiscal position can be transformed by a decision it has no part in taking.

⚠️ **The compounding is what converts a shock into a trap.** ⚠️ Follow the arithmetic slowly. ⚠️ Unpaid interest is added to the principal. ⚠️ Next year's interest is charged on the larger principal. ⚠️ New borrowing is required to pay the interest, which further enlarges the principal. ⚠️ **The debt grows while the country is paying**, and no amount of austerity can reverse it as long as the rate exceeds the growth of the country's capacity to earn.

⚠️ **The consequence is the loss of the development function of the budget.** ⚠️ Every unit of foreign exchange committed to interest is unavailable for imported machinery, medicines, fertiliser or fuel. ⚠️ A state in this position has ceased to be a developmental state and has become a debt-servicing agency, and that transformation was imposed on it by an external rate change rather than chosen by its electorate.

### 4. Scale, drain and the political consequence

✅ By 1985 Latin America owed some $368 billion, and there was a constant drain of capital to the USA, leaving Latin America increasingly impoverished. ✅ By 1987, as export earnings steadily declined, the situation was approaching catastrophe.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ✅ The regional figure is *some* $368 billion **by 1985**. ❌ Do not restate it for another year and ❌ do not decompose it by country.

⚠️ The phrase to carry into an answer is ✅ "a constant drain of capital to the USA". ⚠️ It describes a **net outflow from poor countries to rich ones** and it is the empirical content of the dependency argument in Lesson 20. ⚠️ It is also exactly what the Mexican president described from the other side of the transaction: ✅ "We have reached the limit of being able to sustain this net transfer of resources to the rest, which violates economic logic and is tremendously inequitable."

⚠️ **The combination in 1987 is what made it a catastrophe rather than a difficulty.** ⚠️ Debt service rose because of the rate shock while export earnings fell because of commodity prices. ⚠️ Outgoings up, income down, simultaneously - the two halves of the vice described in Lessons 2 and 7 closing at once.

### 5. The breaks, and a source discrepancy handled openly

✅ Brazil, one of the most prosperous states with its huge natural resources, had large debts, and in February 1987 the government announced that it was suspending interest payments. ✅ Mexico, which owed almost as much, was considering the possibility of repudiating its debts. ✅ Fortunately it didn't quite come to that: the IMF and the World Bank, desperate to avoid an economic catastrophe, arranged credits amounting to several billion dollars for Brazil; ✅ the Mexican government secured an annual loan for the next 30 years from the IMF and was able to reschedule its debts; ✅ similar arrangements helped other debtor countries to survive.

⚠️ **A source note, given openly rather than concealed.** ⚠️ The core account's figures for the Brazilian debt and for the IMF credits are internally inconsistent between passages - the same rescue is given in one place in millions and in another in billions, and the debt total in the summary passage is on an order of magnitude that cannot be reconciled with the regional total of some $368 billion. ⚠️ Because the discrepancy cannot be resolved from the sources held here, this session teaches the **events** - the suspension of interest payments in February 1987, the consideration of repudiation by Mexico, and the arrangement of credits by the two institutions - and does not assert the disputed magnitudes. ⚠️ The divergence is recorded in the final ledger. ❌ Do not reproduce either figure as fact in an answer.

⚠️ What the events establish, independently of the numbers, is the decisive political fact. ⚠️ **Two of the largest economies in the region reached the point of suspension and contemplated repudiation** - and the creditors' institutions moved immediately, because a coordinated default by large debtors would have threatened banks that were earning three-fifths of their profits from exactly those loans.

⚠️ That is the leverage the region briefly possessed and did not use collectively. ⚠️ Debtors who owe individually are weak; debtors who owe enough, together, are systemically important. ⚠️ ✅ Brazil suspended in February 1987 and Mexico considered repudiation - separately. ⚠️ A debtors' cartel was discussed in the region in this period and never formed, and the absence of collective action is a real part of the explanation for the outcome.

### 6. Conditionality: what rescue actually meant

✅ The Mexican case is the fullest illustration. ✅ In 1982 the government introduced desperate measures: banks nationalized, the currency devalued by 70 per cent, and drastic reductions in spending on public services; ✅ President de la Madrid negotiated a deal with the IMF for a loan and a rescheduling of half the country's overseas debts of $96 billion; ✅ Mexico then failed to fulfil the conditions attached, and the IMF was preparing to cancel the agreement when the Mexico City earthquake intervened and the agreement went ahead.

⚠️ Note precisely what a rescue consists of. ⚠️ It is not the forgiveness of debt. ⚠️ It is **new credit, conditional on policy change**, which allows the old credit to be serviced. ⚠️ The debt does not shrink; the creditor is paid; and the policy of the borrowing state is now set jointly with an external institution.

⚠️ And note the sovereignty question that follows, which is what makes this examinable rather than technical. ⚠️ Which public services are cut, by how much and in what order is the most political decision any government makes. ⚠️ Under a conditional programme, that decision is taken inside a framework agreed with a creditor. ⚠️ **Elections continue; the range of outcomes they can produce narrows.** ⚠️ That is the twentieth century's final form of constrained sovereignty, and it required no troops at all.

✅ The same pattern recurs. ✅ Venezuela by the early 1990s was falling into arrears with debt repayments and the government was trying to cope by following IMF requirements, which involved reducing imports and government spending, while unemployment was rising and inflation was running at not far short of 40 per cent. ✅ Argentina saw the IMF come to the rescue twice in 2001 before the near-collapse, the panic and the rioting that forced a president's resignation in December.

### 7. The four channels through which austerity reached society

⚠️ It is not enough to say that the debt crisis had social consequences; that is an assertion until the transmission is specified. ⚠️ Four channels are evidenced, and an answer that names them will be concrete where others are vague.

⚠️ **The budget.** ✅ Drastic reductions in spending on public services in Mexico in 1982; ✅ reduced government spending in Venezuela under IMF requirements. ⚠️ Debt service crowds out the budget lines that reach the poor - health, education, subsidies and public-sector wages.

⚠️ **The currency.** ✅ The Mexican currency devalued by 70 per cent in 1982; ✅ Venezuela reducing imports. ⚠️ Devaluation raises the domestic price of every imported good, including food, fuel and medicine, and it does so immediately and regressively.

⚠️ **The price level.** ✅ Argentina left with inflation of around 900 per cent after the Falklands defeat; ✅ Brazil's hyper-inflation under Sarney and inflation of a thousand per cent when the *Plano Real* began; ✅ Venezuelan inflation not far short of 40 per cent in the early 1990s. ⚠️ Inflation is a regressive tax: it falls hardest on those holding cash and earning fixed wages, and those are the poor.

⚠️ **The employment contract.** ✅ Brazil's account freeze produced a wave of business failures and massive unemployment; ✅ Venezuelan unemployment rose under adjustment; ✅ Mexican privatisation did nothing to solve the unemployment problem. ⚠️ Jobs, and the public-sector employment that the developmental state had provided, are withdrawn.

⚠️ And there is a fifth consequence which is political rather than economic and which is the most important of all. ⚠️ Because adjustment was imposed by creditors rather than chosen by electorates, and because it was administered by newly restored democracies rather than by the military governments that had contracted the debts, **the deepest effect was on the legitimacy of democratic government itself**. ⚠️ Voters who had expected democracy to deliver relief received austerity, and the political consequence appears in Lesson 19.

⚠️ ✅ The account records what that produced in one country: riots and discontent in Venezuela, troops sent into poor neighbourhoods to put down protests in 1992, and a young officer so disgusted that he organised a coup - the beginning of the political career that would define the region's next two decades.

### 8. The recovery that must be conceded

⚠️ A determinist reading in which debt forecloses development permanently is not supported by the record, and an answer that concedes the counter-evidence is stronger for it.

✅ Brazil brought inflation and government debt under control under Cardoso and his *Plano Real*, which the account calls a considerable achievement; ✅ by the end of 2011 Brazil's economy was ranked sixth largest in the world and experts predicted it would probably rise to fifth by the end of 2012. ✅ Argentina under Kirchner showed an impressive annual growth rate of about 8 per cent, and in January 2006 it was announced that Argentina had paid off all remaining debts to the IMF. ✅ Nicaragua had $4.5 billion of its debts waived by the World Bank and the IMF in 2004.

⚠️ Note that the Argentine case is a **political** recovery as much as an economic one: ⚠️ paying off the Fund and saying so publicly was an act of emancipation from the relationship described in section 6, and ✅ the account records that Kirchner won popularity by doing it.

⚠️ The balanced conclusion, then: the crisis was severe, its social transmission is documented through four channels, and its effects were reversible over a longer horizon by governments that recovered fiscal autonomy. ⚠️ ❌ What it was not is a permanent sentence.

### Criticism and reply: whose fault was the debt crisis?

⚠️ **The domestic-responsibility case.** ✅ Mexico borrowed extravagantly on the assumption that oil revenues would always cover interest and abandoned restraint; ✅ the Brazilian generals borrowed to finance a growth strategy; ✅ Argentina's junta left an unprecedentedly high foreign debt; ✅ corruption was widespread, and in Mexico members of a president's family helped themselves to enormous sums.

⚠️ **The external-responsibility case.** ✅ The borrowing was made necessary by the framework the region was obliged to follow; ✅ the lending was extremely profitable to the banks that made it, rising from 2 to 60 per cent of the profits of the seven largest; ✅ the proximate trigger was the doubling of American interest rates in 1979-81, a decision taken elsewhere; ✅ the compounding of unpaid interest was a contractual mechanism the debtors did not design; ✅ there was a constant drain of capital to the USA.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict**, and it is the formulation to write: the region borrowed for real developmental purposes under a framework that encouraged it, from lenders for whom it was the most profitable business available, and the crisis was precipitated by an external monetary decision whose contractual consequences then made the debt self-enlarging. ⚠️ Domestic imprudence and corruption made particular countries more vulnerable; ⚠️ they do not explain why the crisis was simultaneous, regional and, by 1985, some $368 billion in size.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This is the region's contribution to the world-economy strand and its central evidence on North-South financial relations. ⚠️ The general framework of the world economy belongs to the world-economy owner; the region's own sequence is owned here.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "debt and dependency", or the social consequences of the crisis, or the relationship between economic constraint and political sovereignty.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating it as a purely financial event. ✅ The transmission runs through the budget, the currency, the price level and the employment contract.
2. ⚠️ Blaming borrower imprudence alone. ✅ The lending produced 60 per cent of the profits of the seven largest United States banks in 1982 against 2 per cent in 1970.
3. ⚠️ Reproducing the disputed Brazilian debt and credit figures. ⚠️ The source is internally inconsistent; teach the events, not the magnitudes.
4. ⚠️ Misplacing the neo-liberal chronology. ⚠️ Financial deregulation characterised the borrowing period; privatisation and austerity characterised the adjustment that followed.
5. ⚠️ Presenting the crisis as permanent. ✅ Brazil brought debt under control and Argentina repaid the IMF in January 2006.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ By the early 1980s debt and finance had reached crisis proportions; the countries of the region had been obliged to follow policies described as neo-liberalism - privatisation, deregulation of finance, cuts in social spending and austerity - designed to benefit a wealthy elite at home, foreign investors, big business and bankers, particularly in the USA.
2. ✅ Governments were forced to borrow massively from foreign banks to develop amenities and industries; many banks were in the USA; borrowing was at its height from 1973 until 1982.
3. ✅ In 1982 the seven largest United States banks made 60 per cent of their profits from interest on loans to Third World countries, against only 2 per cent in 1970.
4. ✅ With the doubling of American interest rates in 1979-81, many debtor nations could not even pay the interest, let alone repay the debts; unpaid interest was added to the existing debt; they were forced to borrow from new sources merely to keep up interest payments.
5. ✅ By 1985 Latin America owed some $368 billion, with a constant drain of capital to the USA leaving the region increasingly impoverished; by 1987, as export earnings steadily declined, the situation was approaching catastrophe.
6. ✅ Brazil suspended interest payments in February 1987; Mexico considered repudiating its debts; the IMF and the World Bank arranged credits for Brazil, and Mexico secured an annual loan for the next 30 years and rescheduled its debts, with similar arrangements helping other debtors.
7. ⚠️ Source control: the account's Brazilian debt figure and its IMF credit figures are internally inconsistent across passages; the events are taught and the magnitudes are not asserted.
8. ✅ Mexico 1982: banks nationalised, currency devalued 70 per cent, drastic cuts in public services, an IMF loan and rescheduling of half of $96 billion of overseas debts; conditions unfulfilled and cancellation prepared until the earthquake intervened.
9. ✅ De la Madrid: the limit of sustaining this net transfer of resources to the rest, which violates economic logic and is tremendously inequitable.
10. ✅ Venezuela by the early 1990s was in arrears and following IMF requirements by reducing imports and government spending, with rising unemployment and inflation not far short of 40 per cent; Argentina was rescued twice by the IMF in 2001 before the panic, the riots and the president's resignation in December.
11. ⚠️ Four transmission channels: the budget, the currency, the price level and the employment contract; and a fifth political effect - adjustment imposed by creditors and administered by new democracies damaged the legitimacy of democratic government.
12. ✅ Recoveries: Brazil brought inflation and government debt under control, a considerable achievement, and was ranked the sixth largest economy in the world at the end of 2011; Argentina grew at about 8 per cent a year and announced in January 2006 that it had paid off all remaining IMF debts; the World Bank and IMF waived $4.5 billion of Nicaragua's debts in 2004.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: the debt crisis achieved through creditors' conditions what intervention had achieved through force in 1954 - the reversal of state-led development - and did so at far lower political cost to the creditor.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the mechanism by which the debt crisis converted an economic condition into a loss of sovereignty over policy, and state the single piece of evidence that most complicates the view that the crisis was caused by borrower imprudence.

**Model answer:** The mechanism has three stages. First, the trigger was external and unilateral: the doubling of American interest rates between 1979 and 1981 meant that many debtor nations could not even pay the interest, let alone repay the debts - a decision taken elsewhere, in which no debtor participated and which none could offset. Second, the contract turned the shock into a trap: the amount of interest they failed to pay each year was added to the existing debt, and they were forced to borrow from new sources merely to keep up the interest payments, so the debt enlarged while the country was paying. Third, rescue was not relief but conditional credit: Mexico secured an annual loan for thirty years and a rescheduling of half its $96 billion of overseas debts, having already nationalised banks, devalued by 70 per cent and made drastic cuts in public services, and it failed to meet the conditions attached. Which services are cut, by how much and in what order is the most political question a government answers, and under conditionality it is answered jointly with a creditor. Elections continued; the range of outcomes they could produce narrowed - and no troops were required. The evidence that most complicates the imprudence view is the profitability of the lending: in 1982 the seven largest United States banks made 60 per cent of their profits from interest on loans to Third World countries, against only 2 per cent in 1970, which shows credit being actively supplied by lenders for whom it was the most lucrative business available.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that debt permanently forecloses development. Brazil subsequently brought inflation and government debt under control, which the record calls a considerable achievement, and by the end of 2011 had the sixth largest economy in the world; Argentina achieved annual growth of about 8 per cent and announced in January 2006 that it had paid off all remaining debts to the IMF, winning popularity by doing so; and the World Bank and the IMF waived $4.5 billion of Nicaragua's debts in 2004. The crisis was severe and its social transmission is documented, but the recovery is part of the record and an answer that omits it has replaced analysis with fatalism.

---

## Lesson 16 - The neoliberal turn and its politics: why reforms that worked were still rejected

Progress: 16/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: what the term denotes technically and what the sources say it served; the recorded national programmes in Mexico, Argentina, Brazil and Chile; the measurable successes; the measurable failures; the trade-agreement question; why the reforms were resented even where they succeeded; and the partial reversals that followed

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's definition of the policy framework and the country narratives recording the Mexican privatisation and NAFTA, Menem's Argentine programme, Cardoso's Plano Real and the Chilean departure from the framework under Bachelet
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the same programme, four countries, mixed results

| Country and programme | ✅ What it did | ✅ What worked | ✅ What did not |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mexico**, from the later 1980s | Privatisation; joined the North American Free Trade Agreement, in force January 1994 | Inflation brought down to single figures; the budget deficit eliminated; exports to the USA increased and the country opened to United States and Canadian investment | Did nothing to solve the unemployment problem; farmers suffered as imports of American produce, especially meat, increased substantially; by 2000 about a third of the population still lived below the poverty line |
| **Argentina**, Menem from 1991 | Protectionist trade and business regulations removed; strict austerity; a wave of privatisations of state-owned industries | - | "It was all to no avail" - the worst recession for years from September 1998; more austerity under de la Rua; two IMF rescues in 2001; near-collapse, financial panic, December rioting in Buenos Aires and the president's resignation |
| **Brazil**, Cardoso's *Plano Real* from 1994 | Large tax increases; wage reductions for public-service workers; privatisation of many government enterprises | Inflation down from a thousand per cent when the plan began to single figures by 1997; overseas markets revived; marked increases in exports of agricultural produce and manufactured goods; Cardoso re-elected in 1998 | Renewed crisis as Russia and south-east Asia cut imports, with government spending and borrowing still much too high and inflation rising again; massive IMF credits needed; considerable unrest among poverty-stricken working classes and an increase in crime and violence |
| **Chile**, under the dictatorship and after | Market reforms under Pinochet; continuation under the post-1990 coalition | Inflation down from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980; the regime did much to improve Chile's economy | "Clearly the classic neo-liberal economic policies were not sufficient to bring full recovery, and so the government broke the neo-conservative rules with a dose of state intervention" - state copper revenues deployed and capital controls introduced under Bachelet, Codelco having been the state-owned copper corporation since 1976 |

⚠️ Read the last two columns across all four rows. ⚠️ The pattern is consistent and it is the lesson: **the programme was effective against inflation and ineffective against unemployment and poverty.** ⚠️ Since inflation is a problem that governments and creditors feel acutely while unemployment and poverty are problems that voters feel acutely, a policy with exactly this profile will be judged a success by its administrators and a failure by its subjects. ⚠️ That divergence is the whole politics of the period.

### 1. What the term means, and how to handle it fairly

⚠️ The account's characterisation was given in Lesson 15 and is strongly worded. ⚠️ A balanced answer sets it beside the technical description and then judges by the recorded results rather than by either label.

⚠️ **The technical content**, as the account itself enumerates it: ✅ privatization, deregulation of finance, cuts in social spending and other austerity measures - to which the country narratives add ✅ the removal of protectionist trade and business regulations, ✅ tax increases and public-sector wage reductions where stabilisation required them, and ✅ trade agreements opening the domestic market.

⚠️ **The stated purpose**, in the account's own words: ✅ designed to make use of a country's resources in order to benefit a wealthy elite at home, foreign investors, big business and bankers, particularly those in the USA.

⚠️ There is also a **standard designation** for the package, and it is the term an examiner is likely to use: ✅ the policy package adopted across Latin America in this period is conventionally called the **Washington Consensus**, a phrase coined by the economist John Williamson in 1989 to summarise the reform prescriptions then favoured by Washington-based institutions - fiscal discipline, redirected public spending, tax reform, market-determined interest and exchange rates, trade liberalisation, openness to foreign direct investment, privatisation, deregulation and secure property rights.

⚠️ **How to handle the two descriptions.** ⚠️ Do not adopt either as your own voice. ⚠️ Write: "the programme comprised fiscal discipline, trade opening, privatisation and deregulation; the core account judges it to have served a wealthy elite at home together with foreign investors and banks; its recorded results were X and Y." ⚠️ That structure is defensible whichever way the examiner leans, and it is more informative than either slogan.

### 2. The politics of adoption: chosen or imposed?

⚠️ This is the question that makes the topic examinable rather than technical, and the evidence points in one direction without being unanimous.

⚠️ **The imposition evidence.** ✅ The countries had been **obliged** to follow these policies. ✅ Mexico's programme followed a failed IMF agreement and a rescheduling. ✅ Venezuela was trying to cope by following **IMF requirements**, reducing imports and government spending. ✅ Brazil's Lula went along with IMF conditions, reducing public spending in return for the credit needed to stabilise the currency. ⚠️ In each case the policy is attached to a financing agreement.

⚠️ **The choice evidence.** ✅ Menem, elected in 1991, resorted to classic neo-liberal policies. ✅ Cardoso produced the *Plano Real* and was re-elected in 1998 having implemented it. ✅ Mexico's governing party pursued privatisation and joined the free trade area. ⚠️ These were decisions of elected governments, some of which were then endorsed at the polls.

⚠️ **The resolution is the most important sentence in this lesson.** ⚠️ The choice was real but the **option set** was not freely composed. ⚠️ A government facing compounding debt, collapsing revenue and a creditor who controls access to new credit chooses from a menu it did not write. ⚠️ ✅ Lula's case proves it exactly: though a socialist, he felt that the economic crisis was serious enough to require non-socialist solutions, and he went along with the IMF conditions in return for the credit needed to stabilise the currency. ⚠️ A newly elected left-wing president adopting his opponents' programme because the alternative was currency collapse is the clearest demonstration available that the constraint was real.

⚠️ **And that is where the political damage originated.** ⚠️ If the electorate can change the government but cannot change the programme, then the programme is not answerable to the electorate. ⚠️ ✅ The account records the sentiment directly: in 2002 the voters of Brazil were sick of corrupt party politics and neo-liberal economic policies; ✅ in 2006 there were huge protest demonstrations in Ecuador against a proposed free-trade agreement with the USA, widely seen as a ploy to tighten United States control over the economy.

### 3. What the programme achieved, stated fairly

⚠️ An answer that denies the achievements will be marked down, because they are on the record and they were substantial.

✅ **Inflation.** Brazil: from a thousand per cent when the *Plano Real* was first put into action to single figures by 1997. ✅ Mexico: inflation down to single figures and the budget deficit eliminated. ✅ Chile: from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980.

⚠️ That record deserves to be taken seriously rather than dismissed, and the reason is distributive. ⚠️ Hyperinflation is not a technical inconvenience; it is a confiscation of the assets of everyone who holds money and of the income of everyone on a fixed wage. ⚠️ Since the poor hold their small savings in cash and earn fixed wages, **inflation is the most regressive tax available**, and ending it is a genuine gain to exactly the people the programme is accused of harming.

✅ **Trade and investment.** Mexican exports to the USA increased and the country was opened to United States and Canadian investment. ✅ Brazil saw overseas markets revive with marked increases in exports of agricultural produce and manufactured goods.

✅ **Fiscal stabilisation.** The Mexican budget deficit was eliminated.

### 4. What it did not achieve, stated equally fairly
