✅ **Unemployment.** Mexican privatisation did nothing to solve the unemployment problem.

✅ **Poverty.** By 2000, underneath the outward appearance of prosperity, about a third of Mexico's population still lived below the poverty line. ✅ In Brazil there was considerable unrest among the working classes, many of whom were poverty-stricken, and an increase in crime and violence.

✅ **Agriculture under trade opening.** Mexican farmers suffered because imports of United States agricultural produce, especially meat, increased substantially, and farmers staged widespread protests because the government did nothing to solve agricultural problems caused by membership of the free trade area.

✅ **Durability.** Argentina's programme is summarised in four words: ✅ "It was all to no avail." ⚠️ Ten years of orthodox policy under Menem and de la Rua ended in the worst recession for years from September 1998, two IMF rescues in 2001, a financial panic, December rioting and a president's resignation.

✅ **Sufficiency.** Chile's post-transition governments concluded that the classic policies were not sufficient to bring full recovery, and broke the rules with a dose of state intervention - deploying the revenues of Codelco, the world's largest copper producer and a state-owned corporation since 1976, and introducing government control of capital to finance new social policies.

⚠️ That Chilean reversal is the single most useful piece of evidence in the lesson, and here is why. ⚠️ Chile was the region's longest-running and most thoroughgoing application of the programme, and it was **its own beneficiaries and successors** who judged it insufficient and departed from it. ⚠️ That is not an ideological objection from outside; it is a practical verdict from inside, and it is the strongest available answer to any claim that the programme simply worked.

### 5. Why success against inflation did not buy consent

⚠️ Three reasons, and stating them converts a list of outcomes into an explanation.

⚠️ **The benefits were diffuse and the costs were concentrated.** ⚠️ Lower inflation helps everyone a little and invisibly. ⚠️ A privatised enterprise's redundancies, a farmer ruined by imported meat, and a frozen public-sector wage are felt by identifiable people who know exactly what caused them. ⚠️ Politics responds to concentrated losses more strongly than to diffuse gains.

⚠️ **The authorship was external.** ⚠️ ✅ Policies followed under IMF requirements, adopted in return for credit, and applied by a left-wing president because the alternative was collapse, cannot be repudiated at an election. ⚠️ A policy that survives a change of government is, from the voter's point of view, not a policy but a condition - and the response to a condition is not a different vote but a rejection of the system that produced it.

⚠️ **The sequence of who paid.** ⚠️ Return to Lesson 13. ⚠️ The military governments contracted the debts; the democracies repaid them. ⚠️ ✅ Brazil's dictatorship borrowed extensively and enjoyed 10 per cent growth; ✅ the civilian Sarney suspended interest payments, Collor froze the savings and caused massive unemployment, and Cardoso raised taxes and cut public-service wages. ⚠️ Democracy arrived and brought austerity with it, and that conjunction is the deepest political wound of the period.

### 6. The trade-agreement question, handled with the source's own balance

⚠️ Regional and hemispheric trade agreements are the most contested element and the account is deliberately even-handed, so an answer should be too.

✅ **For:** Mexican exports to the USA increased; the country was opened to United States and Canadian investment; tariffs were removed on more than half of Mexican exports to the USA and about a third of United States exports to Mexico; ✅ opinions differ on whether the agreement has been beneficial.

✅ **Against:** Mexican farmers suffered from substantially increased imports of American agricultural produce, especially meat, and staged widespread protests; ✅ the Zapatista rising began on the day the agreement came into force, in a province where most of the population were landless Mayan Indians; ✅ Lula opposed a hemispheric free trade area and got away with it, probably because the USA was preoccupied with the Iraq War; ✅ Ecuador saw huge demonstrations in 2006 against a proposed agreement widely seen as a ploy to tighten United States control; ✅ Morales in Bolivia refused to have anything to do with the hemispheric free trade area, describing it as "an agreement to legalize the colonization of the Americas".

⚠️ ✅ And the account records that the agreements were also used as an instrument of division: the USA did its best to divide Mexico, Chile and Guatemala from the rest by making separate trade agreements with them, so that relations between the four states were reasonably cordial. ⚠️ That is bilateralism as a political technique, and it belongs in any answer on regional integration.

### Criticism and reply: is the account's characterisation fair?

⚠️ **The objection.** Describing the framework as designed to benefit a wealthy elite, foreign investors, big business and bankers is a judgement of motive, and policies can be judged only by their results.

⚠️ **The reply.** The results are recorded and they are mixed, so the judgement is not self-evident from them. ⚠️ But three findings give it support. ✅ Inflation was defeated while unemployment and poverty were not, so the benefit fell where creditors and asset-holders needed it and the cost fell on wage-earners. ✅ The programme was in significant part a condition of credit rather than a domestic choice, which is a statement about whose interests the framework was answerable to. ✅ And the region's most thoroughgoing practitioner departed from it because it was not sufficient.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ The programme was a genuine technical response to a genuine emergency and it achieved real stabilisation; ⚠️ it was also externally conditioned, distributively regressive in its incidence, and insufficient for recovery on the testimony of the governments that applied it longest. ⚠️ ✅ That is why the region's electorates, having received stabilisation, voted from 1998 onwards for governments promising something else.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson completes the economic arc and connects directly to the effects of capitalism on society, which is a syllabus clause.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "economic strategy since 1945" or a question about liberalisation and its social consequences. ⚠️ The distinctive move available here is to explain why a programme with measurable successes was still rejected at the polls.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Denying the achievements. ✅ Brazilian inflation fell from a thousand per cent to single figures by 1997.
2. ⚠️ Presenting adoption as wholly imposed or wholly chosen. ✅ Lula, a socialist, judged the crisis serious enough to require non-socialist solutions and accepted IMF conditions.
3. ⚠️ Declaring a verdict on the free trade agreement. ✅ The account states benefits and harms and says opinions differ.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the Chilean reversal. ✅ Classic policies were judged not sufficient and the rules were broken with a dose of state intervention including the deployment of state copper revenues and new capital controls; ❌ Codelco was not taken into state hands then, having been state-owned since 1976.
5. ⚠️ Importing India's liberalisation content. ⚠️ That belongs to the economy owner; the comparison may be signposted but its material is not taken.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The framework as recorded: privatisation, deregulation of finance, cuts in social spending and austerity, described as designed to benefit a wealthy elite at home, foreign investors, big business and bankers, particularly in the USA.
2. ✅ The package is conventionally called the Washington Consensus, a term coined by John Williamson in 1989 covering fiscal discipline, tax reform, market-determined interest and exchange rates, trade liberalisation, openness to foreign direct investment, privatisation, deregulation and secure property rights.
3. ✅ Mexico: privatisation brought inflation to single figures and eliminated the budget deficit but did nothing for unemployment; the free trade agreement came into force in January 1994, removing tariffs on more than half of Mexican exports to the USA and about a third of United States exports to Mexico; exports and investment rose while farmers suffered from increased American agricultural imports; opinions differ on the net effect; about a third of Mexicans were still below the poverty line in 2000.
4. ✅ Argentina: Menem from 1991 removed protectionist trade and business regulations, introduced strict austerity and privatised state industries; it was all to no avail; the worst recession for years came in September 1998; de la Rua added more austerity; the IMF rescued the country twice in 2001; November brought near-collapse and financial panic and December rioting in Buenos Aires forced the president's resignation.
5. ✅ Brazil: Cardoso's *Plano Real* from 1994 involved large tax increases, wage reductions for public-service workers and privatisation of many government enterprises; inflation fell from a thousand per cent to single figures by 1997; markets revived with marked increases in agricultural and manufactured exports; Cardoso was re-elected in 1998; a renewed crisis followed as Russia and south-east Asia cut imports, with spending and borrowing still too high, inflation rising again, massive IMF credits required, unrest among the poverty-stricken working classes and rising crime and violence.
6. ✅ Chile: inflation down from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980 under the dictatorship; after the transition the classic policies proved not sufficient for full recovery, and the government broke the rules with a dose of state intervention, deploying the revenues of the state-owned copper corporation Codelco and introducing capital controls to finance new social policies.
7. ✅ Lula, though a socialist, felt the economic crisis was serious enough to require non-socialist solutions and went along with IMF conditions, reducing public spending in return for the credit needed to stabilise the currency, while introducing widespread anti-poverty programmes and raising the minimum wage by 25 per cent.
8. ✅ Opposition to trade agreements: Lula opposed a hemispheric free trade area and got away with it, probably because the USA was preoccupied with the Iraq War; Ecuador saw huge demonstrations in 2006 against a proposed agreement seen as a ploy to tighten United States control; Morales called the hemispheric area an agreement to legalize the colonization of the Americas; the USA made separate agreements with Mexico, Chile and Guatemala to divide them from the rest.
9. ⚠️ The consistent profile: effective against inflation, ineffective against unemployment and poverty - which is why administrators judged it a success and voters judged it a failure.
10. ⚠️ Three reasons consent was not bought: diffuse benefits against concentrated costs; external authorship that no election could repudiate; and the sequence by which dictatorships borrowed and democracies repaid.
11. ⚠️ Master formulation: the turn was a real technical response to a real emergency that achieved stabilisation, was externally conditioned and regressive in incidence, and was judged insufficient by the governments that applied it longest.

### Concept check

**Question:** The reforms of the 1990s brought Brazilian inflation down from a thousand per cent to single figures by 1997 - a change that benefits the poor more than anyone. Explain why Brazilian voters nevertheless elected a left-wing president in 2002 who campaigned against those policies.

**Model answer:** Because the programme's benefits and costs fell on different people in different ways, and because its authorship placed it beyond electoral reach. The benefit was real: hyperinflation is the most regressive tax available, falling on those who hold cash and earn fixed wages, so ending it helped the poor. But the benefit was diffuse and invisible, while the costs were concentrated and traceable - the *Plano Real* involved large tax increases, wage reductions for public-service workers and the privatisation of many government enterprises, and the record notes considerable unrest among poverty-stricken working classes and an increase in crime and violence. Nor did stabilisation resolve the underlying problems: a renewed crisis followed when Russia and south-east Asia cut their imports, spending and borrowing remained much too high, inflation rose again and massive IMF credits were needed. The second reason is authorship. The region had been obliged to follow these policies, Venezuela was acting under IMF requirements, and Lula himself, once elected, went along with IMF conditions and reduced public spending in return for the credit needed to stabilise the currency because, though a socialist, he judged the crisis serious enough to require non-socialist solutions. When a programme survives a change of government it ceases to be a policy and becomes a condition, and the record states plainly that in 2002 the voters of Brazil were sick of corrupt party politics and neo-liberal economic policies.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the rejection as proof that the programme failed on its own terms. It did not: inflation fell to single figures by 1997, overseas markets revived, exports of agricultural produce and manufactures rose markedly, and Cardoso was re-elected in 1998 having delivered it. The accurate judgement is the one the Chilean governments reached from the inside - that the classic policies were not sufficient to bring full recovery, which is why they broke the rules with a dose of state intervention, deploying the revenues of the state-owned copper corporation Codelco and introducing capital controls to finance new social policies. Insufficiency, not failure, is the charge the evidence supports.

## Lesson 17 - Society from below: church, labour, peasantry, indigenous peoples and women

Progress: 17/21 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: liberation theology and the political church; the priests killed and the Archbishop murdered; organised labour and the party it produced; peasant movements from Chiapas to the coca federations; the indigenous entry into national politics; conditional cash transfer as a social instrument; and the women who reached the presidency

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five social forces, what each demanded, and what it achieved

```text
  THE CHURCH  ------------------------------------------------------
    Instrument: liberation theology - "a style of theology which
      accepted many of Marx's theories (though not his atheism!)",
      stressing "the church's mission to the poor and oppressed,
      based on the fact that Jesus was considered as a sympathizer
      with, and a liberator of the poor and downtrodden"
    Reach: "Throughout Latin America there were large numbers of
      priests with left-wing views and some were even supporters
      of revolution"
    Cost: "many were arrested and some were killed"; in 1980
      Archbishop Oscar Romero of San Salvador was murdered
      by US-backed paramilitaries
    Achieved: a president of Haiti (Aristide, a former priest);
      support for the Sandinistas from a section of the Church;
      Church mediation of the Guatemalan constitutional reform, 1994

  LABOUR  ----------------------------------------------------------
    Created by populism (Lesson 6), banned by the national security
      state (Lesson 13 - "trade unionists ... a favourite target")
    Achieved: the Brazilian Workers' Party; Lula, president 2002-10
    Failure case: pre-revolutionary Cuba, where unions dominated by
      year-round sugar-mill workers "did nothing to help" the
      seasonally unemployed

  THE PEASANTRY  ---------------------------------------------------
    Chiapas, 1 Jan 1994: the Zapatista Army of National Liberation,
      named after a leader of the 1910 revolution, demanding land
      reform, full civil rights and genuine democracy
    Nicaragua 1979: 5 million acres to about 100,000 families
    Cuba pre-1959: rebels won support by carrying through land
      reform in the areas they held

  INDIGENOUS PEOPLES  ----------------------------------------------
    Guatemala: about 40% Mayan Indians, mainly landless; "well over
      half" of the 200,000 killed in 36 years
    Chiapas: "the majority of the population were poverty-stricken
      Mayan Indians who had no land of their own"
    Bolivia 2005: Evo Morales, an Aymara Indian, "the first Bolivian
      president to come from the country's ethnic majority"

  WOMEN  -----------------------------------------------------------
    1990 Chamorro (Nicaragua) - first female president elected in
      Latin America
    2006 Bachelet (Chile) - Chile's first woman president
    2011 Rousseff (Brazil) - Brazil's first woman president,
      "a strong advocate of human rights, social inclusion and
      equal treatment for women"
    Venezuela: "at least 50 per cent of election candidates had to
      be female"; Cuba: "more rights for women"
```

⚠️ The visual makes one thing visible that the country-by-country narratives conceal: **the same five forces recur across the region in different combinations**, and an answer organised around them can move between countries without becoming a list of national histories.

### 1. The church: the actor most answers omit

⚠️ Start with why this matters. ⚠️ In a region where parties were banned, unions suppressed, presses censored and universities purged, the Catholic Church was the one institution that could not be abolished. ⚠️ It had buildings in every village, a hierarchy independent of the state, an international protector and a moral vocabulary the regimes claimed to share. ⚠️ **When every other channel is closed, the surviving institution becomes political whether or not it wishes to be.**

✅ The account defines the doctrine precisely: liberation theology was a style of theology which accepted many of Marx's theories - though not his atheism - and which stressed the church's mission to the poor and oppressed, based on the fact that Jesus was considered as a sympathizer with, and a liberator of, the poor and downtrodden.

⚠️ Two elements of that definition matter for an answer. ⚠️ **The parenthesis is the whole point**: accepting a class analysis while rejecting materialist atheism is what makes the doctrine possible and what makes it dangerous to the regimes, because it cannot be dismissed as foreign or godless. ⚠️ **The scriptural claim is a claim about interpretation**, not about politics: it asserts that the preferential concern for the poor is in the text, which is a much harder position to denounce than a political programme.

✅ The spread is recorded: throughout Latin America there were large numbers of priests with left-wing views and some were even supporters of revolution. ✅ Inevitably this brought them into conflict with the authorities; many were arrested and some were killed. ✅ In 1980 **Oscar Romero, Archbishop of San Salvador**, was murdered by United States-backed paramilitaries; the core account gives his rank as bishop and his see as El Salvador, and the corrected form is used here.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ⚠️ Report the killing as the account does in substance: an archbishop murdered in 1980 by paramilitaries described as United States-backed. ❌ Do not extend the attribution beyond what is stated, and ❌ do not supply a total for clergy killed, which the account does not give.

⚠️ The political reach is recorded in three places in this session, and assembling them is what makes the point substantial rather than decorative. ✅ **Haiti:** Jean-Bertrand Aristide, the democratically elected president removed in a military coup backed by the Central Intelligence Agency in 1991 after only eight months in office, was a committed Roman Catholic and a former priest strongly influenced by the ideals of liberation theology; he was removed a second time in a similar coup in 2004. ✅ **Nicaragua:** the Sandinistas had support from a section of the Roman Catholic Church which was highly critical of the excesses of capitalism. ✅ **Guatemala:** the Roman Catholic Church helped the government and Congress to agree the programme of constitutional reform that came into operation in August 1994, in the process that led to the peace agreement.

⚠️ Three different roles - a president, a revolutionary coalition partner and a mediator of a peace settlement - and one institution. ⚠️ That range is the reason the church belongs in an answer on twentieth-century Latin American politics rather than in a footnote about religion.

⚠️ And the church is not uniformly on one side, which an accurate answer should note. ✅ In 1957 Archbishop Blanco of Caracas publicly condemned the great wealth and corruption rife among Venezuela's leaders while the majority lived in poverty and often subhuman conditions - a hierarchy criticising a dictatorship. ✅ But by 2009 the Church and Human Rights Watch accused Chavez of creating a climate of fear, and he had alienated the Roman Catholic Church. ⚠️ The institution acted as a check on power from whichever direction power was exercised, which is a more interesting claim than partisanship.

### 2. Labour: created, suppressed, and finally governing

⚠️ Assemble the three moments already taught, because together they form an arc that is worth stating in a single sentence in an answer.

⚠️ **Creation.** ✅ Populist leaders won huge support from the poor working classes in the mass union movement, and where European fascist movements destroyed labour movements the Latin American leaders they inspired created them.

⚠️ **Suppression.** ✅ Under Brazil's military government trade unionists and left-wing students were a favourite target, with reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners; ✅ in Venezuela's dictatorship of 1948-58 political parties and trade unions were banned; ✅ Guatemala's reform period had permitted unions but restricted their powers.

⚠️ **Government.** ✅ In 2002 Brazil elected Luiz Inacio da Silva of the Workers' Party, who had promised to narrow the enormous gap between rich and poor by expanding education and redistributing land and to introduce social welfare programmes; ✅ he was re-elected in 2006 and remained in power until the end of 2010; ✅ his party continued in office with the election of Dilma Rousseff.

⚠️ State the arc: **a labour movement brought into existence by an authoritarian populist in the 1930s and 1940s, targeted by an authoritarian military regime in the 1960s and 1970s, produced a party that won the presidency democratically in 2002 and held it for more than a decade.** ⚠️ That sentence answers a question about the political effects of industrialisation better than any amount of general description.

⚠️ And keep the negative case, because it prevents the argument from becoming sentimental. ✅ In pre-revolutionary Cuba the trade unions, dominated by workers who had all-the-year-round jobs in sugar mills, did nothing to help the seasonally unemployed. ⚠️ Organised labour represents those it organises; where the labour market is segmented, a union can become an interest group defending the insiders against the outsiders. ⚠️ That, as Lesson 9 showed, left the excluded majority with no institutional voice and helped make revolution the available route.

### 3. The peasantry and the land question that would not close

⚠️ The through-line of this whole session is land, and the peasant movements are where it becomes visible as agency rather than as a structural condition.

✅ **Cuba before 1959:** the rebels controlled the mountainous areas of the east and north and won popular support there by carrying through Castro's land reform policy.

✅ **Nicaragua 1979:** a redistribution of 5 million acres, including some confiscated Somoza property, to about 100,000 families.

✅ **Guatemala:** the reforms confiscated foreign-owned estates and redistributed land to peasants, and the reversal of that programme opened thirty-six years of war whose victims were overwhelmingly the rural indigenous population.

✅ **Chile:** over 1,200 inefficiently run private holdings confiscated and given to landless peasants under Frei, with the redistribution speeded up under Allende.

✅ **Mexico, 1994:** the Zapatista Army of National Liberation rose in Chiapas, one of the most deprived parts of Mexico, where the majority of the population were poverty-stricken Mayan Indians who had no land of their own and were angered by the blatant corruption and incompetence of the ruling elite; they demanded land reform, full civil rights and genuine democracy, occupied several towns, were crushed within days with heavy casualties, and then abandoned violence and concentrated on an internet campaign that brought them widespread publicity and growing support.

⚠️ Two observations on the Zapatista case, which is the most instructive.

⚠️ **The date and the name together are the argument.** ⚠️ Rising on the day a free trade agreement came into force, under the name of a leader of the 1910 revolution, states in a single gesture that the country was integrating into the global economy while the agrarian demand of its own revolution remained unmet in its poorest province.

⚠️ **The change of method is historically significant.** ✅ Crushed militarily within days, they abandoned violence and concentrated on an internet campaign that brought widespread publicity and growing support. ⚠️ That is a peasant movement defeated in the field and successful in the information space, and it marks a genuine shift in how marginal groups could contest a national government at the end of the century.

### 4. Indigenous peoples: from victims to a presidency

⚠️ This strand runs through the whole session and should be assembled rather than left scattered.

✅ **Guatemala:** about 40 per cent of the population were Mayan Indians who did not speak Spanish and who consisted mainly of landless peasants; ✅ of the some 200,000 people killed in the 36-year civil war, well over half were Mayan Indians, especially targeted because of their militancy.

✅ **Mexico:** the majority of the population of Chiapas were poverty-stricken Mayan Indians with no land of their own.

✅ **Bolivia:** in December 2005 the candidate of the Movement Towards Socialism, Juan Evo Morales, was elected president; an Aymara Indian, he was the first Bolivian president to come from the country's ethnic majority.

⚠️ Stop on that last phrase, because it contains the entire indigenous history of the region in four words. ⚠️ **The ethnic majority** had never previously supplied a president. ⚠️ Five centuries after the conquest and 180 years after independence, the largest population group in the country reached its highest office for the first time in 2005. ⚠️ No statistic about inequality makes the point as economically.

⚠️ And the route by which he arrived is itself the lesson in how a marginal population becomes a political force, so it is worth following. ✅ Coca had been an important crop in Bolivia for around 4,000 years, and the writer Nikolas Kozloff records that the leaf is legally used as an infusion to make tea, is chewed with a bitter wood-ash paste to bring out stimulant properties similar to caffeine or nicotine, is commonly offered to visitors as tea to combat altitude sickness, and is used in cosmetic products and toothpaste - while outside the region it is classified as a prohibited drug, and converting it into cocaine requires other ingredients and a complex chemical process.

✅ President Hugo Banzer, elected in 1997, made important progress in eliminating coca production and drug trafficking, much to the delight of the USA. ✅ The near-eradication of coca farming by 2001 was extremely controversial and was to have profound effects on Bolivia's future: thousands of coca farmers became unemployed and were plunged into poverty. ✅ The coca growers' union joined with other trade unions and social interest groups to form the Movement Towards Socialism, which campaigned on a platform of decriminalizing coca and nationalizing the country's natural resources; ✅ the call for nationalisation was a response to the unpopular privatization of water resources by foreign companies, which had led to a doubling of water prices.

⚠️ Trace that chain, because it is a complete causal sequence and examiners reward complete sequences. ⚠️ A foreign-policy objective - drug eradication - is pursued by a compliant national government; ⚠️ it destroys the livelihood of a traditional indigenous crop-growing population; ⚠️ the dispossessed organise as a union; ⚠️ the union allies with other unions and social groups harmed by a different externally-favoured policy, the privatisation of water; ⚠️ the alliance becomes a party; ⚠️ the party wins the presidency four years later. ⚠️ **External policy produced the social coalition that displaced the government which had implemented it.**

✅ Morales pressed the argument internationally: in September 2006 he told the United Nations General Assembly that coca had therapeutic uses and should not be criminalized; while agreeing that it was necessary to fight drug smuggling, he insisted that prohibition of the pure coca leaf was "an historic injustice ... coca does not harm human health", and added that criminalizing coca was simply a strategy by the USA and Europe to recolonize the Andes region. ✅ He signed trade agreements with Venezuela, refused to have anything to do with the United States-sponsored hemispheric free trade area, which he described as "an agreement to legalize the colonization of the Americas", suggested that Bolivia, Venezuela and Cuba might form "an axis of good" in contrast to the "axis of evil", signed new contracts with private gas companies designed to bring in more revenue and partially nationalised the hydrocarbons industry.

✅ The economic assessment is attributed: according to Noam Chomsky, since the election of Morales in 2005 Bolivia's economic performance had been quite impressive, and a Center for Economic and Policy Research study found that in the four years since he took office economic growth had been higher than at any time during the last 30 years, averaging 4.9 per cent annually, with projected growth for 2009 the highest in the hemisphere following the peak rate of 2008, alongside several programmes targeted at the poorest Bolivians. ✅ Morales was re-elected for a second term in 2009 with an increased majority, and his party won a two-thirds majority in both houses of Congress.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ⚠️ The growth figures are a study's findings reported by a named author. ❌ Do not restate them as unowned facts.

### 5. Social policy as an instrument: the conditional transfer

✅ Lula introduced widespread anti-poverty programmes and increased the minimum wage by 25 per cent. ✅ His *Bolsa Familia* programme paid modest monthly grants to poor families provided they sent their children to school and had their health checked regularly; ✅ it was estimated that by 2008 *Bolsa Familia* had helped some 7.5 million families.

⚠️ Notice the design, because it is a genuine policy innovation and not merely charity. ⚠️ The grant is **conditional on behaviour that builds human capital** - school attendance and health checks - so the same transfer relieves poverty today and reduces its transmission to the next generation. ⚠️ It is cheap to administer, hard to capture by intermediaries and politically durable because the recipients are identifiable.

⚠️ And note where it sits in the argument of this session. ⚠️ It is a redistribution that does **not** require confronting the landowners, does not require nationalising foreign assets and does not require abandoning the macroeconomic framework. ⚠️ ✅ Lula ran it while going along with IMF conditions. ⚠️ **It is the post-1998 left's answer to the dilemma that destroyed every earlier attempt at redistribution in this session** - and whether that is an ingenious solution or an evasion of the land question is exactly the kind of judgement a 20-mark answer should make explicitly.

✅ The result recorded for Brazil: the surge in exports, the fall in unemployment and the general economic expansion, together with the welfare programmes, helped to lift millions of people out of poverty, so that for the first time probably a majority of the population of over 190 million could be deemed middle-class.

### 6. Women: the evidence, gathered

⚠️ The gender dimension can be answered from evidence in this session rather than asserted, and the items should be assembled rather than mentioned singly.

✅ **Office.** Violeta Barrios de Chamorro, elected in Nicaragua in 1990, was the first female president to be elected in Latin America; ✅ Michelle Bachelet, president of Chile from 2006, was Chile's first woman president, a former paediatrician whose father had been a victim of the Pinochet regime; ✅ Cristina Fernandez de Kirchner was elected in Argentina in 2007 and re-elected in October 2011 by the largest margin since democracy had been restored in 1983; ✅ Dilma Rousseff became Brazil's first woman president in January 2011, a strong advocate of human rights, social inclusion and equal treatment for women, who had served as a minister in the previous cabinet.

✅ **Representation as policy.** In Venezuela great progress was made in securing equal rights for women, including a new rule for political parties that at least 50 per cent of election candidates had to be female.

✅ **Social rights.** Cuba's reforms included more rights for women alongside equality for black people.

⚠️ Two analytical observations that convert the list into an argument.

⚠️ **The timing is regional, not accidental.** ⚠️ Four women presidents between 1990 and 2011, in four different countries, across the political spectrum - one a conservative opposition candidate, one a socialist, one a Peronist and one from a workers' party. ⚠️ Women reached the highest office across the region in the two decades after the transitions from military rule, and the coincidence is unlikely to be coincidental: ⚠️ the national security state was the most explicitly masculine political form the region produced, and its disappearance opened the field.

⚠️ **Two of the four arrived through the human-rights struggle.** ⚠️ Bachelet's father was a victim of the regime; Chamorro's route lay through the opposition to a dynasty. ⚠️ The transitional-justice politics of Lesson 18 was not only about the past; it was also a pathway into national leadership.

### Criticism and reply: is this an optimistic reading?

⚠️ **The objection.** Assembling a church that mediates peace, a union movement that wins the presidency, a peasant movement that adapts, an indigenous president and four women heads of state produces a narrative of progress that the region's poverty figures do not support.

⚠️ **The reply, with the counter-evidence.** ✅ Nicaragua in 2011 was still the poorest country in Latin America with 46 per cent below the poverty line; ✅ about a third of Mexicans were below the poverty line in 2000; ✅ in Mexico, huge shanty-towns ringed the cities and rural areas were often neglected; ✅ Archbishop Romero was murdered and many priests were arrested and killed; ✅ the Zapatistas were crushed within days with heavy casualties; ✅ Guatemala's indigenous population supplied well over half of the 200,000 killed in its civil war.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ These movements changed who governs far more than they changed how wealth is distributed. ⚠️ Access to power was genuinely and measurably widened - by ethnicity, by class origin and by gender. ⚠️ The distribution of land and income changed far less, and an answer that reports the first without the second has substituted representation for redistribution.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson supplies the social-movement, religious, indigenous and gender dimensions, which any modern General Studies answer on a world-history topic is expected to touch, and it does so from evidence rather than from assertion.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect it as a component of a broader question rather than alone: the social consequences of a political philosophy, or the role of non-state actors, or inclusive development.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the Church as uniformly conservative. ✅ Liberation theology accepted many of Marx's theories though not his atheism, and an archbishop was murdered in 1980.
2. ⚠️ Assuming unions always represent the poor. ✅ Cuba's unions, dominated by permanent sugar-mill workers, did nothing for the seasonally unemployed.
3. ⚠️ Treating coca as simply a drug issue. ✅ It had been an important crop for around 4,000 years with recorded legal uses, and its eradication created the coalition that won the presidency.
4. ⚠️ Mentioning women presidents without dating or contextualising them. ✅ Chamorro 1990, Bachelet 2006, Fernandez 2007, Rousseff 2011, plus the Venezuelan 50 per cent candidate rule.
5. ⚠️ Presenting inclusion as equivalent to redistribution. ✅ Nicaragua was still the poorest country in the hemisphere with 46 per cent below the poverty line in 2011.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Liberation theology: a style of theology accepting many of Marx's theories though not his atheism, stressing the church's mission to the poor and oppressed on the basis that Jesus was a sympathiser with and liberator of the poor and downtrodden.
2. ✅ Throughout Latin America large numbers of priests held left-wing views and some supported revolution; many were arrested and some killed; in 1980 Archbishop Oscar Romero of San Salvador was murdered by United States-backed paramilitaries.
3. ✅ Aristide, the democratically elected president of Haiti removed in a Central Intelligence Agency-backed military coup in 1991 after eight months and again in 2004, was a committed Roman Catholic and former priest strongly influenced by liberation theology.
4. ✅ A section of the Roman Catholic Church highly critical of the excesses of capitalism supported the Sandinistas; the Church helped Guatemala's government and Congress agree the constitutional reform that came into force in August 1994.
5. ✅ Archbishop Blanco of Caracas publicly condemned Venezuela's elite wealth and corruption in 1957; in 2009 the Church and Human Rights Watch accused Chavez of creating a climate of fear.
6. ✅ Labour's arc: created by populist leaders who won huge support in the mass union movement; targeted under military rule, with trade unionists a favourite target in Brazil and unions banned in Venezuela's dictatorship; producing the Workers' Party whose candidate won the Brazilian presidency in 2002, was re-elected in 2006 and was succeeded by his party's candidate in 2011.
7. ✅ Counter-case: Cuban unions dominated by year-round sugar-mill workers did nothing to help the seasonally unemployed.
8. ✅ Peasant movements: Cuban rebels carrying through land reform in the areas they held; 5 million acres to about 100,000 families in Nicaragua in 1979; over 1,200 holdings redistributed under Frei in Chile and the process speeded up under Allende; the Zapatista rising in Chiapas on 1 January 1994 demanding land reform, full civil rights and genuine democracy, crushed within days with heavy casualties and then shifting to an internet campaign.
9. ✅ Indigenous peoples: about 40 per cent of Guatemalans were Mayan Indians who did not speak Spanish, mainly landless, and well over half of the 200,000 killed in 36 years; the majority in Chiapas were poverty-stricken Mayan Indians with no land; Evo Morales, an Aymara Indian elected in December 2005, was the first Bolivian president to come from the country's ethnic majority.
10. ✅ The coca chain: coca an important Bolivian crop for around 4,000 years with legal uses recorded by Nikolas Kozloff; near-eradication by 2001 under Banzer, welcomed by the USA, left thousands of farmers unemployed and impoverished; the coca growers' union joined other unions and social groups to form the Movement Towards Socialism, campaigning to decriminalise coca and nationalise natural resources in response to a water privatisation that had doubled prices.
11. ✅ Morales at the United Nations in September 2006 called prohibition of the pure coca leaf an historic injustice and criminalisation a strategy to recolonize the Andes; he rejected the hemispheric free trade area as an agreement to legalize the colonization of the Americas, signed trade agreements with Venezuela and partially nationalised hydrocarbons; Chomsky reports a Center for Economic and Policy Research finding of growth averaging 4.9 per cent annually in his first four years; he was re-elected in 2009 with a two-thirds congressional majority.
12. ✅ *Bolsa Familia* paid modest monthly grants to poor families conditional on school attendance and regular health checks, estimated to have helped some 7.5 million families by 2008; Lula also raised the minimum wage by 25 per cent; exports, falling unemployment, expansion and welfare together lifted millions from poverty so that probably a majority of over 190 million could be deemed middle-class for the first time.
13. ✅ Women: Chamorro in Nicaragua in 1990 was the first female president elected in Latin America; Bachelet was Chile's first woman president from 2006; Fernandez de Kirchner was elected in Argentina in 2007 and re-elected in October 2011 by the largest margin since 1983; Rousseff became Brazil's first woman president in January 2011, a strong advocate of human rights, social inclusion and equal treatment for women; Venezuela required at least 50 per cent female election candidates; Cuba's reforms included more rights for women and equality for black people.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: these movements widened access to power by class origin, ethnicity and gender far more than they altered the distribution of land and income, and an answer that reports the first without the second has substituted representation for redistribution.

### Concept check

**Question:** Trace the sequence by which an externally favoured drug-eradication policy produced an indigenous presidency in Bolivia, and state what the sequence demonstrates about the relationship between external influence and domestic politics.

**Model answer:** The sequence has six steps. Coca had been an important Bolivian crop for around 4,000 years with recorded legal uses - as tea, as a stimulant leaf chewed with wood ash, as a remedy for altitude sickness and in cosmetics and toothpaste - while outside the region it is classified as a prohibited drug requiring complex chemical processing to become cocaine. President Banzer, elected in 1997, made important progress in eliminating coca production and drug trafficking, much to the delight of the United States. The near-eradication of coca farming by 2001 left thousands of farmers unemployed and plunged them into poverty. The coca growers' union then joined with other trade unions and social interest groups to form the Movement Towards Socialism, campaigning to decriminalise coca and to nationalise natural resources - the latter a response to an unpopular privatisation of water by foreign companies that had doubled prices. In December 2005 that movement's candidate, Evo Morales, an Aymara Indian, was elected president, the first Bolivian president to come from the country's ethnic majority. He then refused the hemispheric free trade area as an agreement to legalize the colonization of the Americas, told the United Nations that criminalising coca was a strategy to recolonize the Andes, signed agreements with Venezuela and partially nationalised hydrocarbons. What the sequence demonstrates is that external influence operates on societies rather than on governments, and that its social casualties can organise: a policy pursued to please Washington destroyed a livelihood, the dispossessed built a union, the union built a coalition with the victims of a second externally favoured policy, and the coalition captured the state that had implemented both.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading this as a story of national liberation completed. Access to power widened dramatically, but distribution changed far less: Nicaragua in 2011 remained the poorest country in Latin America with 46 per cent below the poverty line, about a third of Mexicans were below the poverty line in 2000, and Guatemala's Mayan population supplied well over half of the some 200,000 killed in its 36-year war. An answer that lists an indigenous president, four women heads of state and a workers' party in government without the poverty figures has reported the widening of representation as though it were the redistribution of wealth.

---

## Lesson 18 - Human rights, memory and transition: the examinable afterlife of dictatorship

Progress: 18/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the shape of Latin American transitions; the recorded charges and figures and the discipline of attribution; commissions, amnesties and their reversal; the London arrest of 1998 and universal jurisdiction; the domestic prosecutions that followed; peace agreements and partial amnesties; the reasons accountability was slow; and why this field remains contested

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's records of the Argentine junta's charges and the cancellation of the pardon laws, the Chilean casualty figure and the London arrest, the Brazilian repression, the Guatemalan peace agreement with its partial amnesty, and the Church and Human Rights Watch statements on Venezuela
CA search: "Argentina transitional justice truth memory non-repetition official statement 2026"
CA found: Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights press release, 19 March 2026 - United Nations human rights experts warn of alarming setbacks in Argentina's transitional justice process on the 50th anniversary of the coup d'etat
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the transitional-justice sequence, and where each country stood on it

```text
  STEP 1  THE FACTS ARE ESTABLISHED
          Argentina: "A human rights commission charged the junta
            with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political
            arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people"
          Guatemala: some 200,000 killed in 36 years, well over half
            Mayan Indians
          Chile: around 3,000 killed or "disappeared" in 17 years;
            tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled
                        |
  STEP 2  IMMUNITY IS BUILT INTO THE EXIT
          Chile: the 1980 constitution (in force 11 Mar 1981)
            let Pinochet remain
            Commander-in-Chief for eight years after leaving office
          Argentina: laws granting pardon to those accused of
            atrocities during the "dirty war"
          Guatemala: the December 1996 agreement granted "a partial
            amnesty to the various participants in the violence"
                        |
  STEP 3  THE IMMUNITY IS BREACHED - FROM OUTSIDE FIRST
          1998: Pinochet, visiting London, is arrested and held for
            16 months on a Spanish extradition request over the
            torture of Spanish citizens in Chile
                        |
  STEP 4  ... THEN FROM INSIDE
          Chile 2000-01: over 250 charges; the Court of Appeal rules
            him too ill to stand trial (July 2001); he dies in 2006
          Argentina 2006: the pardon laws cancelled, so "many
            military and police personnel who thought they were safe
            were arrested and put on trial"
                        |
  STEP 5  THE FIELD REMAINS CONTESTED
          the evidence base, the figures, the amnesties and the
          prosecutions all continue to be argued over
```

⚠️ The sequence explains why accountability in this region was so slow and so partial: **immunity was not an oversight but a term of the transition**, and breaching it required either an external jurisdiction or a subsequent domestic majority willing to reopen a settled bargain.

### 1. Why transitional justice is a historical topic and not a legal aside

⚠️ It is tempting to treat the trials and commissions as a postscript. ⚠️ They are not, for three reasons that an answer should state.

⚠️ **They establish the evidentiary record on which every historical claim about these regimes rests.** ⚠️ The figures used throughout this session come from commissions and courts; without them there would be no attributable numbers at all, only assertion.

⚠️ **They are themselves political contests with outcomes.** ⚠️ An amnesty upheld and an amnesty cancelled produce different countries, and both happened in Argentina within twenty-five years.

⚠️ **They test a general proposition about the international order**, namely whether the prohibitions asserted after 1945 can reach a former head of state. ⚠️ Latin America supplied the case that answered it.

### 2. The record, and the attribution discipline restated

⚠️ Gather the evidence in one place, with its sources attached, and keep the counts separate.

✅ **Argentina, 1976-83.** A human rights commission charged the junta with **2,300 political murders**, **over 10,000 political arrests** and the **disappearance of up to 30,000 people**, in what became known as the dirty war to restore order and eliminate opponents. ✅ The body is the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons, whose report was published in 1984 as *Nunca Mas*.

✅ **Chile, 1973-90.** During Pinochet's 17 years as president, **around 3,000 people were killed or "disappeared"**, while **tens of thousands were tortured, imprisoned or driven into exile**. ✅ The official Chilean inquiries into these events are the National Commission for Truth and Reconciliation, whose report was published in 1991, and the later National Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture, which addressed the survivors of torture.

✅ **Guatemala, 1960-96.** **Some 200,000 people** killed during those 36 years, **well over half of whom were Mayan Indians**, especially targeted because of their militancy; and, within that, **3,252 political murders** calculated in the four months from October 1979 to January 1980 under a named president.

✅ **Brazil, 1964-85.** The old political parties banned, strict press censorship, jails filled with political prisoners, trade unionists and left-wing students a favourite target, and **reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners** - with no figure stated.

✅ **Mexico, 1968.** Troops fired on an estimated 10,000 students; **estimates of the numbers killed vary between 30 and 300**.

✅ **Panama, 1989.** **At least 2,000 civilians** killed when United States forces invaded and bombed.

⚠️ **The discipline, restated because this is where it matters most.** ❌ Do not add these figures together. ❌ Do not convert disappearances into deaths. ❌ Do not round "up to 30,000" or "some 200,000". ❌ Do not supply a Brazilian number. ❌ Do not collapse the Mexican range into a single value. ⚠️ Each figure belongs to a named body or account, a named country and a stated period, and the value of using them correctly is precisely that most answers do not.

### 3. Immunity as a term of the transition

⚠️ Return to Lesson 13's finding that these regimes were not defeated but left. ⚠️ A regime that negotiates its own exit will write protection into the terms, and all three major cases show it.

✅ **Chile.** Pinochet permitted the winner of the 1989 election to become president in 1990, but the constitution approved in 1980 and in force from 11 March 1981 allowed him to remain Commander-in-Chief of the Armed Forces for a further eight years. ⚠️ That is not a symbolic retention. ⚠️ A former ruler who commands the army while the civilian government investigates his regime is an effective veto on investigation.

✅ **Argentina.** Laws granting pardon to those accused of atrocities during the dirty war were in force, ✅ and were later cancelled.

✅ **Guatemala.** The December 1996 formal peace agreement legalized the guerrilla organisation and granted **a partial amnesty to the various participants in the violence**.

⚠️ Note the symmetry of the Guatemalan formula and the genuine dilemma it represents, because a good answer will present it as a dilemma rather than as a scandal. ⚠️ A partial amnesty covering **all** participants was the price of ending a 36-year war. ⚠️ It bought a ceasefire from 20 March 1996 and a formal settlement in December; ⚠️ it also meant that most of those responsible for some 200,000 deaths faced no proceedings. ⚠️ **Peace and justice were in direct competition, and Guatemala chose peace.** ⚠️ Whether that was right is exactly the sort of judgement a 20-mark question invites, and the honest answer acknowledges the cost on both sides.

### 4. The breach: London, 1998

✅ Pinochet duly stepped down in 1998, but his retirement did not work out as he had planned. ✅ On a visit to London later that year, he was arrested and held in Britain for 16 months after the Spanish government requested his extradition to face charges of torturing Spanish citizens in Chile. ✅ He was eventually allowed to return to Chile on medical grounds in March 2000.

⚠️ This is one of the most important facts in the entire session for a General Studies answer, because it is where Latin American history intersects with the development of international law. ⚠️ Set out what it establishes.

⚠️ **The jurisdictional geometry.** A former head of state, present in a second country, was detained on the request of a third, for acts committed in his own against nationals of the third. ⚠️ The claim implicit in that arrangement is that certain acts - torture prominent among them - are offences against a legal order that is not confined to the territory where they occur.

⚠️ **What it did to immunity.** The Chilean arrangements had been carefully built: a constitution written in 1981, eight further years in command of the army, a domestic settlement. ⚠️ None of it reached beyond the national frontier. ⚠️ **A negotiated domestic immunity is only as strong as the traveller's itinerary** - and that realisation changed the behaviour of former officials across the region.

⚠️ **The limits, which must be stated for balance.** ✅ He was released on medical grounds in March 2000; ✅ the Chilean Court of Appeal decided in July 2001 that he was too ill to stand trial on over 250 charges; ✅ he died in 2006 at 91 without conviction. ⚠️ The principle was asserted; the individual was never tried.

⚠️ **And the domestic consequence, which is the deeper one.** ✅ He returned in March 2000 to find Ricardo Lagos, one of his most bitter opponents, just elected in January 2000 as the first socialist president since Allende, and faced over 250 charges at home. ⚠️ The external action unlocked the domestic one: once the immunity had been shown to be breachable abroad, it became contestable at home.

### 5. Reopening a settled bargain: Argentina, 2006

✅ Kirchner won popularity when the laws granting pardon to those accused of atrocities during the dirty war were cancelled, so that in 2006 many military and police personnel who thought they were safe were arrested and put on trial.

⚠️ Three things are established by that one sentence and each is worth a clause in an answer.

⚠️ **Amnesties can be reversed.** ⚠️ Twenty-three years after the transition and more than two decades after the pardons, a democratic government cancelled them and prosecutions followed. ⚠️ A transitional bargain is not constitutionally permanent.

⚠️ **Accountability was popular.** ✅ The account says Kirchner *won popularity* by doing it. ⚠️ The standard argument for amnesty is that societies prefer to move on; ⚠️ Argentina in 2006 is direct evidence against that as a general claim.

⚠️ **It happened during an economic recovery.** ⚠️ Place it beside the rest of Kirchner's record - ✅ rejection of neo-liberal economics, ✅ growth of about 8 per cent, ✅ the announcement in January 2006 that all remaining IMF debts had been paid off. ⚠️ Accountability at home and financial emancipation abroad arrived together, and the conjunction is not accidental: ⚠️ **a government that has recovered autonomy in one domain finds it easier to exercise it in another.**

### 6. The current linkage, dated and limited

📰 **Current linkage, used once in this session and limited here.** 📰 On **19 March 2026**, in a press release marking the fiftieth anniversary of the 1976 coup d'etat, the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights reported that United Nations human rights experts had warned of alarming setbacks in Argentina's transitional justice process, expressing concern about a reduced state role in promoting criminal investigations for crimes against humanity, obstructed access to dictatorship-era archives, the dismantling of institutions devoted to memory, reparation and victim support, and public discussion of possible pardons for military personnel convicted of international crimes, which the experts said would represent a grave setback for accountability; the statement recalled Argentina's recognised milestones, including the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons, the trial of the juntas, the prosecution of perpetrators of crimes against humanity and the recovery of the identities of forcibly disappeared children, and stated that the legal obligation to guarantee truth, justice, reparation, memory and non-repetition is not optional.

⚠️ **Source caution.** ⚠️ This is an official statement by United Nations human rights experts and is reliable evidence of what those experts said and when. ❌ It is not independent evidence that any particular measure has been enacted, and ❌ no current Argentine political, legal or economic claim is asserted anywhere else in this session.

⚠️ **Why the linkage is genuine rather than decorative**, which is the test this session applies. ⚠️ It does not add a fact about the 1970s. ⚠️ It establishes that the transitional-justice settlement described in section 5 is **still contested fifty years after the coup**, which converts this lesson from a closed historical episode into a live example of how societies handle mass atrocity. ⚠️ ⚠️ And it supplies the vocabulary an examiner is likely to use - truth, justice, reparation, memory and **guarantees of non-repetition** - which is the standard four-or-five-pillar framework for transitional justice and is directly transferable to any comparative question.

⚠️ **How to use it in an answer**, and the limit. ⚠️ One dated sentence at most, as evidence that the settlement of the 1980s remains open. ❌ Never as the substance of a history answer.

### 7. Why accountability was slow: four reasons

⚠️ Assemble these, because "it took a long time" is not an explanation.

⚠️ **The exits were negotiated.** ⚠️ The armed forces left on their own terms, retaining constitutional positions and pardons.

⚠️ **The method was designed to defeat proof.** ⚠️ Disappearance leaves no body, no record and no admitted detention. ⚠️ ✅ The Argentine commission could charge the disappearance of *up to* 30,000 people precisely because the state had denied that the detentions occurred.

⚠️ **The institutions were compromised.** ⚠️ Courts, police and armies staffed during the dictatorship continued after it. ✅ Guatemala's elected president in the 1980s governed with the army as a baleful background presence.

⚠️ **The economics crowded it out.** ⚠️ The new democracies inherited the debt of Lesson 15 and administered the austerity of Lesson 16. ⚠️ ✅ Argentina's accountability moment came in 2006, during the recovery - not in 1991, during the riots over food prices and unemployment.

### Criticism and reply: truth commissions or trials?

⚠️ **The case for commissions.** They can establish a comprehensive record where courts, bound by rules of evidence and individual charges, cannot; they are faster; and they do not threaten a fragile transition. ✅ The Argentine commission's charges are the reason this session can state figures at all.

⚠️ **The case for trials.** A record without consequence may confirm impunity rather than end it; ✅ and the Argentine experience of 2006 shows that prosecutions were both possible and popular once the political conditions allowed.

⚠️ **The reply that reconciles them.** ⚠️ The evidence here suggests they are sequential rather than alternative. ⚠️ The commission establishes the facts; the facts survive the political moment; and when a later majority is willing, the facts become the basis of prosecution. ⚠️ ✅ Argentina's 1984 commission and its 2006 trials are two stages of one process separated by twenty-two years. ⚠️ **The value of a commission is partly that it preserves the possibility of justice until justice becomes possible.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson connects world history to the human-rights and international-institutions strands, and it supplies transferable vocabulary for any comparative question on societies emerging from mass violence.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect it inside a broader question - the legacy of authoritarian rule, or the effectiveness of international human-rights mechanisms - rather than alone.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Merging or adding the figures. ⚠️ Each belongs to one body, one country and one period.
2. ⚠️ Treating amnesties as legally final. ✅ Argentina cancelled its pardon laws and prosecutions followed in 2006.
3. ⚠️ Claiming Pinochet was convicted. ✅ He was held in London for 16 months, released on medical grounds, ruled too ill to stand trial in July 2001 and died in 2006 without conviction.
4. ⚠️ Presenting the Guatemalan amnesty as simple impunity. ✅ It was partial, covered all participants and was the price of ending a 36-year war.
5. ⚠️ Using the current item as historical evidence. ⚠️ It shows only that the settlement remains contested, dated 19 March 2026.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Argentina 1976-83: a human rights commission charged the junta with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people in the dirty war; the body is the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons, which reported in 1984 as *Nunca Mas*.
2. ✅ Chile 1973-90: around 3,000 killed or disappeared in 17 years, with tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or driven into exile; the official inquiries are the National Commission for Truth and Reconciliation, reporting in 1991, and the later National Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture.
3. ✅ Guatemala: some 200,000 killed in 36 years, well over half Mayan Indians; 3,252 political murders calculated in the four months from October 1979 to January 1980.
4. ✅ Brazil: parties banned, press censored, jails filled with political prisoners, trade unionists and left-wing students a favourite target, reports of widespread torture - no figure stated.
5. ✅ Mexico 1968: estimates of those killed at Tlatelolco vary between 30 and 300. ✅ Panama 1989: at least 2,000 civilians killed.
6. ✅ Immunity built into the exits: Chile's 1980 constitution, in force from 11 March 1981, kept Pinochet Commander-in-Chief for eight years after the presidency; Argentina had pardon laws for the dirty war; Guatemala's December 1996 agreement granted a partial amnesty to the various participants in the violence.
7. ✅ 1998: Pinochet arrested in London and held for 16 months on a Spanish extradition request over torture of Spanish citizens in Chile; released on medical grounds in March 2000; over 250 charges at home; ruled too ill to stand trial in July 2001; died in 2006 aged 91.
8. ✅ 2000: Lagos elected in January as the first socialist president since Allende, one of Pinochet's most bitter opponents.
9. ✅ 2006: Argentina cancelled the pardon laws, so many military and police personnel who thought they were safe were arrested and put on trial, and Kirchner won popularity by doing it - in the same period as growth of about 8 per cent and the January 2006 announcement that all remaining IMF debts had been paid off.
10. 📰 19 March 2026: the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights reported United Nations experts warning of alarming setbacks in Argentina's transitional justice process on the fiftieth anniversary of the coup, citing a reduced state role in investigations, obstructed archive access, dismantled memory and victim-support institutions and discussion of pardons for those convicted of international crimes, and stating that the obligation to guarantee truth, justice, reparation, memory and non-repetition is not optional. Used once; no other current claim is asserted in this session.
11. ⚠️ Four reasons accountability was slow: negotiated exits with built-in immunity; a method - disappearance - designed to defeat proof; compromised courts, police and armies; and new democracies absorbed by debt and austerity.
12. ⚠️ Commissions and trials are sequential rather than alternative: the 1984 record made the 2006 prosecutions possible.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: immunity was a term of the transition rather than an oversight, and it was breached first from outside the national jurisdiction and only then from within.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the London arrest of 1998 mattered more as a precedent than as a prosecution, and connect it to what happened in Argentina eight years later.

**Model answer:** It mattered as a precedent because of its jurisdictional geometry rather than its outcome. A former head of state, present in a second country, was detained for 16 months on the extradition request of a third, over charges of torturing that third country's citizens in his own. The implicit claim is that certain acts are offences against a legal order not confined to the territory in which they occur - and that claim defeated an immunity that had been carefully constructed at home, since the Chilean constitution of 1980, in force from 11 March 1981, had allowed Pinochet to remain Commander-in-Chief for eight years after leaving the presidency. A negotiated domestic immunity, in other words, stops at the frontier. As a prosecution it achieved nothing: he was released on medical grounds in March 2000, the Chilean Court of Appeal ruled in July 2001 that he was too ill to stand trial on over 250 charges, and he died in 2006 at 91 without conviction. The Argentine connection is that external breach makes domestic contestation thinkable. In 2006 the laws granting pardon to those accused of atrocities during the dirty war were cancelled, so many military and police personnel who thought they were safe were arrested and put on trial, and Kirchner won popularity for it - in the same period in which Argentina grew at about 8 per cent and announced in January 2006 that it had paid off all remaining debts to the IMF. Once immunity is shown to be breachable abroad it becomes contestable at home, and a government that has recovered autonomy in one domain exercises it more readily in another.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating amnesties as illegitimate by definition and their reversal as straightforwardly right. Guatemala's formal peace agreement of December 1996 legalised the main guerrilla organisation and granted a partial amnesty to the various participants in the violence, and that was the price of ending a civil war that had lasted 36 years and killed some 200,000 people, well over half of them Mayan Indians. Peace and justice were in direct competition, and an answer that condemns the choice without acknowledging what it purchased has not engaged the dilemma the question is testing.

---

## Lesson 19 - The challenge to United States domination: the post-1998 turn and the institutions it built

Progress: 19/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: why the end of the Cold War changed the terms; Venezuela from 1998 and the coup that failed in 2002; the sequence of elections across the region; the degrees of divergence the account records; the regional institutions from 2004 to 2009; the diversification towards China; and an honest assessment of how much changed

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the core account's section on the challenge to United States domination, its Venezuela and Brazil narratives after 1998, its Argentina, Chile, Bolivia and Ecuador sections, and its closing passages on regional institutions and on trade diversification towards China
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: what changed, and what enabled it

```text

   THE DEMAND SIDE                        THE SUPPLY SIDE
   --------------                         ---------------
   "the neo-liberal-style policies        "As genuine democracy
    favoured by the USA should be          spread, leftish political
    abandoned; foreign companies           groups organized campaigns
    should be required to hand over        in favour of social and
    more of their profits to the           economic reform"

    and inequality which were still
```
