```text
                          |
                          v
   THE SEQUENCE OF ELECTIONS
   1998  Venezuela   Chavez, 56% of the vote
   2002  Brazil      Lula
   2003  Argentina   Nestor Kirchner
   2005  Bolivia     Morales (December)
   2006  Chile       Bachelet wins runoff, 15 January
   2006  Ecuador     Correa (November)
   2006  Nicaragua   Ortega returns, 62%
   -- and in the opposite direction --
   2000  Mexico      Fox (centre-right), ending 71 years
                          |
                          v
   THE DEGREES, AS THE ACCOUNT GRADES THEM
   MOST DECISIVE in rejecting neo-liberalism:
      Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador, Argentina
   MIDDLE OF THE ROAD, a mixture of policies:
      Brazil, Nicaragua, Uruguay
   KEPT CLOSE TO WASHINGTON by separate trade agreements:
      Mexico, Chile, Guatemala
                          |
                          v
   THE INSTITUTIONS BUILT
   2004  ALBA - Bolivarian Alternative for the Americas
   2008  UNASUR - Union of South American Nations (12 members
         by 2011), merging two existing customs unions
   2009  Bank of the South, initial capital $20 billion
   plus   Mercosur (1991), the Andean Development Corporation
          and the Venezuelan development bank BANDES
```

⚠️ The diagram separates three things that are usually run together: **the permission** created by the end of the Cold War, **the demand** created by the failures of Lesson 16, and **the institutions** built afterwards. ⚠️ An answer that has all three is analytical; one that lists presidents is not.

### 1. The enabling condition: what the end of the Cold War removed

✅ The international situation changed towards the end of the twentieth century with the ending of the Cold War. ✅ The demise of the communist enemy - the Soviet Empire - removed the Americans' justification for their constant interventions. ✅ After half a century of United States domination, Latin American states had more freedom to take control of their own affairs; no longer could the USA accuse them of aiming to become part of a communist power bloc. ✅ Since the Cold War was over and the USSR had ceased to exist, the USA could no longer get rid of left-leaning governments on the grounds that they were aiming to form alliances with the communist bloc.

⚠️ Be precise about what changed, because the distinction is examinable. ⚠️ What disappeared was not the capability but the **justification**. ⚠️ The economic weight, the intelligence services, the credit relationships and the military predominance all remained. ⚠️ What went was the argument that made their use acceptable - at home in the United States, among allies, and at the Organization of American States.

⚠️ That matters because it explains a specific pattern: **the instruments continued to be used, but with less success and more cost.** ✅ In 2002 right-wing forces backed by United States finance tried to overthrow Chavez and failed; ✅ American agents intervened in Nicaraguan elections in 1989 and 2001; ✅ during the 1990s the Americans helped to suppress left-wing movements in Mexico, Colombia, Ecuador and Peru. ⚠️ ✅ And the account is explicit that this sort of policy was bound to cause resentment, especially now that it could not be justified as part of the campaign against the advance of global communism.

⚠️ There is a second enabling condition which is not in the diagram and should be added in an answer: **the failure of the alternative**. ⚠️ Lesson 16 established that the programme defeated inflation and did not defeat poverty or unemployment. ⚠️ ✅ The demand recorded here follows directly: that the neo-liberal-style policies favoured by the USA should be abandoned and that foreign companies should be required to hand over more of their profits to the state to help tackle the poverty and inequality which were still rife throughout Latin America.

⚠️ And a third, which the account names and which is easy to miss: ✅ **as genuine democracy spread**, leftish political groups organized campaigns in favour of social and economic reform, and people were prepared to vote for them because their programmes were attractive. ⚠️ The turn was made possible by the transitions of Lessons 12, 13 and 18. ⚠️ **Democratisation was the precondition of the left turn**, which is a neat and true irony: the regimes installed to prevent socialism ended by creating the electoral conditions in which it could be voted for.

### 2. Venezuela: the first case and the template

✅ Venezuela was the first country to throw off United States influence when, in 1998, Hugo Chavez was elected president on a programme of greater spending on social services to help alleviate poverty, and of making trading agreements with Cuba - absolute anathema to the USA. ✅ The first major challenge to United States influence came in 1998 when he won the presidential election with 56 per cent of the vote on a programme of increased social spending and an attack on poverty.

⚠️ Recall from Lesson 2 where he came from: ✅ a young military officer so disgusted when the government sent troops into poor neighbourhoods to put down protests in 1992 that he organised a coup to overthrow the dictatorship; ✅ the coup failed but brought him to public attention and demonstrated the split in the ranks of the military; ✅ after a second failed coup he decided to run for president in 1998, campaigning on increased social spending and trading agreements with Cuba, and won a convincing victory as voters turned away from the two main parties.

⚠️ That trajectory - failed coup, public recognition, electoral victory six years later - is itself a statement about the period. ⚠️ **The route to power had changed from the barracks to the ballot**, and it had changed for the left as well as the right.

✅ His general aim was to free Venezuela from United States influence and create a network of countries sympathetic to his project. ✅ He didn't try to abandon capitalism, but simply moved away from the type of neo-liberal capitalism favoured by the USA and other leaders such as Margaret Thatcher in the UK. ✅ From the beginning he spoke out publicly against the USA, ending Venezuela's long-standing military ties and giving economic support to Cuba, which provided a lifeline for a country that had lost its main supporter when the USSR collapsed.

⚠️ Note the qualification and keep it: ✅ he did not try to abandon capitalism. ⚠️ An answer that describes this as a socialist revolution has overstated it; the account describes a move away from one variety of capitalism, not away from capitalism.

✅ The measures: one of his earliest moves was to tighten control over the state oil company set up in the 1970s, which had been contributing less and less to the state treasury while managers paid themselves vastly inflated salaries; ✅ this was immediately put right and a Hydrocarbons Law was introduced making it illegal for any private company to own more than 50 per cent of the shares in joint oil ventures with the state. ✅ Large parts of the economy were taken into state control: the oil industry was fully nationalized in 2007, followed by the electricity supply and telecommunications, and in 2011 the gold industry followed.

✅ **The 2002 coup and its failure.** All of this upset many traditional interests - sections of the army, oil executives and right-wingers in general. ✅ An alliance of these groups, partly financed by the USA, staged street demonstrations demanding his resignation; ✅ a group of hostile officers kidnapped him, but he was rescued by officers loyal to him, and amid massive pro-Chavez street demonstrations he was enabled to stay in power. ✅ This proved to be the first serious blow against United States influence in South America and the beginning of a new era.

⚠️ Compare this directly with Guatemala in 1954 and Chile in 1973, because the comparison is the clearest demonstration in the session of what had changed. ⚠️ **The same coalition, the same external financing, the same method - and a different outcome.** ⚠️ Two things account for the difference: ✅ the army split rather than acting as one, with officers loyal to the president rescuing him; ✅ and a mass mobilisation in the streets on the government's side. ⚠️ In 1954 the Guatemalan army abstained and nobody came out. ⚠️ **Coup-proofing plus popular mobilisation is the difference between 1954 and 2002**, and it is worth stating in exactly those terms.

✅ **The social record, with its own figures attributed.** Another aim was to help the poverty-stricken masses by spreading some of the country's wealth more widely; ✅ according to United Nations statistics, in 1998 when he came to power 54 per cent of the population were living below the poverty line. ✅ A social welfare programme known as the Bolivar Plan 2000, named after the nineteenth-century revolutionary leader and founding father of Venezuela, included plans to improve public health care, housing projects and loans to enable people to start small businesses; ✅ thousands of worker-owned co-operatives were set up with government help; ✅ extra cash was made available to tackle the AIDS epidemic; ✅ great progress was made in securing equal rights for women, including a new rule that at least 50 per cent of political parties' election candidates had to be female; ✅ in 2008 the government announced a $111 million plan to upgrade dozens of hospitals. ✅ United Nations statistics show that 54 per cent of the population lived in poverty in 1998 compared to 28 per cent in 2008.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ⚠️ Both poverty figures are United Nations statistics for two stated years. ❌ Do not project them forward; ⚠️ the account's own speculation about what might follow by 2018 is explicitly conditional and should not be reported as a finding.

✅ **The criticisms, which belong in a balanced answer.** By 2012 he had alienated most of the business class, the Roman Catholic Church, and left-wingers who felt that he had become too authoritarian; ✅ in 2009 the Church and Human Rights Watch accused him of creating a climate of fear; ✅ local elections in 2010 and 2011 showed a fall in his socialist party vote; ✅ he had health problems, having been recently diagnosed with cancer; ✅ he was nevertheless still seen as a hero by the majority of the working class and won the 2012 election comfortably.

✅ **The structural weakness, unchanged.** The main economic weakness was that Venezuela, the world's fifth largest oil exporter, was still overdependent on oil production, with around 90 per cent of revenue from all exports coming from oil; ✅ it meant that whenever world oil prices fell the economy suffered, and in 2009 the economy shrank by around 3 per cent because of the world recession.

⚠️ That is the single most important judgement to make about the whole post-1998 turn, and it should be stated as a general finding rather than a Venezuelan one. ⚠️ **The politics of the relationship with the United States changed; the commodity structure of the economy did not.** ⚠️ A country drawing around 90 per cent of export revenue from one commodity in 2011 was in the position described in Lesson 1 for the beginning of the century. ⚠️ Fourteen years of a self-consciously anti-imperialist government had redistributed the rent without altering the dependence on it.

### 3. The sequence, and its variation

✅ Similar trends followed in some other important states: in 2002 the left-wing Luiz Inacio da Silva won the Brazilian presidential election on a programme similar to that of Chavez; ✅ the following year Argentina followed suit with the election of Nestor Kirchner; ✅ in Chile the centre-left Michelle Bachelet won the presidential runoff on **15 January 2006** to become Chile's first woman president - the core account places the victory in January 2005, which cannot be reconciled with the presidency it dates from 2006; ✅ like-minded presidents were elected in Bolivia and Ecuador in 2006; ✅ and in the same year Daniel Ortega became president of Nicaragua for the second time after a gap of 16 years. ✅ Meanwhile in Mexico the trend seemed to be in the opposite direction: after 71 years of rule by nominally left-wing governments, voters turned to a moderately conservative party.

⚠️ **The Mexican exception is not an inconvenience; it is the proof of the variation discipline.** ⚠️ The same region, the same decade, the opposite direction - because what Mexican voters were rejecting was a nominally left-wing party that had governed for seventy-one years. ⚠️ Voters were turning against incumbents who had failed them, and the ideological direction of the turn depended on who the incumbent was.

⚠️ **And the account itself grades the divergence, which is a gift to an answer.** ✅ Of course there were different degrees of change: the USA did its best to divide Mexico, Chile and Guatemala from the rest by making separate trade agreements with them, so that relations between the four states were reasonably cordial; ✅ Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador and Argentina were the most decisive in their rejection of neo-liberalism, while Brazil, Nicaragua and Uruguay were middle of the road, with a mixture of policies.

⚠️ Reproduce that three-way grading in any answer on the left turn. ⚠️ It demonstrates immediately that you are not treating "the pink tide" as a single phenomenon, and it names the instrument by which Washington maintained influence after the Cold War: **bilateral trade agreements as a technique of division.**

⚠️ Two cases show what "most decisive" meant in practice.

✅ **Argentina under Kirchner.** An admirer of Chavez, he was determined to make a break with the past and reject neo-liberal economics; in his public pronouncements he savagely criticized the IMF and foreign investors; he abandoned what he called "automatic alignment" with the USA in favour of closer ties with other Latin American countries, especially Venezuela, and with Mercosur, a common market and customs union set up in 1991 to encourage free trade and political co-operation, whose original members were Argentina, Brazil, Uruguay and Paraguay and which Venezuela joined in 2006. ✅ He raised wages and pensions for those most in need, set up a new state-run oil company and signed energy and trade agreements with Venezuela, and encouraged greater government involvement in the energy sector, though he stopped short of renationalising the main oil company, which had been sold to a Spanish company under Menem. ✅ The economy recovered: Venezuela began importing Argentine cattle and agricultural machinery, exports to Venezuela quadrupled by 2008, growth ran at about 8 per cent, and in January 2006 all remaining IMF debts were paid off. ✅ Under Cristina Fernandez de Kirchner, against the predictions of many neo-liberal economists, the boom continued: 8 per cent growth was maintained, the poverty rate had been halved by the October 2011 election, employment had risen to a record high and a lucrative export market had been developed in China.

✅ **Ecuador under Correa.** He announced that "the long neo-liberal night" had come to an end and promised an economic revolution to renegotiate the foreign debt and channel as much money as possible into health and education; ✅ in 2010 legislation required foreign oil companies to renegotiate their contracts so that more of the profits went to the government for the campaign against poverty, with nationalisation and expulsion threatened for refusal; ✅ by 2012 both unemployment and poverty levels had fallen, expenditure on roads, hospitals and schools had vastly increased, and the economy grew by 7.8 per cent in 2011, helped by higher oil prices.

⚠️ **And the criticisms, in both cases, belong in the answer.** ✅ Correa proposed a new constitution that would weaken Congress, strengthen the presidency and allow him two further terms; critics accused him of trying to make himself into a dictator; it was approved by 64 per cent in a referendum in September 2008. ✅ After he ended bonuses and other benefits for the police, protests broke out in September 2010 in which the police were heavily involved, roadblocks were set up, protesters invaded the National Assembly and the state television station, and the president was kidnapped and held hostage until an army unit rescued him from a hospital. ✅ Human-rights groups accused him of making himself too powerful and the middle classes complained about rising prices and taxation.

⚠️ Note the recurring pattern across the whole turn, because naming it is an analytical point rather than a moral one: ⚠️ **redistribution funded by commodity rents, combined with constitutional changes concentrating executive power and extending terms.** ⚠️ ✅ Chavez re-elected in 2000, 2006 and 2012; ✅ Correa's constitution allowing two further terms; ✅ Ortega's Supreme Court cancelling the ban on consecutive terms; ✅ Morales re-elected with a two-thirds congressional majority. ⚠️ The pattern is consistent enough to be part of the assessment.

### 4. The institutions: what was actually built

⚠️ This is the part of the turn that is most often omitted and is most useful in an answer, because institutions outlast presidents.

✅ **ALBA, 2004.** Named after Simon Bolivar, the Bolivarian Alternative for the Americas was the creation of Chavez in 2004; in the words of Nikolas Kozloff, it was an initiative designed to encourage greater trade, solidarity and exchange between nations standing outside the usual market-based strictures - that is, outside the United States orbit. ✅ Its activities went far beyond simple free-trade agreements to include mutual economic and social assistance: Venezuela supplied Cuba with oil from its state-owned refineries at very reasonable prices and in return Cuba sent thousands of doctors and teachers to work in Venezuela, and Cuban doctors also worked in Bolivia and provided medical supplies. ✅ By 2012 membership included Venezuela, Cuba, Bolivia, Ecuador, Nicaragua, Haiti and various small Caribbean islands, and discussions were well under way on adopting a common currency, the sucre, although there were problems with small Caribbean islands already in the Eastern Caribbean monetary union.

⚠️ The oil-for-doctors exchange deserves a sentence in any answer on South-South cooperation, because it is a genuinely novel form: ⚠️ a barter of complementary endowments between two states, conducted outside the dollar economy and outside the institutions of Lesson 16, in which each partner's greatest asset is what the other most lacks.

✅ **UNASUR, 2008.** The Union of South American Nations was set up at a meeting in the Brazilian capital in 2008, bringing together two existing customs unions - Mercosur and the Andean Community of Nations. ✅ By 2011 there were 12 member states: Argentina, Brazil, Bolivia, Chile, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Paraguay, Peru, Suriname, Uruguay and Venezuela. ✅ The plan was eventually to set up a South American parliament situated at Cochabamba, the third largest city in Bolivia.

⚠️ Note the membership. ⚠️ Twelve states across the political spectrum - including Colombia and Chile, the region's closest partners of the United States. ⚠️ **This is not an alliance of the left; it is a regional institution that the left initiated and the whole region joined**, which is a stronger fact than a bloc would have been.

✅ **The Bank of the South, 2009.** Launched with initial capital of $20 billion, the bulk supplied by Argentina, Brazil and Venezuela with smaller contributions from Bolivia, Ecuador, Paraguay and Uruguay; ✅ it provides loans for approved social and infrastructure improvements to any Latin American country **as an alternative to the IMF**. ✅ Some governments preferred smaller regional funds such as the Andean Development Corporation and the state-run Venezuelan development bank BANDES, which has branches in Ecuador, Bolivia and Uruguay and has been especially helpful in financing Bolivian educational programmes.

⚠️ Go back to Lesson 15 and the reason this matters. ⚠️ The Fund's leverage rested on being the only available lender to a country in distress. ⚠️ **An alternative lender, however small, breaks the monopoly** - because the borrower can now decline the conditions. ✅ The account records the assessment: whichever alternative the countries chose, the outcome would have been similar, and the IMF was on the verge of being eclipsed as a force within the region. ✅ Jason Tockman, an expert on ALBA and Bolivia, declared that United States influence through international financial institutions like the IMF had collapsed.

⚠️ **Attribution control.** ⚠️ That last judgement is a named expert's, reported as such. ❌ Do not state it as a finding of fact.

### 5. Diversification: the second buyer, again

✅ Some Latin American countries began a diversification of markets and investment, with China as an increasingly important partner. ✅ Venezuela, the leading oil exporter in the hemisphere, delivered quite a blow to Washington's energy policies: having built up probably the closest relations with China of any Latin American country, it planned to export increasing amounts of oil to China as part of its effort to reduce its dependence on the openly hostile United States government. ✅ Chavez had begun moving much of Venezuela's gold reserves out of Western banks and into countries he counted as allies - Russia, China and Brazil - and as the debt crisis in Europe worsened, more reserves were transferred to China, a move welcomed by Beijing, which had invested heavily in Venezuela.

✅ Latin America as a whole was increasing trade and other relations with China, in particular raw-materials exporters such as Brazil, Peru and Chile; ✅ for Brazil, now often called "the farmer of the world", China became its largest trading partner. ✅ Argentina developed a lucrative export market in China by 2011. ✅ These increases were part of the move toward a more diverse world that was causing considerable agitation among American planners and businessmen who had assumed for a long time that their global domination would continue indefinitely. ✅ A trade agreement with China did much to steady the Brazilian economy under Lula. ✅ The account also records that these developments had serious consequences for the USA, which was losing economic, diplomatic and military advantages in South America, and that some Latin American states came to enjoy greater prosperity - by 2011 Brazil was viewed as one of the world's great emerging economies.

⚠️ This is the third appearance in this session of the same structural mechanism, and naming the pattern is worth more than any individual instance. ⚠️ ✅ Mexico in 1938, boycotted, sold oil to Germany and Italy and obtained a compensation agreement. ✅ Cuba in 1960, threatened with the loss of its sugar market, found a buyer in the USSR. ✅ Latin America after 2000, seeking room for manoeuvre, found a buyer in China. ⚠️ **A second buyer is the single most reliable source of autonomy for a commodity-exporting state**, and it has been so in every decade of this topic.

⚠️ But the qualification is equally important and a good answer will supply it unprompted. ⚠️ ✅ The commodities being exported to China are raw materials - Brazil, Peru and Chile are named as raw-materials exporters, and Brazil is called the farmer of the world. ⚠️ **Diversifying the buyer does not diversify the product.** ⚠️ A country that exports the same primary commodities to a different partner has changed its counterparty and not its position in the world division of labour, and that is precisely the condition that Lesson 7 identified as the region's original problem.

⚠️ ✅ Noam Chomsky's assessment is recorded and should be attributed: the former colonies in Latin America had a better chance than ever before to overcome centuries of subjugation, violence, repression and foreign intervention, and if those hopes could be realised even partially the results could not fail to have a large-scale global impact.

### Criticism and reply: how much actually changed?

⚠️ **The case that the change was fundamental.** ✅ A coup attempt against Chavez failed in 2002, where equivalent attempts had succeeded in 1954, 1964 and 1973; ✅ governments across the region were elected on explicitly anti-Washington programmes and served full terms; ✅ regional institutions were built with an alternative lender; ✅ the Organization of American States voted 20 to 1 against the United States over Panama; ✅ Venezuelan poverty fell from 54 per cent in 1998 to 28 per cent in 2008 on United Nations figures; ✅ Argentina paid off the IMF and grew at 8 per cent.

⚠️ **The case that it was shallower than it looked.** ✅ Venezuela still drew around 90 per cent of export revenue from oil and shrank by around 3 per cent in 2009 when prices fell; ✅ the new trade relationships were commodity relationships; ✅ Mexico moved in the opposite direction and the USA divided Mexico, Chile and Guatemala from the rest by separate trade agreements; ✅ Nicaragua was still the poorest country in the Western hemisphere with 46 per cent below the poverty line in 2011; ✅ several of the leaders were accused of concentrating power, with Correa's critics alleging dictatorship, Ortega's cancelling the term limit, and the Church and Human Rights Watch accusing Chavez of creating a climate of fear.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict, and the one to write.** ⚠️ The post-1998 turn changed the region's **political** relationship with the United States decisively and its **economic** structure only marginally. ⚠️ Latin American states acquired the capacity to elect governments Washington disliked, to survive attempts at their removal, to decline IMF conditions and to find alternative markets. ⚠️ They did not acquire diversified economies, and the redistribution they achieved was financed by a commodity boom whose reversal they could not control.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson is the region's principal evidence for Global South agency and for the emergence of a more multipolar order, both of which are examinable framings.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect an evaluation demand, often built on a strong claim - that 1998 began a transformation - which the answer is expected to qualify rather than simply accept or reject.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the left turn as uniform. ✅ The account grades it three ways and names Mexico as moving in the opposite direction.
2. ⚠️ Saying the Cold War's end removed American capability. ⚠️ It removed the justification; the instruments were still used, notably in 2002.
3. ⚠️ Describing Chavez as abandoning capitalism. ✅ He moved away from a type of neo-liberal capitalism and did not try to abandon capitalism.
4. ⚠️ Omitting the criticisms. ✅ The Church and Human Rights Watch, the falling party vote, the concentration of executive power and the term-limit changes are all on the record.
5. ⚠️ Presenting the China relationship as liberation. ✅ The exports concerned are raw materials, which changes the buyer without changing the position.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The end of the Cold War removed the justification for constant intervention; states had more freedom and could no longer be accused of aiming to join a communist bloc.
2. ✅ The demand: abandon the neo-liberal-style policies favoured by the USA and require foreign companies to hand over more profits to the state to tackle poverty and inequality still rife throughout the region; as genuine democracy spread, leftish groups organised and people voted for them.
3. ✅ Chavez elected in 1998 with 56 per cent on increased social spending, an attack on poverty and trading agreements with Cuba; a young officer who had organised a failed coup in 1992 after troops were sent into poor neighbourhoods, and a second failed coup in 1994.
4. ✅ He did not try to abandon capitalism but moved away from neo-liberal capitalism; ended long-standing military ties with the USA; gave economic support to Cuba, a lifeline after the Soviet collapse; tightened control of the state oil company and introduced a Hydrocarbons Law barring private majority ownership in joint ventures; fully nationalised oil in 2007, then electricity and telecommunications, and gold in 2011.
5. ✅ 2002: an alliance of army sections, oil executives and right-wingers, partly financed by the USA, staged demonstrations and hostile officers kidnapped him; officers loyal to him rescued him amid massive pro-Chavez demonstrations; the first serious blow against United States influence in South America.
6. ✅ Social record: Bolivar Plan 2000 for health care, housing and small-business loans; thousands of worker co-operatives; extra funds against AIDS; at least 50 per cent female election candidates required of parties; a $111 million hospital upgrade announced in 2008; United Nations statistics of 54 per cent in poverty in 1998 against 28 per cent in 2008.
7. ✅ Criticisms: by 2012 he had alienated most of the business class, the Roman Catholic Church and left-wingers who thought him too authoritarian; the Church and Human Rights Watch accused him in 2009 of creating a climate of fear; his party's vote fell in the 2010 and 2011 local elections; he had cancer; he won the 2012 election comfortably.
8. ✅ Structural weakness: the world's fifth largest oil exporter, still overdependent with around 90 per cent of export revenue from oil; the economy shrank by around 3 per cent in 2009.
9. ✅ The sequence: Lula in Brazil 2002; Kirchner in Argentina 2003; Bachelet in Chile in January 2006; like-minded presidents in Bolivia and Ecuador in 2006; Ortega's return in Nicaragua in 2006; Mexico moving the opposite way to Fox.
10. ✅ Degrees: most decisive in rejecting neo-liberalism - Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador, Argentina; middle of the road - Brazil, Nicaragua, Uruguay; divided from the rest by separate United States trade agreements - Mexico, Chile, Guatemala.
11. ✅ Kirchner: rejection of neo-liberal economics; savage criticism of the IMF and foreign investors; the end of automatic alignment; closer ties with Venezuela and Mercosur, set up in 1991 with Argentina, Brazil, Uruguay and Paraguay and joined by Venezuela in 2006; higher wages and pensions; a new state oil company; quadrupled exports to Venezuela by 2008; growth of about 8 per cent; all remaining IMF debts paid off in January 2006; under Fernandez the boom continued with the poverty rate halved, record employment and a lucrative Chinese market by the October 2011 election.
12. ✅ Correa: "the long neo-liberal night" declared over; renegotiation of foreign debt and money channelled into health and education; 2010 legislation forcing foreign oil companies to renegotiate contracts on pain of nationalisation; unemployment and poverty falling by 2012 with vastly increased spending on roads, hospitals and schools and growth of 7.8 per cent in 2011; a new constitution weakening Congress and strengthening the presidency approved by 64 per cent in September 2008; the September 2010 police revolt in which he was kidnapped and rescued by an army unit.
13. ✅ Institutions: ALBA, created by Chavez in 2004 and named after Bolivar, described by Nikolas Kozloff as designed to encourage trade, solidarity and exchange outside the usual market-based strictures, with Venezuelan oil exchanged for thousands of Cuban doctors and teachers, membership by 2012 of Venezuela, Cuba, Bolivia, Ecuador, Nicaragua, Haiti and small Caribbean islands, and discussion of a common currency, the sucre; UNASUR, set up in the Brazilian capital in 2008 merging Mercosur and the Andean Community, with 12 members by 2011 and a planned parliament at Cochabamba; the Bank of the South, launched in 2009 with initial capital of $20 billion, mostly from Argentina, Brazil and Venezuela, lending for approved social and infrastructure projects as an alternative to the IMF; plus the Andean Development Corporation and BANDES.
14. ✅ Jason Tockman: United States influence through international financial institutions like the IMF has collapsed - an attributed expert judgement, with the account also stating that the IMF was on the verge of being eclipsed as a force within the region.
15. ✅ China: Venezuela's closest Chinese relations of any Latin American country and planned increases in oil exports; gold reserves moved to Russia, China and Brazil; raw-materials exporters Brazil, Peru and Chile increasing trade, with China becoming Brazil's largest trading partner; Argentina's lucrative Chinese market; agitation among American planners and businessmen; the USA losing economic, diplomatic and military advantages in South America; Chomsky's assessment that the former colonies had a better chance than ever to overcome centuries of subjugation.
16. ⚠️ Master formulation: the turn changed the political relationship decisively and the economic structure only marginally; the second buyer supplied autonomy, as it had in 1938 and 1960, but diversifying the buyer is not diversifying the product.

### Concept check

**Question:** A coup attempt against Chavez in 2002 used the same coalition and methods that had succeeded in Guatemala in 1954 and Chile in 1973, and it failed. Explain the difference, and say what it does and does not prove about the region's position by then.

**Model answer:** The coalition was recognisably the same - sections of the army, oil executives and right-wingers in general, partly financed by the United States, staging street demonstrations demanding the president's resignation, followed by hostile officers kidnapping him. Two things differed. First, the armed forces split rather than acting as a body: he was rescued by officers loyal to him, whereas in Guatemala the official army took no part in the coup but equally did not defend Arbenz, and in Chile the opposition won the support of the army before the generals moved. Second, there was mass mobilisation on the government's side, with massive pro-Chavez street demonstrations enabling him to stay in power; nobody came into the streets for Arbenz. Behind both lay the enabling condition the record states: with the Cold War over and the USSR gone, the United States could no longer remove left-leaning governments on the grounds that they were aiming to join a communist bloc, so an intervention now carried its full political cost. What it proves is that the region's governments had acquired the capacity to survive the classic removal mechanism - the account calls it the first serious blow against United States influence in South America and the beginning of a new era. What it does not prove is structural emancipation: Venezuela was still the world's fifth largest oil exporter drawing around 90 per cent of export revenue from oil, and the economy shrank by around 3 per cent in 2009 when world prices fell.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the post-1998 turn as a single regional movement. The record grades it explicitly: Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador and Argentina were the most decisive in rejecting neo-liberalism, Brazil, Nicaragua and Uruguay were middle of the road with a mixture of policies, and the United States did its best to divide Mexico, Chile and Guatemala from the rest by making separate trade agreements with them. Mexico, indeed, moved in the opposite ideological direction in 2000. An answer that describes a uniform regional turn has failed the variation discipline that this topic tests more severely than any other.

---

## Lesson 20 - Explaining the century: dependency, domestic institutions, and how to use a theory in an answer

Progress: 20/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the four competing explanations recorded for the debt crisis; the vocabulary of dependency and where it came from; the evidence that supports it; the evidence that constrains it; the domestic-failure explanation and its own limits; the synthesis the sources themselves recommend; and how to deploy a theoretical frame without letting it replace evidence

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the topic's advanced knowledge file and its table of four competing explanations of the debt crisis and its recommended answer style, together with its analysis of how United States domination worked and its long-run reading of the later left turns
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four explanations, and what each one can and cannot account for

| Explanation | ✅ What it explains well | ⚠️ What it cannot explain | ⚠️ Evidence from this session |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Excess borrowing and fragile export earnings** | Why the 1980s crisis spread so widely across countries with different politics | Why the trigger arrived in 1979-81 rather than earlier or later | Borrowing at its height 1973-82; Mexico borrowing against expected oil revenue; Brazil's generals borrowing to finance growth |
| **Global financial shocks** | Why the crisis was simultaneous and regional | Why some states were far more exposed than others | The doubling of American interest rates 1979-81; unpaid interest added to principal; the seven largest United States banks earning 60 per cent of profits from Third World loans in 1982 against 2 per cent in 1970 |
| **Structural dependency** | Why crisis reduced sovereignty rather than merely income, and why IMF-style discipline followed | Why outcomes differed so widely within the region, and why some states recovered autonomy | A constant drain of capital to the USA; a president describing a net transfer of resources that violates economic logic; conditionality setting the range of budget outcomes |
| **Domestic governance failures** | Why particular countries were more vulnerable, and prevents blaming everything on outsiders alone | Why the crisis was simultaneous across states with very different governments | Mexican governments abandoning restraint; enormous sums taken by a president's family; Nicaraguan and Guatemalan corruption; Batista's foreign fortunes |

> 🔑 The recorded recommendation: **combine dependency with domestic institutional weakness.** Neither alone is an answer.

⚠️ That table is the lesson in compressed form, and it is worth reproducing mentally before writing any 20-mark answer on this topic. ⚠️ Each explanation is strong exactly where the others are weak, which is why the sources themselves recommend combination rather than selection.

### 1. What dependency actually claims

⚠️ The word is used loosely, so begin with a precise statement of the claim, because precision here is itself a discriminator.

⚠️ **Dependency theory holds that the underdevelopment of the periphery is not an earlier stage of development but a product of the way the periphery is integrated into the world economy.** ⚠️ On this view a poor country is not "behind"; it occupies a position whose function is to supply primary commodities and absorb manufactures, and the operation of the system reproduces that position rather than eroding it.

⚠️ Three intellectual steps produced that claim, and the names are worth knowing, because the analytical vocabulary is usually used without attribution.

✅ **Step one: structuralism.** The United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America, known as CEPAL, led from 1950 by **Raul Prebisch**, argued that the world economy divides into an industrial centre and a primary-producing periphery, and that the terms of trade tend to move against the periphery over time - so a country specialising in what it already exports falls behind even when it exports more. ⚠️ The policy conclusion was deliberate industrialisation, which is Lesson 7.

✅ **Step two: dependency proper.** Writers including **Andre Gunder Frank** argued that integration into global capitalism actively produced underdevelopment rather than merely failing to relieve it, so that the same process enriched one pole and impoverished the other.

✅ **Step three: the qualified version.** **Fernando Henrique Cardoso** and **Enzo Faletto** argued that dependency operates through domestic class coalitions and the state rather than by external imposition alone, and that a limited, "associated" development was possible within it.

⚠️ There is a fact here that is too good to omit from an answer and that most candidates never notice. ✅ **Cardoso, the co-author of the most sophisticated dependency analysis of the 1960s, was the Brazilian president who implemented the *Plano Real* from 1994** - large tax increases, public-sector wage reductions and the privatisation of many government enterprises - and was re-elected in 1998 having brought inflation down from a thousand per cent to single figures. ⚠️ A theorist of dependency governed by applying the stabilisation programme that dependency theory was used to criticise, and the sequence makes the point that a theory of constraint is not automatically a programme of action.

### 2. The evidence in this session that supports dependency

⚠️ Assemble it, because a claim asserted is worth nothing and a claim evidenced is worth a great deal.

⚠️ **The net transfer.** ✅ By 1985 Latin America owed some $368 billion and there was a constant drain of capital to the USA, leaving the region increasingly impoverished. ✅ The Mexican president described reaching the limit of sustaining "this net transfer of resources to the rest, which violates economic logic and is tremendously inequitable". ⚠️ Capital flowing from poor countries to rich ones is the empirical core of the dependency claim.

⚠️ **The reproduction of the commodity position.** ✅ Venezuela still drew around 90 per cent of export revenue from oil after fourteen years of an avowedly anti-imperialist government; ✅ Cuba's post-revolutionary economy still depended on the sugar harvest, the world sugar price and a single willing buyer; ✅ the new Chinese relationships are with raw-materials exporters. ⚠️ Three different political systems, one unchanged position in the division of labour.

⚠️ **The conversion of economic exposure into political constraint.** ✅ Conditionality setting the range of budget outcomes; ✅ Bolivia required in the 1950s to use scarce capital for debt service rather than development; ✅ an elected Guatemalan president calibrating reform against anticipated intervention.

⚠️ **The profit that left.** ✅ Heavy foreign investment caused problems because much of the profit was taken out of the countries; ✅ the United Fruit Company preferring railway profits to national roads.

### 3. The evidence that constrains it

⚠️ Now the other side, stated with equal force, because an answer that only prosecutes is not an answer.

⚠️ **Outcomes differed enormously within the same structural position.** ✅ Brazil ended the century with arguably the most advanced industrial sector in Latin America at about a third of gross domestic product, exporting motor vehicles, aircraft, machinery, computers and petrochemicals, and was ranked the sixth largest economy in the world by the end of 2011; ✅ Nicaragua was the poorest country in the Western hemisphere. ⚠️ A theory that predicts the position but not the outcome has limited explanatory power over the outcome.

⚠️ **Autonomy was recovered.** ✅ Argentina paid off all remaining IMF debts in January 2006 and grew at about 8 per cent; ✅ Brazil brought inflation and government debt under control, described as a considerable achievement; ✅ the Bank of the South was launched as an alternative to the IMF and the Fund was judged to be on the verge of eclipse within the region. ⚠️ Structures that can be exited in a decade are constraints, not cages.

⚠️ **Domestic failure is independently documented and is not reducible to external causes.** ✅ Zelaya looting the Nicaraguan treasury and invading two neighbours; ✅ Batista giving too little time to affairs of state while attending to his foreign fortunes; ✅ a Mexican president's family helping themselves to enormous sums; ✅ Collor impeached for corruption; ✅ a Nicaraguan former president sentenced to twenty years for corruption and embezzlement; ✅ governments abandoning restraint in borrowing on the assumption that oil revenues would always cover interest. ⚠️ None of that was caused from outside.

⚠️ **The agency record contradicts the passive reading.** ✅ Cuba shaped outcomes in Angola and Ethiopia; ✅ Arias assembled a Central American peace plan and won the Nobel Peace Prize; ✅ the Latin American parliament condemned United States policy 16 to 2; ✅ the Organization of American States voted 20 to 1 against the United States over Panama; ✅ a coup attempt failed in 2002 because officers and crowds acted.

### 4. The domestic-failure explanation, and its own limits

⚠️ The mirror-image error is equally available and equally weak, so state its limits too.

⚠️ **What it explains.** ✅ Why particular countries were more vulnerable than others - Mexico's extravagant borrowing, Argentina's junta leaving an unprecedentedly high foreign debt, the corruption documented across the region. ✅ And it prevents an error the sources explicitly warn against: blaming everything on outsiders alone.

⚠️ **What it cannot explain.** ⚠️ Why the crisis arrived **simultaneously** across states with radically different governments - military Brazil, single-party Mexico, democratic Venezuela, socialist Cuba - in the same two years. ⚠️ Simultaneity across political variety points to a common external cause, and the common external cause is documented: ✅ the doubling of American interest rates in 1979-81.

⚠️ ⚠️ **And it cannot explain the pattern of intervention.** ⚠️ Corruption did not attract intervention; reform did. ✅ The Somozas were blatantly corrupt and were supported for four decades; ✅ Batista was corrupt and brutal and was supported until 1958; ✅ Arbenz, Goulart and Allende were not accused of corruption and all three were removed. ⚠️ **If the criterion had been governance quality, the interventions would have fallen on different governments**, and pointing this out is one of the sharpest observations available in this topic.

### 5. The synthesis, and how to write it

✅ The recorded recommendation is explicit: the best answer style combines **dependency** with **domestic institutional weakness**. ⚠️ Here is how to make that more than a formula.

⚠️ **State the mechanism that joins them**, which is the two-variables discipline from the opening. ⚠️ Concentrated landholding produced governments that could not survive redistribution, so those governments required external support; external support preserved the landholding; and the resulting narrow domestic market limited industrialisation, which forced external borrowing, which converted the economic constraint into a political one.

⚠️ **Show that neither variable is prior.** ⚠️ The external actor did not create the oligarchy; the oligarchy did not summon the Cold War. ⚠️ ✅ They met, and the meeting is the century.

⚠️ **Use the divergent cases as the test.** ⚠️ Where the domestic variable differed, outcomes differed: ✅ Cuba coup-proofed its army and survived; ✅ Chile's army had abstained from politics for decades and then did not; ✅ Mexico incorporated its social sectors and had neither revolution nor coup after 1920; ✅ Venezuela's army split in 2002 and a coup failed. ⚠️ **The external constraint was roughly constant; the domestic configuration determined the result.** ⚠️ That sentence is the synthesis, and it is worth writing verbatim.

⚠️ **Admit what remains unresolved.** ⚠️ Nothing in the evidence here settles whether the region's position in the world economy is reproducible indefinitely or whether Brazil's industrial development and the post-2000 diversification represent a genuine escape. ⚠️ Saying so is a strength, not a hedge: the examiner is testing judgement, and judgement includes knowing the limits of the evidence.

### 6. How to deploy a theory without being captured by it

⚠️ Three practical rules, because the commonest failure on this topic is an answer that recites a framework instead of arguing from evidence.

⚠️ **Rule one: theory as a claim to be tested, not a conclusion to be applied.** ⚠️ Write "the dependency reading is supported by X and strained by Y", never "because of dependency, Z happened".

⚠️ **Rule two: name the mechanism, not the label.** ⚠️ "Profits were repatriated, so investment raised output without raising national income" is evidence. ⚠️ "Neo-colonial exploitation" is a label. ⚠️ ✅ The same point is made about instability: an answer that treats Latin America as one political unit has already lost marks, and the same applies to treating it as one economic mechanism.

⚠️ **Rule three: the qualification is the argument.** ⚠️ The strongest formulation available on this topic is that external domination and internal inequality were not competing explanations but a single mechanism - oligarchic landholding created governments that needed external backing, and external backing preserved oligarchic landholding. ⚠️ Learn that as a sentence; it converts a choice into an analysis.

### Criticism and reply: is dependency theory now obsolete?

⚠️ **The objection.** Brazil became the world's sixth largest economy, Argentina grew at 8 per cent and repaid the Fund, Chile and Peru diversified their markets, and the IMF was judged to be losing its grip on the region. ⚠️ A theory of permanent subordination does not survive that record.

⚠️ **The reply.** ⚠️ The theory's strongest version - permanent, unalterable subordination - does not survive it, and should be abandoned. ⚠️ The qualified version does survive, and it was always the more defensible one: ⚠️ development is possible within dependence, but it is shaped by it, and the shape is visible. ✅ Venezuela at around 90 per cent oil; ✅ Brazil as "the farmer of the world" with agricultural produce at over 30 per cent of exports despite its industrial sector; ✅ China becoming the principal buyer of the region's raw materials; ✅ a 2009 contraction produced by a world recession the region did not cause.

⚠️ **The balanced verdict.** ⚠️ Dependency is better used as a description of a **position** than as a prediction of a **fate**. ⚠️ It correctly identifies why commodity-exporting states experience external shocks as political crises and why creditors acquire policy influence; ⚠️ it does not determine what any particular government can achieve, and the region's own record between 1998 and 2012 is the evidence for that qualification.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson supplies the analytical apparatus for the whole topic and connects to the syllabus clause on political philosophies and their effect on society.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect a quotation-based demand - a strong statement about domination or about dependency that the answer is invited to evaluate.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Selecting one explanation. ✅ The recorded recommendation is to combine dependency with domestic institutional weakness.
2. ⚠️ Using dependency as a conclusion. ⚠️ It is a claim to be tested against the divergent outcomes within the region.
3. ⚠️ Attributing the framework to no one, or to the wrong source. ✅ CEPAL and Prebisch supplied the structuralist analysis; Frank and Cardoso with Faletto developed dependency; Cardoso later governed by a very different programme.
4. ⚠️ Blaming everything on outsiders. ⚠️ Domestic governance failure is named among the competing explanations precisely to prevent that.
5. ⚠️ Blaming everything on domestic failure. ⚠️ It cannot explain simultaneity across states with utterly different political systems, nor why reform rather than corruption attracted intervention.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Four recorded explanations of the debt crisis: excess borrowing with fragile export earnings; global financial shocks; structural dependency; domestic governance failures.
2. ✅ The recorded recommendation: combine dependency with domestic institutional weakness.
3. ✅ The recorded account of how domination worked: economic leverage through investment, trade and finance shaping policy space; strategic intervention justified by anti-communism; political signalling against governments seen as hostile; and United States power usually working through domestic elites, armies and anti-left coalitions.
4. ✅ The analytical vocabulary and its authors: CEPAL under Raul Prebisch from 1950 supplied the centre-periphery and terms-of-trade analysis; Andre Gunder Frank argued that integration into global capitalism actively produced underdevelopment; Cardoso and Faletto argued that dependency works through domestic class coalitions and the state and that an associated development is possible.
5. ⚠️ The Cardoso point: the co-author of the qualified dependency analysis later governed Brazil and implemented the *Plano Real*, bringing inflation from a thousand per cent to single figures by 1997.
6. ⚠️ Supporting evidence: the constant drain of capital to the USA and some $368 billion owed by 1985; the net-transfer quotation; Venezuela at around 90 per cent oil; Cuba's unchanged sugar dependence with a new buyer; conditionality narrowing budget choice; Bolivia's capital directed to debt service; profit repatriation and the United Fruit Company's preference for railway profits over roads.
7. ⚠️ Constraining evidence: Brazil's industrial sector at about a third of gross domestic product and the sixth largest economy by 2011 against Nicaragua as the poorest country in the hemisphere; Argentina's repayment of the IMF in January 2006 and 8 per cent growth; the Bank of the South as an alternative lender; documented domestic corruption from Zelaya to Collor; and the agency record from Cuba in Angola to the Arias plan and the failed 2002 coup.
8. ⚠️ The limits of the domestic-failure explanation: it cannot explain simultaneity across military, single-party, democratic and socialist states in the same two years, and it cannot explain why corrupt allies were sustained while non-corrupt reformers were removed.
9. ⚠️ The joining mechanism: concentrated land produced governments that could not survive redistribution and therefore needed external backing; external backing preserved the land structure; the resulting narrow market limited industrialisation; industrialisation required borrowing; borrowing converted economic exposure into political constraint.
10. ⚠️ The synthesis sentence: the external constraint was roughly constant across the region, and the domestic configuration determined the result.
11. ⚠️ Three rules for using theory: test it rather than apply it; name mechanisms rather than labels; and treat the qualification as the argument.
12. ⚠️ Master formulation: dependency describes a position rather than a fate - it explains why external shocks became political crises and why creditors acquired policy influence, and it does not determine what a particular government could achieve.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why neither dependency nor domestic governance failure can independently explain the debt crisis, and state the formulation that combines them.

**Model answer:** Dependency alone cannot explain the divergence of outcomes among states occupying the same structural position. Brazil ended the period with arguably the most advanced industrial sector in Latin America, at about a third of gross domestic product, and was ranked the world's sixth largest economy by the end of 2011, while Nicaragua was the poorest country in the Western hemisphere with 46 per cent below the poverty line; Argentina paid off all remaining IMF debts in January 2006 while growing at about 8 per cent, and a Bank of the South was launched as an alternative lender. A structure that can be partially exited within a decade constrains rather than determines. Domestic governance failure alone cannot explain simultaneity. The crisis struck military Brazil, single-party Mexico, democratic Venezuela and socialist Cuba within the same two years, which points to a common external cause, and the record identifies it: the doubling of American interest rates between 1979 and 1981, after which unpaid interest was added to the principal and new borrowing was required merely to service the old. It also cannot explain the pattern of intervention, since corruption was tolerated for decades in Somoza's Nicaragua and Batista's Cuba while reform was reversed in Guatemala, Brazil and Chile. The combining formulation is that external domination and internal inequality were not competing explanations but a single mechanism: concentrated landholding produced governments that could not survive redistribution and therefore required external backing, and that backing preserved the landholding - so that the external constraint was roughly constant across the region while the domestic configuration determined each outcome.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the synthesis as a diplomatic compromise between two positions. It is a substantive causal claim with testable consequences, and this session supplies the test. Where the domestic configuration differed, the outcome differed under an identical external constraint: Cuba's revolutionary army defeated the Bay of Pigs invasion where Guatemala's professional army had abstained in 1954; Mexico's incorporation of labour, peasant and military sectors into one party produced neither coup nor revolution after 1920; and Venezuela's army split in 2002 so that a coup with the same coalition and backing as earlier successful ones failed. An answer that writes "both factors mattered" without showing the variation has stated the conclusion without the argument.

## Lesson 21 - Revolution, reform and the answer architecture: converting a century into marks

Progress: 21/21 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the controlled comparison of revolutionary and reformist paths; the four conditions that decided each outcome; regional comparison and Global South agency; the Cold War connection stated without reducing the region to a pawn; the three owner boundaries restated in learner-facing terms; the demand map; the mark-scaled structures; the qualified theses and the verdict scaffolds
