Book context: queried - the topic's main knowledge file with its directive and demand map, qualified thesis templates, mark-scaled structures, verdict scaffolds and factual-risk controls, together with the advanced knowledge file's Mains frameworks and recorded probable questions and the core account's end-of-chapter questions for the Latin America chapter
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 18 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five attempts at social change, sorted by four variables

| Case | Route | Army's position | External patron available? | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Mexico 1910-17** | Armed revolution after a fraudulent election | Fragmented, then absorbed into the ruling party | Not needed; the United States intervened in 1916-17 and did not install a government | Survived; institutionalised into a party that held power for 71 years |
| **Guatemala 1944-54** | Elected reform, compensated and lawful | Professional army abstained - took no part in the coup and did not defend the president | None; a small agricultural exporter of bananas and coffee | Reversed in June 1954; 36 years of civil war followed |
| **Cuba 1953-59** | Armed revolution after the electoral route closed in 1952 | Batista's army crumbled; the new state built its own revolutionary army | Yes - the USSR promised to buy the sugar when the USA stopped | Survived; the Bay of Pigs invasion crushed; a non-invasion pledge obtained in 1962 |
| **Chile 1970-73** | Elected reform on 36 per cent, no congressional majority | Previously abstinent army drawn in by a massive strike and the opposition | None sought; relations restored with Cuba, China and East Germany but no economic patron | Destroyed in September 1973; 17 years of dictatorship |
| **Nicaragua 1979-90** | Armed revolution against a dynasty | Somoza's guard destroyed; a new army built | Cuba, but Cuba itself was under embargo and could not underwrite an economy | Survived militarily, exhausted economically; lost the 1990 election and accepted the result |

⚠️ Read the four middle columns across. ⚠️ **The two variables that discriminate are the army's position and the availability of an external patron.** ⚠️ Where the existing army was replaced by a revolutionary one, the government survived removal; where the professional army abstained or was recruited, it did not. ⚠️ And where a rival buyer or patron existed, the economic siege could be withstood; where it did not, the siege worked.

### 1. The comparison stated as an argument

⚠️ The commonest question on this topic, in one form or another, asks why revolution succeeded where reform failed, or the reverse. ⚠️ Here is the argument in five steps, each evidenced from the preceding lessons.

⚠️ **Step one: the provocation was the same in both routes.** ✅ Arbenz redistributed uncultivated land with compensation; ✅ Castro nationalised American-owned estates and factories; ✅ Allende nationalised the remainder of copper, textiles and banks; ✅ the Sandinistas redistributed 5 million acres. ⚠️ Reform and revolution reached for the same assets, because the same assets were the problem.

⚠️ **Step two: the response was therefore also the same in kind.** ✅ Guatemala: weapons for rebels, invasion, bombing. ✅ Cuba: sugar threat, embargo, invasion, sabotage. ✅ Chile: economic destabilisation and a coup prepared. ✅ Nicaragua: aid stopped, blockade, credit squeeze, financed insurgency. ⚠️ Route did not determine reaction; **the asset touched did.**

⚠️ **Step three: what differed was the capacity to absorb the response.** ⚠️ A reformist government inherits the state it found, including its army, its courts and its creditors. ⚠️ A revolutionary government has already destroyed the old army in the course of winning, and builds a new one loyal to itself. ⚠️ ✅ That is precisely why the Bay of Pigs failed where the Guatemalan operation succeeded.

⚠️ **Step four: but revolution's advantage is military and its vulnerability is economic.** ⚠️ A new army defeats an invasion; it does not find a buyer for the sugar. ⚠️ ✅ Cuba solved that by acquiring a superpower patron; ✅ Nicaragua could not, since its patron was itself embargoed - and Nicaragua was worn down economically, ending as the poorest country in the Western hemisphere.

⚠️ **Step five: so the honest conclusion is not that revolution works and reform does not.** ⚠️ ✅ Reform succeeded where it stayed inside the permitted band - Frei's Chile, which confiscated over 1,200 holdings and half the American copper holdings with compensation and received lavish American aid and admiration. ⚠️ ✅ Revolution succeeded where it could both defend itself and find an external economic patron - which in the entire century happened once. ⚠️ **The scarce resource was not revolutionary will; it was an alternative patron**, and there was only ever one on offer.

### 2. Regional comparison and Global South agency

⚠️ A question may ask how Latin America compares with Asia and Africa, or may invite a claim about the periphery's passivity. ⚠️ Three comparative points and three agency points are available.

⚠️ **Comparative point one: timing.** ⚠️ Latin America decolonised more than a century before Asia and Africa, which makes it the world's best evidence on what formal independence does and does not deliver. ⚠️ The answer it supplies - that sovereignty without structural change produces constrained sovereignty - is transferable to any question about post-colonial states.

⚠️ **Comparative point two: the form of domination.** ⚠️ Asia and Africa experienced direct colonial administration; Latin America experienced hegemony without administration. ⚠️ The comparison shows that **the absence of a colonial administration does not entail the absence of external control**, and that informal control can be harder to contest precisely because there is no colonial power to expel.

⚠️ **Comparative point three: the shared debt experience.** ✅ The mechanisms of the 1980s crisis - compounding interest, rescheduling under conditions, austerity reaching households through the budget, the currency, the price level and employment - are the same mechanisms that operated across the developing world in the same decade. ⚠️ The region is a case within a global phenomenon, and the thematic framework belongs to the world-economy owner.

⚠️ **Agency point one: Cuba as a world actor.** ✅ Backing the Marxist MPLA in Angola in 1975 and Mengistu in Ethiopia in 1977; ✅ regarded as a hero by most Third World countries.

⚠️ **Agency point two: regional diplomacy without the superpowers.** ✅ The Arias plan of 1987, assembled by a Central American president and awarded the Nobel Peace Prize; ✅ the Latin American parliament's 16 to 2 condemnation; ✅ the Organization of American States voting 20 to 1 against the United States over Panama.

⚠️ **Agency point three: institution-building after 2004.** ✅ ALBA, UNASUR with twelve members, and a Bank of the South offering loans as an alternative to the IMF. ⚠️ These are Global South institutions built by Global South states to reduce their exposure to Northern conditionality.

### 3. The Cold War connection, without the pawn reduction

⚠️ This is the most frequently mishandled aspect of the topic, so state the position precisely.

⚠️ **What the Cold War did.** ✅ It dragged the region into a global confrontation; ✅ it made Republican administrations fear a Soviet-Latin American Axis on the doorstep; ✅ it supplied a doctrine, carried multilaterally at the Organization of American States 17 to 1, under which any government could be classified as a hemispheric security threat; ⚠️ and it thereby converted domestic distributional conflicts into international security questions, removing them from the arena where they might have been settled.

⚠️ **What the Cold War did not do.** ⚠️ It did not create the land concentration, the commodity dependence, the militarised politics or the foreign ownership. ✅ All four were in place before 1945 and several before 1900. ⚠️ It did not supply the actors: ✅ the landowners who used the army, ✅ the officers who acted, ✅ the peasants who took the land, ✅ the priests who preached liberation and ✅ the unions that struck were all domestic.

⚠️ **And it did not survive its own ending as an explanation.** ✅ Panama in 1989 and Haiti in 1994 followed the Cold War's end, and ✅ the account itself notes that intervention could no longer be justified as part of the campaign against global communism. ⚠️ An explanation that stops working in 1991 was never the whole explanation.

⚠️ **The formulation to write:** the Cold War was the **frame through which** the region's conflicts were interpreted and the **licence under which** they were acted upon; it was not their cause, and the actors on every side were local.

### 4. The three boundaries, in learner-facing terms

⚠️ Demonstrating control of scope is itself worth marks, and it takes one sentence each.

⚠️ **The independence boundary.** The wars of liberation, their leaders, the five routes to independence, the colonial social hierarchy and caudillismo as a nineteenth-century form belong to the independence topic. ⚠️ This session used them as the inheritance it began from and did not narrate them.

⚠️ **The Cold War boundary.** The origins of the superpower confrontation, containment, the arms race, the crisis management of October 1962, alliance systems, deterrence, non-alignment and detente belong to the Cold War topic. ⚠️ This session taught what the confrontation did to Latin American states and retained from the missile crisis only what is a fact about Cuba - the non-invasion pledge, Castro's fury at being traded over his head, the expulsion from the regional organisation and the cooled relations that followed.

⚠️ **The world-economy boundary.** The thematic history of the world economy, North-South relations, the general framework of aid, trade and globalisation belongs to the world-economy topic. ⚠️ This session taught the region's own sequence from commodity dependence to industrialisation to borrowing to crisis to liberalisation, and handed the general framework across.

### 5. The demand map: what each question is really testing

| If the question says | ⚠️ It is really testing | ❌ Do not write |
|---|---|---|
| *Analyse the nature of United States domination* | Informal empire - influence without formal colonial rule, exercised through graduated instruments | "The USA controlled Latin America" |
| *Debt and dependency* | Economic constraint translating into political constraint, through a named mechanism | A finance summary |
| *Compare two Latin American states* | Real divergence in path and outcome, with the reason for the divergence | Two country notes side by side |
| *Assess the Cuban Revolution's significance* | Why it succeeded where reform elsewhere was reversed | A narrative of Castro's career |
| *Why was the region so unstable?* | Internal structure **plus** external leverage, interacting | Choosing one of the two |
| *Economic strategy since 1945* | The sequence from commodities to industrialisation to debt to liberalisation | Naming policies without the sequence |
| *Effects of the Cold War on the region* | The conversion of domestic distributional conflict into international security policy | A list of interventions |
| *The challenge to United States domination* | Graded divergence, institutions built, and what did not change | A list of presidents |

### 6. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis -> **two** mechanisms of United States influence **or two** structural problems -> one named case -> qualified verdict |
| **15** | Thesis -> the structural problems as mechanisms -> how domination actually worked -> **two contrasting cases** -> qualified verdict |
| **20** | All of the above -> **plus** the commodity-to-industry-to-debt-to-liberalisation sequence and genuine intra-regional variation -> **plus** one counter-position honestly stated -> qualified verdict |

⚠️ The 20-mark addition of "one counter-position honestly stated" is the discriminator at that level. ⚠️ ✅ The counter-positions available are named throughout this session: that domestic governance failure is independently documented and not reducible to external causes; that the military regimes produced real macroeconomic results; that the stabilisation of the 1990s genuinely defeated a regressive hyperinflation; and that the post-1998 turn changed politics far more than structure.

### 7. Qualified theses, ready to deploy

⚠️ Each of these is a first or last sentence that states a position and its qualification together, which is what an examiner means by a thesis.

⚠️ **On informal empire.** "Twentieth-century Latin America was governed less by direct rule than by structural dependence: United States strategic, financial and commercial leverage set the boundaries within which sovereign governments made real choices."

⚠️ **On the interaction.** "External domination and internal inequality were not competing explanations but a single mechanism: oligarchic landholding created governments that needed external backing, and external backing preserved oligarchic landholding."

⚠️ **On Cuba.** "Cuba matters not because it was the region's only revolution but because it was the only one that survived United States hostility - and it survived by exchanging one external patron for another."

⚠️ **On the economic arc.** "The region's twentieth-century economic history is a single arc: commodity dependence, an attempt to escape it through state-led industrialisation, a debt crisis that ended the attempt, and a liberalisation that restored openness without resolving inequality."

⚠️ **On the Cold War.** "The Cold War did not create Latin America's conflicts; it internationalised them, and in doing so removed them from the domestic arena in which they might have been settled."

⚠️ **On the post-1998 turn.** "After 1998 the region acquired the political capacity to elect governments Washington disliked and to survive attempts at their removal, without acquiring the economic diversification that would have made that capacity secure."

### 8. Verdict scaffolds

⚠️ **"Nature of United States domination?"** -> "Hegemony rather than empire: the United States rarely governed and consistently determined the limits of what could be governed, which is why the region produced sovereign states with constrained sovereignty."

⚠️ **"Debt and dependency?"** -> "The 1980s crisis achieved through creditors' conditions what intervention had achieved through force in 1954 - the reversal of state-led development - and did so with far less political cost to Washington."

⚠️ **"Why did revolution rarely succeed?"** -> "Because reform that touched land or foreign assets converted a domestic dispute into an international one, and only Cuba found an external patron willing to underwrite the consequences."

⚠️ **"Compare two states."** -> "Brazil industrialised without democratising and Mexico institutionalised a revolution without pluralising it - two different ways of postponing the same social question."

⚠️ **"Was the region a pawn of the Cold War?"** -> "It was a theatre, not a pawn: the frame was imposed from outside, and every decisive act within it - the coup, the abstention, the rising, the election, the acceptance of defeat - was taken locally."

### 9. Factual-risk controls: the list to check before submitting

⚠️ Run through these before writing the final sentence of any answer on this topic.

1. ❌ Do not say that United States influence meant there was no local agency. ✅ Domestic elites, armies, parties and social movements mattered throughout.
2. ❌ Do not say that commodity wealth guaranteed prosperity. ✅ Venezuela is the warning example, still at around 90 per cent oil in 2011 and shrinking in 2009.
3. ❌ Do not say that revolution solved inequality. ✅ Mexico and Nicaragua both show the limits.
4. ❌ Do not treat the debt crisis as purely financial. ✅ It reached society through the budget, the currency, the price level and employment.
5. ❌ Do not date the key episodes loosely. ✅ Mexican Revolution from 1910 with the constitution in 1917; Guatemala 1944-54 and the peace agreement of December 1996; Cuba 1959, the Bay of Pigs 1961 and the missile crisis 1962; Chile 1970-73 and the transition of 1990; Brazil 1964-85; Argentina 1976-83; the Sandinista victory of 1979 and the election of 1990; the Mexican debt emergency of 1982; the regional total by 1985; Chavez from 1998.
6. ❌ Do not attribute the "international police power" phrase to Monroe. ✅ It is the Roosevelt Corollary of 1904.
7. ❌ Do not name individual nineteenth-century caudillos. ✅ The category and its mechanism are source-grounded; no list is held.
8. ❌ Do not merge, add, round or convert any mortality, disappearance or torture figure. ✅ Each belongs to one body, one country and one period.
9. ❌ Do not assert the disputed Brazilian debt and IMF credit magnitudes. ⚠️ The source is internally inconsistent; teach the events.
10. ❌ Do not import India's liberalisation material. ⚠️ Cross-refer to the economy owner and keep the comparison to a signposted clause.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ⚠️ This lesson is the conversion layer: it turns the twenty preceding lessons into structures that fit a General Studies answer under time pressure.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Every framing in section 5 has appeared in one form or another in the owners' recorded probable questions or in the core account's end-of-chapter questions, which are listed in the verified-question section below.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing the structures without the evidence. ⚠️ A thesis with no named case is an assertion.
2. ⚠️ Using all six qualified theses in one answer. ⚠️ Choose the one that matches the demand.
3. ⚠️ Treating the mark-scaled structures as templates to be filled identically. ⚠️ The 20-mark structure specifically requires a counter-position, which the others do not.
4. ⚠️ Crossing a boundary because the material is attractive. ⚠️ A missile-crisis narrative inside a Latin America answer is off-demand however well written.

### Revision notes

1. ⚠️ The comparison's two discriminating variables: the army's position, and the availability of an external patron.
2. ⚠️ Reform and revolution reached for the same assets, so the reaction was determined by the asset touched, not by the route.
3. ⚠️ Revolution's advantage is military - a new army defeats invasion; its vulnerability is economic - a new army does not find a buyer.
4. ⚠️ Reform succeeded inside the permitted band: Frei's Chile received lavish American aid after confiscating over 1,200 holdings and half the American copper holdings with compensation.
5. ⚠️ Comparative points: Latin America decolonised a century earlier and shows what formal independence does not deliver; hegemony without administration can be harder to contest than colonial rule; the 1980s debt mechanisms were global.
6. ⚠️ Agency points: Cuba in Angola and Ethiopia; the Arias plan and its Nobel Peace Prize; the 16 to 2 and 20 to 1 votes; ALBA, UNASUR and the Bank of the South.
7. ⚠️ The Cold War was the frame and the licence, not the cause; it did not create the land structure, the commodity dependence, the militarised politics or the foreign ownership, and interventions continued after it ended.
8. ⚠️ Three boundaries: independence wars to the independence owner; the superpower confrontation to the Cold War owner; the thematic world economy to the world-economy owner.
9. ⚠️ The demand map converts eight framings into what each is actually testing.
10. ⚠️ Mark-scaled structures: thesis plus two mechanisms plus one case at 10; add the domination mechanism and two contrasting cases at 15; add the full economic sequence, genuine variation and one honest counter-position at 20.
11. ⚠️ Six qualified theses and five verdict scaffolds are available; choose by demand rather than by preference.
12. ⚠️ Ten factual-risk controls, headed by the agency control and the attribution control.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation for the whole topic: the century is the story of sovereign states with constrained sovereignty, in which the external constraint was roughly constant and the domestic configuration determined the outcome.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the four variables that distinguish the five cases, explain in one connected argument why only one attempt at fundamental social change in twentieth-century Latin America both survived removal and kept its programme.

**Model answer:** The provocation was constant, so it cannot be the discriminator: Arbenz redistributed uncultivated land with compensation, Castro nationalised American-owned estates and factories, Allende nationalised the remainder of copper along with textiles and banks, and the Sandinistas redistributed 5 million acres to about 100,000 families. The response was correspondingly constant in kind - weapons for rebels and bombing in Guatemala, a sugar threat, embargo, invasion and sabotage against Cuba, economic destabilisation and a prepared coup in Chile, and a blockade, credit squeeze and financed insurgency against Nicaragua. What differed was capacity to absorb the response, and it turned on two variables. The first is the army. A reformist government inherits the state's existing army: Guatemala's took no part in the coup but did not defend the president, and Chile's, previously abstinent from politics, was won over by an opposition that had first organised a massive strike. A revolutionary government has destroyed the old army in winning and built its own, which is why about 1,400 men at the Bay of Pigs were crushed by Castro's forces and two jet planes. The second is the external patron. A new army defeats an invasion but cannot sell the sugar crop; when Eisenhower carried out the threat to stop importing it in July 1960, the USSR promised to buy it, and in 1962 the settlement yielded a promise that the United States would not invade again. Nicaragua had the first variable and not the second, since its ally Cuba was itself embargoed, so it survived militarily and was exhausted economically, leaving office in 1990 with the economy in ruins. Only Cuba possessed both, which is why only Cuba both survived and kept its programme.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that revolution is therefore the superior route. Reform succeeded wherever it stayed inside the permitted band: Frei in Chile confiscated over 1,200 inefficiently run private holdings for landless peasants and took over about half the holdings in the American-owned copper mines with suitable compensation, and the American government admired his reforms and poured in lavish economic aid. The scarce resource was never revolutionary determination - it was an alternative patron willing to underwrite the consequences, and in the whole century exactly one was on offer.

---

✅ **From the canonical owner:** analyse the nature of United States domination in twentieth-century Latin America; discuss the role of debt and dependency in shaping Latin American politics; compare two Latin American states to show the variety within the region. ✅ Its directive and demand map additionally identifies the framings on the Cuban Revolution's significance, on why the region was so unstable, and on economic strategy since 1945.

✅ **From the advanced owner:** examine the nature of United States domination in Latin America during the twentieth century; why did debt become a central political issue in Latin America; and the statement for comment that "Latin American instability was produced jointly by domestic inequality and external intervention". ✅ Its Mains framework table adds three flows - the nature of domination, why instability persisted, and an assessment of the challenge to United States dominance.

✅ **From the core account's own end-of-chapter questions for the Latin America chapter:** what were the problems facing the countries of Latin America at the end of the Second World War, and why was progress in solving them so slow; in what ways and with what motives was the United States involved in the affairs of Latin America during the second half of the twentieth century; how far do you agree that "the Cold War was to have profound effects on the economic and political systems of Latin America"; and, on the statement that the election of Hugo Chavez in 1998 was the beginning of a left-wing revolution that was to transform Latin America over the next decade, what happened in that transformation and whether the statement is an accurate assessment or an exaggeration.

## What a topic with no owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a nil return over nine years should change preparation rather than reduce it, and three things follow.

1. ⚠️ **The topic is examined through other papers and other owners.** ✅ Every question in the span that touches a Latin American subject is routed to geography, economy, environment or international relations. ⚠️ The material taught here is the historical foundation of answers written in other papers, and a candidate who treats a world-history nil return as permission to skip the topic will meet Venezuela, Brazil and Chile elsewhere without the depth that makes an answer good.
2. ⚠️ **The Paper I syllabus clause covers it directly.** ✅ The syllabus names world history from the eighteenth century onward, including colonization and decolonization, and political philosophies such as communism, capitalism and socialism, their forms and their effect on society. ⚠️ Latin America is the syllabus's best available case of what follows the **completion** of decolonisation, and it supplies non-European evidence for every one of the three named philosophies - a socialist state in Cuba, capitalism's regional form in the commodity and debt sequence, and the distinctively Latin American populist synthesis of Lesson 6.
3. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** ⚠️ Lesson 21 lists six qualified theses and five verdict scaffolds. ⚠️ The two that transfer fastest to an unanticipated stem are the interaction thesis - that oligarchic landholding created governments needing external backing and that the backing preserved the landholding - and the variation thesis, that the external constraint was roughly constant while the domestic configuration determined the outcome.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - the inheritance and the instruments, Lessons 1 to 3

### Cumulative check A1 - one mechanism, five inheritances

**Question:** State the five inheritances the twentieth century received from the independence settlement, and show each one producing a specific twentieth-century consequence.

**Model answer:** The first is sovereignty without an established civilian political class: Iberian rule left no tradition of colonial self-government, so the record states that there was no tradition of democracy except in Chile and that states were dominated by wealthy landowners and run by military dictators called caudillos. The second is concentrated land: Peru was dominated by huge estates whose owners ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs, which produced both a domestic market too poor to sustain industry and a political class that could not survive redistribution - the consequence visible in the reform-blocking coups of Guatemala in 1954, Bolivia and Brazil in 1964, Argentina in 1966 and Chile in 1973. The third is single-commodity export structure - Chile on copper, Cuba on sugar and tobacco, Bolivia on tin at 80 per cent of revenue in the 1950s - which made a price fall a political rather than an economic event, as it was in Venezuela in the early 1980s, Mexico in 1986 and Ecuador in 1998. The fourth is an army made by the wars of liberation and habituated to arbitration, which in the twentieth century shifted from fighting rival strongmen to blocking social reform. The fifth is the hemispheric frame: Monroe's declaration of December 1823 closed the Americas to European recolonisation, and Roosevelt's corollary of December 1904 converted it into a claimed international police power, so that the outside power which mattered became continental rather than transatlantic.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the five as a list of grievances rather than as mechanisms. Each one has to be stated with the consequence attached - land concentration suppressing the domestic market, single-commodity exposure making the budget a function of foreign prices - or the answer has described the region's condition without explaining its politics.

### Cumulative check A2 - the ladder, and why it stayed low

**Question:** Explain why the United States climbed its escalation ladder so rarely, and what that tells us about the correct description of its predominance.

**Model answer:** Because the lower rungs usually worked, and they were cheaper. The bottom rung was structural presence: American companies held controlling interests in all Cuban industries, owned half the land, three-fifths of the railways, all electricity and the entire telephone system, the United Fruit Company was Guatemala's biggest landowner, and the American ambassador in Havana was called the second most important man in Cuba. The second rung was economic leverage: aid was cut when Castro and Allende came to power and resumed when governments changed, loans were tied to purchases of United States products, and Venezuelan steel imports were cut from 550,000 tonnes a year to 110,000 for five years in March 1985. Covert destabilisation was the third, proxy invasion the fourth and troops the fifth, and the record shows the higher rungs used sparingly - 20,000 troops in the Dominican Republic in 1965 and an invasion of Panama in 1989 in which at least 2,000 civilians were killed. Higher rungs were also costly: after Guatemala, anti-American feeling spread and "Yankee go home" became a common slogan, and the Eisenhower government was judged to have overestimated the communist threat while violating non-intervention and souring relations with the region. The correct description is therefore hegemony rather than empire - predominance exercised through ownership, trade, credit, intelligence and local allies, with government left in local hands.

**Misconception to avoid:** Inferring that hegemony was therefore mild. It included the bombing of a capital city, a decade of financed insurgency against schools and clinics, the preparation of a coup in a functioning democracy and an invasion with at least 2,000 civilian deaths. Hegemony describes the form of predominance, not its severity.

## Block B - the country experiments, Lessons 4 to 14

### Cumulative check B1 - the outer limit of permissible policy, located precisely

**Question:** Using four cases, locate the exact boundary beyond which a Latin American government attracted a removal attempt, and identify the one case that shows the boundary moving.

**Model answer:** The boundary ran along major foreign-owned property and external alignment, not along ideology or radicalism. Guatemala shows how mild a measure could cross it: the reform of June 1952 took uncultivated land on large holdings and compensated owners in government bonds, yet because United Fruit Company holdings were among those affected the government was removed in June 1954 by an invasion from Honduras and Nicaragua with American air support. Chile shows that the boundary could be crossed by an elected Marxist nationalising the remainder of copper, textiles and banks and restoring relations with Cuba, China and East Germany. Brazil shows it could be crossed without socialism at all: Goulart, a millionaire landowner and devout Roman Catholic, proposed to widen the franchise, limit multinational profit remittance, nationalise oil refineries, open relations with the USSR and oppose sanctions on Cuba, and was removed in April 1964. Chile also shows where the boundary was not: Frei confiscated over 1,200 private holdings for landless peasants and took about half the American-owned copper holdings with compensation, and the American government admired his reforms and poured in lavish aid. The case that shows the boundary moving is Mexico, which nationalised oil outright in 1938 under the Good Neighbor Policy and met a trade boycott and a compensation agreement in November 1941 rather than a coup.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the boundary was drawn at reform as such. Frei's programme was substantial and was rewarded, and the Alliance for Progress launched in 1961 was explicitly designed to encourage moderate reforming governments. What attracted the higher rungs of the ladder was reform that was uncompensated, that reached a whole sector or a major American asset, or that came attached to an alignment read as hostile.

### Cumulative check B2 - writing about mass violence in this topic

**Question:** Set out the rules this topic requires for writing about political killing, and apply them to five episodes.

**Model answer:** The rules are four. Every figure travels with the body or account that produced it and the period it covers; no figure is rounded, totalled, averaged or merged across cases; qualifiers such as "some", "up to", "around" and "estimates vary" are retained because they name the measurement's limits; and responsibility is attributed to named governments, agencies, armies, militias and leaders rather than to nations. Applied: for Argentina between 1976 and 1983, a human rights commission charged the junta with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people - three separate counts of three different things. For Chile, around 3,000 people were killed or disappeared during Pinochet's 17 years, with tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or driven into exile. For Guatemala, some 200,000 people were killed during the 36 years of civil war, well over half of them Mayan Indians targeted because of their militancy, and separately 3,252 political murders were calculated in the four months from October 1979 to January 1980 under a named president. For Mexico in 1968, troops fired on an estimated 10,000 students and estimates of those killed vary between 30 and 300. For Panama in December 1989, at least 2,000 civilians were killed when United States forces invaded and bombed. Brazil's military government is recorded with reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners and no figure at all, which must be reported as such.

**Misconception to avoid:** Producing a regional total. No source in this session offers one, the counts measure incompatible things across incompatible periods, and an answer that adds a Chilean figure to an Argentine one has manufactured a number that does not exist. The discipline is not squeamishness: an examiner can verify an attributed figure and cannot verify an aggregate.

### Cumulative check B3 - the army as the swing variable

**Question:** Show that the position of the national army, rather than the strength of external pressure, determined the outcome in four cases.

**Model answer:** In Guatemala in June 1954 the invading force was small and the official army took no part in the coup but equally made no attempt to defend Arbenz; that abstention, by an officer corps allied to the landed elite the reform threatened, decided the outcome. In Cuba in April 1961 a comparable operation - about 1,400 men landing at the Bay of Pigs from American bases in Guatemala with deep Central Intelligence Agency involvement - was crushed without difficulty by Castro's forces and two jet planes, because the old army had been destroyed in winning power and the new one was the revolution's own. In Chile in September 1973 an army that had traditionally played little part in politics was drawn in after the opposition organised a massive strike and won its support, and leading generals then established the dictatorship. In Venezuela in 2002 the same coalition as in earlier successful coups - sections of the army, oil executives and right-wingers, partly financed by the United States - kidnapped the president, and he was rescued by officers loyal to him amid massive street demonstrations. External pressure was present in all four; the army's position differed in all four, and it tracks the outcome exactly.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that external pressure was therefore unimportant. It supplied the weapons and the air support in Guatemala, prepared the Chilean coup with Brazilian assistance, financed the Venezuelan opposition and rehearsed invasions of Cuba. The claim is not that the external variable was absent but that it was roughly constant, so the variation in outcomes must be explained by the variable that changed.

## Block C - structures, society and the long argument, Lessons 15 to 21

### Cumulative check C1 - one arc, four joints

**Question:** Set out the region's economic century as a single arc and name the force that drove each transition.

**Model answer:** Stage one is commodity export dependence - Chile on copper, Cuba on sugar and tobacco, Bolivia on tin at 80 per cent of revenue in the 1950s - whose weakness is that a fall in the world price of that commodity would be a major disaster. The first joint is an external shock creating an opportunity: the Depression and then the Second World War made European manufactures almost impossible to obtain, which acted as a stimulus to factory industry. Stage two is import-substituting industrialisation behind protection, with state investment, subsidised credit and planning - Brazilian economic planning under Vargas, Venezuelan steel plants that made iron and steel the country's most valuable export. The second joint is an internal limit: markets were restricted because the vast majority were too poverty-stricken to provide purchasing power, capital goods and fuel still had to be imported, and fiscal deficits followed. Stage three is external borrowing, at its height from 1973 until 1982, sustaining the strategy; Brazil's generals borrowed extensively and grew at 10 per cent a year from 1968 to 1974. The third joint is an external shock removing the financing: the doubling of American interest rates between 1979 and 1981, after which unpaid interest was added to principal. Stage four is crisis, austerity and liberalisation - some $368 billion owed by 1985, Brazil suspending interest payments in February 1987, and stabilisation programmes that defeated inflation without defeating unemployment or poverty.

**Misconception to avoid:** Narrating the four stages without naming the forces at the joints. The examinable content is that the first transition was an external opportunity, the second an internal constraint rooted in the land structure, and the third an external shock - which is why the arc cannot be explained by domestic policy alone or by external forces alone.

### Cumulative check C2 - what the post-1998 turn did and did not change

**Question:** Assess the claim that the election of Chavez in 1998 began a transformation of Latin America, using evidence on both sides.

**Model answer:** Politically the change was real. The end of the Cold War removed the justification for constant intervention, and governments elected on explicitly anti-Washington programmes served full terms: Chavez in 1998 with 56 per cent, Lula in 2002, Kirchner in 2003, Morales in December 2005, Bachelet in January 2006, Correa and Ortega in 2006. A coup attempt in 2002 by the familiar coalition failed because officers loyal to the president rescued him amid mass demonstrations. Institutions followed - ALBA in 2004, UNASUR in 2008 with twelve members by 2011, and a Bank of the South in 2009 lending as an alternative to the IMF, after which the Fund was judged to be on the verge of eclipse in the region. Social results are recorded: United Nations statistics show Venezuelan poverty at 54 per cent in 1998 against 28 per cent in 2008, and Argentina grew at about 8 per cent and paid off all remaining IMF debts in January 2006. Structurally the change was marginal. Venezuela remained overdependent on oil at around 90 per cent of export revenue and shrank by about 3 per cent in 2009; the new Chinese relationships were with raw-materials exporters; Nicaragua in 2011 was still the poorest country in Latin America with 46 per cent below the poverty line; and the United States divided Mexico, Chile and Guatemala from the rest by separate trade agreements while Mexico moved in the opposite ideological direction.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the turn as uniform. The record grades it three ways - Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador and Argentina most decisive; Brazil, Nicaragua and Uruguay middle of the road; Mexico, Chile and Guatemala held close by separate agreements - and an answer that describes a single regional movement has failed the variation test this topic applies most severely.

### Cumulative check C3 - society, and the difference between access and distribution

**Question:** Show that the region's social movements widened access to power far more than they altered the distribution of wealth.

**Model answer:** Access widened measurably on three dimensions. By class origin, a labour movement created by populist leaders and targeted under military rule - trade unionists were a favourite target in Brazil - produced the Workers' Party candidate who won the Brazilian presidency in 2002 and was re-elected in 2006. By ethnicity, Evo Morales, an Aymara Indian elected in December 2005, was the first Bolivian president to come from the country's ethnic majority, and his route ran through a coca growers' union formed after eradication by 2001 destroyed thousands of livelihoods. By gender, Chamorro in Nicaragua in 1990 was the first female president elected in Latin America, Bachelet was Chile's first woman president from 2006, Fernandez de Kirchner was elected in 2007 and re-elected in October 2011 by the largest margin since 1983, and Rousseff became Brazil's first woman president in January 2011; Venezuela required at least 50 per cent female candidates from political parties. Distribution changed far less. About a third of Mexico's population was still below the poverty line in 2000; Nicaragua in 2011 remained the poorest country in Latin America with 46 per cent below the line; huge shanty-towns still ringed Mexican cities and rural areas were often neglected; and Guatemala's Mayan population supplied well over half of the some 200,000 killed in a war fought over land.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reporting the widening of representation as though it were redistribution. The two are distinct achievements with distinct evidence, and the strongest answers state the gap between them explicitly rather than letting a list of firsts stand in for a change in the distribution of land and income.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions below are **original practice**, because this topic owns no verified question in the 2018-2026 span. ⚠️ Each model answer is written to the stated word ceiling and is not to be exceeded; each carries a distinct scoring note.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Question:** Examine why the twentieth-century states of Latin America are best described as sovereign states with constrained sovereignty rather than as colonies. *(Answer in 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** They were not colonies. Every state had been independent since the 1820s, with its own constitution, army and elections; Mexico nationalised oil in 1938 and kept it, Venezuela raised its oil share to 60 per cent in 1958, and Brazil's generals governed on their own terms. Yet a narrow policy band existed within which action attracted an external response. Guatemala's compensated reform of uncultivated land brought an invasion in June 1954; Goulart's proposal to limit multinational profit remittance preceded his removal in April 1964; Allende's nationalisations preceded a coup the Central Intelligence Agency helped prepare. The United States expected political influence in return for aid and intervened where a government was deemed unacceptable. That is a claim about limits, not about administration. The accurate description is hegemony: predominance through ownership, trade, credit, intelligence and local allies, leaving government in local hands and the boundary of permissible policy set abroad.

> **Scoring note.** The discriminator is the refusal of both extremes. A script that writes "colony" or "the USA controlled Latin America" loses the mechanism; a script that treats independence as substantive loses the constraint. Name one concession (1938, 1958) and one limit case (1954, 1964 or 1973) and the band is established in two sentences.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Question:** Discuss how the debt crisis of the 1980s reached Latin American households. *(Answer in 150 words.)*

**Model answer:** Through four channels. The budget: Mexico made drastic reductions in public-service spending in 1982 and Venezuela cut government spending under IMF requirements, so debt service displaced health, education and subsidies. The currency: Mexico devalued by 70 per cent, raising the domestic price of imported food, fuel and medicine immediately and regressively. The price level: Argentina was left with inflation of around 900 per cent, Brazil suffered hyper-inflation under Sarney, and Venezuelan inflation ran at not far short of 40 per cent - inflation being a regressive tax on those holding cash and earning fixed wages. Employment: Brazil's account freeze deprived the economy of capital and produced business failures and massive unemployment, while Mexican privatisation did nothing for unemployment. A fifth effect was political. Adjustment was imposed by creditors and administered by new democracies rather than by the military governments that had borrowed, so democracy itself became associated with hardship.

> **Scoring note.** The fifth, political effect is where the marks separate. Most scripts stop at austerity; this answer must name the transfer of the bill from the dictatorships that contracted the debt to the democracies that repaid it, because that is what explains the electoral turn after 1998.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Question:** "Latin American instability was produced jointly by domestic inequality and external intervention." Critically examine. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The statement is correct, and its value lies in the word "jointly" - the two were one mechanism rather than two causes.

The domestic component is documented. Agriculture remained backward because plentiful cheap peasant labour removed any incentive to modernise, and Peru's estate owners ruled their peasants like feudal monarchs. There was no tradition of democracy except in Chile; states were dominated by wealthy landowners and run by military dictators. When post-war democratic governments attempted reform, landowners used the army to block them or remove them - in Guatemala in 1954, Bolivia and Brazil in 1964, Argentina in 1966 and Chile in 1973.

The external component is equally documented. Aid came with mixed motives, political influence was expected in return, and governments deemed unacceptable were removed in Guatemala, Brazil, the Dominican Republic, Chile, Nicaragua, Panama and Haiti.

The joining mechanism is precise. Concentrated landholding produced governments that could not survive redistribution and therefore required an external guarantor; the guarantor, having guaranteed them, preserved the landholding; the resulting narrow domestic market limited industrialisation; industrialisation required external borrowing; and borrowing converted an economic exposure into a political constraint.

Two qualifications prevent determinism. Domestic failure is independently real and not externally caused - Zelaya looted the Nicaraguan treasury and invaded two neighbours, and Batista attended to his foreign fortunes rather than to government. And outcomes varied under an identical constraint, because the domestic configuration differed: Cuba's revolutionary army defeated an invasion that Guatemala's professional army had declined to resist.

> **Scoring note.** The mark ceiling is reached by the joining mechanism, not by the two lists. A script that alternates domestic and external evidence without stating how each sustained the other has agreed with the quotation rather than examined it.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Question:** Compare the Cuban and Chilean attempts at social transformation, and account for their different outcomes. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The programmes were comparable and the routes were opposite.

Cuba's came after the electoral route closed: Batista seized power by military coup in 1952, introduced no reforms and ruled corruptly and brutally, so the feeling grew that violent revolution was necessary. Castro, before power more a liberal nationalist than a communist, built support in the mountains by carrying through land reform in the areas he held, and Batista fled on 1 January 1959.

Chile's came through the constitution: Allende won 36 per cent against 35 in September 1970, the world's first Marxist leader voted into power democratically, and his coalition's vote rose to 49.7 per cent in April 1971.

Both then reached for the same assets - Castro nationalising American-owned estates and factories, Allende the remainder of copper, textiles and banks - and both met the same response: embargo, sabotage and invasion against Cuba, economic destabilisation and a prepared coup against Chile, with the Central Intelligence Agency helped by Brazil's military regime.

Two variables explain the divergence. The army: Cuba's old army had been destroyed in winning, so about 1,400 men at the Bay of Pigs were crushed, whereas Chile's abstinent army was won over by an opposition that first organised a massive strike. The patron: when Eisenhower stopped importing Cuban sugar in July 1960 the USSR promised to buy it, and the 1962 settlement yielded a promise not to invade again; Chile had no economic patron and faced a copper price falling about 30 per cent.

> **Scoring note.** Credit the concession that Chile's economic difficulties were substantially self-inflicted - farmers stopped sowing, investors withdrew, copper production fell - because a script that omits them cannot explain why a domestic opposition was available to solicit the army.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Question:** Assess the proposition that in Latin America the Cold War internationalised domestic conflicts rather than causing them. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The proposition is sound, with one qualification.

The conflicts predated the Cold War. Land concentration, commodity dependence, militarised politics and foreign ownership were in place before 1945: the United Fruit Company had been expanding in Guatemala since 1901 and controlled almost half its best agricultural land by the Second World War; Mexico's revolution began in 1910; and the claim to an international police power dates from 1904.

What the Cold War supplied was a frame and a licence. The region was dragged into a confrontation it had not generated, Republican administrations feared a Soviet-Latin American Axis on the doorstep, and a resolution declaring communist domination of any hemispheric state a threat to all members was carried at the Organization of American States 17 to one. Under that frame a domestic dispute about estates became a security question, removed from the arena where it might have been settled. Goulart, a millionaire landowner and devout Roman Catholic, was accused of communism when the charge could not be taken seriously.

The qualification: the frame does not exhaust the explanation. Interventions continued at its end and beyond - Panama in 1989 and Haiti in 1994 - and the record concedes that the demise of the communist enemy removed the justification rather than the practice. Anti-communism was dominant but not the sole driver.

The decisive point is agency. The abstention, the coup, the rising, the election and the acceptance of defeat were all local acts. The region was a theatre, not a pawn.

> **Scoring note.** Two things lift this to the top band: dating a structural cause before 1945, and using a post-1991 intervention to show that the Cold War frame does not explain everything. A script that only demonstrates local agency has answered half the proposition.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Question:** "The region's twentieth-century economic history is a single arc rather than a sequence of separate episodes." Evaluate with reference to the period from the Second World War to the early twenty-first century. *(Answer in 250 words.)*

**Model answer:** The arc holds, and its joints carry the argument.

It begins in commodity dependence: Chile on copper, Cuba on sugar and tobacco, Bolivia drawing 80 per cent of revenue from tin in the 1950s, where a fall in the world price would be a major disaster. The Depression and the war then supplied involuntary protection, since European manufactures became almost impossible to obtain, and governments converted that accident into policy - import-substituting industrialisation behind tariffs, with state investment, subsidised credit and planning.

The strategy met an internal ceiling. Markets were restricted because the vast majority were too poverty-stricken to provide purchasing power, and since the strategy deliberately avoided land reform the rural majority never became consumers. Capital goods and fuel still had to be imported, so Brazil's growth fell from 10 per cent a year to around 5 after 1974.

External credit postponed the contradiction. Borrowing peaked between 1973 and 1982, and by then the seven largest United States banks drew 60 per cent of their profits from Third World interest, against 2 per cent in 1970. The doubling of American interest rates in 1979-81 ended it; unpaid interest was added to principal, and by 1985 the region owed some $368 billion amid a constant drain of capital northwards.

Liberalisation followed and defeated inflation without defeating poverty. The evaluation must concede variation - Brazil built the region's most advanced industrial sector - but the arc's unity shows in its closing condition: Venezuela at around 90 per cent oil in 2011.

> **Scoring note.** The top band requires the demand ceiling to be traced to the land structure. A script that lists the four stages accurately but treats the market constraint as a technical problem rather than as the agrarian question in another form has described the arc without explaining why it bent.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ Five error families, each with the correction and the evidence that settles it. ⚠️ These are the mistakes that most often cost marks on this topic, drawn from the traps recorded lesson by lesson.

## Error family A - dates and what they date

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Attributing "international police power" to the Monroe Doctrine | Monroe, December 1823, declared the Americas closed to future European colonization; the police-power claim is Theodore Roosevelt's corollary of December 1904 |
| Dating the Mexican Revolution to the constitution | The revolution begins in 1910 with the fraudulent election; the constitution is promulgated at Queretaro on 5 February 1917; the ruling party consolidates after 1920 |
| Treating 1954 as the end of the Guatemalan episode | The civil war ran on to the formal peace agreement of December 1996 - 36 years and some 200,000 dead |
| Placing Castro's declaration of Marxism before the Bay of Pigs | The invasion was in April 1961; the declaration came later the same year |
| Dating the Chilean coup to Allende's election | Elected September 1970; overthrown September 1973; the transition to Aylwin came in 1990 |
| Collapsing the Nicaraguan sequence | Marines from 1912 to 1925 and again to 1933; Somoza rule to 1979; Sandinista victory 1979; Contra war through the 1980s; election lost 1990; return by election 2006 |
| Running the debt dates together | Borrowing peaks 1973-82; rates double 1979-81; Mexico's emergency 1982; the regional total by 1985; Brazil suspends February 1987 |

## Error family B - figures and their attribution

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Producing a regional death toll | No source offers one; the counts measure different things over different periods and must never be added |
| Merging the Argentine charges | 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people are three separate charges by a human rights commission |
| Giving a single Tlatelolco figure | Estimates of the numbers killed vary between 30 and 300 |
| Supplying a Brazilian torture figure | The record states reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners and gives no number |
| Reporting the Brazilian debt or IMF credit magnitudes | The core account is internally inconsistent between passages; teach the events - the February 1987 suspension and the arrangement of credits - not the magnitudes |
| Restating attributed economic figures as fact | Bolivia's growth of 4.9 per cent is a study's finding reported by Chomsky; Venezuela's 54 and 28 per cent are United Nations statistics for 1998 and 2008; the murder-rate comparison is a named journalist's in a dated article |
| Generalising Argentina's inflation | Around 900 per cent is stated for Argentina after the Falklands defeat and for no other country |

## Error family C - mechanisms and causation

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| "Poverty caused instability" | Poverty suppressed the domestic market, which prevented industrialisation from completing, which kept employment low - state the loop, not the label |
| Attributing the reform-blocking coups to military ambition | Landowners used the army to block reforms or remove governments; the initiative is social and the army is the instrument |
| Treating the 1910 fraud as the cause of the Mexican Revolution | It closed the last non-violent channel after three decades of economic, organisational and national exclusion |
| Explaining the Cuban alignment as a prior plan | The escalation ran nationalisation, sugar threat, Soviet purchase, confiscation, rupture, invasion - and only then the declaration |
| Explaining the Chilean coup by one cause | Four domestic pressures plus external destabilisation plus a constitutional fear about September 1976 |
| Crediting the Brazilian miracle to authoritarian efficiency | Lenders who refused Goulart financed the generals; the growth measures the political preferences of creditors |
| Treating the Contra war as a military campaign | The targets were bridges, schools, health clinics and crops - the outputs of the reform programme |

## Error family D - categories and comparisons

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Calling the region a colony | Independent since the 1820s; the correct category is hegemony, and the correct formula is constrained sovereignty |
| Calling Vargas and Peron fascists | Influenced by fascism and adopting mass mobilisation, but summed up as nationalism plus social reform; European fascism destroyed labour movements while the Latin American leaders it inspired created them |
| Treating the military regimes as caudillo dictatorships | They were institutional regimes of the armed forces with a doctrine of internal war, delegated economic policy and negotiated exits |
| Describing the Sandinista economy as socialist | They allowed a mixed economy of state and privately owned business |
| Treating the post-1998 turn as uniform | Graded three ways: most decisive - Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador, Argentina; middle of the road - Brazil, Nicaragua, Uruguay; held close by separate trade agreements - Mexico, Chile, Guatemala |
| Treating "Latin America" as one political unit | The variation discipline: any answer that does so has already lost marks |
| Treating representation as redistribution | Access widened by class, ethnicity and gender; poverty figures changed far less |

## Error family E - boundaries, scope and voice

| ❌ The error | ✅ The correction |
|---|---|
| Narrating the missile crisis | Owned by the Cold War topic; this session retains only the non-invasion pledge, Castro's fury, the regional expulsion and the cooled relations |
| Narrating the independence wars | Owned by the independence topic; used here only as the inheritance |
| Importing the general framework of the world economy | Owned by the world-economy topic; the region's own debt sequence is owned here |
| Importing India's liberalisation content | Owned by the economy topic; cross-refer and keep any comparison to a signposted clause |
| Naming individual nineteenth-century caudillos | The category and mechanism are source-grounded; no list of individuals is held, and inventing one is a direct factual risk |
| Denying local agency | Domestic elites, armies, parties and social movements mattered throughout; no government in this session is described as a puppet |
| Using the current item as historical evidence | The dated statement of 19 March 2026 shows only that the transitional-justice settlement remains contested |
