# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

## 1. The inheritance from independence

Independence from Spain, and for Brazil from Portugal, in the early nineteenth century; shared industrial and agricultural underdevelopment, poverty, illiteracy and unstable political systems, with revolutions, coups and assassinations commonplace. Five inheritances: sovereignty without an established civilian political class; concentrated land, with Peru's estate owners ruling their peasants like feudal monarchs; single-commodity export structures; armies habituated to arbitration, the twentieth-century successors to nineteenth-century caudillismo; and a hemispheric frame set by Monroe in December 1823 and converted by Roosevelt's corollary of December 1904 into a claimed international police power. Master formulation: independence transferred sovereignty without transforming social power, producing sovereign states with constrained sovereignty rather than colonies. Boundary: the wars, leaders and five routes to independence belong to the independence owner.

## 2. The four structural problems

One: economically underdeveloped industrially and agriculturally; factory industry existed and was stimulated by the Second World War but stood well below Europe, the USA and Japan; markets restricted by mass poverty; exports difficult against advanced industrial competition; narrow export bases - Chile copper, Cuba sugar and tobacco, Bolivia tin at 80 per cent of revenue in the 1950s; agriculture backward because cheap plentiful labour removed the incentive to modernise. Two: massive population rise from advances in medicine and hygiene and the Church's refusal to promote birth control; holdings too small; scarce urban jobs; shanty towns, called favelas in Brazil, without water, sewage disposal or electricity; a widening rich-poor gap. Three: political systems inadequate; no democratic tradition except in Chile; domination by wealthy landowners and military dictators; landowners using the army to block reform or remove reformers in Guatemala 1954, Bolivia 1964, Brazil 1964, Argentina 1966 and Chile 1973 - 1950 was Arbenz's election year, not the reversal. Four: heavy foreign investment with profits taken out; American-owned oil extraction in Bolivia and Venezuela; the United Fruit Company as Guatemala's biggest landowner; Chilean copper and Cuban sugar under United States control. Remedies attempted: the Organization of American States and the Latin American Common Market of 1960 with some success in reducing tariffs; United Nations technical experts and conferences; the Alliance for Progress launched by Kennedy in 1961, with genuine intent, good use of some aid, mixed motives, an anti-communist purpose and loans tied to purchases of United States products.

## 3. How United States power worked

Motive as recorded: aid provided, motives not entirely selfless, political influence expected in return to prevent socialist or communist governments, and no hesitation in intervening where a government was deemed unacceptable; regimes labelled communist were often simply pursuing policies against American interests. The Good Neighbor interlude: the Montevideo non-intervention principle of December 1933, the Platt Amendment abrogated in 1934, marines withdrawn from Nicaragua in 1933 and Haiti in 1934. After 1945 the region was dragged into the Cold War, with Republican presidents fearing a Soviet-Latin American Axis. The intervention list: Guatemala 1954, Cuba 1961 (the Bay of Pigs; the 1962 crisis was the missile confrontation, not a removal attempt), Brazil 1964, the Dominican Republic 1965, Chile 1970-3, Nicaragua from 1979, Panama 1989, Haiti 1994; the attempt to remove Castro failed. The five-rung ladder: structural presence; economic leverage, including aid cut for Castro and Allende and resumed on a change of government, tied loans, and Venezuelan steel imports cut from 550,000 to 110,000 tonnes for five years in March 1985; covert destabilisation, including Operation Mongoose from November 1961, bombing of Cuban plant, Nixon's reported demand to make the Chilean economy scream and Johnson's instruction on Goulart; proxy invasion in Guatemala, at the Bay of Pigs and through the Contras; and troops, with 20,000 in the Dominican Republic in 1965 and at least 2,000 civilians killed in Panama in December 1989. Multilateral use: 17 to 1 against Guatemala; Cuba expelled in 1962; and, in the other direction, 20 to 1 against the United States over Panama and 16 to 2 against United States policy on Nicaragua in April 1986. Limits: a local partner was needed, the Bay of Pigs failed, Guatemala spread the slogan "Yankee go home", and Congress stopped Contra aid in March 1987.

## 4. Mexico: revolution, 1876-1920

Texas a state of the USA in 1845; about a third of Mexican territory lost in the war of 1846-8, the treaty providing a $15 million payment plus United States assumption of $3.25 million of its citizens' claims, which the account states as $18 million paid with debts waived. Diaz ruled 1876-1910 with relative stability, developing oil, mining, manufacturing, education, health care and infrastructure largely through foreign investment, while most industry was foreign-owned, little wealth percolated down, unions were quickly suppressed and dependence on the USA grew uncomfortable. In 1910 Diaz, aged 80, was declared winner by a huge majority in a blatantly fraudulent election and revolution forced his resignation. The following decade was extremely confused and became a civil war between revolutionaries and counter-revolutionaries; the connecting figures are Madero, Zapata, Villa, Carranza and Obregon. United States troops entered northern Mexico in 1916-17 and war was narrowly averted. The Constitution was promulgated at Queretaro on 5 February 1917: Article 27 asserted national ownership of land, water and subsoil and authorised expropriation including the ejido; Article 123 guaranteed union rights, the right to strike, an eight-hour day, a minimum wage and workplace protections. After 1920 the party later known as the Institutional Revolutionary Party gradually gained control with a programme of economic reform to narrow the rich-poor gap.

## 5. Mexico: the institutional order, 1920-2000

Party sequence 1929, restructured in 1938 to incorporate labour, peasant, military and popular sectors, renamed in 1946; in power until 2000. Cardenas nationalised oil in 1938 to public delight; the USA and UK boycotted Mexican goods; Mexico sold to Nazi Germany and Fascist Italy; a compensation agreement followed in November 1941; Mexico joined the Allies in 1942 and the "Aztec Eagles" flew with the American Fifth Air Force in the liberation of the Philippines in 1945. Twenty-five post-war years of progress with growth averaging 3-4 per cent, the sixth largest petroleum producer in the world, and manufacturing employment overtaking agriculture by 1960; strain from the late 1960s through extravagant borrowing against expected oil revenue. On 2 October 1968 troops fired on an estimated 10,000 students; estimates of those killed vary between 30 and 300; the snipers were presidential guards ordered to provoke the army; the Olympics opened ten days later and the USA sent riot control equipment. By 1980 it was estimated that about nine-tenths of Mexico's wealth was owned by fewer than half a million people out of 85 million. In 1982 banks were nationalised, the currency devalued by 70 per cent and public services drastically cut, with an IMF loan and rescheduling of half of $96 billion; the earthquake of 8.1 magnitude killed at least 7,000 and the agreement proceeded; de la Madrid described the limit of sustaining a net transfer of resources that violates economic logic and is tremendously inequitable; oil prices collapsed in 1986. Privatisation brought inflation to single figures and eliminated the deficit without helping unemployment; the free trade agreement entered force in January 1994; the Zapatistas rose in Chiapas on 1 January 1994 and Colosio was shot in March; the peso collapsed, with Peter Calvert recording the effect on international finance's willingness to support governments dependent on foreign loans; about a third of Mexicans were below the poverty line in 2000; Congress was lost in 1997 with 38 per cent, and Fox won in 2000 with 43 per cent against 36, ending 71 years of single-party rule.

## 6. Populism: Vargas and Peron

Brazil's independence from Portugal declared 7 September 1822 and recognised in 1825; a monarchy to 1889, ruled mainly by military dictatorships to 1930. In 1930 the army replaced the ultra-conservative landowners' government with Vargas; economic planning began and industry was encouraged, with jobs in electrical and steel manufacture; he acted as a dictator from 1937 with no elections and led the Estado Novo from 1937 to 1945; the army forced him out in 1945, he was re-elected in 1950, stayed to 1954 and committed suicide calling his death a sacrifice on behalf of the Brazilian workers. Kubitschek won in 1955, raised army pay as his first act, built Brasilia and completed his term in 1961; Quadros resigned after seven months and Goulart succeeded. Peron held office in Argentina 1943-55 and 1973-4 - posts in the military government from 1943, then elected president on 24 February 1946 and inaugurated on 4 June 1946. Both were impressed by Fascist Italy and Nazi Germany and adopted mass mobilisation, both won huge support from the poor working classes in the mass union movement, neither was really like Mussolini or Hitler, and their governments are best summed up as nationalism plus social reform; Hobsbawm's formulation is that European fascist movements destroyed labour movements while the Latin American leaders they inspired created them. The populist bargain gave recognition and benefits to urban workers, protection and credit to industrialists and patronage to state clients, financed by growth and avoiding the landed elite; the landless rural population stayed outside it, so land reform stayed off the agenda, and when growth stopped the army was asked to resolve the impasse.

## 7. The economic arc: commodities to industry

A commodity price fall hits export earnings, government revenue, employment and the currency at once and is outside government control. Recurrence: Venezuelan oil revenue in the early 1980s, Mexican oil in 1986, Ecuadorean oil in 1998 with inflation over 40 per cent and poverty around 70 per cent, Cuban sugar and tobacco fungus in 1980, Chilean copper falling about 30 per cent under Allende. The war stimulated factory industry by making European manufactures almost impossible to obtain. Import-substituting industrialisation replaced imported manufactures behind tariff and licensing protection with state investment, subsidised credit and planning; its doctrine came from the United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America, known as CEPAL, led from 1950 by Raul Prebisch, whose centre-periphery analysis held that terms of trade move against primary producers. The strategy began as improvisation and was theorised afterwards. Four internal limits followed in order: a domestic market too poor to absorb output, because the strategy industrialised around the agrarian question; continued imports of capital goods, fuel and inputs, with Brazilian growth falling from 10 to around 5 per cent after 1974 as oil imports rose; protection removing the pressure to become competitive; and fiscal deficits. Borrowing then sustained the strategy, at its height from 1973 to 1982, with lenders who had refused Goulart happy to finance a right-wing regime that had eliminated communist influence.

## 8. Guatemala, 1944-1996

United Fruit Company active from 1901; by the Second World War it controlled almost half the best agricultural land and was majority share-owner in the railways and electricity system, and was reluctant to finance new roads because that would cut railway profits. About 40 per cent of the population were Mayan Indians who did not speak Spanish, mainly landless. October 1944: general strike and the fall of Jorge Ubico, dictator since 1931, to a mixed uprising of officers, students and liberal intellectuals. Arevalo, elected in 1945 for a six-year term and serving to 1951, confiscated many foreign-owned estates and redistributed them, introduced a minimum wage, built houses, hospitals and schools, required landowners to house farm labourers and permitted parties and unions with restricted union powers. Under Arbenz land reform proceeded, compensation was offered and it was claimed to be insufficient; the standard facts are Decree 900 of June 1952 redistributing uncultivated land on large holdings with compensation in government bonds, uncultivated United Fruit Company holdings among those affected, and rejection of the compensation by the company and the United States government. Early 1954: an Organization of American States resolution on communist domination passed 17 to 1 with Guatemala dissenting. June 1954: an American-backed force under Colonel Castillo Armas invaded from Honduras and Nicaragua while American planes bombed Guatemala City; the Guatemalan army took no part and did not defend Arbenz, who resigned; the operation's codename was PBSUCCESS and the resignation came on 27 June 1954. Recorded verdict: the Eisenhower government overestimated the threat and was prepared to sacrifice a reforming government despite violating non-intervention and souring regional relations; "Yankee go home" spread. Aftermath: Armas dictator, parliament disbanded, communists arrested; Armas assassinated 1957; Ydigoras next; a 1960 uprising put down with American help; over thirty years of virtual civil war with guerrilla attacks, vigilante groups and government death squads; 3,252 political murders calculated in the four months from October 1979 to January 1980 under General Fernando Romeo Lucas Garcia; Rios Montt installed March 1982 and replaced August 1983; an elected civilian president in the later 1980s feared removal by United States intervention if reform went too far; constitutional reform in August 1994 with Church and United Nations involvement; a ceasefire from 20 March 1996 and a formal peace agreement in December 1996 legalising the Guatemalan National Revolutionary Unity and granting a partial amnesty; some 200,000 killed in 36 years, well over half Mayan Indians targeted because of their militancy.

## 9. Cuba: why Castro came to power

Five recorded causes. One: resentment of American influence dating to 1898, with American companies holding controlling interests in all Cuban industries, owning half the land, about three-fifths of the railways, all electricity and the entire telephone system, the USA the main export market, and the American ambassador in Havana called the second most important man in Cuba - a connection that need not have been resented so much had it produced an efficiently run country. Two: prosperity by regional standards but excessive dependence on sugar, wealth concentrated in few hands, unemployment of about 8 per cent during the five harvest months and over 30 per cent for the rest of the year, no unemployment benefit, unions dominated by year-round sugar-mill workers doing nothing to help, and high social tension. Three: no effective political system; Batista's coup of 1952; no reforms; Hugh Thomas recording that he gave too little time to affairs of state while attending to his private affairs and foreign fortunes; a corrupt and brutal regime. Four: with no prospect of peaceful social revolution, the feeling grew that violent revolution was necessary; Castro a young lawyer defending the poor, more a liberal nationalist than a communist, seeking to remove Batista and corruption and to introduce limited land reform; the 1953 attempt and two years in jail; guerrilla warfare and urban sabotage; control of the mountainous east and north and popular support won by carrying through land reform there - the connecting facts being the Moncada attack of 26 July 1953 giving the 26th of July Movement its name, the Granma landing of December 1956 with about eighty companions including Raul Castro and Che Guevara, and the Sierra Maestra base. Five: savage reprisals, torture and murder of suspects driving middle-class support to Castro; army morale crumbling in the summer of 1958; the USA cutting off arms supplies; Che Guevara taking the main road across the island in September; Batista fleeing on 1 January 1959 and a liberal government set up with Castro at its head.

## 10. Cuba: revolution into alignment

Relations did not deteriorate immediately and Castro was thought at worst a social democrat. The escalation: nationalisation of American-owned estates and factories; Eisenhower's threat to stop importing sugar, forcing a trade agreement with Russia; the threat carried out in July 1960 and the USSR promising to buy the sugar; confiscation of all remaining American property; diplomatic relations broken in January 1961 with Soviet aid already arriving; about 1,400 men landing at the Bay of Pigs in April 1961 from American bases in Guatemala and being crushed by Castro's forces and two jet planes; and only later that year Castro's announcement that he was a Marxist and Cuba a socialist country. The campaign continued with sunken merchant ships, sabotage and invasion exercises, and Operation Mongoose was approved in November 1961; Castro appealed to the USSR for military help, and the missile deployment was in part a gesture of solidarity with an ally under constant threat. Owned here from the settlement: the promise that the USA would not invade again; Castro's fury at Khrushchev for deserting him apparently without consulting the Cubans, with relations extremely cool for several years; expulsion from the Organization of American States in 1962, which only made Cuba more dependent on the USSR; and cool relations and regional suspicion afterwards. The strategic causes, the quarantine, the crisis management, the hotline and the Test Ban Treaty belong to the Cold War owner.

## 11. Cuba: the record and the cost

Harkness records that in his first ten years Castro took a poor and backward country by the scruff of the neck and shook it into new and radically different patterns of life. Land taken over with collective farms, factories and businesses nationalised, attempts to modernise sugar and introduce new industries, social reform in education, housing, health, medical facilities and communications, equality for black people and more rights for women, and touring cinemas, theatres, concerts and art exhibitions. By the end of the 1970s all children were receiving some education instead of fewer than half before 1959, sanitation, hygiene and health care were much improved, unemployment and corruption were reduced, and the government seemed popular with the vast majority - achieved against continual harassment, a trade embargo and bomb attacks on factories, oil refineries and sugar refineries, with the campaign under Nixon amounting to United States government-sponsored state terrorism and the embargo more stringent than ever in the 1990s, condemned by the European Union. Exported revolution: Argentina crushed in early 1964, rebels helped to power in Algeria, unsuccessful involvement in the former Belgian Congo, Che Guevara's Bolivian expedition ending in capture and execution in October 1967 - elsewhere recorded as murder on Central Intelligence Agency orders after the army with United States advisers defeated the campaign - the Marxist MPLA backed in Angola in 1975 and Mengistu in Ethiopia in 1977; Castro a hero to most Third World countries until his popularity slumped over the Soviet invasion of Afghanistan in 1979. Diversification was disappointing, so the economy still depended on the sugar harvest, the world sugar price and a single willing buyer; the 1980 fungus infections produced crisis, rising unemployment, emigration, food rationing and heavy Soviet subsidy; the USSR's break-up in 1991 removed the most powerful supporter, yet the regime survived, boosted by tourism, by Venezuelan oil at favourable prices agreed in October 2000 and later supplied in exchange for thousands of Cuban doctors and teachers, and constrained by continued isolation - the only country in the Americas not invited to the Quebec summit in 2001 - and by a further crisis in 2002. Raul Castro took the presidency in February 2008; mobile phones were legalised in March 2008; peasants were allowed to cultivate unused collective land with improved agricultural pricing. Master judgement: the revolution transformed distribution and did not transform production.

## 12. Chile, 1970-1990

Chile alone had a democratic tradition, three party groups, an army playing little part in politics and a usually respected constitution barring immediate re-election. Frei, elected 1964, cut inflation from 38 to 25 per cent, made the rich pay taxes, built 360,000 houses, more than doubled schools, confiscated over 1,200 inefficiently run holdings for landless peasants and took about half the American-owned copper holdings with compensation, earning American admiration and lavish aid; by 1967 the left wanted full copper nationalisation and the right thought he had gone too far, the 1969 drought cost a third of the harvest, and miners were killed by government troops. Allende won September 1970 with 36 per cent against 35, the world's first Marxist leader voted into power, believing communism could succeed without violent revolution. He inherited inflation over 30 per cent, unemployment at 20 per cent, stagnating industry and 90 per cent of the population in such poverty that half the children under fifteen suffered malnutrition. Policies: wage increases of about 40 per cent with prices frozen; the remainder of copper, plus textiles and banks, nationalised, the copper nationalisation enacted in 1971; land redistribution speeded up; the army given the biggest pay rise; relations restored with Cuba, China and East Germany. Support held at 49.7 per cent in the municipal elections of April 1971 and slightly more seats in 1973. Five pressures: farmers due to lose land stopped sowing and slaughtered cattle, cutting production; investors were frightened off; copper brought strikes, falling production and a price fall of about 30 per cent; the Movement of the Revolutionary Left seized farms; and the USA did everything in its power to undermine the economy while neighbours feared exported revolution. The precipitant was the September 1976 election, which Allende could not contest, and the fear - perhaps with justification, or so they afterwards claimed - that he would change the constitution or postpone it by referendum, since no Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out of power. A massive strike and the army's support produced the September 1973 coup organised by leading generals with Pinochet coming to the fore; left-wing leaders were murdered or imprisoned; Allende was reported to have committed suicide, with the cause controversial and a 2011 Chilean television report of a document said to prove assassination; the Central Intelligence Agency, helped by Brazil's military regime, played a vital role, had considered a coup since 1970, and Nixon was reported as demanding that the Chilean economy be made to scream. American aid, reduced under Allende, was stepped up afterwards. Inflation fell from around 1,000 per cent to manageable proportions by 1980; around 3,000 people were killed or disappeared in 17 years with tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled. Elections were allowed in 1989 and Pinochet's candidate lost with under 30 per cent; Aylwin became president in 1990 while the 1980 constitution, in force from 11 March 1981, kept Pinochet Commander-in-Chief for eight more years; he stepped down in 1998, was arrested in London and held 16 months on a Spanish request over torture of Spanish citizens, returned on medical grounds in March 2000, faced over 250 charges, was ruled too ill to stand trial in July 2001 and died in 2006 aged 91. After 1990: Aylwin, Frei, Lagos from 2000 as the first socialist president since Allende, and Bachelet from 2006 as Chile's first woman president, whose father had been a victim of the regime; state copper revenues deployed and capital controls introduced when the classic policies proved insufficient, Codelco having been the state-owned copper corporation since 1976; Pinera won the run-off in January 2010; an earthquake shortly afterwards killed 500, left around a million homeless and caused damage estimated at between $15 and 30 billion.

## 13. Military regimes and the national security state

Goulart proposed a wider franchise, limits on multinational profit remittance, diplomatic relations with the USSR, nationalisation of oil refineries and opposition to sanctions on Cuba; a millionaire landowner and devout Roman Catholic, the communist charge against him could not by any stretch be taken seriously. Johnson told the American ambassador that everything possible must be done to help overthrow this left-wing government; Goulart was removed in April 1964 and naval vessels with ammunition and fuel were readied in case the Brazilian military needed assistance. Twenty years of military rule: parties banned, strict press censorship, jails filled with political prisoners, trade unionists and left-wing students a favourite target, reports of widespread torture and violent treatment of prisoners, and gradual relaxation after Geisel became president in 1974. The economic miracle was financed by extensive foreign borrowing from lenders who had refused Goulart, with 10 per cent annual growth and quadrupled exports from 1968 to 1974, falling to around 5 per cent after 1974; by 1980 there were huge foreign debts, a slump in export markets, a yawning gap between rich and poor and unrest among the rural poor in the north east. In 1985 the military decided it was an appropriate time to hand power to civilians; Neves died before being sworn in; Sarney suspended interest payments in February 1987; Collor devalued, cut expenditure and froze accounts over 50,000 cruzeiros for 18 months, depriving the economy of some $80 billion and causing business failures and massive unemployment, before being impeached at the end of 1992. Argentina's junta of 1976-83 conducted the dirty war; a human rights commission charged it with 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people - the body being the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons, whose 1984 report is *Nunca Mas*; the April 1982 Falklands invasion, attempted for popularity, ended in defeat and left an unprecedentedly high foreign debt and inflation of around 900 per cent. Menem's programme from 1991 removed protections, imposed austerity and privatised, to no avail; recession from September 1998; two IMF rescues in 2001; near-collapse, panic and December rioting forcing the president's resignation. Bolivia's economy was controlled by the USA after the Second World War, which processed all its tin exports and in the 1950s insisted that scarce capital be used for debt service rather than development; the military seized power in 1964, defeated Che Guevara's campaign in 1967 with United States advisers, and held control until 1982, after which presidents took care not to antagonise the USA. Also on the record: from 1975 the Southern Cone governments of Chile, Argentina, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia and Brazil coordinated cross-border pursuit, detention, transfer and killing of opponents under the name Operation Condor.

## 14. Nicaragua, 1912-1990

Zelaya, president 1893-1909, improved education, transport and communications, expanded coffee exports and built Managua as a neutral capital, while having opponents arrested, tortured and executed, looting the state, selling concessions and invading Guatemala in 1906 and then Honduras. United States marines arrived in 1912 and stayed to 1925; violence resumed within months and forces returned; the Somoza family came to power with American approval in 1927 and troops stayed until 1933. The family ruled with an iron fist until 1979, exiling opponents and amassing fortunes, and Nicaraguan territory and troops were used three times for attacks on governments the USA disapproved of - Guatemala 1954, Cuba 1961, the Dominican Republic 1965. Anastasio Somoza became an embarrassment even to the USA, and Carter's urging of reform had little effect. In 1979 the Sandinista National Liberation Front drove him out, named after Augusto Sandino, who led an unsuccessful revolution in 1933 and was murdered on Somoza's orders; the movement had widespread popular support and support from a section of the Roman Catholic Church critical of the excesses of capitalism. Reforms: 5 million acres redistributed including confiscated Somoza property to about 100,000 families, a literacy drive, and health improvements that eliminated polio; Oxfam reported in 1985 that the government's efforts and commitment were exceptional; a mixed economy of state and private business was allowed. The Reagan administration from 1981 saw them as dangerous communists, stopped all aid, began a trade blockade and credit squeeze and financed the Contras, who blew up bridges, schools and health clinics and burned crops; after they mined three harbours the International Court of Justice condemned the Central Intelligence Agency's backing and ordered compensation, which the USA rejected. The Latin American parliament condemned United States policy 16 to 2 in April 1986; Congress stopped Contra aid in March 1987 after Irangate; Arias of Costa Rica won the Nobel Peace Prize for a regional peace plan that proved difficult to carry out because destabilisation continued and Honduras and El Salvador declined to cooperate under United States pressure. In 1990, despite a campaign threat that violence would continue if the Sandinistas won, Violeta Barrios de Chamorro was elected - the first female president elected in Latin America - and Ortega and the Sandinistas accepted the result; she disarmed guerrilla groups, most fighting ceased and some social reforms survived, but the economy was in ruins. Ortega lost again in 1996 and 2001 and returned in November 2006 with 62 per cent, less revolutionary and claiming Christian rather than Marxist motivation; the 1998 hurricane killed 9,000, left around 2 million homeless and caused $10 billion of damage; $4.5 billion of debt was waived in 2004; the country was the poorest in the Western hemisphere by January 2007 and still the poorest in Latin America in 2011 with 46 per cent below the poverty line; the Supreme Court cancelled the consecutive-term ban and he won November 2011 with 62 per cent, while democrats claimed he was becoming a dictator like the Somozas.

## 15. The debt crisis of the 1980s

The region had been obliged to follow policies described as neo-liberalism - privatisation, deregulation of finance, cuts in social spending and austerity - characterised as designed to benefit a wealthy elite at home, foreign investors, big business and bankers, particularly in the USA. Governments were forced to borrow massively from foreign banks to develop amenities and industries, with borrowing at its height from 1973 until 1982; in 1982 the seven largest United States banks made 60 per cent of their profits from interest on Third World loans, against 2 per cent in 1970. The doubling of American interest rates in 1979-81 left many debtors unable to pay even the interest; unpaid interest was added to the existing debt and new borrowing was required merely to service the old. By 1985 the region owed some $368 billion, with a constant drain of capital to the USA, and by 1987, as export earnings declined, the situation approached catastrophe. Brazil suspended interest payments in February 1987 and Mexico considered repudiation; the IMF and the World Bank arranged credits for Brazil, and Mexico secured an annual loan for thirty years and rescheduled its debts, with similar arrangements helping other debtors. Source control: the account's Brazilian debt and IMF credit magnitudes are internally inconsistent across passages and are not asserted here. Conditionality: rescue is new credit conditional on policy change rather than forgiveness, so the range of budget outcomes an election can produce narrows. Four transmission channels reached households - the budget, the currency, the price level and the employment contract - and a fifth political effect followed, since adjustment was imposed by creditors and administered by new democracies rather than by the military governments that had borrowed. Recoveries must be conceded: Brazil brought inflation and government debt under control and was the sixth largest economy by the end of 2011; Argentina grew at about 8 per cent and paid off all remaining IMF debts in January 2006; $4.5 billion of Nicaraguan debt was waived in 2004.

## 16. The neoliberal turn and its politics

The package comprised privatisation, deregulation of finance, cuts in social spending, austerity, removal of protectionist trade and business regulation and trade agreements; it is conventionally called the Washington Consensus after John Williamson's 1989 formulation. Mexico's privatisation brought inflation to single figures and eliminated the deficit without helping unemployment, and the free trade agreement of January 1994 raised exports and investment while farmers suffered from increased American agricultural imports, with opinions differing on the net effect. Argentina's programme from 1991 was all to no avail and ended in recession, two IMF rescues, panic, riots and a resignation. Brazil's *Plano Real* from 1994 used large tax increases, public-sector wage reductions and privatisation to cut inflation from a thousand per cent to single figures by 1997, revived overseas markets and won re-election in 1998, before a renewed crisis required massive IMF credits amid unrest, crime and violence. Chile's post-transition governments judged the classic policies not sufficient for full recovery and broke the rules with a dose of state intervention, deploying the revenues of the state-owned copper corporation Codelco and introducing capital controls. Lula, though a socialist, judged the crisis serious enough to require non-socialist solutions and accepted IMF conditions while introducing anti-poverty programmes and raising the minimum wage by 25 per cent. Opposition to trade agreements included Lula's rejection of a hemispheric area, Ecuadorean demonstrations in 2006 and Morales's description of the area as an agreement to legalize the colonization of the Americas; the USA made separate agreements with Mexico, Chile and Guatemala to divide them from the rest. Profile: effective against inflation, ineffective against unemployment and poverty - which is why administrators judged it a success and voters a failure. Three reasons consent was not bought: diffuse benefits against concentrated costs, external authorship that no election could repudiate, and the sequence by which dictatorships borrowed and democracies repaid.

## 17. Society from below

Liberation theology accepted many of Marx's theories though not his atheism and stressed the church's mission to the poor and oppressed; large numbers of priests held left-wing views, some supported revolution, many were arrested and some killed, and Archbishop Oscar Romero of San Salvador was murdered by United States-backed paramilitaries in 1980. Aristide, twice removed from the Haitian presidency in 1991 and 2004, was a former priest influenced by liberation theology; a section of the Church supported the Sandinistas; the Church helped mediate Guatemala's constitutional reform of August 1994; Archbishop Blanco condemned Venezuela's elite in 1957 and the Church with Human Rights Watch accused Chavez of creating a climate of fear in 2009. Labour's arc runs from creation by populist leaders, through targeting under military rule, to the Workers' Party presidency from 2002; the counter-case is Cuba, where unions of permanently employed sugar-mill workers did nothing for the seasonally unemployed. Peasant movements: land reform carried through in Cuban rebel zones; 5 million acres to about 100,000 families in Nicaragua; over 1,200 holdings under Frei; the Zapatista rising of 1 January 1994 in Chiapas, crushed in days and then converted into an internet campaign. Indigenous peoples: about 40 per cent of Guatemalans were Mayan Indians, mainly landless, supplying well over half of the some 200,000 killed; the Chiapas majority were poverty-stricken Mayan Indians without land; Evo Morales, an Aymara Indian elected in December 2005, was the first Bolivian president from the country's ethnic majority, reaching office through a coca growers' union formed after near-eradication by 2001 destroyed livelihoods, allied with the opponents of a water privatisation that had doubled prices. *Bolsa Familia* paid monthly grants conditional on school attendance and health checks, helping some 7.5 million families by 2008, alongside a 25 per cent rise in the minimum wage, so that probably a majority of over 190 million Brazilians could be deemed middle-class for the first time. Women: Chamorro in 1990, Bachelet from 2006, Fernandez de Kirchner from 2007 and re-elected in October 2011, Rousseff from January 2011; Venezuela required at least 50 per cent female candidates; Cuba's reforms included more rights for women and equality for black people. Master judgement: access to power widened by class, ethnicity and gender far more than the distribution of land and income changed.

## 18. Human rights, memory and transition

The record: 2,300 political murders, over 10,000 political arrests and the disappearance of up to 30,000 people charged against the Argentine junta by a human rights commission, the body being the National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons, which reported in 1984 as *Nunca Mas*; around 3,000 killed or disappeared in Chile over 17 years with tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled, addressed by the National Commission for Truth and Reconciliation reporting in 1991 and the later National Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture; some 200,000 killed in Guatemala in 36 years, well over half Mayan Indians, with 3,252 political murders in four months from October 1979; reports of widespread torture in Brazil with no figure; estimates of 30 to 300 killed at Tlatelolco; at least 2,000 civilians killed in Panama in December 1989. Immunity was a term of the transitions: Chile's 1980 constitution, in force from 11 March 1981, kept Pinochet Commander-in-Chief for eight years after the presidency, Argentina had pardon laws, and Guatemala's December 1996 agreement granted a partial amnesty to all participants as the price of ending a 36-year war. The breach came from outside first - Pinochet arrested in London in 1998 and held 16 months on a Spanish extradition request over torture of Spanish citizens - and then from inside, with over 250 charges in Chile, a ruling in July 2001 that he was too ill to stand trial, and the cancellation of the Argentine pardon laws in 2006, after which many military and police personnel who thought they were safe were arrested and tried, to the government's popularity. Four reasons accountability was slow: negotiated exits with built-in immunity, a method designed to defeat proof, compromised courts, police and armies, and new democracies absorbed by debt and austerity. Commissions and trials are sequential rather than alternative. Current linkage, used once: on 19 March 2026 the Office of the United Nations High Commissioner for Human Rights reported United Nations experts warning of alarming setbacks in Argentina's transitional justice process on the fiftieth anniversary of the coup, and restating that the obligation to guarantee truth, justice, reparation, memory and non-repetition is not optional.

## 19. The challenge to United States domination

The end of the Cold War removed the justification rather than the capability, and states could no longer be accused of aiming to join a communist bloc; the demand was that the neo-liberal-style policies favoured by the USA be abandoned and that foreign companies hand over more profits to the state; and the spread of genuine democracy supplied the mechanism. Chavez, elected 1998 with 56 per cent after two failed coups, aimed to free Venezuela from United States influence without abandoning capitalism, ended military ties, supported Cuba, tightened control of the state oil company, introduced a Hydrocarbons Law barring private majority ownership in joint ventures, and nationalised oil in 2007, then electricity, telecommunications and gold in 2011; the 2002 coup by army sections, oil executives and right-wingers partly financed by the USA failed when loyal officers rescued him amid mass demonstrations - the first serious blow against United States influence in South America. United Nations statistics record poverty at 54 per cent in 1998 and 28 per cent in 2008, alongside the Bolivar Plan 2000, worker co-operatives, AIDS funding, a 50 per cent female candidate rule and a $111 million hospital plan in 2008; criticisms include alienation of business, Church and left, the 2009 climate-of-fear accusation, falling local votes and continued oil dependence at around 90 per cent of export revenue with a contraction of about 3 per cent in 2009. The sequence: Lula 2002, Kirchner 2003, Morales December 2005, Bachelet January 2006, Correa and Ortega 2006, with Mexico moving the other way to Fox in 2000. Graded three ways: most decisive - Venezuela, Bolivia, Cuba, Ecuador, Argentina; middle of the road - Brazil, Nicaragua, Uruguay; divided from the rest by separate United States trade agreements - Mexico, Chile, Guatemala. Kirchner rejected neo-liberal economics and automatic alignment, criticised the IMF, strengthened ties with Venezuela and Mercosur, and paid off all remaining IMF debts in January 2006 amid 8 per cent growth continued under Fernandez, with the poverty rate halved and record employment by October 2011. Correa declared the long neo-liberal night over, forced oil contract renegotiation in 2010, achieved 7.8 per cent growth in 2011, carried a new constitution with 64 per cent in September 2008 and survived a police revolt and kidnapping in September 2010. Institutions: ALBA in 2004, with Venezuelan oil exchanged for thousands of Cuban doctors and teachers and a proposed currency, the sucre; UNASUR in 2008 with twelve members by 2011 and a planned parliament at Cochabamba; the Bank of the South in 2009 with initial capital of $20 billion, lending as an alternative to the IMF, which Jason Tockman judged to have collapsed as a channel of United States influence. Diversification: Venezuela's closest Chinese relations in the region and reserves moved to Russia, China and Brazil; China becoming Brazil's largest trading partner; Argentina's lucrative Chinese market; American planners agitated and the USA losing economic, diplomatic and military advantages. Master judgement: the political relationship changed decisively and the economic structure only marginally.

## 20. Explaining the century

Four recorded explanations of the debt crisis: excess borrowing with fragile export earnings; global financial shocks; structural dependency; and domestic governance failures - with the recorded recommendation to combine dependency with domestic institutional weakness. The recorded mechanisms of domination: economic leverage shaping policy space, strategic intervention justified by anti-communism, political signalling against governments seen as hostile, and United States power usually working through domestic elites, armies and anti-left coalitions. The analytical vocabulary: CEPAL under Prebisch from 1950 on centre-periphery and terms of trade; Frank on integration producing underdevelopment; Cardoso and Faletto on dependency operating through domestic class coalitions with an associated development possible - and Cardoso himself later implementing the *Plano Real*. Supporting evidence: the constant drain of capital northwards and some $368 billion by 1985; the net-transfer quotation; Venezuela at around 90 per cent oil; Cuba's unchanged sugar dependence; conditionality narrowing budget choice; Bolivian capital directed to debt service; profit repatriation and the roads-versus-railways case. Constraining evidence: Brazil's industrial sector at about a third of gross domestic product and sixth largest economy by 2011 against Nicaragua as the poorest in the hemisphere; Argentina's IMF repayment; the Bank of the South; documented domestic corruption from Zelaya to Collor; and the agency record from Angola and Ethiopia to the Arias plan and the failed 2002 coup. The domestic-failure explanation cannot account for simultaneity across military, single-party, democratic and socialist states in the same two years, nor for the fact that corrupt allies were sustained while non-corrupt reformers were removed. Synthesis: concentrated land produced governments needing external backing, backing preserved the land structure, the narrow market limited industrialisation, industrialisation required borrowing, and borrowing converted exposure into constraint - so the external constraint was roughly constant and the domestic configuration determined the result. Dependency describes a position rather than a fate.

## 21. Revolution, reform and the answer architecture

Two variables discriminate among the five attempts at social change: the army's position and the availability of an external patron. Provocation was constant across both routes, so the reaction was determined by the asset touched, not by the route; revolution's advantage is military and its vulnerability is economic; reform succeeded inside the permitted band, as Frei's Chile shows. Comparative points: Latin America decolonised a century earlier and shows the limits of formal independence; hegemony without administration can be harder to contest than colonial rule; and the 1980s debt mechanisms were global. Agency points: Cuba in Angola and Ethiopia; the Arias plan and its Nobel Peace Prize; the 16 to 2 and 20 to 1 votes; ALBA, UNASUR and the Bank of the South. The Cold War was the frame and the licence, not the cause: it did not create the land structure, the commodity dependence, the militarised politics or the foreign ownership, and interventions in Panama in 1989 and Haiti in 1994 followed its end. Three boundaries: the independence wars, the superpower confrontation and the thematic world economy belong to their own owners. The demand map converts eight framings into what each tests; the mark-scaled structures add the domination mechanism and two contrasting cases at 15 and the full economic sequence, genuine variation and an honest counter-position at 20. Six qualified theses and five verdict scaffolds are available, and ten factual-risk controls should be checked before submission, headed by the agency control and the attribution control. Master formulation for the whole topic: the century is the story of sovereign states with constrained sovereignty, in which the external constraint was roughly constant and the domestic configuration determined the outcome.

## Discrepancies found in the evidence base and how they were handled

| Discrepancy | ⚠️ Handling |
|---|---|
| **The Brazilian debt magnitude.** The chapter's crisis section states a Brazilian debt figure whose order of magnitude cannot be reconciled with the regional total of some $368 billion stated four lines earlier | ⚠️ Lesson 15 teaches the **event** - the suspension of interest payments in February 1987 and the consideration of repudiation by Mexico - and expressly does not assert the magnitude. ❌ Neither figure appears anywhere in this session, and a remediation entry warns against reproducing them |
| **The IMF credit magnitude for Brazil.** The same rescue is given in the crisis section in one denomination and in the Brazil country section in another, differing by three orders of magnitude, and a second Brazilian rescue later in the same section repeats the larger figure | ⚠️ Handled identically: the arrangement of credits by the IMF and the World Bank is taught as an event, the magnitudes are not asserted, and the divergence is recorded here |
| **The chronological placement of the neo-liberal framework.** The crisis section places the privatisation-and-austerity framework **before** the borrowing of 1973-82, while the country narratives place privatisation in Mexico after 1986, in Argentina from 1991 and in Brazil from 1994 | ⚠️ Lesson 16 states the problem openly and resolves it by distinguishing two phases: financial deregulation and openness to foreign capital characterising the borrowing period, and privatisation and austerity characterising the adjustment that followed. ⚠️ The account's characterisation of whom the framework served is preserved and attributed rather than adopted in the session's own voice |
| **The Nicaraguan nineteenth-century party chronology.** The extraction gives a conservative period ending in a year that cannot be right, since the liberal period that follows is dated from the same year to 1909 | ⚠️ Lesson 14 teaches the structure - alternation between liberals based in Leon and conservatives based in Granada, with Zelaya president in the period ending in 1909 - and does not reproduce the internally impossible year. ⚠️ No substitute year is invented |
| **The Guatemalan presidential name in the 1979-82 period.** The degraded page renders the surname as "Romero Garcia", which collides both with the earlier reformist president and with the archbishop murdered in El Salvador in 1980 | ✅ **Corrected in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** The president under whom the October 1979 to January 1980 count was made is **General Fernando Romeo Lucas Garcia**, and that form is now used in Lesson 8 and in register note 8. ⚠️ The elected Christian Democrat president of the later 1980s is still taught by office and party, because that surname remains unreadable in the extraction |
| **The rank and see of Oscar Romero.** The core account calls him a bishop of El Salvador | ✅ **Corrected in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** He was **Archbishop of San Salvador**, and Lesson 17, Map 1 and register note 17 now use that form while the account's wording is recorded here. ⚠️ The session renders Spanish names without diacritics throughout, so the form used is "Archbishop Oscar Romero" |
| **The Chilean election year for Bachelet.** The challenge-to-domination section dates the centre-left victory to January 2005 while the country section gives her presidency as 2006-10 | ✅ **Corrected in the repair pass of 29 September 2026.** Bachelet won the presidential runoff on **15 January 2006** and took office in March 2006, so the account's January 2005 is wrong and its own 2006-10 term is right. ⚠️ The election-sequence visual, Lesson 19, the cumulative check and register note 19 now all carry January 2006 |
| **Brazilian independence.** The core account states that Brazil gained its independence from Portugal in 1825 | ✅ **Corrected.** Independence was **declared on 7 September 1822**; **1825** is the year Portugal recognised it. ⚠️ Both dates are now taught, with the distinction stated, in Lesson 6 and register note 6 |
| **The span of the Estado Novo.** The core account dates Vargas's Estado Novo from 1939 to 1945 | ✅ **Corrected.** The Estado Novo was proclaimed in **1937** and ran to 1945, which is consistent with the same account's own statement that Vargas had been acting as a dictator since 1937. ⚠️ Lesson 6, Map 1 and register note 6 carry 1937-1945 |
| **Peron's dates.** The core account describes Peron as leader of Argentina from 1943 to 1955 | ✅ **Corrected.** He held posts in the military government that seized power in **1943** and became head of state only on being **elected president on 24 February 1946** and **inaugurated on 4 June 1946**, holding the presidency to 1955 and again in 1973-4. ⚠️ Lesson 6, the populism visual, Map 1, the concept check and register note 6 now distinguish the two, and every one of them carries the exact election and inauguration dates |
| **Arevalo's term.** The core account states that Arevalo was elected president for five years in 1945 | ✅ **Corrected.** The term was **six years, 1945 to 1951**. ⚠️ Lesson 8, Map 1 and register note 8 carry the corrected term |
| **The Guatemalan entry in the reform-blocking list.** The core account lists the army-backed reversal of reform in Guatemala under the year 1950 | ✅ **Corrected.** **1950** was the year **Arbenz was elected**; the reversal came in **June 1954**. ⚠️ Lessons 1 and 2, both visuals, Map 2, the concept check, the cumulative check, the third Mains model answer and register note 2 now carry 1954, with the 1950 election stated |
| **The Cuban entry in the intervention list.** The core account lists the attempt to remove the Cuban government under the year 1962 | ✅ **Corrected.** The removal attempt was the **Bay of Pigs landing of April 1961**; the confrontation of **October 1962** was the missile crisis, which ended in a United States pledge *not* to invade and was therefore not a removal attempt. ⚠️ Lesson 3, the opening overview and register note 3 now carry 1961 with the distinction stated |
| **The Mexican treaty payment of 1848.** The core account states that the United States paid Mexico $18 million and waived its debts | ✅ **Clarified.** The treaty provided a payment of **$15 million** to Mexico together with United States **assumption of $3.25 million** of claims by its own citizens against Mexico, which is how the larger combined figure arises. ⚠️ Lesson 4 and register notes 4 and 5 state both the account's figure and the treaty terms |
| **The Bank of the South's initial capital.** The extraction gives the figure as $20 million | ✅ **Corrected.** The bank was launched with initial capital of **$20 billion**; a $20 million capitalisation would be far too small to function as an alternative to the IMF, which is the role the same passage assigns it. ⚠️ Lesson 19, its visual and register note 19 now carry $20 billion |
| **The Chilean local-election year under Allende.** The core account places Unidad Popular's 49 per cent local result in 1972 | ✅ **Corrected.** The contest was the **municipal election of April 1971**, in which the coalition took **49.7 per cent**. ⚠️ Lesson 12, its concept check, the fourth Mains model answer and register note 12 carry the corrected date and figure |
| **Codelco's ownership.** The core account's wording implies that Codelco was taken into state hands under the post-1990 governments | ✅ **Corrected.** Chilean copper was nationalised **under Allende in 1971**, and Codelco has been the **state-owned copper corporation since 1976**, remaining state-owned through the dictatorship and after it. ⚠️ What changed after 1990 was the deployment of its revenues and the introduction of capital controls. ⚠️ Lessons 12 and 16, the programme table, the traps, the misconception note and register notes 12 and 16 are corrected |
| **The name of the North American agreement.** The core account calls it the North American Free Trade Association | ✅ **Corrected.** It is the North American Free Trade **Agreement**. ⚠️ All five occurrences, the Lesson 5 progress line and the opening overview now use the correct name |
| **The date of the Chilean constitution.** The core account refers to the constitution as introduced in 1981 | ✅ **Clarified.** The constitution was **approved in 1980** and **came into force on 11 March 1981**. ⚠️ Lessons 12 and 18, the transitional-justice visual, the concept check and register notes 12 and 18 now state both |

## The single bounded material-fact pass

⚠️ One verification pass was conducted for this session on 29 September 2026, limited to a finite list of standard connecting names and dates fixed before searching. ⚠️ The list was declared as: the hemispheric declarations of 1823 and 1904 and the Good Neighbor measures of 1933-34; the Mexican constitutional settlement of 1917 with its principal articles, the revolution's connecting names and the ruling party's naming sequence; the Guatemalan agrarian reform law and the codename and resignation date of the 1954 operation; the standard designations of the Cuban revolutionary movement and its two founding episodes; the standard designation of the Southern Cone coordinated repression; the names of the Argentine and Chilean truth bodies; the standard institutional and personal attribution of the region's structuralist and dependency vocabulary; and the standard designation of the liberalisation package. ⚠️ Each was needed because the bounded base either omits the item entirely or is damaged at exactly the point where it would appear, and its omission would leave a real syllabus gap. ⚠️ The minimum reliable source set was used, the pass stopped when the declared list was resolved, and no other item was pursued.

| Item verified | Where used | Status |
|---|---|---|
| Monroe's message of December 1823 declaring the American continents not subjects for future European colonization; the Roosevelt Corollary of December 1904 claiming an international police power | Lesson 1 section 5; Map 1; register note 1 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because neither canonical owner nor the core account states either declaration, and the topic's required scope includes the framing of United States involvement |
| The Good Neighbor Policy from 1933; the Montevideo non-intervention principle of December 1933; the abrogation of the Platt Amendment in 1934; the withdrawal of marines from Nicaragua in 1933 and the end of the Haitian occupation in 1934 | Lesson 3 section 2; Lesson 5 section 2; Map 1; register note 3 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the required scope names the Good Neighbor Policy expressly and the bounded base does not mention it |
| The Mexican Constitution promulgated at Queretaro on 5 February 1917, with Article 27 on national ownership of land, water and subsoil and the ejido and Article 123 on labour rights; the revolutionary figures Madero, Zapata, Villa, Carranza and Obregon; the ruling party's sequence of 1929, 1938 and 1946 | Lesson 4 sections 4 and 6; Lesson 5 section 1; register notes 4-5 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account carries the revolution's outcome only as "the party eventually known as" and names no constitution, while the required scope includes the Mexican Revolution and institutional order |
| The Guatemalan agrarian reform law of June 1952 redistributing uncultivated land on large holdings with compensation in government bonds, affecting uncultivated United Fruit Company holdings, with the compensation rejected as inadequate; the Central Intelligence Agency operation codename and the resignation of 27 June 1954 | Lesson 8 section 4; register note 8 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the page carrying the reform and the compensation dispute is materially degraded in the extraction and preserves only the structure of the dispute |
| The Moncada barracks attack of 26 July 1953 giving the 26th of July Movement its name; the Granma landing of December 1956 with about eighty companions including Raul Castro and Che Guevara; the Sierra Maestra base | Lesson 9 section 4; register note 9 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account describes the 1953 attempt and the guerrilla campaign without naming the movement a question would refer to |
| Operation Condor from 1975, coordinating cross-border pursuit, detention, transfer and killing of opponents among the military governments of Chile, Argentina, Uruguay, Paraguay, Bolivia and Brazil | Lesson 13 section 6; register note 13 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account records Brazilian assistance to the Chilean coup without naming the coordinated system, and the required scope includes military regimes and human rights |
| The Argentine National Commission on the Disappearance of Persons and its 1984 report *Nunca Mas*; the Chilean National Commission for Truth and Reconciliation reporting in 1991 and the later National Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture | Lesson 13 section 4; Lesson 18 section 2; register notes 13, 18 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account attributes the Argentine charges only to "a human rights commission" and names no Chilean body, while the required scope includes human rights and transitions |
| The United Nations Economic Commission for Latin America, known as CEPAL, led from 1950 by Raul Prebisch, and its centre-periphery and terms-of-trade analysis; Andre Gunder Frank on integration producing underdevelopment; Cardoso and Faletto on dependency operating through domestic class coalitions | Lesson 7 section 3; Lesson 20 section 1; register notes 7, 20 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the advanced owner uses "structural dependency" as a category without attribution and the basic owner's own control forbids naming an economist or institution **within that file**, leaving the standard vocabulary unsourced for a session that must teach the dependency debate |
| The Washington Consensus as the conventional designation of the liberalisation package, coined by John Williamson in 1989 | Lesson 16 section 1; register note 16 | ✅ Verified. ⚠️ Needed because the core account uses "neo-liberalism" with a strong characterisation of motive and supplies no neutral technical designation |

## Open gaps recorded rather than filled

1. ⚠️ **The Arbenz land reform as the core account states it.** The page carrying the decree, the extent of the expropriation and the compensation dispute is materially degraded and preserves only that compensation was offered and was claimed to be insufficient. Lesson 8 teaches that structure, adds the declared verification-pass facts and records the gap. ❌ No extent, acreage or valuation figure from the account is reproduced.
2. ⚠️ **The Mexican sequence from the 1985 earthquake to the 1986 oil collapse.** The page is partly unusable. Lesson 5 uses the phrases legible with confidence - the loss of popularity, the criticism of earthquake relief, the first electoral challengers since the 1930s, the 1986 price collapse and the de la Madrid quotation - and does not narrate the intervening steps.
3. ⚠️ **The transition passage between the chapter summary and the first section.** The page is partly unusable and supplies only the Chavez re-election years and the observations on China and on Brazil as an emerging economy. Lesson 19 uses those and records the gap.
4. ⚠️ **Haiti, the Dominican Republic, Uruguay, Peru, Colombia and Paraguay.** The core account treats these states only in passing within this chapter, and this session accordingly uses only what it states - the two Haitian coups and Aristide's formation, the 20,000 troops in the Dominican Republic in 1965, Peru's estate structure, Uruguay's middle-of-the-road position and the membership lists of the regional bodies. ⚠️ This is a bounded omission rather than an oversight: the chapter itself states that there is insufficient space to consider all the countries of Latin America and selects five for closer treatment, and this session follows that selection while adding the states the later sections treat.
5. ⚠️ **Post-2012 developments.** The core account closes at 2012 and the canonical owners carry only the dated current items recorded above. ❌ No claim about any Latin American government, economy, leader or alignment after 2012 is asserted anywhere in this session, except the single dated transitional-justice item in Lesson 18.
6. ⚠️ **Quantitative series of any kind.** ❌ No growth, output, trade, population or poverty series is asserted beyond the specific dated figures the sources state, each of which is carried with its year and, where applicable, its reporting body. ❌ No regional aggregate for debt beyond the stated total by 1985, and ❌ no regional aggregate for political killings of any kind.
7. ⚠️ **Individual caudillos.** ❌ None is named anywhere in this session, in accordance with the independence owner's explicit control, and the category is used only as a source-grounded mechanism.
8. ⚠️ **Peronist domestic policy.** The sources held for this session treat Peron only comparatively. Lesson 6 states this limit in the teaching text, teaches the form of populism from the Brazilian evidence and the comparative judgement, and declines to construct an Argentine narrative. ⚠️ This is recorded as a genuine gap in the bounded base rather than as a completed treatment.
9. ⚠️ **The two Chilean transitional-justice commissions.** Their existence, titles and reporting years were verified; ❌ their findings, figures and recommendations were not pursued and are not asserted, because the core account supplies the Chilean casualty record independently.
